Capital-First Trading

Capital-First Trading

An offline study book for learning to trade the Indian market (NSE/BSE, Zerodha Kite) with one rule above all others: protect the capital first.

This is an education book. It does not recommend any stock, strategy or broker action, and it never promises profit. Most beginners lose money; the aim here is to lose little, learn fast and decide with evidence.

Where to start

  1. Track 0: Start here - what trading is and is not, the capital-first rules, how to use this book.
  2. Track 1: Market foundations - shares, exchanges, settlement, order books, circuits, indices.
  3. Track 2: Money and math - costs, taxes, risk per trade, position sizing, drawdowns, expectancy.
  4. Track 3: Chart reading - candles, support and resistance, trends, breakouts, gaps.
  5. Track 4: Kite and execution - a safe tour of Kite using dummy data only.
  6. Track 5: Indicators - moving averages, ATR, RSI, MACD and more, and why they can disagree.
  7. Track 6: Strategy - trading styles, edge, a simple swing plan, and when not to trade.
  8. Track 7: Testing and validation - how to test an idea on past and paper data, and the traps that fool you.
  9. Track 8: Psychology and journaling - fear, greed, discipline, losing streaks, and when to stop.
  10. Track 9: Advanced (theory only) - futures, options, hedging and market data, to understand and not to trade.
  11. Track 10: Graduation gates - seven checks of what you have learned. Passing a gate never tells you to trade.

How to use this book

  • Read in order. Each chapter is short. Do the “Try it” exercises and the quiz before you move on.
  • Demos on the page are for practice. Change the numbers and see what happens.
  • All money examples are made up and for learning only.
  • A small yellow check tag means a fact (a charge, tax rate or rule) can change. Confirm it on the official source before you rely on it.
  • Some tools, such as the trade journal, the backtester and the forward-test tool, are still being built. Until then, do the paper tasks on paper.

0. Start here

  1. What trading is and is not
  2. The capital-first rules
  3. How to use this book

What trading is and is not

Hook

Asha is a hypothetical beginner. She has read a few articles about making money from the market. Her plan is to risk Rs 3,000 on one idea. She aims to make Rs 500 when it works. Before she buys or sells anything, she needs one number. That number is how often she must be right just to break even. Most beginners never work this out. This book asks this question first.

The idea

A share is a piece of a company. Buying a share does not buy a ticket with a price on it. It buys a part of the business. If a company has 1,000 shares in total and you hold 10, you own 1 percent of it. You get that same share of its profits and assets.

People often mix up three words. Each one describes how you hold money and how long you plan to hold it. None of them names a type of asset.

  • Investing means holding a business for years. Your return comes from its profits, its dividends and the growth in its value. You manage risk by spreading money across many companies, which is called diversification, and by waiting.
  • Trading means buying and selling over days to months, and following written rules. Your return comes from price moves between when you buy and when you sell. You manage risk with limits on each trade.
  • Speculation means betting on a short price move. The result is highly uncertain. People often borrow money to do it.

The label depends on how you hold something, not on what it is. The same share can be an investment for one person and a speculation for another.

A price is a meeting point, not a final judgement. A price is where one buyer and one seller agreed, at one moment. It is not a guarantee of what the company is worth. It is not a promise of where the price goes next.

Trading moves money from one person to another, before costs. In one trade, one person’s gain is another person’s loss. The exchange sits in the middle and matches the buy and sell orders. After brokerage, taxes and other charges, an active trader usually starts behind. Later chapters put numbers on this. SEBI, the market regulator, studied individual traders in the equity derivatives (F&O) segment. Its paper is called “Profitability of Individual Traders in the Equity Derivatives Segment (FY25-FY26)” and was published in August 2026. It found that 87.7% of individual traders in that segment lost money in FY26 (the year from April 2025 to March 2026). In FY25 the share was 90.9%. The study covers F&O trading only, not ordinary share buying. It looked at the top 15 brokers, which cover about 90% of individual traders. It also found that individual traders paid about Rs 25,000 crore a year in trading costs. This book looks at the study in more detail later. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI Department of Economic and Policy Analysis. Reports get updated, so check the SEBI site for the latest.)

Never mix three kinds of result.

  • Theoretical: a calculation written down before anything happens.
  • Backtested: a test run on old price data.
  • Live: real money, with orders that really get done, and real feelings.

A backtest that made money is still not a live record. This book keeps all three labels on every number.

This book promises no income. Learning to think clearly about trading is not the same as making money from it.

See it

InvestingTradingSpeculation
Time horizonYearsDays to monthsVery short; the bet is on a near move
Where the return comes fromEarnings, dividends, growth in valuePrice moves between buying and sellingA short, uncertain price move
Typical risk controlSpreading money and timeWritten limits on each tradeOften none stated; may use borrowed money
Typical mistakeSelling when prices fall; following a story everyone is excited aboutTrading too often; putting too much into one trade; having no written rulesBorrowing to buy more; treating a guess as a plan

Read down the Speculation column. Its risk-control row says that no limit is stated. That is not a method. Compare the “typical mistake” row with any habit you already have.

Worked example

Hypothetical plan: risk Rs 3,000 to make Rs 500 per trade. Let W be the win rate. This is the share of trades that win, written as a fraction. Break-even means the average gain equals the average loss.

Average gain per trade = W x 500 Average loss per trade = (1 - W) x 3,000

Set them equal. Then solve for W:

StepWorkingResult
1Set average gain equal to average loss500 x W = 3,000 x (1 - W)
2Multiply out the right side500W = 3,000 - 3,000W
3Add 3,000W to both sides3,500W = 3,000
4Divide both sides by 3,500W = 3,000 / 3,500 = 6/7
5Turn it into a percentage6/7 = 0.857…, so 85.7%

Check: when W = 6/7, the average gain is 6/7 x 500 = Rs 428.57. The average loss is 1/7 x 3,000 = Rs 428.57. The two are equal.

So the trader must win about 86 of every 100 trades to break even. This is before any costs. Costs push the needed win rate higher. See Costs on a trade.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: Sort four hypothetical plans into investing, trading or speculation.

(a) Hold a company for 5 years and reinvest the dividends. (b) Buy before results are announced and sell the next day. (c) Borrow money to double a position. (d) Make fixed monthly purchases and hold them for 10 years.

Hints:

  1. Ask “how long?”
  2. Ask “where does the return come from?”
  3. Ask “what limits the loss?”

Answer:

  • (a) Investing. The horizon is 5 years. The return comes from dividends and growth in the business’s value.
  • (b) Speculation. It is a one-day bet on an uncertain result. No written limit is described. If the plan had written per-trade rules, it would be trading. The description gives no rules.
  • (c) Speculation. The plan uses borrowed money. It has no stated limit on the loss.
  • (d) Investing. The horizon is 10 years. The plan buys regularly and earns the return from the business over time.

Independent exercise

Task: Write one paragraph about something you plan to do with money in the markets. Sort it as investing, trading or speculation. Use no hints.

Check your answer

There is no single correct answer. Check your paragraph for three things. Look for the time horizon. Look for the source of the return. Look for what limits the loss. If one is missing, your label is not yet backed up. If you have no plan yet, writing “no plan yet” is an honest answer.

Quiz

Six questions check the three definitions, the break-even sum and the claims about income.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in a journal, or on paper. No trades.

  1. Write one paragraph about why you want to learn trading.
  2. Write what would make you stop. For example: “a loss bigger than the money I set aside for trading before I started”.
  3. Do not record any live account or real trade.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The break-even win rate leaves out costs. With costs, the real figure is higher. See Costs on a trade.
  • A calculation is not a result. A backtest is not a live record.
  • Knowing the definitions shows you are learning. It does not show you make a profit.

Remember

  • Investing, trading and speculation differ by time frame, source of return and loss control. They do not differ by what you buy.
  • A price is where buyers and sellers agreed at one moment. It is not a guarantee.
  • If you risk Rs 3,000 to make Rs 500, the break-even win rate is 6/7, or 85.7%. This is before costs.
  • Keep theoretical, backtested and live results apart. Always.
  • This book promises no income.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • SEBI investor education pages.
  • SEBI study, “Profitability of Individual Traders in the Equity Derivatives Segment (FY25-FY26)”, August 2026.
  • Zerodha Varsity, as a reference only (link only, no text copied).

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • The SEBI study’s title, date and figures (87.7% of individuals lost money in FY26, 90.9% in FY25, and about Rs 25,000 crore a year in costs) come from SEBI’s own paper. The figures are for equity derivatives only. Check the SEBI site for newer reports.

The capital-first rules

Hook

A hypothetical household has Rs 4,00,000 in savings. After two bad weeks of trading, the trader thinks, “I can earn it back.” Within a month, the trading money is gone. The trader then moves money from the emergency fund to win it back. Each step broke a rule. Every one of those rules could have been written down in advance.

The idea

Capital-first means one thing. Protect the money you can least afford to lose. Do this before you think about what you might gain. The rules below are short. Each one blocks one specific way of losing money. This book calls that a loss path.

Household money is separate. Emergency funds and bills you must pay are never trading money. Do not use them to cover trading losses. Do not use them to top up a losing account.

Trading capital is money you can afford to lose entirely. You set the amount before your first trade. The number is your choice. This book shows example amounts, but it does not set one for you.

No leverage or borrowed money in the learning stage or the early live stage. Leverage means controlling a bigger position than your own money pays for. It makes losses bigger, just as it makes gains bigger (Leverage and margin).

No F&O as a starting activity. F&O means futures and options. In this book, F&O is covered as theory only, in Track 9. It is not a starting path for a beginner.

Never average down. Averaging down means buying more of something that has fallen, so your average cost goes down. It puts more money into an idea that is already failing (Portfolio exposure and concentration).

Never increase size after losses. Putting more money in after a loss makes the fall from your peak deeper and faster (Drawdown, recovery and compounding).

No-trade days are a valid result. Not trading is a decision. It is not a failure.

No daily income target drives trades. A goal like “I must earn this much today” pushes you to trade when there is no clear reason to trade. That is overtrading (Overtrading and why daily profit targets encourage it).

Read the list as a map of ways to lose money. For each rule, ask which loss path it blocks. If you cannot name the path, you do not understand the rule yet.

See it

RuleLoss mechanism it prevents
Household money is separateEmergency money used to pay for losses
Trading capital is set before any trade and is money you can afford to loseA number picked after a loss; trading with money you cannot afford to lose
No leverage or borrowed moneyLosses bigger than the money you put in (Leverage and margin)
No F&O as a starting activityBets with leverage before the basics are learned
Never average downAdding money to a failing idea (Portfolio exposure and concentration)
Never increase size after lossesA faster fall after losses (Drawdown, recovery and compounding)
No-trade days are validPressure to trade without a clear reason
No daily income target driving tradesOvertrading (Overtrading and why daily profit targets encourage it)

Scan the right-hand column first. Each entry is a way an account can shrink. Then check each rule on the left against it.

Worked example

Hypothetical household savings: Rs 4,00,000.

Trading capital set by you: Rs 50,000.

StepWorkingResult
1Trading capital as a share of savings50,000 / 4,00,000 = 0.125
2As a percentage12.5%
3Savings left untouched4,00,000 - 50,000 = Rs 3,50,000
4The trading account falls 30%30% of 50,000 = 0.30 x 50,000 = Rs 15,000
5Trading account after the fall50,000 - 15,000 = Rs 35,000
6Total savings after the loss35,000 + 3,50,000 = Rs 3,85,000
7Loss as a share of total savings15,000 / 4,00,000 = 3.75%

Check: 4,00,000 - 15,000 = 3,85,000, which matches step 6.

The loss of Rs 15,000 hurts. But it is 3.75% of total savings. The Rs 3,50,000 you did not use is still there. Before you start, ask this question: could the household absorb this loss without harm? If the honest answer is no, the trading capital is too large. This is true whatever the amount is.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: A friend asks to borrow Rs 2 lakh for “a sure intraday trade” (a trade bought and sold on the same day). Using the rules above, list the rules that are broken. Then say what you would reply.

Hints:

  1. Check the source of the money.
  2. Check for leverage.
  3. Look at the word “sure.”
  4. Ask what happens if the trade loses.

Answer: Rules broken:

  • Borrowed money. The trade would be paid for with a loan. The rules do not allow that at this stage.
  • Leverage. The borrowed money would buy a bigger position than your own capital.
  • A claim of certainty. No trade is sure. The word “sure” is a warning sign.
  • Intraday trading. In this book it is paper-only, and it is an optional later module. It is not a starting activity with real money.
  • If the trade loses, you still owe Rs 2 lakh. The loss is added to that debt.

Reply: “No. I do not borrow to trade, and no trade is sure. If I ever start, it will be with money I set aside from my own savings, and I will write the rule down first.”

Independent exercise

Task: Write three capital-first rules of your own. Give each one a number. Use no hints.

Check your answer

Check that each rule has a number. Check that it names the loss path it blocks. Here is one format that works: “Trading capital is at most [your number] of my savings, set before my first trade.” This book does not give you the number. You choose it. A rule with no number cannot be checked. A rule that cannot be checked will be bent after a loss.

Quiz

Six questions check the rules on leverage, averaging down, no-trade days and household money. They also check the capital sums.

Paper task

Mode: learning note, in Lab 3. Write it in your journal, or on paper if the journal app is not ready yet.

  1. Write your capital statement in three lines. Include total savings, the trading capital you set, and the amount left untouched.
  2. Use made-up or blank figures. Do not use your real savings.
  3. Do not record a live account.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This book does not set a trading-capital number for you. That choice is yours. You make it before your first trade.
  • The rules block some loss paths. They do not promise that any trade will work.
  • A rule written after a loss is made in the worst moment. Write your rules in advance.
  • The worked example is hypothetical. Real households differ. The test is always the same: can the loss be absorbed?

Remember

  • Household money and emergency funds are never trading capital.
  • Set trading capital before any trade. It must be money you can afford to lose.
  • Leverage, borrowed money, averaging down and adding size after losses each open a loss path.
  • A no-trade day is a valid result.
  • Rs 50,000 of trading capital out of Rs 4,00,000 is 12.5% (hypothetical). A 30% fall costs Rs 15,000, or 3.75% of savings.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • SEBI investor education pages on leverage and margin.
  • Zerodha support on margin.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • This chapter quotes no margin or leverage figures. Margin rules differ by product and change over time, and each broker sets its own policy within SEBI’s rules. Before you rely on any margin number, read the current margin pages on the Zerodha support site and SEBI’s circulars on margin. Leverage and margin explains the idea in more detail.
  • The capital sums in this chapter are round numbers for practice. The book does not suggest how much money you should put into trading.

How to use this book

Hook

A learner finishes three sections in one sitting. The learner feels confident and decides to “start trading” that evening. The book did not ask for that. Its structure is built to slow that moment down. Each chapter ends with a quiz, a paper task and a list of what the chapter does not tell you. The real-money decision sits behind separate gates. You pass those on your own schedule.

The idea

Every chapter follows the same eleven steps. Knowing the order tells you what each part is for.

  1. Hook: a rupee scenario or a common mistake.
  2. Concept: the idea, explained from the basics.
  3. Visual: a diagram or a static card.
  4. Worked example: rupee figures, with every step shown.
  5. Interactive demo, where the chapter has one.
  6. Guided exercise, with hints.
  7. Independent exercise, with the answer hidden until you try.
  8. Quiz, with a reason for each wrong option.
  9. Paper task: a journal entry, a lab run, or a Kite demo task.
  10. “What this does NOT tell you”: the limits and common misuse.
  11. Sources, with verification status.

Reading order follows the track dependencies. A dependency means one chapter needs another one first. Read Track 0 first, then Track 1, then Track 2. Finish Track 2 (money and math) before any strategy chapter.

Labs. The book comes with five small tools: Lab 1 (position size), Lab 2 (trade costs), Lab 3 (your trade journal), Lab 4 (testing a strategy on past prices) and Lab 5 (a forward test on paper). Some are still being built. Until they are ready, do each paper task on paper.

Journal modes. Every journal entry has one mode. The modes are:

  • learning note: a reflection or a note on a concept. Not a trade.
  • paper: a planned trade on paper, never sent to a broker.
  • kite_demo: a task done by looking at the Kite demo screens. The demo uses dummy data and cannot place orders.
  • forward: a paper trade, or a forward test, recorded as it happens.
  • backtest: a simulated result from the past.
  • live: a real trade with real money.

Entries made before the gates are passed are never marked live.

Offline use. After installation, every page works without internet. Adding the app to your home screen is recommended.

Backups. Export a backup file regularly. Keep a copy outside the device. By default, the app reminds you every 14 days, and after every 10 new trades. You can change both.

Gates test what you have learned. Gates G1 to G7 check your learning. If you fail a gate, you can take it again, with no timer. Passing a gate does not mean you should trade real money. That decision is still yours, and it is separate.

No pressure. There is no deadline to start real trading.

See it

flowchart LR
  R[Read the concept] --> E[Complete the exercise]
  E --> Q[Take the quiz]
  Q --> J[Record in the journal]
  J --> G[Pass a gate, when ready]

Follow the arrows from left to right. Each chapter is one pass through this loop. A gate is not part of a chapter. It is a separate check you take later.

  T02 --> T03[*How to use this book* How to use this book]

  T11 --> T12[*Exchanges, SEBI and brokers* Exchanges and participants]
  T12 --> T13[*Demat accounts and settlement* Demat and settlement]
  T13 --> T14[*How prices move: the order book* Prices and the order book]
  T02 --> T21[*Why money math comes before charts* Money math first]
  T14 --> T21

This map shows the Track 0 to Track 2 dependencies covered so far. Track 2 goes on past Why money math comes before charts. Read each arrow as “must be read before”.

Worked example

A 20-minute session:

MinutesActivity
5Read a concept chapter
2Complete one exercise
13Record one learning note, then take the quiz

Sample learning note (mode: learning note):

Trading capital: Rs 50,000 (hypothetical) Risk budget: 1% = Rs 500 Working: 1% of 50,000 = 0.01 x 50,000 = Rs 500. What I learned today: the difference between a risk budget and a trading capital figure.

The first line sets the trading capital. The second line sets the risk budget for one trade. The risk budget is the most you accept losing on one trade. Both figures are hypothetical. The sum is 1% of Rs 50,000, which is Rs 500.

Hints:

  2. If Lab 3 is not ready, write the note on paper and keep it with your study materials.

Answer: The learning note titled “What I expect to learn” exists. The backup reminder is set to the default of 14 days, or to a time you chose. One backup file is exported and copied outside the device. The place in the app depends on the journal tool once it is ready.

Check your answer

There is no single correct schedule. Check that your plan follows the arrows. For example, read What trading is and is not before The capital-first rules and How to use this book. Read Companies, shares and listings before Exchanges, SEBI and brokers. Check that the plan includes quizzes and paper tasks, not only reading. Your plan should set no deadline to start trading. This book sets none either.

Quiz

Six questions check the journal modes, the gates, the backup timing and the limits of the book.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Set up the journal. Record your first learning note. Export your first backup. Store that backup outside the device.

  1. Open the journal, or prepare a paper notebook if the app is not ready.
  2. Record the learning note “What I expect to learn”.
  3. Export the first backup and store a copy outside the device.
  4. Note the date of your next backup reminder.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A learning record is not a trading record. Do not label a learning note as a trade.
  • Passing a gate shows you learned something. It is not proof of profit. It is not permission to trade real money.
  • The book cannot place orders for you. It works offline and has no broker connection.
  • Entries before the gates are passed are never marked live.

Remember

  • Every chapter follows the same eleven steps.
  • Track dependencies set the reading order. Track 2 comes before any strategy chapter.
  • Journal modes: learning note, paper, kite_demo, forward, backtest, live.
  • Back up at least every 14 days by default, and keep a copy outside the device.
  • Gates test learning, can be retaken with no timer, and do not decide anything about real money.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Apple’s Safari team (WebKit) says Safari deletes a website’s saved data after seven days of Safari use without you visiting that site. A web app added to the Home Screen has its own day count, which resets each time you use it, and Apple does not expect its data to be deleted. This is why regular backups matter. (As of 11 Nov 2026, WebKit blog, “Full Third-Party Cookie Blocking and More”. Browser rules change, so check webkit.org.)
  • Browsers can ask permission to keep a site’s data safe from automatic clean-up. This is called persistent storage. Support differs between browsers and versions, so check the MDN page on “StorageManager: persist()” for the current list. (As of 11 Nov 2026, MDN Web Docs.)
  • The place in the app for the backup reminder is not fixed yet, because the journal tool is not built.

Companies, shares and listings

Hook

A hypothetical trader hears that a company is “about to list.” They buy shares at its first public offer. They think the company gets the money from that purchase. A week later, they buy more on the exchange from another investor. They believe the company is funded again. Both beliefs mix up two different markets. The difference decides where the money goes.

The idea

A share is a unit of ownership. A company has a fixed number of shares, and they are all equal parts. Each part gives its holder a share of the business, in proportion to the number held.

Ownership percentage is the shares you hold, divided by the total number of shares. In symbols:

Ownership % = (shares held / total shares outstanding) x 100

Value of a holding is the number of shares times the price of one share. In symbols: holding value = shares x price.

Why companies sell shares. A company can raise money from new shareholders instead of borrowing it. Existing owners can also sell part of their stake. These are general reasons. Each company’s own filings give its specific reasons.

Primary market. The company issues new shares. The money goes to the company.

Secondary market. Investors trade shares that already exist. The money goes to the seller, not to the company. Buying and selling on the exchange takes place in the secondary market.

IPO (initial public offer). A company’s first public offer of shares to the public. This chapter explains pricing and allotment (who gets the shares) only in general terms. In a book-building IPO, the company gives a price band, and investors bid inside that band. Think of an auction with a floor and a ceiling. Once the issue closes, the shares are allotted, and SEBI’s rule since December 2023 is that they must start trading within 3 working days after the issue closes (called T+3). (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI circular of 9 Aug 2023. Rules change, so check the SEBI site.)

An IPO is not automatically profitable. The listing-day price is the first price at which the shares trade on the exchange. It can be above or below the issue price, which is the price set for the offer.

Dilution. When new shares are issued, the total number of shares rises. Each existing share is then a smaller part of the company. So an existing holder’s percentage falls, unless they buy some of the new shares.

See it

flowchart LR
  subgraph Primary
    I1[Investor] -->|money for new shares| C[Company]
    C -->|new shares| I1
  end
  subgraph Secondary
    B[Buyer] -->|money, matched on exchange| S[Seller]
    S -->|existing shares| B
  end

Look at the company box. It appears only in the primary flow. In the secondary flow, money passes between buyer and seller. The company receives nothing.

Worked example

Hypothetical company: 10,00,000 shares outstanding, trading at Rs 200 each.

StepWorkingResult
1. Company value10,00,000 x Rs 200Rs 20,00,00,000 (Rs 20 crore)
2. Holder’s ownership1,000 / 10,00,0000.1%
3. Holder’s value1,000 x Rs 200Rs 2,00,000
4. Secondary trade, 100 shares100 x Rs 200Rs 20,000 paid to the seller; the company receives nothing
5. Dilution: company issues 5,00,000 new sharesTotal becomes 10,00,000 + 5,00,000 = 15,00,000; holder’s 1,000 / 15,00,0000.0667%

Check on step 5: 1,000 / 15,00,000 = 1 / 1,500 = 0.000667, which is 0.0667%.

The holder’s ownership fell from 0.1% to 0.0667%, because the share count rose. The holder still has 1,000 shares. Whether their value falls depends on the price after the new issue. The arithmetic above does not decide that.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: A holder owns 250 shares in a company with 20,00,000 shares in total. The share price is Rs 150. Work out the holder’s ownership percentage and the value of the holding.

Hints:

  1. Divide the holder’s shares by the total shares to get the fraction.
  2. Multiply the fraction by 100 to get the percentage.
  3. Multiply the shares by the price to get the value.

Answer: Ownership = 250 / 20,00,000 = 0.000125, which is 0.0125%. Value = 250 x Rs 150 = Rs 37,500.

Independent exercise

Task: The company in the worked example issues 10,00,000 new shares. Work out the holder’s new ownership percentage. Explain why the holder’s value is not automatically lower. Use no hints.

Check your answer

New total = 10,00,000 + 10,00,000 = 20,00,000 shares. Holder’s ownership = 1,000 / 20,00,000 = 0.005%.

The holder’s value is shares x price. The holder still has 1,000 shares. If the price stayed at Rs 200, the holding would still be worth Rs 2,00,000. Trading sets the price. The new money may change the company’s prospects, so the price could move either way. The percentage falls for certain. The value does not fall automatically.

Quiz

Five questions check ownership sums, the money flow in each market, and the claims about IPOs and dilution.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal, or on paper. No purchase.

  1. Find the public offer document for any past IPO. You can find it on the exchange or SEBI website.
  2. Find the section called “objects of the issue.” It says what the money is for.
  3. Write, in your own words, what the company said it would use the money for.
  4. Record the source, the date you accessed it, and the company name. Do not buy anything.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This chapter does not tell you whether any IPO is worth buying. An IPO does not get an exemption from the capital-first rules.
  • Hype about an IPO, and excitement about getting shares, often lead to rash buying.
  • The listing-day price can be above or below the issue price. This chapter does not predict either.
  • The percentage and value sums are a snapshot. The price moves, so the value moves too.

Remember

  • A share is a unit of ownership. Ownership % = shares held / total shares, times 100.
  • Primary market: money goes to the company. Secondary market: money goes to the seller.
  • An IPO is a company’s first public offer. It is not automatically profitable.
  • Dilution: new shares lower each existing holder’s percentage, unless they buy into the issue.
  • For 1,000 of 10,00,000 shares, ownership is 0.1%. After 5,00,000 new shares, it is 0.0667%.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • SEBI ICDR regulations.
  • SEBI investor education pages on IPOs.
  • NSE and BSE IPO education pages.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • The 3-working-day listing time after an IPO closes comes from a SEBI circular dated 9 Aug 2023. It became compulsory for issues opening on or after 1 Dec 2023.
  • The detailed IPO steps, price band limits and allotment rules are in SEBI’s ICDR regulations. Read them on the SEBI site before you apply to any IPO.
  • Current IPO figures are out of scope for this chapter.

Exchanges, SEBI and brokers

Hook

A hypothetical beginner believes their broker is the exchange. They think their order is matched inside the broker’s office. When a trade goes wrong, they cannot say who to ask, or in what order. Knowing which institution does which job turns a confusing situation into a list of steps.

The idea

Many institutions sit between your order and the shares in your name. Each one has one job. This chapter defines each role once. Keep the roles separate in your mind.

SEBI is the securities regulator. It makes the rules for brokers, exchanges and listed companies. It does not set stock prices.

Exchanges (NSE and BSE) run the marketplace where orders are matched. They are the two main stock exchanges in India. SEBI keeps the official list of recognised exchanges on its website. A buy order is matched with a sell order there. The exchange does not hold your money.

The clearing corporation works after a trade. It records what each side owes because of the trade. It is part of how settlement is assured. Settlement is the final transfer of shares and money. Think of a trusted middle person at a market who promises each side that the other will pay or deliver. NSE’s clearing corporation is called NSE Clearing Limited. BSE’s is called Indian Clearing Corporation Limited (ICCL).

Depositories hold shares electronically. India has two: NSDL and CDSL. They work like a bank for shares, keeping a record of who owns what. Your shares are recorded in your name at a depository, not in a paper certificate kept in a drawer.

The broker (trading member) accepts your orders, gives you the trading platform, and passes your orders to the exchange. The broker is the firm you deal with day to day. It is not the exchange. It is not where your shares are held.

The depository participant (DP) is the firm that keeps your demat account for you, on the depository’s behalf. The depository records the holding. The DP keeps the account. These are two different roles, even when one firm does both.

Banks hold the funds used for trading and settlement. Your trading account is linked to a bank account.

The grievance route is the order for complaints. Start with the broker. Then go to the exchange. Then go to SEBI’s complaint portal, which is called SCORES (scores.sebi.gov.in). SEBI’s investor page says to approach the firm you have a complaint against first, and to use SCORES only if you are still not satisfied. The old SCORES site (scores.gov.in) was closed on 28 Mar 2024, so use the new one. SEBI’s helpline numbers are 1800-266-7575 and 1800-22-7575. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI investor site. Check it for the latest steps.)

One practical check. Before you open an account with any broker, check its registration on the official lists. SEBI’s investor site has a tool called “Check Registration Status of Market Intermediary”, and SEBI also keeps a list of recognised intermediaries on its website. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI investor site.)

See it

flowchart LR
  You[You, the buyer] --> Broker[Broker: accepts and passes on the order]
  Broker --> Exchange[Exchange: matches the order]
  Exchange --> Clearing[Clearing corporation: records the obligation]
  Clearing --> Depository[Depository, through a DP: holds the shares]
  Depository --> You

Follow the arrows. The order goes from you to the broker, then to the exchange, then to clearing. The shares come back to you through the depository. Notice that the broker and the exchange are two separate boxes.

Worked example

Hypothetical: buy 20 shares at Rs 250.

StepWorkingResult
1. Trade value20 x Rs 250Rs 5,000

Now follow the order. Note who holds the money at each step.

StepInstitutionWhat happensWho holds the money at this step
1YouPlace the buy order for 20 shares at Rs 250Your linked funds, Rs 5,000 needed
2BrokerAccepts the order and passes it to the exchangeMoved through the broker’s process
3ExchangeMatches the order with a sellerThe exchange does not hold your money
4Clearing corporationRecords the obligation from the tradeThe clearing system moves funds
5Depository, through the DPAfter settlement, the 20 shares are credited to your demat accountSettlement moves the shares and the money, one working day after the trade (T+1, see Demat accounts and settlement)

The main point: no step asks the exchange to hold your money.

Try it

Interactive demo (conceptual mode).

Guided exercise

Task: Label the institutions on a diagram for a sale of 20 shares at Rs 250.

Hints:

  1. Start with yourself.
  2. Follow the order: you, broker, exchange.
  3. Then follow the shares: clearing, then the depository.

Answer: You place the sell order for 20 shares at Rs 250. The trade value is Rs 5,000. The broker accepts the order and passes it to the exchange. The exchange matches it with a buyer. The clearing corporation records the obligation. The depository, through the DP, moves the shares out of your demat account when the trade settles, one working day after the trade (see Demat accounts and settlement).

Independent exercise

Task: Explain to a friend, in three sentences, why the exchange is not the place where you open an account. Use no hints.

Check your answer

One answer: “The exchange is the marketplace where orders are matched. You open an account with a broker, who takes your orders and passes them to the exchange. Your shares are recorded at a depository, through a DP, not at the exchange itself.”

Check that your three sentences separate matching (exchange), access (broker) and holding (depository).

Quiz

Six questions check the broker-exchange difference, the clearing role, the grievance order, and who holds shares.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal, or on paper.

  1. List each institution in this chapter: SEBI, NSE, BSE, the clearing corporation, NSDL, CDSL, the broker, the DP and the bank.
  2. Write its role in your own words, one line each.
  3. Add a link to the official page that describes it.
  4. Check each role against the SEBI, NSE and BSE pages before you rely on it.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not recommend any broker. Zerodha appears as a broker example only, not as a recommendation.
  • Registration checks are your responsibility. Check the official list before you open an account.
  • Never send money to anyone who claims to be a broker or adviser until you have verified them.
  • The roles described here are general. Each firm’s exact structure is in its own documents.

Remember

  • SEBI regulates. It does not set prices.
  • The exchange matches orders. It does not hold your money.
  • The broker takes your orders. It is not the exchange.
  • The depository holds shares electronically, through a DP.
  • The grievance route is broker, then exchange, then SEBI.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • SEBI website: about SEBI, intermediaries, investor grievance.
  • NSE website: clearing and membership pages.
  • BSE website.
  • NSDL and CDSL websites.
  • Zerodha support pages on accounts, as a broker example and not as a recommendation.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • The clearing corporations are NSE Clearing Limited and Indian Clearing Corporation Limited. The depositories are NSDL and CDSL.
  • The complaint route and the new SCORES address come from SEBI’s investor pages.
  • The registration check tool comes from SEBI’s investor support page.
  • The full list of recognised exchanges is kept by SEBI. This chapter does not copy it, so check the SEBI site.

Demat accounts and settlement

Hook

A hypothetical beginner buys 20 shares at Rs 250 in the morning. They see the order filled. They assume the shares are already in the demat account, and ready to sell the same afternoon. But between the fill and the holding there are clearing and settlement steps. The cash and the shares do not move at the same moment.

The idea

Three accounts are involved. Each holds something different.

  • Trading account: where orders are placed and funds are tracked.
  • Demat account: where shares are held electronically.
  • Linked bank account: where money moves in and out of the trading account.

A trade moves through three stages.

  1. Execution: the order matches at the exchange. This is when the trade happens.
  2. Clearing: each side’s obligations are worked out. Many trades are combined.
  3. Settlement: the shares and the money actually change hands.

Netting combines many trades in one settlement cycle into one net figure for each share and for each cash amount. Buys and sells offset each other before money or shares move.

T+1 means settlement happens one working day after the trade date. SEBI’s T+1 cycle for all listed shares has been in force since 27 Jan 2023. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI circular of 7 Sep 2021 and SEBI’s master circular. Rules change, so check the SEBI site.) “T” is the trade day. “+1” means one working day later, not one calendar day.

Shares reach your demat account only after settlement. If you buy on Monday, they are credited after settlement on Tuesday. Some brokers let you sell them on Tuesday before they arrive. This is called BTST, buy today and sell tomorrow. It carries a risk called short delivery. If the shares you sold do not arrive in time, the exchange can run an auction, and the penalty can be up to 20% of the value. Money from selling shares that have not yet reached your account also cannot be used the same day. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on BTST. Broker rules differ and change, so check with your broker.)

Holding and position are different. A delivery holding is settled shares sitting in your demat account. An intraday position is one you open and close on the same day. It is not a delivery holding. Intraday positions are covered in Order book.

The timeline is not instant. Execution, clearing and settlement happen at different times. The holding appears after settlement. It does not appear at the moment of the fill.

See it

flowchart LR
  A["Order placed (trading account)"] --> B["Execution: trade matched on exchange"]
  B --> C["Clearing: obligations calculated and netted"]
  C --> D["Settlement at T+1"]
  D --> E["Shares credited to demat account"]

Follow the arrows from left to right. The gap between execution and settlement is why a holding does not appear the moment an order fills.

Worked example

Netting illustration, concept only. A broker has three buy trades of Rs 5,000 each. It also has one sell trade of Rs 2,000. All four are in the same settlement cycle.

StepWorkingResult
1. Total buys3 x 5,000Rs 15,000
2. Sell proceeds1 x 2,000Rs 2,000
3. Net payable15,000 - 2,000Rs 13,000

Check: 13,000 + 2,000 = 15,000, which equals the total buys. The example is a round-number picture of the idea. In real settlement, NSE’s FAQ says netting is done for each client in each share. It does not apply to stocks in the trade-for-trade segment or to auction trades, where each trade is settled in full. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

Timeline example. A buy executed on a hypothetical Monday settles on Tuesday, if Tuesday is a working day. If the next day is a holiday, the settlement moves to the next working day. NSE publishes its holiday list each year. See Trading sessions, timings and holidays.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: Fill in the blanks in the trace for a buy of 20 shares at Rs 250, a trade value of Rs 5,000.

StageWhich account or institutionWhich dayBlank to fill
Order placed______Trade day______
Trade matched______Trade day______
Obligation recorded______Trade day______
Settlement______Next working day, T+1______
Shares arrive______After settlement______

Hints:

  1. Which account holds the order and the funds? Which holds the shares?
  2. Which institution matches the trade, and which records the obligation?
  3. Which day is the trade day, and which is the settlement day?

Answer: Order placed: trading account, on the trade day. Trade matched: the exchange. Obligation recorded: the clearing corporation. Settlement: on the next working day, T+1. The Rs 5,000 leaves the linked bank account. The shares are credited to the demat account through the depository and the DP. Shares arrive in the demat account after settlement, not at the moment of the fill.

Independent exercise

Task: A trade is done on a Friday, before a public holiday. List the sources you would need to find its settlement date. Do not work out the date without the calendar. Use no hints.

Check your answer

Sources needed:

  • The current T+1 rule and its effective date. These are in the notes above.
  • The exchange’s holiday list for the year, which NSE publishes on its site.
  • The trade date, and the weekend days between the trade and the candidate settlement date.

You can count working days only after you have these three. You cannot fix the date from memory, because holidays are set each year.

  2. Write each label in your own words, in your journal or on paper.
  3. Label the layout “check your own Kite for the current layout”.
  4. The Kite demo uses dummy data for interface practice. Zerodha does not provide a paper-trading account. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page. Check it for any change.)

Limits and common mistakes

  • Settlement risk is low in a learning exercise. It is real for live trades. Do not assume you can sell on the same day. Check your broker’s current rule first.
  • The dates and netting rules here explain a concept. The real rules are in the exchange and SEBI documents.
  • A holding in your demat account is not the same as cash in your account. The money is in the bank and the trading account.
  • The Kite demo’s layout may change. Check your own Kite for the current layout.

Remember

  • Trading account holds orders and funds. Demat account holds shares. The bank account is linked.
  • A trade is executed, then cleared, then settled. The holding appears after settlement.
  • Netting reduces many trades to one net figure for each share and each cash amount. In the example, 15,000 of buys minus 2,000 of sells is a net Rs 13,000 payable.
  • T+1 means one working day after the trade date.
  • Shares arrive in your demat account after settlement. Selling them before they arrive (BTST) carries a short-delivery risk.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • SEBI circulars on the settlement cycle.
  • NSE clearing and settlement pages.
  • NSDL and CDSL websites.
  • Zerodha support pages on demat accounts.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • T+1 has applied to all listed shares since 27 Jan 2023. SEBI’s master circular on settlement sets the daily schedule (pay-in by 11:00 am, funds pay-out by 1:30 pm, shares pay-out by 3:30 pm). SEBI also began an optional same-day (T+0) settlement trial for a small set of shares on 28 Mar 2024. This chapter does not teach it.
  • Settlement falls on the next working day when the usual day is a holiday. The 2026 holiday list is in Trading sessions, timings and holidays.
  • BTST, short delivery and the auction penalty come from Zerodha’s support page on BTST.
  • Netting rules come from NSE’s Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market.
  • Demat account charges are not quoted in this chapter. See Costs on a trade and the Zerodha charges page.
  • The Kite demo and the lack of a paper-trading account come from Zerodha’s support site.

How prices move: the order book

Hook

A hypothetical trader sees a stock at Rs 250. They decide to buy 250 shares “at Rs 250.” That looks like Rs 62,500. But the buy fills at a total of Rs 62,650. The cheapest 100 shares are gone, and the next 150 are offered at Rs 251. The price on screen was the last trade. It was not the price of the whole order.

The idea

The price is the last trade. The price on screen is the price of the most recent matched trade. It is not a fixed value. It is not a fair price on its own.

An order book is a list of waiting orders. Orders that have not yet matched wait in the book. Each waiting buy order names the price it will pay. Each waiting sell order names the price it will accept. The book shows how many shares wait at each price.

Demand and supply. Demand is the buy orders at or above a given price. These are buyers willing to pay that much. Supply is the sell orders at or below a given price. These are sellers willing to accept that much. The price moves where these meet, as orders arrive and match.

Two kinds of order.

  • A market order takes the best available prices now. It fills at whatever the book offers, from the lowest sell price up.
  • A limit order sets a maximum buy price or a minimum sell price. It fills only at that price or better, and it may not fill at all.

Price moves when orders use up the best prices. A large market buy takes the cheapest sell shares first. When those are used up, it moves to the next price. Each price it uses can raise the price.

News changes expectations, and expectations change orders. The price responds to the orders that news creates, not to the news itself. A price change shows that orders moved. It does not show which information caused them.

No price is fair by itself. The price is what a buyer and a seller agreed to at that moment. A change in price does not show whether the earlier price was right or wrong.

See it

Here is the sell side of a made-up order book. Each row is a price, with shares waiting to be sold at that price.

Ask price (Rs)Shares waiting at this priceRunning total
250100100
251150250
252200450

A market buy fills from the top row down. Read the running total. A buy of 250 shares uses the first two rows exactly. A buy of 300 would reach into the third row. The price of the last trade moves up as the buy climbs the rows.

Worked example

Hypothetical ask side: 100 shares at Rs 250, 150 at Rs 251, 200 at Rs 252. A market buy of 250 shares.

StepWorkingResult
1. Fill 100 at the lowest ask100 x Rs 250Rs 25,000
2. Fill the next 150 at Rs 251150 x Rs 251Rs 37,650
3. Total cost for 250 shares25,000 + 37,650Rs 62,650
4. Average price62,650 / 250Rs 250.60
5. Last traded pricelast fillRs 251

Check on step 2: 150 x 251 = 150 x 250 + 150 = 37,500 + 150 = 37,650.

The buy paid an average of Rs 250.60 per share, not Rs 250. The Rs 250 on screen was only the first price.

Try it

Interactive demo. Use the order-book simulator to walk a market buy up the ask side. Watch the price step up as each price is used up.

Guided exercise

Task: Using the same made-up book, work out the cost of a market buy of 100 shares, and then of 200 shares.

Hints:

  1. Fill from the lowest ask upward.
  2. For 100 shares, all of the fill is at Rs 250.
  3. For 200 shares, the first 100 are at Rs 250 and the next 100 are at Rs 251.

Answer:

  • 100 shares: 100 x Rs 250 = Rs 25,000. Average Rs 250.00.
  • 200 shares: 100 x Rs 250 = Rs 25,000, plus 100 x Rs 251 = Rs 25,100. Total Rs 50,100. Average 50,100 / 200 = Rs 250.50.

Independent exercise

Task: A market buy of 300 shares uses the same book. Work out the total cost, the average price and the new last traded price. Use no hints.

Check your answer
  • First 100 at Rs 250: 100 x 250 = Rs 25,000.
  • Next 150 at Rs 251: 150 x 251 = Rs 37,650.
  • Last 50 at Rs 252: 50 x 252 = Rs 12,600.
  • Total: 25,000 + 37,650 + 12,600 = Rs 75,250.
  • Average: 75,250 / 300 = Rs 250.83 (rounded to two decimals).
  • New last traded price: Rs 252, the last price the order reached.

Check the total: 100 + 150 + 50 = 300 shares, and the three amounts add to 75,250.

Quiz

Six questions check the average price of a walk up the book, the last price, the limit order, a thin-stock case and the claim about information.

Paper task

Mode: kite_demo. Open the market depth view for one demo stock. Write down the best bid and best ask in your own words, in your journal or on paper.

  1. Open the market depth view for one stock in the Kite demo.
  2. Note the best bid (the highest buy price waiting) and the best ask (the lowest sell price waiting).
  3. Write both in your own words.
  4. Zerodha says the market depth view shows the best 5 buy prices and the best 5 sell prices. It also shows the open, high, low and close, the volume and the circuit limits. Layouts change, so write “check your own Kite for the current layout” next to your note. The demo uses dummy data. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on market depth.)

Limits and common mistakes

  • This book does not tell you where any price will go. It explains how a price is formed, not what it will do.
  • The price on screen is not the price a large order gets. The average can be higher.
  • In thin stocks, a market order can fill far from the last price. Use a limit order with a known maximum. Do not assume the price on screen is what you will pay.
  • A price move is not proof that anyone knows something. It shows that orders moved.

Remember

  • The price on screen is the last matched trade. It is not a fixed or fair value.
  • A market buy fills from the lowest ask up. In the example, 250 shares cost Rs 62,650, an average of Rs 250.60.
  • A limit order is a maximum for a buy. It may not fill.
  • A price change shows that orders moved. It does not show why.
  • A thin book moves more for the same order size.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • NSE education pages on order matching.
  • Zerodha Varsity, as a reference only (link only, no text copied).
  • Zerodha support pages on order types.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Zerodha’s standard market depth view shows the best 5 bids and offers. A separate 20-level depth view exists as another feature. (Zerodha support site.)
  • The exact layout of the Kite demo can change, so this chapter does not describe it. Check it on screen.
  • Orders at the same price are matched in the order they arrive. See Order matching, spread and liquidity for the rule and its source.

Order matching, spread and liquidity

Hook

A stock is quoted at around Rs 250. Hypothetically, you buy 40 shares at the higher of the two prices on screen. You sell them straight away at the lower price. You pay Rs 10,020 and receive Rs 9,980. So you are down Rs 40, before any brokerage or charges. Nothing in the market moved. The loss came from the gap between two prices that were both on screen.

The idea

A price on screen is not one number. At any moment there are two prices that matter.

  • Best bid: the highest price any buyer is currently waiting to pay.
  • Best ask: the lowest price any seller is currently waiting to accept.
  • Spread: the gap between them. Spread in rupees = best ask minus best bid.
  • Spread percent: the spread divided by a reference price. Here the reference is the middle point between the bid and the ask.
  • Market depth: how many shares are waiting at each price, not just at the best one.
  • Liquidity: how easily you can trade a given size without moving the price much.

The spread is not a fee. Your broker does not set it. It is the market’s own gap between what buyers offer and what sellers ask.

Round trip. A round trip means buying, then selling. If you buy at the ask and sell at the bid, you give up the whole spread. Round-trip spread cost = spread x quantity, before any charges.

Matching. The exchange pairs buy and sell orders when their prices meet. Orders are matched first by price. Orders at the same price are matched by time of arrival. This is called price-time priority. Think of a queue at a ticket counter: the best price goes first, and at the same price the person who came first is served first. NSE uses this rule for limit orders in the main trading session. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE pre-open and trading pages. Rules change, so check the NSE site. BSE’s matching rule is not checked here.)

Tick size. The smallest allowed step in price. The step depends on the share price, as in the table below. The examples in this chapter use steps of Rs 0.50 and Rs 1.00 only to show the arithmetic. They are round numbers for practice, not the real steps for those prices.

Share price on NSETick size
Below Rs 250Rs 0.01
Rs 250 to Rs 1,000Rs 0.05
Rs 1,000 to Rs 5,000Rs 0.10
Rs 5,000 to Rs 10,000Rs 0.50
Rs 10,000 to Rs 20,000Rs 1.00
Above Rs 20,000Rs 5.00

(As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE circulars of 24 May 2024 and 13 Mar 2025, in force from 15 Apr 2025. NSE reviews the tick size each month using the last close of the month before. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

Signs of low liquidity in a displayed book:

  • a wide gap between the best bid and the best ask,
  • very few shares waiting at each price,
  • large jumps from one price to the next.

A deep book means you can trade a larger quantity close to the best price. It does not mean you can trade any size at the shown price. A deep book reduces price impact, which is how much your own order moves the price. It does not remove that impact.

See it

Look at the gap between the top buy price and the top sell price. That gap is the spread. It is the first cost you pay on a round trip. Check that the round-trip counter matches the worked example below. The figures are made up for illustration.

Worked example

Hypothetical: best bid Rs 249.50, best ask Rs 250.50, quantity 40 shares.

StepCalculationResult
Spread in rupees250.50 - 249.50Rs 1.00
Reference price (midpoint)(250.50 + 249.50) / 2Rs 250.00
Spread percent1.00 / 250.000.40%
Buy 40 at the ask40 x 250.50Rs 10,020
Sell 40 at the bid40 x 249.50Rs 9,980
Loss before charges10,020 - 9,980Rs 40

Check: 40 x Rs 1.00 spread = Rs 40. The loss is about 0.40% of the Rs 10,020 paid, which matches the spread percent.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Bid is Rs 99.00 and ask is Rs 101.00. For a round trip of 100 shares, find the spread in rupees, the spread percent of the reference price, and the round-trip cost.

Hints:

  1. Subtract the bid from the ask.
  2. The reference price is the midpoint: add bid and ask, then divide by 2.
  3. Multiply the spread by the quantity.

Answer: Spread = Rs 2.00. Reference price = Rs 100. Spread percent = 2.00 / 100 = 2.0%. Round-trip cost = 100 x 2.00 = Rs 200.

Check: buy 100 x 101 = Rs 10,100; sell 100 x 99 = Rs 9,900; difference Rs 200.

Independent exercise

Task. Stock A: bid 500.00, ask 500.50. Stock B: bid 50.00, ask 50.20. For 100 shares of each, work out the round-trip spread cost in rupees. Which stock is cheaper per rupee traded?

Check your answer
  • Stock A: spread Rs 0.50 x 100 = Rs 50. Value traded is about 100 x 500 = Rs 50,000. Cost per rupee is about 50 / 50,000 = 0.10%.
  • Stock B: spread Rs 0.20 x 100 = Rs 20. Value traded is about 100 x 50 = Rs 5,000. Cost per rupee is about 20 / 5,000 = 0.40%.

Stock A is cheaper per rupee traded, at about 0.1% against about 0.4%. Stock B has the smaller gap in rupees per share. But the comparison that matters for your money is the cost per rupee you put to work.

Quiz

Six questions on spread arithmetic, depth, liquidity and matching priority. Each wrong answer has a short explanation.

Paper task

Mode: kite_demo. Kite demo data is dummy data, so the numbers here are for practice only. Layouts change, so check your own Kite for the current layout.

  1. Open the Kite demo and pick one demo stock. Check your own Kite for the current layout.
  2. Note the best bid and best ask at three different times during the demo session.
  3. For each reading, compute the spread in rupees and the spread percent.
  4. Write the three readings in your journal, or on paper. Mark them “kite_demo” and “dummy data”.
  5. Do not place any order.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A spread is the cost at one moment, for one size. A larger order can fill at prices further from the best price.
  • A deep book now does not promise the same depth later, or when you need to exit.
  • The spread says nothing about whether a price is right or wrong.
  • Spread cost is before brokerage, taxes and other charges. Those are covered in Costs on a trade.
  • Matching rules and tick sizes are not stated as facts here. Use the official values once they are verified.

Remember

  • You buy at the ask and sell at the bid, so a round trip gives up the spread.
  • Spread = ask minus bid. Spread percent = spread divided by the reference price.
  • Liquidity is the ability to trade a size without moving the price much.
  • A deep order book reduces price impact. It does not remove it.
  • The spread is the market’s price gap, not a broker fee.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • NSE market microstructure and trading rules pages.
  • BSE trading rules.
  • Zerodha Varsity, as reference only (link only, no text copied).
  • Zerodha support pages on order types.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Price-time priority comes from NSE’s trading rules. BSE’s rule was not checked for this chapter.
  • The tick size table comes from NSE circulars dated 24 May 2024 and 13 Mar 2025. ETFs are left out of the below-Rs-250 rule.
  • The Kite demo layout can change, so this chapter does not describe it.

Trading sessions, timings and holidays

Hook

On Friday evening a stock closes at Rs 400. You write a plan. You will buy on the next trading day if the stock stays above Rs 400. This is a hypothetical example. Monday is an exchange holiday, so your order waits until Tuesday. Tuesday’s opening price could be far from Rs 400. Your plan must say what to do then. Many beginners ignore the calendar.

The idea

A trading day is not one block of time. It has phases. Each phase handles orders in its own way.

  • Pre-open: orders are collected. An opening price is worked out before continuous trading starts. On NSE it runs from 9:00 am to 9:15 am. Orders are taken in the first minutes. Then the exchange matches them, and then trading moves to the normal market at 9:15 am. The price found in pre-open becomes the day’s open price. If no price is found, the first trade in the normal market is the open. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE pre-open page and NSE circulars of 2026. Times change, so check the NSE site.)
  • Continuous trading: orders are matched as they arrive, as described in Order matching, spread and liquidity.
  • Closing session: a closing price is set near the end of the day. NSE began a Closing Auction Session on 3 Aug 2026, starting with stocks that have futures and options. It runs after the normal market closes at 3:15 pm for those stocks. A reference price is taken from the trades between 3:00 pm and 3:15 pm. Orders can then be placed within 3% of that reference price. The closing price is the price where the most shares can trade. Other stocks keep the older closing method, and their normal market runs until 3:30 pm. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE closing auction session page and NSE circulars. NSE may widen this to more stocks, so check the NSE site.)

Auctions. In the pre-open and closing phases, many orders are matched at one price. Put simply, the exchange picks the price where the most shares can trade. Think of a village auction where the seller and buyers all agree on one price at the end. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE pre-open page.)

Market timings are set by the exchange. They can differ on special sessions. The table in the next section gives the timings in force on 11 Nov 2026. Check the NSE site before you rely on them, because they have changed this year.

Holidays. The exchange publishes a holiday list every year. No trading happens on those days. NSE’s 2026 list has 16 holidays that fall on weekdays. One of them, 15 Jan, was added after the first list came out. They are listed in the table below the timings. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE circular of 12 Dec 2025. Check the NSE site for any change.)

Special sessions. The exchange may announce a special session on some days. For example, a special Muhurat trading session was held on Sunday 8 Nov 2026 for Diwali. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE circular of 19 Oct 2026. Check the NSE site for new ones.)

Weekends are non-trading days.

How to find the next session. Start on the day after your starting day. Skip weekends. Skip exchange holidays. The first day you did not skip is the next session.

Decisions after the close. If you decide after the market closes, your order goes to the next session. That session can open at a different price from the close you used in your plan. This gap is covered in Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk.

Orders placed when the market is closed follow the rules of your broker and the exchange. Some brokers let you queue an order for the next session and some do not. Check your broker’s help pages for how it treats orders placed after the close.

See it

The first table shows one week with a holiday. The holiday is made up for practice. The second table shows the phases of one trading day. The times come from NSE and were checked on 11 Nov 2026.

DayTrading?Note
MondayNoExchange holiday (made up for practice)
TuesdayYesFirst session after the holiday
WednesdayYes
ThursdayYes
FridayYes
SaturdayNoWeekend
SundayNoWeekend
PhaseWhat happensTime
Pre-openOrders collected; opening price worked out9:00 am to 9:15 am
Continuous tradingOrders matched as they arrive9:15 am to 3:30 pm for most stocks. 9:15 am to 3:15 pm for stocks with futures and options
Closing sessionClosing price set3:15 pm to 3:35 pm for stocks with futures and options, in the first phase
Post-closeTrades at the closing price3:50 pm to 4:00 pm

(As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE pre-open and closing auction pages and NSE circulars of 2026. Times change, so check the NSE site. The futures and options segment trades until 3:40 pm.)

The 2026 trading holidays that fall on a weekday, from NSE’s list of 12 Dec 2025:
