Some other holidays fall on a weekend, so they cost no trading day: Mahashivratri on Sunday 15 Feb, Id-Ul-Fitr on Saturday 21 Mar, Independence Day on Saturday 15 Aug and Diwali Laxmi Pujan on Sunday 8 Nov. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE circular of 12 Dec 2025. Dates can change, so check the NSE site.)

Worked example

This is a hypothetical plan. Friday’s close is Rs 400. In a made-up holiday list, Monday is a holiday.

StepDayTrading day?What happens
1FridayYesPlan is written after the close
2SaturdayNoWeekend
3SundayNoWeekend
4MondayNoMade-up holiday
5TuesdayYesNext session. The plan’s order is placed here

Count the calendar days from Friday to Tuesday. Saturday is 1. Sunday is 2. Monday is 3. Tuesday is 4. That is 4 calendar days. But only one trading day falls in that span, and it is Tuesday.

If your plan uses the Rs 400 close, Tuesday’s opening price may be well above or below Rs 400. Your plan must say what to do in that case.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. In a made-up holiday list, Friday is a holiday. Start after a Thursday. Find the next three trading dates.

Hints:

  1. List the days after Thursday, in order.
  2. Skip Saturday and Sunday.
  3. Skip the made-up holiday, which is Friday.

Answer: Monday, Tuesday and Wednesday of the following week.

Independent exercise

Task. Three cases. Each has its own made-up holiday list. For each, find the next trading session.

a. The trade date is a Tuesday. Wednesday is a made-up holiday. b. The trade date is a Friday. Monday and Tuesday are made-up holidays. c. The trade date is a Friday. There are no made-up holidays.

Check your answer

a. Wednesday is a holiday, so the next session is Thursday. b. Saturday and Sunday are weekends. Monday and Tuesday are holidays. The next session is Wednesday. c. Saturday and Sunday are weekends. Monday has no holiday, so the next session is Monday.

Quiz

Five questions. They cover the order of phases, finding the next session, weekends, pre-open and closing prices, and gap risk over a holiday.

Paper task

Mode: learning note.

  1. Find the official NSE holiday list for the current year.
  2. Copy it into your journal, with the source link and the date you copied it.
  3. Using that list, write the next 10 trading dates from today. Skip weekends and listed holidays.
  4. Add one line saying what you checked and where.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Phases, times, holidays and special sessions can change. The times here were checked on 11 Nov 2026. Check the NSE site again before you rely on them.
  • A holiday list from a previous year is not this year’s list.
  • Knowing the next session does not tell you the price you will get in it.
  • Special sessions can change the normal timings.
  • A stop or plan based on one close can meet a very different opening price after a weekend or holiday. This is called gap risk. See Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk.

Remember

  • A trading day has phases. Each handles orders differently.
  • Weekends are non-trading days. Holidays remove trading days.
  • To find the next session: skip weekends, then skip holidays.
  • The pre-open price and the closing price are different phases. Do not treat them as the same.
  • A plan that covers a long weekend or holiday must say what to do if the next open is far from the close.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • NSE market timings and holiday pages.
  • NSE circulars.
  • BSE timings page.
  • Zerodha support on market hours, for reference.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Pre-open timings (9:00 to 9:15 am) and the rule that the pre-open price becomes the open come from NSE’s pre-open page and NSE circulars of 2026. The new pre-open steps took effect on 7 Sep 2026.
  • The Closing Auction Session began on 3 Aug 2026 (first phase, stocks with derivatives). Its timings, 3% price band and reference price come from NSE’s closing auction page and SEBI’s circular of 16 Jan 2026.
  • Normal market close (3:30 pm, or 3:15 pm for stocks with derivatives), the post-close session (3:50 to 4:00 pm) and the derivatives close (3:40 pm) come from NSE and Zerodha’s market timings page.
  • The 2026 holiday list and the Muhurat session come from NSE circulars of 12 Dec 2025 and 19 Oct 2026.
  • How brokers treat orders placed after the close was not checked. See your broker’s help pages.

Circuit limits and halts

Hook

You hold 100 shares bought at Rs 400. Your stop-loss is at Rs 380. Your planned loss is Rs 2,000. This is a hypothetical example. The next day, the stock falls straight to the lower price band of Rs 360. There are only sellers and no buyers. Your stop is triggered in theory, but no trade happens at Rs 380. You now face a Rs 4,000 loss on paper. You cannot get out at the price you planned.

The idea

A price band, often called a circuit limit, is the most a stock’s price may move in one day. The move is measured from the previous close. The band depends on the stock’s category. NSE uses bands of 2%, 5%, 10% and 20% in either direction. All other stocks that have no band set by the exchange get 20%. Stocks that have futures and options (F&O) have no fixed band. For them the exchange uses a 10% operating range that can be widened or narrowed during the day. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market and SEBI’s master circular on trading. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

  • Upper circuit: the highest price allowed that day.
  • Lower circuit: the lowest price allowed that day.
  • Reference point: the band is measured from the previous close, not from today’s open. NSE’s FAQs state that the band is measured from the previous day’s close.

In plain words:

  • Upper band = previous close, plus the band percentage of the previous close.
  • Lower band = previous close, minus the band percentage of the previous close.

Locked circuit. A stock reaches a band. All the orders are on one side. For example, there are only sellers at the lower band. No trade can happen beyond that price. The price stays locked there until buyers and sellers meet, or until the band changes.

Trading halts and index-level circuit breakers. The exchange can halt trading in a stock. It can also halt trading across the market. This happens when an index moves past set levels. The levels and rules are below.

Market-wide circuit breakers use the Nifty 50 or the Sensex, whichever moves past a level first. The levels are 10%, 15% and 20% from the previous close.

Fall ofWhen it happensWhat happens
10%Before 1:00 pmTrading stops for 45 minutes, then a 15-minute pre-open
10%1:00 pm to 2:30 pmTrading stops for 15 minutes, then a 15-minute pre-open
10%After 2:30 pmNo stop
15%Before 1:00 pmTrading stops for 1 hour 45 minutes, then a 15-minute pre-open
15%1:00 pm to 2:00 pmTrading stops for 45 minutes, then a 15-minute pre-open
15%After 2:00 pmTrading stops for the rest of the day
20%Any timeTrading stops for the rest of the day

(As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE circuit breaker page and SEBI circular of 28 Jun 2001 as amended in 2013. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

For stocks with futures and options, the 10% range is widened by steps if the price keeps pushing against it. SEBI’s rule is 5% twice, then 3% twice, then 2%, with a cooling-off pause each time (15 minutes, then 30 minutes, then 60 minutes). (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI master circular on trading, Dec 2024.)

Bands can change. The band for a stock can be revised over time. NSE’s FAQs say bands are lowered under surveillance measures, and raised only in a review done every two months if the stock meets the criteria. Some stocks are also placed in a “trade-for-trade” segment, where every trade must end in delivery and the shares cannot be sold the same day. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market and Zerodha support.)

A circuit limits the price, not the loss. The band stops the price from moving further in one day. It does not stop your position from losing value. If the stock is locked, you may not be able to exit at all until trading resumes.

See it

Watch the stop-loss line at Rs 380 and the lower band at Rs 360. The price can pass the stop and reach the band. No trade happens at the stop price. The fill is blocked while the circuit is locked. The band percentage in this view is a made-up value, used only for the arithmetic.

Worked example

This is a hypothetical example. The previous close is Rs 400. The band is a made-up 10%. The 10% figure is a round number for practice. For most stocks the real band is 2%, 5%, 10% or 20%, as in the table in the section above. Stocks with futures and options use a flexible 10% range instead of a fixed band, so a lock like this one works differently for them.

StepCalculationResult
Upper band400 x 1.10Rs 440
Lower band400 x 0.90Rs 360
Stop-lossSet on the holdingRs 380
Planned loss per share400 - 380Rs 20
Planned loss on 100 shares100 x 20Rs 2,000
Loss if locked at Rs 360 with no exit100 x (400 - 360)Rs 4,000

The loss at the locked band is 2 times the planned loss of Rs 2,000. The plan assumed a sale at Rs 380. The locked figure shows what happens when a sale at Rs 380 is not possible.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. The previous close is Rs 250. The made-up band is 20%. Compute the upper and lower bands.

Hints:

  1. Find 20% of Rs 250.
  2. Add that amount to Rs 250 for the upper band.
  3. Subtract it from Rs 250 for the lower band.

Answer: 20% of 250 = 50. Upper band = Rs 300. Lower band = Rs 200. Check: 250 x 1.20 = 300; 250 x 0.80 = 200.

Independent exercise

Task. Three stocks. Each has a previous close and a made-up band. The first is Rs 120 with a 5% band. The second is Rs 800 with a 10% band. The third is Rs 1,500 with a 20% band. Compute each band. Which position is most exposed to a one-day move?

Check your answer
  • Rs 120, 5%: 120 x 0.05 = 6. Upper Rs 126, lower Rs 114.
  • Rs 800, 10%: 800 x 0.10 = 80. Upper Rs 880, lower Rs 720.
  • Rs 1,500, 20%: 1,500 x 0.20 = 300. Upper Rs 1,800, lower Rs 1,200.

The Rs 1,500 stock with the 20% band is the most exposed. Its band allows a move of 20% in a day. That is the largest percentage. It also allows Rs 300 per share. That is the largest rupee amount.

Quiz

Five questions. They cover band arithmetic, what a circuit does and does not prevent, stops in a locked circuit, and the reference point for the band.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Use a hypothetical plan only. Do not act on a real account.

  1. Write a hypothetical holding: 100 shares, previous close Rs 400, stop-loss at Rs 380, made-up band of 10%.
  2. Write what your plan says if the stock locks at the lower band on day one.
  3. Write what your plan says if it is still locked on day two.
  4. Write the largest loss you would accept on this holding. Check it against your trading money. See The capital-first rules and Costs on a trade.
  5. Write this in your journal.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The band percentages in the examples are round numbers for practice. Real bands are 2%, 5%, 10% and 20%, and stocks with futures and options use a flexible 10% range.
  • A band does not cap your loss. A locked position can stay open and lose value.
  • A stop-loss is a trigger, not a guaranteed exit price. Its fill depends on trading being possible.
  • Halts and circuit breakers have their own rules, and those rules change. Check the current circulars.
  • Bands can change for a stock. Do not assume a band from last month still applies.

Remember

  • A band is measured from the previous close, as a percentage in each direction.
  • Upper band = previous close x (1 + band). Lower band = previous close x (1 - band).
  • A locked circuit can stop any trade beyond the band, including your exit.
  • A circuit limits the price move. It does not limit your loss.
  • Your position size must allow for locked days. See Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • NSE circuit limits and price band pages.
  • BSE price band pages.
  • SEBI circulars on price bands and circuit breakers.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Band percentages, the previous-close reference and the revision rule come from NSE’s Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market.
  • The flexible range for stocks with futures and options comes from SEBI’s master circular on trading (Dec 2024).
  • Market-wide circuit breaker levels and halt times come from NSE’s circuit breaker page and SEBI’s circular of 28 Jun 2001, as amended in 2013.
  • The trade-for-trade rule comes from Zerodha support and NSE’s FAQs.

Indices: Nifty and Sensex

Hook

On a day when an index rises 1.6%, a friend says, “The market is up. My holdings must be up too.” This is a hypothetical example. You hold Rs 50,000 in one stock. That stock falls 2% that day. Your holding is down Rs 1,000, while the index is up. The index sums up many stocks. It does not sum up your stock.

The idea

An index is one number that sums up a group of stocks. Nifty and Sensex are two such indices. The number is a level, measured in points. It is not a price you can pay. You cannot buy one share of an index.

Weighting. Each stock in the group has a weight. The weight is its share of the index. All the weights add up to 100%. The common method is based on market size, with an adjustment for shares that are free to trade. This is called the free-float market-capitalisation method. The Nifty 50 has 50 stocks. The Sensex has 30 stocks. Both use this method. The three-stock and five-stock examples below are made up to keep the arithmetic short. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Indices methodology document, June 2026, and BSE Indices methodology, May 2026. Methods change, so check the NSE and BSE sites.)

Point change and percent change.

  • Point change = new level minus previous level.
  • Percent change = point change divided by previous level, times 100.

Contribution of one stock. Weight times that stock’s move. The index move is the total of all the contributions.

Breadth. Breadth counts how many stocks in the group rose and how many fell. An index can rise even when many stocks fall. This happens if the heavily weighted stocks rise. Breadth is context, not a forecast.

Broad and sectoral indices measure different groups. A broad index covers a wide set of stocks. A sectoral index covers one sector.

Index-linked products are not covered in this track. They are covered later in the book.

Rebalancing. The stocks in an index are reviewed on a schedule. For the Nifty 50, NSE reviews the list twice a year, using six months of data that end in January and July. Changes take effect from the last trading day of March and September, with about four weeks of notice. NSE allows at most 5 changes in a calendar year. The Sensex is reviewed twice a year too. Its changes take effect on the Monday after the third Friday of June and December. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Indices methodology document, June 2026, and BSE Indices methodology, May 2026. Check the NSE and BSE sites for the latest.)

What an index move tells you. It describes the group of stocks. It does not tell you what one stock will do. It is not a trade signal by itself.

See it

The table shows a three-stock index. It uses the weights and moves from the worked example. The last column is weight times move.

StockWeightMoveContribution to index
A50%+4%+2.0%
B30%-2%-0.6%
C20%+1%+0.2%
Total100%+1.6%

Read the contribution column. Stock A moves the index most. It has the largest weight and the largest move. Stock B falls. The index still rises, because the fall in B is smaller than the rise in A.

Worked example

This is a hypothetical index move. The index goes from 20,000 to 20,400.

StepCalculationResult
Point change20,400 - 20,000+400 points
Percent change400 / 20,000 x 100+2.0%

This is a hypothetical weighted example, for illustration only. The weights and moves come from the table above.

StepCalculationResult
Stock A contribution0.50 x 4+2.0
Stock B contribution0.30 x (-2)-0.6
Stock C contribution0.20 x 1+0.2
Index move2.0 - 0.6 + 0.2+1.6%

A holding of Rs 50,000 in Stock B falls 2%. That is 50,000 x 0.02 = Rs 1,000. The index still rose 1.6%.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. An index falls from 18,000 to 17,640. Compute the point change and the percent change.

Hints:

  1. Point change = new level minus old level.
  2. Percent change = point change divided by the old level.
  3. Keep the minus sign.

Answer: Point change = 17,640 - 18,000 = -360 points. Percent change = -360 / 18,000 = -2.0%. Check: 2% of 18,000 = 360.

Independent exercise

Task. A three-stock index has weights of 40%, 35% and 25%. The moves are +3%, -1% and -4%, in the same order. Compute the index move. Then compute the effect on a Rs 20,000 holding in the first stock.

Check your answer
  • Contributions: 0.40 x 3 = +1.2; 0.35 x (-1) = -0.35; 0.25 x (-4) = -1.0.
  • Index move = 1.2 - 0.35 - 1.0 = -0.15%. The index falls, even though the first stock rose.
  • Effect on Rs 20,000 in the first stock: 20,000 x 0.03 = Rs 600 gain.

Quiz

Five questions. They cover point and percent change, weights, what an index does and does not say, and the index level as a number.

Paper task

Mode: learning note.

  1. Pick one broad index.
  2. Open the exchange site for that index. Find the current layout yourself.
  3. Record its closing value for five trading days. Write down the source page and the date for each value.
  4. Compute each day’s point change and percent change.
  5. Write no conclusions. Write your numbers in your journal.

Limits and common mistakes

  • An index move describes a group of stocks. It does not describe your stock.
  • A rising index does not mean every stock rose. Breadth and weights matter.
  • The index level is a number of points, not a price in rupees.
  • An index move is not permission to trade.
  • Weights and methods change at rebalancing. The stocks shown here may not be current.

Remember

  • An index is a weighted group of stocks, summed up as one number.
  • Percent change = point change divided by the previous level.
  • Index move = the total of weight x stock move.
  • A heavily weighted stock can move the index more than a light one.
  • An index is market context. It is not a trade signal.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • NSE Indices methodology documents.
  • BSE index pages.
  • Zerodha Varsity, for reference only (link only, no text copied).

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • The Nifty 50 has 50 stocks and uses the free-float market-capitalisation method. Its review schedule comes from NSE Indices’ methodology document of June 2026.
  • The Sensex has 30 stocks, is drawn from the BSE 100, and is reviewed twice a year. This comes from BSE’s Indices methodology of May 2026.
  • This chapter does not list index constituents or their weights, because they change. Check the NSE and BSE sites.

Corporate actions

Hook

You hold 100 shares of a stock priced at Rs 1,000. Overnight, the chart shows the price at Rs 500. That looks like a fall of 50%. This is a hypothetical example. The company has split each share into two. You now hold 200 shares. Each is worth about Rs 500. Your holding is still worth Rs 1,00,000. The chart fell, but you did not lose anything.

The idea

A corporate action is an event that a company decides on. It changes the company’s shares, the share price, or the cash that holders receive.

  • Split: each existing share becomes more shares. Each new share is worth less. The total value does not change at the moment of the split. A “1:2 split” means each share becomes two.
  • Bonus issue: the company gives existing holders free extra shares, in a stated ratio. A “1:1 bonus” gives one extra share for each share you hold. The total value does not change at the moment of the issue.
  • Dividend: cash paid for each share you hold. On the ex-date, the share price drops by about the dividend amount. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on ex-date and record date. Rules change, so check the exchange site.)
  • Rights issue: existing holders can buy new shares at a stated price. The company’s offer document says who can buy, how many shares, at what price and by what date. Read it for each rights issue, because the terms differ. This chapter does not teach the SEBI rights issue rules.

Key dates. The company announces the action. It then sets a record date and an ex-date. The record date is the day the company checks its list of owners to see who gets the benefit. The exchange goes by the record date. Since settlement became T+1, the ex-date and the record date are the same day. To get the benefit you must buy at least one trading day before that day. Think of a cut-off day for a prize list: your name must already be on the list. For bonus issues announced on or after 1 Oct 2024, the bonus shares are credited one working day after the record date and begin trading the day after that. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE corporate actions FAQ updated 7 Jul 2026, Zerodha support, and SEBI circular of 16 Sep 2024. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

Formulas in words.

  • New share count = old share count x the ratio. For a 1:2 split, the ratio is 2.
  • Approximate new price = old price divided by the same ratio.
  • Holding value = shares held x price, plus any cash received.

Adjusted and unadjusted prices.

  • An unadjusted chart shows the real trading price on each day. A split shows up as a sudden fall.
  • An adjusted chart fixes past prices for splits and bonuses. The line stays smooth. Tools differ in how they adjust, and some do not adjust for dividends at all. Check the setting of your own charting tool. Zerodha says Kite charts show corporate actions from the ex-date.

Where announcements come from. The company and the exchange publish corporate actions. Read the official filing. Do not rely on a forwarded summary.

A price drop on a corporate action is not a sell signal. A split or an ex-date drop changes the number on the screen. On its own, it does not change what your holding is worth.

See it

The table shows the same holding before and after each event. The numbers are hypothetical.

EventBeforeAfterValue check
Split 1:2 (each share becomes two)100 shares at Rs 1,000 = Rs 1,00,000200 shares at about Rs 500200 x 500 = Rs 1,00,000
Bonus 1:1 (one free share per share held)100 shares at Rs 1,000 = Rs 1,00,000200 shares at about Rs 500200 x 500 = Rs 1,00,000
Dividend Rs 10 per share (made up)100 shares at Rs 1,000, no cash100 shares at about Rs 990, Rs 1,000 cash100 x 990 + 1,000 = Rs 1,00,000

Look at the value column. Each row ends at the same total. This is before market movement and before tax. The number of shares and the price change. The value of the holding does not.

Worked example

This is a hypothetical example with round figures.

Split 1:2. You hold 100 shares at Rs 1,000.

StepCalculationResult
Value before100 x 1,000Rs 1,00,000
New shares100 x 2200
Approximate new price1,000 / 2about Rs 500
Value after200 x 500Rs 1,00,000

Dividend Rs 10 per share (made up). You hold 100 shares at Rs 1,000.

StepCalculationResult
Cash received100 x 10Rs 1,000
Approximate new price on ex-date1,000 - 10about Rs 990
Value of shares100 x 990Rs 99,000
Total value99,000 + 1,000Rs 1,00,000

This is before market movement and before tax. Tax on dividends is covered in Taxes on trading profits.

Unadjusted chart. The price drops from Rs 1,000 to Rs 500 on the split day. The fall on the chart is (500 - 1,000) / 1,000 = -50%. This is a change in the share count and the price. It is not a loss.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. You hold 300 shares at Rs 600. That is a total of Rs 1,80,000. A 1:1 bonus is announced. Compute the new share count and the approximate new price.

Hints:

  1. Each share gets one free share, so the new count is the old count times 2.
  2. The approximate new price is the old price divided by 2.
  3. Multiply the new count by the new price to check the value.

Answer: 600 shares at about Rs 300 each. Check: 600 x 300 = Rs 1,80,000.

Independent exercise

Task. A stock trades at Rs 900. You hold 50 shares. The company announces a 3:1 split. Each share becomes three. Compute the new share count, the approximate new price and the value.

Check your answer
  • New shares: 50 x 3 = 150.
  • Approximate new price: 900 / 3 = Rs 300.
  • Value: 150 x 300 = Rs 45,000. Check: 50 x 900 = Rs 45,000.

Quiz

Five questions. They cover split arithmetic, dividend value, unadjusted charts, and whether a split or dividend changes total value.

Paper task

Mode: learning note.

  1. Find one past corporate action announcement for a listed company, on the exchange website.
  2. Write down its type, ratio, record date and ex-date in your own words. Mark any date you cannot confirm.
  3. Check whether a chart you use is adjusted or unadjusted, using your own platform’s settings.
  4. Write no trading decision based on the announcement.
  5. Write your notes in your journal.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A split or bonus does not change the value of your holding at the moment it happens.
  • A dividend does not always increase your total value. The price adjusts on the ex-date. The market moves on top of that.
  • A drop on an ex-date or split is not a sell signal. An announcement alone is not a reason to trade.
  • Dates, ratios and rights rules must be checked against the official filing.
  • Tax on dividends is covered in Taxes on trading profits, not here.

Remember

  • A split or bonus changes the share count and the price. The total value stays the same at that moment.
  • A dividend is cash for each share. On the ex-date, the price drops by about that amount.
  • An unadjusted chart can show a false crash after a split.
  • Adjusted charts fix past prices so the line is smooth.
  • Read the official announcement, not a summary of it.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • SEBI LODR regulations.
  • NSE corporate actions page.
  • BSE corporate actions page.
  • Company announcements on exchange filings.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • The record date and ex-date are the same day under T+1. The exchange goes by the record date, which companies set under SEBI’s listing rules (Regulation 42).
  • The bonus timing rule comes from SEBI circular CIR/CFD/PoD/2024/122 of 16 Sep 2024.
  • Rights issue terms and chart adjustment methods were not checked here. Read each company’s offer document, and the help pages of your charting tool.
  • Tax on dividends is not covered here. See Taxes on trading profits.

Market participants and misleading tips

Hook

A headline says, “Foreign investors bought heavily today, so prices will rise.” This is a hypothetical example. On that day, foreign investors bought Rs 4,000 crore of shares. They sold Rs 3,500 crore. So the net buying was Rs 500 crore. The number is real. But it does not say which stocks were bought, or why, or what happens next. A message in a group also offers “assured Rs 10,000 a month” on a Rs 50,000 account. That is a claim to check. It is not information to act on.

The idea

Many kinds of people trade in the Indian market. Knowing who they are helps you read the data. It does not help you predict prices.

  • Retail investors: individuals trading their own money.
  • Institutions: funds, insurers and banks. They have research teams and different cost structures.
  • FII / FPI: foreign investors. DII: domestic institutional investors. These are plain descriptions, not legal definitions. The legal terms are in SEBI’s rules.
  • Proprietary traders and market makers: firms that trade their own money. Some firms also post buy and sell prices at the same time.

Net flow. For a group of participants, net flow = buy value minus sell value. A positive figure is net buying. A negative figure is net selling. NSE publishes FII and DII trading figures on its website, and the page offers downloads. The figures are provisional. NSE builds them from the trading codes that its members enter, and final foreign investor data comes later from the depository, NSDL. NSE’s page gives the latest day’s data, but this chapter does not state the exact hour it appears. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE FII/DII trading activity page. Check the NSE site.)

What a net flow tells you. The total money one group moved on one day.

What a net flow does not tell you. Which stocks were bought. Why they were bought. What the buyers expected. What the price will do next.

The information ladder. Sources sit on three levels:

  • Primary: the company’s own filings, exchange announcements, and regulator orders. These are the original documents.
  • Secondary: news reports, analysis and research. They repeat or explain primary information.
  • Unverified: tips, forwarded messages and social media posts. They have no source you can check.

Start from primary sources. Use secondary sources for context. Treat unverified claims as claims to check, not as facts.

Signs of a misleading tip:

  • assured or promised returns,
  • urgency, such as “join now or lose the chance”,
  • pressure to join a group or pay for a signal,
  • no registration details,
  • claims of insider information.

Advisers and research analysts must be registered with SEBI. SEBI keeps public lists of registered Research Analysts and Investment Advisers on its website. SEBI’s investor site also has a tool to check the registration status of any market intermediary, and a tool called SEBI Check to confirm that a payment ID or account belongs to a registered intermediary. SEBI has also told regulated firms not to work with people who give securities advice without registration, or who promise returns without permission. People who only teach investing are left out of that rule. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI investor support page and SEBI circular of 29 Jan 2025, updated 8 May 2026. Rules change, so check the SEBI site.) Being in a messaging group proves nothing about registration.

The book does not recommend stocks. No source in this chapter is an endorsement.

See it

The table shows the three levels of the information ladder. Each level has a written warning. The warnings are in words, so you do not need to read colour to understand them.

LevelExamplesUse it forWarning
PrimaryCompany filing, exchange announcement, SEBI orderChecking the factsCheck the date and the issuer
SecondaryNews article, analysis reportContext and cross-checkingCheck the author’s method and any conflicts
UnverifiedTip, forwarded message, social media postNothing, until checked against a primary sourceAssured returns, urgency, or payment for signals

Worked example

This is a hypothetical day with made-up numbers.

Net flow.

StepCalculationResult
FII buy valuegivenRs 4,000 crore
FII sell valuegivenRs 3,500 crore
Net flow4,000 - 3,500Rs 500 crore net buying

The figure shows the total flow. It does not show which stocks were bought, or why.

Tip claim. “Assured Rs 10,000 a month from my signals” on a Rs 50,000 account.

StepCalculationResult
Monthly amountgivenRs 10,000
Account sizegivenRs 50,000
Monthly return implied10,000 / 50,000 x 10020% a month

The book treats a promised 20% a month, with a guarantee, as a red flag. It is not information to act on.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Sort these five made-up messages into primary, secondary or unverified. Give a reason for each.

  1. A company’s filing on the exchange website, announcing a board meeting date.
  2. A business news article that sums up that filing.
  3. A forwarded message: “Assured 3x in a month, join now, only 10 seats left.”
  4. A SEBI order published on SEBI’s website.
  5. A social media post with a screenshot of a broker statement and no source given.

Hints:

  1. Who published it: the company, the exchange, the regulator, or someone repeating it?
  2. Is it an original document, an explanation, or a claim with no source?
  3. Can you check it against an official page?

Answer:

  1. Primary. It is the company’s own filing on the exchange.
  2. Secondary. It repeats and sums up a primary source.
  3. Unverified. No source, and it carries assured returns and urgency.
  4. Primary. It is a regulator’s own order.
  5. Unverified. The screenshot has no source you can check, so it is a claim to check.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a checklist of six steps to check a tip before you act on it. Include “do nothing” as an allowed result.

Check your answer

A sample checklist:

  1. Find the original source of the claim.
  2. Find the primary document (filing, exchange announcement or regulator order) that supports it.
  3. Check whether the person or firm is SEBI-registered, using the official lookup on the SEBI website.
  4. Check for red flags: assured returns, urgency, payment for signals, group pressure.
  5. Write down what would prove the claim wrong, and check for it.
  6. Decide. “Do nothing” is an allowed result. Do not act on a tip alone.

Quiz

Five questions. They cover net flow arithmetic, what a net flow does and does not show, red flags in tips, and checking adviser registration.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. No action on tips.

  1. Create a journal entry titled “Information sources I used”.
  2. For each source you read this week, write the date, the source name, its type (primary, secondary or unverified) and its verification status.
  3. Mark any unverified claim as “not checked”.
  4. Take no action on any tip, group or signal service.
  5. Write your entry in your journal.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A net flow figure shows total money moved. It does not show which stocks, why, or what happens next.
  • A registration check is not an endorsement of the adviser’s views.
  • The book does not recommend stocks, groups, or signal services.
  • A source that looks official is not verified. Check the primary document.
  • Scams often use assured returns and pressure to act fast. Treat both as warning signs, not as information.

Remember

  • Net flow = buy value minus sell value. It is a total for a group, not a stock pick.
  • Start from primary sources. Use secondary sources for context. Treat unverified claims as claims.
  • Red flags: assured returns, urgency, payment for signals, group pressure, insider claims, no registration.
  • Being in a group does not mean an adviser is registered. Check the SEBI list.
  • Doing nothing is an allowed result. Often it is the right one.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • SEBI investor education and alerts pages.
  • SEBI registered intermediaries list.
  • NSE FII/DII page.
  • Exchange announcement pages.

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • The FII/DII data source and its provisional nature come from NSE’s FII/DII trading activity page. The time of day it is published can change, so check the NSE page.
  • The registration check tools and the SEBI helpline come from SEBI’s investor support page. The rule on unregistered advice comes from SEBI’s circular of 29 Jan 2025.
  • The participant descriptions are plain-English summaries and were not checked against SEBI’s legal definitions.
  • Current SEBI investor alerts change often. Read them on SEBI’s investor site.

Why money math comes before charts

Hook

Two traders start with Rs 50,000 each. They take the same ten hypothetical trades, in the same order. Both have the same five wins and five losses. One finishes at Rs 52,500. The other finishes at Rs 75,000. But on the way, it fell to Rs 30,000. That is a 40% fall from where it started. The only difference is how many rupees each trader risked on each trade. (This is a hypothetical example. It shows arithmetic, not a strategy.)

The idea

Every trade has two parts.

  • Direction: the trade wins or loses. You do not control this part.
  • Size: how many rupees you win or lose. You do control this part, before the trade.

Most beginners look only at direction. That is why they quote a win rate and stop there. The win rate is the share of trades that win. It tells you how often you win. It does not tell you how much.

R is the rupee amount you risk on one trade. Once you fix R, you can write every result as a multiple of R.

  • A loss of the planned size is -1R.
  • A win twice the planned size is +2R.

Writing results in R lets you compare trades and traders. You do not need to know their account size. The arithmetic is also easier to check.

Two numbers describe a set of results:

  • Win rate describes frequency: wins divided by total trades.
  • Average win and average loss describe size: how many rupees (or R) a typical winner and a typical loser are worth.

You need both. A high win rate with tiny wins and large losses can still shrink an account. A low win rate with large wins can grow one. Neither number is enough alone.

Keep the same results but change only the size. You get a different equity path. This is the running account balance after each trade. The drawdown is the fall from the highest balance reached so far. It is shown as a percentage of that high point. The same results with a bigger R give bigger swings and deeper drawdowns.

Put in one line: equity after a trade = equity before the trade + (multiple of R) x R. In symbols, E is equity and m is the R-multiple of the result. So E(new) = E(old) + m x R.

Charts show price. They do not show how many shares you hold, how much you risk, or what costs you pay. Money math turns a chart idea into a result in rupees. If you do not set size and costs first, a chart cannot tell you what a plan would do to your account.

Everything in this chapter is hypothetical. The numbers are chosen to be easy to check. They do not describe any real strategy. A sequence of ten trades is far too short to judge one.

See it

Look at the two lines. They start at the same point and end at different heights. Trader B’s line dips much lower before it recovers. Change the R for Trader B to a smaller number and watch the dip shrink. The win and loss pattern stays the same.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Both traders start with Rs 50,000. The sequence is four losses, then five wins, then one loss. That is 5 wins and 5 losses, a 50% win rate. Each win is +2R. Each loss is -1R.

Trader A risks R = Rs 500, which is 1% of Rs 50,000.

TradeResultChange in rupeesEquity after trade
Start50,000
1Loss, -1R-50049,500
2Loss, -1R-50049,000
3Loss, -1R-50048,500
4Loss, -1R-50048,000
5Win, +2R+1,000 (2 x 500)49,000
6Win, +2R+1,00050,000
7Win, +2R+1,00051,000
8Win, +2R+1,00052,000
9Win, +2R+1,00053,000
10Loss, -1R-500 (1 x 500)52,500

Trader A ends at Rs 52,500. Net result: 52,500 - 50,000 = Rs 2,500. That is 2,500 / 50,000 = 5%. The worst drawdown is from Rs 50,000 down to Rs 48,000. That is 2,000 / 50,000 = 4%.

Trader B risks R = Rs 5,000, which is 10% of Rs 50,000. The sequence is the same.

TradeResultChange in rupeesEquity after trade
Start50,000
1Loss, -1R-5,00045,000
2Loss, -1R-5,00040,000
3Loss, -1R-5,00035,000
4Loss, -1R-5,00030,000
5Win, +2R+10,000 (2 x 5,000)40,000
6Win, +2R+10,00050,000
7Win, +2R+10,00060,000
8Win, +2R+10,00070,000
9Win, +2R+10,00080,000
10Loss, -1R-5,000 (1 x 5,000)75,000

Trader B ends at Rs 75,000. Net result: 25,000 / 50,000 = 50%. The worst drawdown is from Rs 50,000 down to Rs 30,000. That is 20,000 / 50,000 = 40%.

Reversed order. Keep the same ten results. But move the five wins to the front, then the five losses. Trader B’s equity runs: 50,000, 60,000, 70,000, 80,000, 90,000, 100,000 after the five wins. Then it runs 95,000, 90,000, 85,000, 80,000, 75,000 after the five losses. The end is again Rs 75,000. But the path is different. The high point is now Rs 1,00,000. The fall to Rs 75,000 is 25,000 / 1,00,000 = 25%. Same ending, different ride.

Lesson. Trader A and Trader B had the same win rate, the same R-multiples and the same sequence. Only R changed. Trader B’s bigger size gave a bigger final number and a much deeper fall. A bigger final number does not make a bigger size safer.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Fill in the equity after each trade for Trader A (R = Rs 500), using the sequence in the worked example. Check that trade 10 ends at Rs 52,500.

Hints:

  1. Each loss subtracts 500 from the equity before it.
  2. Each win adds 1,000 (that is 2 x 500).
  3. Write the balance after every row before starting the next row.

Answer: 49,500; 49,000; 48,500; 48,000; 49,000; 50,000; 51,000; 52,000; 53,000; 52,500.

Independent exercise

Task. Trader C uses R = Rs 1,000 (2% of Rs 50,000) on the same sequence of ten results. Compute the lowest equity, the final equity, the net return and the worst drawdown.

Check your answer
  • Lowest equity: 50,000 - 4 x 1,000 = Rs 46,000 (after four losses).
  • Final equity: after the four losses, 46,000. The five wins add 5 x 2,000 = 10,000, giving 56,000. The last loss subtracts 1,000, giving Rs 55,000.
  • Net return: 5,000 / 50,000 = 10%.
  • Worst drawdown: from 50,000 down to 46,000 = 4,000 / 50,000 = 8%.

Quiz

The quiz checks the difference between win rate and size, and the arithmetic of the equity path.

Paper task

Mode: learning note (not a trading record). Write it in your journal.

  1. Make a table with ten rows, one per trade, for Trader A (R = Rs 500) and Trader B (R = Rs 5,000). Use the sequence L, L, L, L, W, W, W, W, W, L.
  2. For each row, write the change in rupees and the equity after the trade.
  3. Write down the worst drawdown for each trader as a percentage.
  4. Add one line: “Same win rate, different size, different drawdown.” Mark the note as hypothetical.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not tell you that any trading approach is profitable. The sequence is made up and has only ten trades.
  • It does not show that a 50% win rate is good or bad. Win rate alone never settles the result. For the rest of the picture, see Expectancy and profit factor.
  • It ignores costs. Real trades pay costs (Costs on a trade), and those change every line of the table.
  • It does not show that a particular R is safe. Real losing streaks can be longer than four trades (Consecutive losses and losing streaks).

Remember

  • Every trade has a direction you cannot control and a size you can.
  • R is the rupee amount risked on a trade. Express results as multiples of R.
  • Win rate describes frequency. Average win and average loss describe size. You need both.
  • Same results, bigger R: a bigger swing and a deeper drawdown.
  • Set size and costs before you discuss any chart.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, position sizing concepts, for reference only (link only, no text copied).

Costs on a trade

Hook

A trader buys Rs 5,000 of a share. A few days later, the trader sells it at exactly the same price. Nothing moved, so the trader expects to break even. But the statement shows a loss. The difference is costs. There are several small charges on the buy, and more on the sell. This chapter shows how those charges add up. It uses a DUMMY model with round numbers, so you can check every line by hand.

The idea

A trade has two legs: the buy and the sell. Each leg can carry several charges. Your total cost is the sum of all of them.

The charge types you will meet on Indian equity trades are these.

  • Brokerage: what the broker charges for carrying out the order.
  • Securities transaction tax (STT): a government tax on trades. On delivery trades it is charged on both the buy and the sell. On intraday trades it is charged only on the sell.
  • Exchange transaction charge: a small fee the exchange charges on the value traded.
  • SEBI fee: the regulator’s small fee on the value traded.
  • Stamp duty: a government duty on the buy side only. Since 1 July 2020 it is the same in every state.
  • GST: a tax on some of the other charges, not on the whole trade. It is charged on brokerage, SEBI fee and exchange transaction charge.
  • Depository (DP) charge: a charge when you sell shares from your demat account. It is charged once for each share name sold on a day, whatever the quantity.

Charges fall into two groups:

  • Proportional charges grow with turnover. Turnover is price x quantity for one leg. Double the trade, and the charge doubles.
  • Fixed charges do not grow with turnover. A fixed amount per order, or per stock per day, is the same for a small trade and a large one.

This is why costs weigh more on small trades. Fixed charges take a bigger share of a small position.

Two formulas:

  • Turnover of a leg = price x quantity.
  • Total cost = sum of all charges on both legs.

Break-even move, in words: total cost divided by the position value. In symbols, with C for the total cost and V for the position value, the break-even move is about C / V. It is only approximate. The exit price changes the proportional charges. Use it as a rule of thumb, not as an exact figure.

DUMMY model. The arithmetic below uses this model. It is not any broker’s, exchange’s or government’s rate.

  • Proportional: 0.1% of turnover on each leg.
  • Fixed: Rs 20 per order, on each leg.
  • DP charge: Rs 50 per stock, on the sell leg only.

These are round numbers for practice. They are not the real charges. Do not quote them as Zerodha or government figures. The real charges are in the next table.

Brokerage on delivery is not in the practice model. Intraday costs are not worked out here.

The real charges today

The table below shows what Zerodha lists for equity trades on its charges page. The exchange, SEBI and government charges are set outside Zerodha.

ChargeEquity deliveryEquity intraday
BrokerageZero0.03% or Rs 20 per executed order, whichever is lower
STT0.1% on buy and on sell0.025% on the sell side only
NSE transaction charge0.00307% of the value traded0.00307% of the value traded
BSE transaction charge0.00375% of the value traded0.00375% of the value traded
SEBI feeRs 10 per croreRs 10 per crore
Stamp duty (buy side only)0.015%0.003%
GST18% on brokerage + SEBI fee + transaction charge18% on brokerage + SEBI fee + transaction charge
DP chargeRs 15.34 for each share name sold in a day (Rs 3.50 depository fee + Rs 9.50 Zerodha fee + Rs 2.34 GST), whatever the quantityNot charged, because nothing is sold from the demat account

A few more Zerodha charges are worth knowing. Orders placed through a dealer (call and trade) cost Rs 50 per order. When an intraday position is closed for you by auto square-off, Rs 50 plus 18% GST is charged for each order closed. For new accounts, the yearly account fee (AMC) is free for the first year. After that, the amount depends on the account type and the value of your holdings, so read it on the charges page.

(As of 11 Nov 2026, from the Zerodha charges page, the NSE circular of 27 Feb 2026 and the SEBI note on stamp duty of June 2020. Rules and rates change, so check the Zerodha charges page and brokerage calculator before you rely on any figure.)

Zerodha’s page says all statutory and regulatory charges are levied “at actuals”. It does not state a rounding rule for every line. STT is rounded to the nearest rupee. The other lines may differ by a few paise from your own sum. The official brokerage calculator is the check.

One more point. A zero on one line is not a zero cost. A broker may show zero brokerage. The other charges in the list above still apply. Add every component.

See it

The table below splits the dummy cost of two round trips into proportional, fixed and DP parts. Read down the columns.

Component (DUMMY)Rs 5,000 round tripRs 50,000 round trip
Proportional, 0.1% per leg5 + 5 = 1050 + 50 = 100
Fixed per order, Rs 20 per leg20 + 20 = 4020 + 20 = 40
DP charge on sell, Rs 505050
Total100190
Share that is fixed (order + DP)90 of 100 = 90%90 of 190 = 47.4%

The point of the table is the shares. Fixed charges make up most of the cost on the small trade. They matter much less on the large trade.

Use the walkthrough to step through each leg. Change the trade value to see which lines move and which stay the same. The DUMMY label means the rates are not real.

Worked example

Hypothetical, DUMMY model (round numbers for practice, not the real charges). The buy and sell prices are the same, so the price does not move.

Round trip of Rs 5,000

StepCalculationResult
Buy turnover5,000Rs 5,000
Buy proportional0.1% x 5,000 = 5Rs 5.00
Buy fixedRs 20Rs 20.00
Buy leg total5.00 + 20Rs 25.00
Sell turnover5,000Rs 5,000
Sell proportional0.1% x 5,000 = 5Rs 5.00
Sell fixedRs 20Rs 20.00
Sell DP chargeRs 50Rs 50.00
Sell leg total5.00 + 20 + 50Rs 75.00
Total cost25.00 + 75.00Rs 100.00

Total cost as a share of the position: 100 / 5,000 = 0.02 = 2.0%. The break-even move is about 2.0%.

Round trip of Rs 50,000

StepCalculationResult
Buy leg total50 + 20Rs 70.00
Sell leg total50 + 20 + 50Rs 120.00
Total cost70 + 120Rs 190.00

Total cost as a share of the position: 190 / 50,000 = 0.0038 = 0.38%.

The DP charge in both cases. It is Rs 50 each time. As a share of the trade, it is 50 / 5,000 = 1.0% for the small trade. It is 50 / 50,000 = 0.1% for the large one. The same rupee charge is ten times the percentage on the small trade.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Using the DUMMY model, compute the total round-trip cost for a Rs 10,000 round trip. The buy and sell prices are the same. Give the total in rupees and as a percentage of Rs 10,000.

Hints:

  1. Compute the buy leg first. Find the proportional charge on Rs 10,000, then add the fixed Rs 20.
  2. Compute the sell leg. Add the proportional charge, the fixed Rs 20 and the Rs 50 DP charge.
  3. Add the two legs, then divide by Rs 10,000.

Answer: Buy leg: 10 + 20 = Rs 30. Sell leg: 10 + 20 + 50 = Rs 80. Total: Rs 110, which is 110 / 10,000 = 1.1%.

Independent exercise

Task. Buy 100 shares at Rs 200 (Rs 20,000). Later, sell all 100 at Rs 210 (Rs 21,000). Using the DUMMY model, compute the total cost, the gross profit before costs and the net profit after costs.

Check your answer
  • Buy leg: proportional 0.1% x 20,000 = 20; fixed 20; total 40.
  • Sell leg: proportional 0.1% x 21,000 = 21; fixed 20; DP 50; total 91.
  • Total cost: 40 + 91 = Rs 131.
  • Gross profit: (210 - 200) x 100 = Rs 1,000.
  • Net profit: 1,000 - 131 = Rs 869.

This is DUMMY arithmetic. It is not an estimate of any real trade.

Quiz

The quiz checks the arithmetic of the dummy model. It checks the difference between fixed and proportional charges, and why you must verify a rate before you use it.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal.

  1. Open the official Zerodha charges page and the official brokerage calculator. Write down the date you looked.
  2. For one Rs 5,000 delivery trade and one Rs 50,000 delivery trade, write each component line and the total the calculator gives.
  3. Write the list of component names in your own words, next to the source link.
  4. Do not copy these figures into the book or into any calculation yet. Mark the note as a learning note, with no trade placed.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not tell you any real charge. Every rate here is DUMMY. The official sources are the only place for current rates.
  • It does not include tax. Tax on gains is a separate question (Taxes on trading profits).
  • It does not include slippage, the difference between the expected and the actual fill price (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk). A real trade can cost more than the table shows.
  • It does not say that a small trade is a bad idea. It says that fixed charges weigh more on small trades. It also says you must include them in every plan.

Remember

  • Costs are the sum of every charge on both legs, not just brokerage.
  • Proportional charges grow with turnover. Fixed charges do not.
  • On a Rs 5,000 round trip in the dummy model, the total is Rs 100, or 2.0%. On Rs 50,000, it is Rs 190, or 0.38%.
  • A zero on one line is not a zero cost.
  • Verify every rate from the official source, with a date, before you use it.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha charges page (zerodha.com/charges), read on 11 Nov 2026, and the Zerodha brokerage calculator.
  • Zerodha support pages on STT (STT rates and rounding), DP charges and auto square-off charges, read on 11 Nov 2026.
  • NSE circular NSE/FA/73061 of 27 Feb 2026, revision in transaction charges, in force from 1 Mar 2026.
  • SEBI note on stamp duty rates from 1 July 2020 (FAQ on the Indian Stamp Act, June 2020).
  • SEBI turnover fee: as listed on the Zerodha charges page. SEBI sets this fee, so check SEBI’s own pages too.

Taxes on trading profits

Hook

A trader makes Rs 10,000 gross in a year. The trader pays Rs 300 in trading costs. The trader assumes the remaining Rs 9,700 is the profit. Months later, a tax notice arrives. It is for an amount that is not in the trader’s plan at all. The mistake was not the arithmetic. The mistake was assuming that tax is simply a percentage of trading turnover. The trader did not check how the activity is classified. This chapter explains the ideas first. It then lists the main rules as the official sources state them.

The idea

Tax is a separate layer from trading costs. Costs (Costs on a trade) are paid to brokers, exchanges and the government on each trade. Tax is worked out on your net gains for the year. The rules depend on what kind of activity it is.

Three ideas matter:

  • Classification. The same stock can be taxed in different ways. It depends on whether you hold it as an investment, trade it as a business, or do something else. The classification depends on facts. Those facts include how long you hold, how often you trade, and what you intend. The law treats a trade that is not settled by taking delivery of the shares (for example, buying and selling the same day) as a speculative transaction. If such trading is done as a business, it is a separate “speculation business”.
  • Holding period. For listed shares, a gain is short-term if you held the shares for twelve months or less. It is long-term if you held them for more than twelve months. The rates are different for the two.
  • Loss set-off. Some losses can cancel out some gains. Some losses can be carried forward to later years. The rules below show which losses, against which gains, and for how long.

The main rules in one place

These are the rules for listed shares bought and sold on a stock exchange where STT is paid. They apply from the 2026-27 tax year, when the new Income-tax Act, 2025 came into force on 1 April 2026. Your own case may differ. A qualified tax professional should confirm what applies to you.

TopicWhat the official sources say
Short-term gain on listed shares (held 12 months or less)Taxed at 20%
Long-term gain on listed shares (held more than 12 months)The first Rs 1,25,000 of long-term gains in a year is not taxed. The part above that is taxed at 12.5%, with no indexation
STT conditionThese special rates apply only where STT was paid. For long-term gains on shares, STT must have been paid when you bought and when you sold, with some exceptions
Capital loss set-offA short-term loss can be set against any capital gain. A long-term loss can be set only against long-term gains
Capital loss carry-forwardAn unused capital loss can be carried forward for up to 8 years. It can be used only against capital gains
Business income that is not speculativeA loss can be set against other heads of income in the same year (but not salary). An unused loss can be carried forward for up to 8 years and used only against business income
Speculation business (for example, intraday trades with no delivery)A loss can be set only against profit from another speculation business. An unused loss can be carried forward for up to 4 years
Tax auditA person with business income must have the accounts audited if turnover is over Rs 1 crore. The limit is Rs 10 crore if cash receipts and cash payments are each 5% or less of the total
ITR form (individual)Capital gains with no business income are reported in ITR-2. Business income, including trading as a business, is reported in ITR-3
DividendsDividend is added to your income and taxed under “income from other sources”. The company may deduct tax before paying it. Only interest on money borrowed to earn it can be deducted, up to 20% of the dividend
Tax regimeThe new tax regime is the default. A person with business income who wants the old regime must choose it by the return due date, and the choice can be changed back only once

(As of 11 Nov 2026. Sources: the Income-tax Act, 2025 as published by the Income Tax Department, and the Income Tax Department e-filing help pages on loss set-off and on ITR forms. Rates and limits change with each Budget, so check incometax.gov.in each year.)

The same rules applied under the older Income-tax Act, 1961 for income up to 31 March 2026. Section numbers changed, but the rates above did not. Losses from earlier years carry forward under the new Act as they did under the old one.

Costs matter too. STT is not allowed as a deduction when you work out capital gains. For business income, STT paid on trades done in the course of the business can be claimed as a business expense. Other costs, such as brokerage, depend on the head of income. Ask a professional which costs apply to you.

What the book does and does not do. This book does not work out your tax. Your tax depends on your own facts, your other income and the year. The practice sum below uses a made-up rate to show the algebra only.

The formula, in words first. Net result = gross gain - trading costs - tax. Tax = rate x taxable base. The taxable base depends on the classification. Whether costs can be deducted from it depends on the head of income, as the notes above show.

In symbols, G is the gross gain, C is the trading costs, B is the taxable base, and t is a tax rate. In the practice sum, t is a made-up number.

  • If costs can be deducted, B = G - C.
  • Tax = t x B.
  • Net = G - C - t x B.

The symbol t is a placeholder. The practice sum below gives it a made-up value only to show the algebra. The real rates are in the table above.

Questions about classification. Before a tax figure means anything, you need answers. For example: How long did I hold this? How many trades did I make in the year? Was I investing, or running trading as a business? A qualified tax professional should answer these questions with you. Writing your answers in your journal helps. The journal becomes the record you will need.

See it

The decision tree below shows the kinds of questions that decide classification. It does not give a result. Each branch ends in a question for a professional.

flowchart TD
    A["What is the activity?"] --> B{"How long is the holding period?"}
    A --> C{"How often do you trade, and what is the intent?"}
    B -->|"12 months or less"| D["Short-term capital gain may apply"]
    B -->|"More than 12 months"| E["Long-term capital gain may apply"]
    C -->|"Frequent, business-like"| F["Business income may apply, and an audit limit may matter"]
    C -->|"Occasional, investment-like"| G["Capital gains may apply"]
    D --> H[Ask a qualified tax professional]
    E --> H
    F --> H
    G --> H

Read the tree from the top. The branches show which facts you would need to bring to a professional. The rates are in the table above.

Worked example

Hypothetical, symbolic. The gross gain is Rs 10,000. Trading costs are Rs 300. For this practice sum, assume costs can be deducted from the taxable base. In real life this depends on the head of income, as noted above.

StepExpressionValue
Gross gainGRs 10,000
Trading costsCRs 300
Taxable baseB = G - C10,000 - 300 = Rs 9,700
Taxt x Bt x 9,700
Net resultG - C - t x B10,000 - 300 - t x 9,700 = 9,700 - 9,700t

DUMMY illustration, for algebra only (a made-up round number, not your tax). If t were 20%, tax would be 0.20 x 9,700 = Rs 1,940. The net would be 9,700 - 1,940 = Rs 7,760. The 20% here only shows that the algebra works. It is not a calculation of anyone’s real tax. Do not use it as a tax figure.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. A made-up year has a gross gain of Rs 4,000 and trading costs of Rs 200. Write the net expression using the placeholder rate t. Keep t as a symbol. Do not put a number in it.

Hints:

  1. Find the taxable base first. It is the gross gain minus the costs.
  2. Tax is t multiplied by the taxable base.
  3. Net is the gross gain minus the costs minus the tax.

Answer: Taxable base = 4,000 - 200 = 3,800. Tax = t x 3,800. Net = 4,000 - 200 - t x 3,800 = 3,800 - 3,800t, which is 3,800 x (1 - t).

Independent exercise

Task. List five questions you would bring to a qualified tax professional before your first year of trading.

Check your answer

There is no single correct list. Here are examples of good questions:

  1. How does my holding period change the treatment of my gains?
  2. At what level of trading activity does my activity count as a business, and does that change my treatment?
  3. Which of my losses can cancel which of my gains? Can any be carried forward, and for how long?
  4. Do my trading costs reduce my taxable base, and which costs?
  5. Do I need to meet a tax audit requirement, and at what turnover?

Quiz

The quiz checks the main ideas. Tax is not a flat percentage of turnover. Holding period and classification matter. Loss set-off has rules.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal.

  1. For each trade you log, add a classification note column with three entries: holding period, intent, and how often you trade.
  2. Do not calculate any tax figure. The column is a record for later, and a professional can use it.
  3. Mark the entries as learning notes, not live records.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not tell you what tax you will pay. It lists the main rules as the official sources state them. Your own case may differ.
  • This is not tax advice. The book does not give tax advice. Talk to a qualified professional.
  • Gains and losses are not always treated the same way across activities. The differences are the reason to get advice.
  • It does not show that a tax bill is small or large for any plan. A tax bill can be larger than expected in a year with losses, depending on the rules.

Remember

  • Tax is separate from trading costs. It applies to net gains under rules that depend on the activity.
  • Classification, holding period and loss set-off decide much of the treatment. For listed shares, the dividing line is 12 months. Short-term gains are taxed at 20%. Long-term gains above Rs 1,25,000 a year are taxed at 12.5%.
  • The formula is net = gross gain - costs - tax, and tax = rate x taxable base.
  • Keep a classification record for every trade from the start.
  • Take the questions to a qualified tax professional.

Sources and verification

  • Income-tax Act, 2025 (Act 30 of 2025), published by the Income Tax Department (incometaxindia.gov.in). It covers the rates on listed shares, the Rs 1,25,000 exemption, loss set-off and carry-forward, speculation business, tax audit limits and the new tax regime. Read on 11 Nov 2026.
  • Income Tax Department e-filing help pages (incometax.gov.in) on set-off and carry-forward of losses, and on the ITR form for business income. Read on 11 Nov 2026.
  • The same rates and holding period applied under the older Income-tax Act, 1961 for transfers on or after 23 July 2024.
  • Zerodha Varsity tax section, for reference only (link only, no text copied).

Risk per trade and stop-loss distance

Hook

Imagine you have Rs 50,000. You decide in advance to risk Rs 500 on a trade. Two plans both risk Rs 500 in total. One buys 50 shares. The other buys 25. The difference is not the budget. It is where each plan puts its stop, the price at which the idea is wrong. A stop far from the entry allows fewer shares for the same rupee risk. This chapter shows the arithmetic, step by step.

The idea

Start with the rule that comes first. Set the risk per trade before you make the trade. Turn it into a rupee amount based on your capital.

  • Capital is the money you have set aside for trading (The capital-first rules).
  • Risk percentage is the share of that capital you accept losing on one trade. A common planning figure is 1%. In this book it is a planning setting, not a fact.
  • Risk budget is capital x risk percentage, in rupees.

The stop is the price at which the trade idea is wrong. You pick it because of the idea, not to fit a budget. This order matters. If you pick the stop to fit the budget, the stop no longer says anything about the trade.

Once the stop is chosen, the rest is arithmetic. For a long trade (you buy first and sell later). This track covers long trades only:

  • Risk per share = entry price - stop price. This is the rupee loss on each share if the stop is hit.
  • Shares = risk budget / risk per share, rounded down to a whole number.
  • Stop distance as a percentage of entry = risk per share / entry price.
  • Position value = shares x entry price.
  • Planned loss at the stop = shares x risk per share.

Rounding down matters. If the formula gives 31.25 shares, you buy 31, not 32. Rounding up would risk more than the budget.

Two rules follow from the arithmetic:

  • A tighter stop (closer to entry) gives a smaller risk per share. So the same budget buys more shares.
  • A wider stop (further from entry) gives a larger risk per share. So the same budget buys fewer shares.

Two numbers are easy to confuse:

  • Risk percentage (1% of capital) is the planned loss as a share of capital.
  • Position percentage is the position value as a share of capital.

A 1% risk does not mean a 1% position. In the example below, a 1% risk gives a position of 25% of capital.

Rules for the stop itself:

  • A stop above the entry price is invalid for a long trade. The stop must sit below the entry. If the plan has a stop above the entry, reject the plan or fix it before you do any arithmetic.
  • Never move a stop further away after entry to avoid a loss. That turns a planned loss into an unplanned one.
  • Ways to place stops, based on how much a price usually moves, are taught in Track 5. This chapter covers only the arithmetic.

See it

Move the stop closer to the entry and watch the share count rise. Move it further away and watch the share count fall. The rupee risk stays near Rs 500 in each case. Look at the position value too. It changes with the share count, not with the risk budget.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1% = Rs 500. Entry Rs 250.

Plan 1: stop at Rs 240

StepCalculationResult
Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
Risk per share250 - 240Rs 10
Stop distance10 / 2504%
Raw shares500 / 1050
Position value50 x 250Rs 12,500
Position as % of capital12,500 / 50,00025%
Planned loss50 x 10Rs 500

Plan 2: stop at Rs 245 (tighter)

StepCalculationResult
Risk per share250 - 245Rs 5
Raw shares500 / 5100
Position value before limit100 x 250Rs 25,000
Position as % of capital25,000 / 50,00050%

A 50% position is above the default 25% limit for one position. This limit is used in Lab 1. With the limit, the largest allowed position is 12,500 / 250 = 50 shares. The planned loss becomes 50 x 5 = Rs 250, not Rs 500. Here the limit, not the risk budget, sets the size. Position sizing step by step works through this in full.

Plan 3: stop at Rs 230 (wider)

StepCalculationResult
Risk per share250 - 230Rs 20
Shares500 / 2025
Position value25 x 250Rs 6,250
Position as % of capital6,250 / 50,00012.5%
Planned loss25 x 20Rs 500

Lesson. Plans 1 and 3 each plan a Rs 500 loss, with 50 and 25 shares. Plan 2 shows that a tighter stop does not always mean a bigger planned loss. The limit cut its size to 50 shares and the planned loss to Rs 250. The stop distance sets the share count before limits are applied. The limits and the risk budget then decide the final size.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Capital Rs 50,000, risk 1%, entry Rs 400, stop Rs 384. Work out the risk budget, the risk per share, the shares (rounded down), the position value, the position as a percentage of capital and the planned loss.

Hints:

  1. Find the risk budget first. It is 1% of Rs 50,000.
  2. Find the raw share count. Then round down before you do anything else with it.
  3. Work out the planned loss from the rounded share count, not the raw one.

Answer:

  • Risk budget: Rs 500.
  • Risk per share: 400 - 384 = Rs 16.
  • Raw shares: 500 / 16 = 31.25, so 31 shares.
  • Position value: 31 x 400 = Rs 12,400.
  • Position as % of capital: 12,400 / 50,000 = 24.8%.
  • Planned loss: 31 x 16 = Rs 496. The unused part of the budget is Rs 4.

Independent exercise

Task. Capital Rs 30,000, risk 0.5%, entry Rs 120, stop Rs 111. Work out the risk budget, the risk per share, the shares, the position value, the position percentage and the planned loss.

Check your answer
  • Risk budget: 30,000 x 0.5% = Rs 150.
  • Risk per share: 120 - 111 = Rs 9.
  • Raw shares: 150 / 9 = 16.67, so 16 shares.
  • Position value: 16 x 120 = Rs 1,920.
  • Position as % of capital: 1,920 / 30,000 = 6.4%.
  • Planned loss: 16 x 9 = Rs 144.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the stop rules, rounding down, and the difference between risk percentage and position percentage.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Use Lab 1, if it is available. Run its sensitivity strip. This shows the results for stop distances of Rs 2, Rs 5, Rs 10 and Rs 20 on one hypothetical entry price.
  2. If Lab 1 is not yet available, do the same sums by hand with capital Rs 50,000, risk 1% and entry Rs 250.
  3. Record the shares and the planned loss for each stop distance in a learning-note entry.
  4. Mark the entry as hypothetical. No order is placed.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not tell you where a good stop is. The stop must come from the reason for the trade. This chapter only does the arithmetic.
  • It does not show that a 1% planned risk is a 1% maximum loss. A gap (a jump in price with no trading in between) or a circuit (a trading halt set by the exchange) can make the loss bigger than planned. See Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk.
  • It does not make a tight stop safer. A very tight stop can be hit by ordinary small price moves. Then you can take many small losses.
  • It does not change the rule. Never raise the risk percentage after losses.

Remember

  • Set the risk budget first. It is capital x risk percentage, in rupees.
  • For a long trade, risk per share = entry - stop. A stop at or above the entry is invalid.
  • Shares = risk budget / risk per share, rounded down.
  • A tighter stop allows more shares for the same rupee risk. A wider stop allows fewer.
  • Risk percentage is not position percentage. 1% risk gave a 25% position in the example.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity position sizing concepts, as a reference only (link, no text copied).
  • The 1% risk and the 25% position limit are settings chosen for this book. They are not facts.

Position sizing step by step

Hook

Imagine you have Rs 10,000. You set a tight stop, Rs 2 below an entry of Rs 250. The risk formula says you can buy 50 shares. But 50 shares at Rs 250 cost Rs 12,500. That is more than your whole account. The tight stop did not make the position bigger. It made the risk formula ask for a size your account cannot buy. This chapter shows how to find the real size, step by step. It also shows which limit decides it.

The idea

Position sizing takes three limits and keeps the smallest one. The three limits are:

  1. Risk limit: how many shares the risk budget allows.
  2. Cash limit: how many shares the cash you hold can buy.
  3. Concentration limit: how many shares keep one position within a set share of your capital.

The five steps:

  • Step 1, risk budget. Risk budget = capital x risk %.
  • Step 2, risk per share. Risk per share = entry - stop, for a long trade.
  • Step 3, raw shares. Raw shares = floor(risk budget / risk per share). “Floor” means round down to a whole number.
  • Step 4, cash cap. Cash cap = floor(capital / entry). A long delivery trade means you pay for the shares and keep them. You need the cash to buy them. This track uses no leverage, which means no borrowed money. So the cash you hold limits the size.
  • Step 5, concentration cap. Concentration cap = floor(capital x max position % / entry). This cap limits how much of your capital one position can use. The 25% figure is a setting in Lab 1. You can change it. It is not a market rule.

Then:

  • Shares = the minimum of the three limits (raw shares, cash cap, concentration cap).
  • Binding constraint is the limit that gives the smallest number. That limit sets your size. Name it. If two limits tie, say so.

Some points to keep in mind:

  • This track uses whole shares throughout.
  • Tick size is the smallest step a price can move. It can matter if a plan rounds prices. On NSE cash shares the tick size depends on the share price. It is Re 0.01 for prices below Rs 250, Rs 0.05 from Rs 250 to Rs 1,000, and Rs 0.10 above Rs 1,000 up to Rs 5,000. It gets larger still for higher prices. NSE reviews the tick size of each share every month. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE circulars on tick size. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)
  • Lot size is the smallest number of shares you can trade at once. For shares traded in demat form on NSE, the market lot is one share. This book treats cash equity as whole shares. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE trading system page. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)
  • Lab 1 adds costs to show the true loss. The costs themselves are covered in Costs on a trade.

The main lesson: a tighter stop does not mean a larger position. A tighter stop raises the raw share count. The cash and concentration caps can then cut it. The stop changes what the risk formula allows. The caps decide what you can actually hold.

See it

The three bars show the three limits. The shortest bar is the answer. For Example A, the risk limit and the concentration cap tie at 50 shares. The cash cap is far above them. Change the stop to Rs 248 and look at how the risk bar grows. The concentration bar stays put.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Example A. Capital Rs 50,000. Entry Rs 250. Stop Rs 240.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
2. Risk per share250 - 240Rs 10
3. Raw sharesfloor(500 / 10)50
4. Cash capfloor(50,000 / 250)200
5. Concentration capfloor(50,000 x 25% / 250) = floor(12,500 / 250)50
Sharesminimum of 50, 200, 5050

Binding constraint: the risk limit. The concentration cap ties with it at 50 shares. Position value: 50 x 250 = Rs 12,500, which is 25% of capital. Planned loss: 50 x 10 = Rs 500.

Hypothetical. Example B. Capital Rs 10,000. Entry Rs 250. Stop Rs 248.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget10,000 x 1%Rs 100
2. Risk per share250 - 248Rs 2
3. Raw sharesfloor(100 / 2)50
4. Cash capfloor(10,000 / 250)40
5. Concentration capfloor(10,000 x 25% / 250) = floor(2,500 / 250)10
Sharesminimum of 50, 40, 1010

Binding constraint: concentration cap. The raw figure of 50 shares would cost 50 x 250 = Rs 12,500. That is more than the Rs 10,000 account. The cash cap of 40 shares would cost Rs 10,000. That is the whole account. Neither of these fits the 25% limit. Position value: 10 x 250 = Rs 2,500 (25%). Planned loss: 10 x 2 = Rs 20, which is 0.2% of capital. The risk budget of Rs 100 was not fully used. The concentration cap was the smaller limit.

Lesson. A tight stop gave a larger raw figure, not a larger position. The caps reduced it. If your plan’s position costs more than the cash you have, reject the plan. You cannot carry it out.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1%. Entry Rs 400. Stop Rs 380. Work out the risk budget, risk per share, the raw shares, the cash cap, the concentration cap, the shares, the binding constraint, the position value and the planned loss.

Hints:

  1. Work out all three limits before you take the minimum.
  2. Round down each limit. Do not round up any of them.
  3. The concentration cap uses 25% of capital in this example.

Answer:

  • Risk budget: Rs 500.
  • Risk per share: 400 - 380 = Rs 20.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 20) = floor(25) = 25.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 400) = floor(125) = 125.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 400) = floor(31.25) = 31.
  • Shares: minimum of 25, 125, 31 = 25.
  • Binding constraint: risk budget.
  • Position value: 25 x 400 = Rs 10,000 (20% of capital).
  • Planned loss: 25 x 20 = Rs 500.

Independent exercise

Task. Capital Rs 25,000. Risk 1%. Entry Rs 200. Stop Rs 190. Work out the three limits, the shares, the binding constraint, the position value and the planned loss.

Check your answer
  • Risk budget: 25,000 x 1% = Rs 250.
  • Risk per share: 200 - 190 = Rs 10.
  • Raw shares: floor(250 / 10) = 25.
  • Cash cap: floor(25,000 / 200) = 125.
  • Concentration cap: floor(25,000 x 25% / 200) = floor(6,250 / 200) = floor(31.25) = 31.
  • Shares: minimum of 25, 125, 31 = 25.
  • Binding constraint: risk budget.
  • Position value: 25 x 200 = Rs 5,000 (20% of capital).
  • Planned loss: 25 x 10 = Rs 250.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks which limit binds, rounding down, and why a tight stop can give a smaller position.

Paper task

Mode: learning note, or paper if you prefer. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write three hypothetical setups. Each one has capital, risk %, entry and stop.
  2. For each setup, write the five steps as separate lines. Include the three limits and the minimum.
  3. Record the planned shares, the binding constraint and the planned loss.
  4. Mark each entry as hypothetical. Do not record a live account.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not tell you which entry or stop to choose. Those come from the trade idea, which is outside this chapter.
  • It does not include costs. Lab 1 adds costs to show the true loss (Costs on a trade). The arithmetic in this chapter does not add them.
  • It does not make a position safe. Caps reduce size. They do not reduce the risk of the idea. A gap (a jump in price with no trading in between) can still cause a loss bigger than the plan (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk).
  • It does not allow you to buy more of a losing share to lower your average price. This is called averaging down. This track forbids it.

Remember

  • Shares = the minimum of three limits: raw risk limit, cash cap, concentration cap. Round each down.
  • Name the binding constraint every time. If two tie, say so.
  • Risk per share is entry minus stop. Never divide the budget by the entry price.
  • A tighter stop can raise the raw figure. The caps can then make it smaller.
  • If the position costs more than the cash you hold, the plan is rejected.

Sources and verification

  • NSE circulars on tick size for the cash market (revision of June 2024 and April 2025), read on 11 Nov 2026, for rounding prices in a plan.
  • NSE trading system page, for the market lot of one share.
  • The 25% limit is a setting in this book, not a market rule.

Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk

Hook

Hypothetical account: Rs 50,000, risk 1% = Rs 500. The plan buys at Rs 250 with a stop at Rs 240. That is 50 shares and a planned loss of Rs 500. Overnight, news breaks. The stock opens at Rs 232, below the stop. The stop fills at the open. The actual loss is Rs 900, not Rs 500. The plan’s arithmetic was right. The mistake was to treat the stop as the exact price you would get out at.

The idea

A trade has two risk numbers. They are not the same thing.

  • Planned risk = (entry - stop) x shares. This is the loss the plan expects if the exit happens exactly at the stop.
  • Execution risk = the loss you actually get at the price your exit order fills.

Key terms:

  • Stop-loss: an instruction to sell once the price reaches a level. It is an instruction. It does not promise you will exit at that level.
