  • Trigger: the price at which a stop becomes active.
  • Fill: the price at which an order is actually carried out.
  • Gap: the price opens, or jumps, past a level with no trading in between. A stop triggers at the first price available, which can be far from the stop.
  • Slippage: the difference between the price you expected and the price you got. It comes from the bid-ask spread. That is the gap between the price buyers offer and the price sellers ask. It also comes from thin depth, which means few orders are waiting at each price. The order type matters too (Order matching, spread and liquidity).
  • Circuit: a limit on how far a price can move in one day, set by the exchange (Circuit limits and halts). A stock stuck at its circuit may have no buyers or sellers on the exit side. Then your exit can be blocked completely.

In words: the actual loss is the entry price minus the exit fill price, times the shares. In symbols: loss = (entry - fill) x shares. To express it in R, divide by the planned risk in rupees. 1R means one planned loss.

Why this matters for swing trades. A swing trade is a trade held for days. A trade held overnight faces gap risk. News, global moves and company events can happen while the market is closed. Overnight gap risk is part of holding overnight. It is not a rare accident you can design out of the plan.

Order types. Stop orders come in two forms on Kite: SL and SL-M. They behave differently at the trigger (see Order types).

  • An SL (stoploss limit) order has a trigger price and a limit price. When the price reaches the trigger, a limit order goes to the exchange. It fills only at your limit price or better. If the price falls fast past your limit, the order can stay open and unfilled.
  • An SL-M (stoploss market) order has only a trigger price. When the price reaches the trigger, a market order goes to the exchange. It is very likely to fill, but at whatever price is available. That price can be worse than the trigger.
  • A stoploss order is valid only for the trading day. If it does not trigger, it ends at the close.
  • NSE has stopped the SL-M order type for options. For shares, both forms are available.

(As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support pages on stoploss orders. Rules change, so check Zerodha support.) Do not assume one form behaves like the other.

Circuits. The exchange sets a daily price band for each share. Zerodha says the bands range from 2% to 20%, depending on the share. Shares that have futures and options have no fixed daily band. They have a moving 10% band instead. An order outside the band is rejected. If the price is stuck at the band, orders wait until the exchange moves the band. If you are in an intraday (MIS) position and cannot close it because of a circuit, Zerodha says the position turns into a delivery trade. Zerodha may close positions but is not required to. You may then have to pay for the shares, or face a penalty for not delivering them if you had sold. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support pages on circuit limits. Rules change, so check Zerodha support and the NSE site.)

The stress test. You cannot know the next opening price. You can choose a gap assumption. Decide how big a gap loss you can accept. Then size the position so the assumed gap stays inside that limit. The assumption is your choice. A different assumption gives a different size.

See it

Look at three things. The trigger sits at Rs 240. The market opens at Rs 232. The fill lands near the open. Compare the planned loss bar (Rs 500) with the actual loss bar. Then move the open price down and set slippage to 0. See how the fill follows the open, not the stop.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000, risk 1%, entry Rs 250, stop Rs 240.

StepWorkingResult
Risk budget1% x 50,000Rs 500
Risk per share250 - 240Rs 10
Shares500 / 1050
Planned loss (1R)50 x 10Rs 500
Overnight openopens at Rs 232, below the stopstop fills at the open
Loss per share250 - 232Rs 18
Actual gap loss50 x 18Rs 900
Multiple of R900 / 5001.8R
Share of capital900 / 50,0001.8%

Now add slippage. Illustrative: assume a further Rs 2 per share of slippage. The fill is then at Rs 230.

StepWorkingResult
Loss per share250 - 230Rs 20
Actual loss50 x 20Rs 1,000
Multiple of R1,000 / 5002R
Share of capital1,000 / 50,0002%

The planned 1% became an actual loss of 2% of capital.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: Use the same plan (entry Rs 250, stop Rs 240, 50 shares). Hypothetical: the stock opens at Rs 236 and the stop fills at the open. Work out the actual loss in rupees and in R.

Hints:

  1. Find the loss per share first: entry minus fill.
  2. Multiply by 50 shares.
  3. Divide by the planned Rs 500 to get R.

Answer: Loss per share = 250 - 236 = Rs 14. Loss = 50 x 14 = Rs 700. In R: 700 / 500 = 1.4R. The gap loss is 40% above the plan.

Independent exercise

Task: The entry is Rs 250 and the stop is Rs 240. You assume the price gaps down to Rs 220. You want the loss at that gap to stay within Rs 500. How many whole shares can you hold? Remember, the gap is your assumption. It is not a prediction.

Check your answer

Loss per share at the assumed gap = 250 - 220 = Rs 30. Shares = 500 / 30 = 16.67, rounded down to 16. Gap loss = 16 x 30 = Rs 480. Seventeen shares would give 17 x 30 = Rs 510, which is above the limit. The gap size is an assumption you choose. A larger assumed gap means fewer shares.

Quiz

Six questions cover planned and actual loss, gap fills, sizing for a stress test, and stop order types.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write down three hypothetical trades, each with entry, stop and shares.
  2. For each one, calculate the planned loss: (entry - stop) x shares.
  3. For each one, add a hypothetical opening price below the stop. Calculate the actual loss at the open.
  4. Record the slippage fields (slippage_entry_inr and slippage_exit_inr) for each trade. Use zero where no slippage applies.
  5. Write the planned and actual loss side by side. Note which one your plan had described.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A stop is an instruction. It does not fix the exit price. In a circuit it may not produce an exit at all.
  • Planned risk is the plan. It is not a ceiling on what you can lose.
  • The Rs 232 open and the Rs 2 per share slippage are hypothetical. They are not a forecast of how any stock will open.
  • The gap size you assume is your own choice. A real gap bigger than you assumed will cause a bigger loss than your stress test allowed.
  • A smaller size for a stress test does not reduce the risk of the idea itself.

Remember

  • Planned risk = (entry - stop) x shares. It describes the plan. It is not a maximum.
  • Actual loss = (entry - fill) x shares. After a gap, the fill can be far from the stop.
  • Gaps and circuits can move or block the exit.
  • Holding overnight always carries gap risk.
  • Size the position for a stress test you have chosen, not only for the chart you are looking at.

Sources and verification

Sources, read on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Zerodha support: “What are stoploss orders and how to use them”, “What is trigger price in stoploss orders”.
  • Zerodha support: “What are circuit limits or price bands” and the page on what happens to intraday positions when a circuit is hit.
  • NSE pages on price bands and circuit breakers. Check them for the current band of any share.
  • Chapter Circuit limits and halts (circuit limits and price bands).

Reward-to-risk, win rate and break-even

Hook

Two hypothetical plans both enter at Rs 250 with a Rs 240 stop. Plan P targets Rs 260. Plan Q targets Rs 270. Each wins 50 of 100 hypothetical trades. Before costs, Plan P needs a 50% win rate just to break even. Plan Q needs only about 33.3%. The same win rate means very different things for the two plans.

The idea

Reward-to-risk (R) compares what a plan could make with what it could lose, per share. For a long trade:

  • Risk per share = entry - stop.
  • Reward per share = target - entry.
  • R = reward / risk.

Break-even win rate before costs. Take a plan with R. Let W be the win rate. The loss rate is 1 - W. The plan breaks even when the expected wins equal the expected losses. In units of the risk, a win pays R and a loss costs 1. So W x R = (1 - W) x 1. Solving gives W = 1 / (1 + R). Equivalently, W = risk / (risk + reward).

Costs raise the break-even. A cost paid on each share adds to the risk and takes from the reward. Both changes push the required win rate up.

Other points:

  • Win rate and R trade off in most approaches. A higher target is often reached less often. Neither number decides the result alone.
  • A target is a plan, not a forecast. Many approaches do not use a fixed target at all. Those are covered in Track 6.
  • Break-even here is before costs, before slippage and before gaps (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk). Those are separate losses.

See it

Target (Rs)Reward per shareRBreak-even win rate before costs
260Rs 10150.0%
270Rs 20233.3%
280Rs 30325.0%
270, with dummy cost of Rs 2 per shareRs 18 (after cost)1.5 (costed)40.0%

Read down the table. As R rises, the win rate needed to break even falls. The last row is the same Rs 270 target after a dummy cost of Rs 2 per share. The break-even rises from 33.3% to 40.0%. The cost is a dummy figure, used only for the arithmetic. The Rs 2 figure is a round number for practice, not the real charge. For a real trade, the charges depend on the product and the price. As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists zero brokerage on delivery trades, and on intraday trades 0.03% of the trade value or Rs 20 per order, whichever is lower, plus taxes and exchange fees. The full table is in Costs on a trade. Rules and rates change, so check the Zerodha charges page.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Entry Rs 250, stop Rs 240, target Rs 270.

StepWorkingResult
Risk per share250 - 240Rs 10
Reward per share270 - 250Rs 20
R20 / 102
Break-even before costs1 / (1 + 2)1/3 = 33.3%

Now add a DUMMY cost. Assume Rs 100 for the round trip (buying and selling) on 50 shares. That is Rs 2 per share. This is not a real charge.

StepWorkingResult
Risk per share, with cost10 + 2Rs 12
Reward per share, after cost20 - 2Rs 18
Costed R18 / 121.5
Costed break-evenW x 18 = (1 - W) x 12, so 30W = 12W = 0.4 = 40%

Check: 0.4 x 18 = 7.2, and 0.6 x 12 = 7.2. Both sides match.

Other targets, same entry and stop, before costs: target Rs 260 gives R = 1 and break-even 50%. Target Rs 280 gives R = 3 and break-even 25%.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: Entry Rs 400, stop Rs 380, target Rs 460. Work out R and the break-even win rate before costs.

Hints:

  1. Find risk per share (entry minus stop) and reward per share (target minus entry).
  2. R = reward / risk.
  3. Break-even = 1 / (1 + R).

Answer: Risk Rs 20. Reward Rs 60. R = 3. Break-even = 1 / 4 = 25%.

Independent exercise

Task: Entry Rs 150, stop Rs 144, target Rs 165. Work out R and the break-even win rate before costs. Then, with a DUMMY cost of Rs 0.50 per share, work out the break-even again.

Check your answer

Before costs: risk Rs 6, reward Rs 15, R = 2.5, break-even = 1 / 3.5 = 28.6%.

With the Rs 0.50 dummy cost: risk Rs 6.5, reward Rs 14.5, break-even = 6.5 / (6.5 + 14.5) = 6.5 / 21 = 31.0%. The cost raised the break-even.

Quiz

Six questions cover break-even arithmetic, the effect of costs, the rule for a minimum R, and the trap of judging on win rate alone.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in Lab 3 (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Choose three hypothetical plans. Each one has an entry, a stop and a target.
  2. For each plan, record planned R and the break-even win rate before costs.
  3. Add a DUMMY cost of Rs 0.50 per share. Work out the break-even again for each plan.
  4. Save each plan to the journal with its planned R field (Lab 1 save-to-journal), in learning-note mode.

Limits and common mistakes

  • R is a plan. It is not a forecast, and target prices are not reached reliably.
  • Do not choose a target to make R look good. The rule is set before the trade.
  • Break-even before costs ignores slippage, fills and gaps (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk). Real break-evens are higher.
  • A higher R is not automatically better. It can come with a lower chance of reaching the target.
  • The costs in this chapter are DUMMY figures. They are not real rates from any broker or from the government.

Remember

  • R = reward per share / risk per share, for a long trade.
  • Break-even win rate before costs = 1 / (1 + R).
  • Costs add to the risk and take from the reward. So they raise the break-even.
  • A win rate only means something next to R.
  • A plan below your minimum R fails your rule, however good its story sounds.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • Standard expectancy arithmetic, computed in this chapter. No external figure is used.

The costs here are round numbers for practice. Do not quote them as real charges. The real charges, with dates and sources, are in Costs on a trade.

Expectancy and profit factor

Hook

Nine wins out of ten sounds like a strong record. In this hypothetical system, each of the nine wins is Rs 300. The one loss is Rs 4,000. Over the ten trades, the wins add up to Rs 2,700. The loss takes away Rs 4,000. The net result is minus Rs 1,300. The win rate was 90%, and the account still went down.

The idea

A trade result has two parts. One is how often you win (the win rate). The other is how big the wins and losses are (the average win and average loss). You need both.

  • Expectancy per trade is what one trade is worth on average. In words: the win rate times the average win, minus the loss rate times the average loss. In symbols: E = W x avgWin - (1 - W) x avgLoss. Here W is the win rate, and 1 - W is the loss rate.
  • In R, measure wins and losses in units of R (the planned risk per trade): E_R = W x avgWinR - (1 - W) x avgLossR.
  • Gross profit is the total of the winning trades. Gross loss is the total of the losing trades, as a positive number.
  • Profit factor = gross profit / gross loss. Above 1 means gross profit is bigger than gross loss in that sample. With no losing trades it cannot be worked out, because the divisor is zero.

Points to keep in mind:

  • Expectancy is an average over many trades. It is not a prediction of the next trade.
  • Costs must be included to get net expectancy. The examples below are before costs.
  • A few trades can produce any average. How sure you can be is taught in Metrics and their limits. Lab 3 shows a “too few trades to conclude anything” note below 30 trades.
  • Win rate is a frequency, not a result. Expectancy combines frequency and size.

See it

System X (hypothetical)System Y (hypothetical)
Win rate90%40%
Average winRs 300 (0.3R)Rs 2,000 (2R)
Average lossRs 4,000 (4R)Rs 1,000 (1R)
Expectancy per trademinus Rs 130 (minus 0.13R)plus Rs 200 (plus 0.2R)
Profit factor over 10 trades0.6751.33

The table shows X with frequent, small wins and one large loss. Y has fewer, larger wins. Y has the higher expectancy, even though it wins less often.

Worked example

Hypothetical. R = Rs 1,000, which is 2% of Rs 50,000.

System X: 9 wins of Rs 300 (0.3R) and 1 loss of Rs 4,000 (4R, a gap through the stop).

StepWorkingResult
Win part0.9 x 300Rs 270
Loss part0.1 x 4,000Rs 400
Expectancy270 - 400minus Rs 130 per trade (minus 0.13R)
Gross profit (10 trades)9 x 300Rs 2,700
Gross loss (10 trades)1 x 4,000Rs 4,000
Profit factor2,700 / 4,0000.675
Net over 10 trades10 x minus 130minus Rs 1,300, which is 2.6% of Rs 50,000

System Y: 40% wins of 2R (Rs 2,000) and 60% losses of 1R (Rs 1,000).

StepWorkingResult
Win part0.4 x 2,000Rs 800
Loss part0.6 x 1,000Rs 600
Expectancy800 - 600plus Rs 200 per trade (plus 0.2R)
Gross profit (10 trades)4 x 2,000Rs 8,000
Gross loss (10 trades)6 x 1,000Rs 6,000
Profit factor8,000 / 6,0001.33

Y has the higher expectancy despite the lower win rate. Neither example is evidence that either approach works.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: System Z (hypothetical): 60% wins of Rs 1,000 and 40% losses of Rs 800. Over 10 trades, work out the expectancy per trade and the profit factor.

Hints:

  1. Work out the win part (0.6 x 1,000) and the loss part (0.4 x 800) for one trade.
  2. Over 10 trades, count 6 wins and 4 losses.
  3. Profit factor = gross profit / gross loss over those 10 trades.

Answer: Expectancy = 600 - 320 = plus Rs 280 per trade. Over 10 trades: gross profit = 6 x 1,000 = Rs 6,000. Gross loss = 4 x 800 = Rs 3,200. Profit factor = 6,000 / 3,200 = 1.875.

Independent exercise

Task: System W (hypothetical): 75% wins of Rs 500 and 25% losses of Rs 2,000. Work out the expectancy per trade and the profit factor on 4 trades.

Check your answer

Expectancy = 0.75 x 500 - 0.25 x 2,000 = 375 - 500 = minus Rs 125 per trade.

On 4 trades, the expected split is 3 wins and 1 loss. Gross profit = 3 x 500 = Rs 1,500. Gross loss = 1 x 2,000 = Rs 2,000. Profit factor = 1,500 / 2,000 = 0.75.

Quiz

Six questions cover expectancy arithmetic, the profit factor with no losses, the comparison of two systems, and the trap of judging on win rate alone.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in Lab 3 (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Enter ten hypothetical rows for System X: nine rows at Rs 300 and one row at minus Rs 4,000.
  2. Open the Lab 3 statistics view.
  3. Check that the expectancy shows minus Rs 130 per trade (minus 0.13R), and that the profit factor shows 0.675.
  4. Write one sentence. Explain why a 90% win rate can still give a negative expectancy.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Expectancy shown here is before costs. Net expectancy is lower. Check it with costs included.
  • A handful of trades is noise. The minimum of 30 trades (gate G6, PLAN section 6) is a floor for a check. It is not a verdict on any system.
  • One large loss can dominate a sample. Check the largest loss before you trust an average.
  • These systems are invented for arithmetic. Neither example says anything about a real strategy.
  • A high win rate is not proof of a good system on its own. Use expectancy and profit factor, and state the sample size.

Remember

  • Expectancy = win rate x average win - loss rate x average loss.
  • Profit factor = gross profit / gross loss. It cannot be worked out with no losses.
  • Expectancy is an average over many trades, not a prediction.
  • Include costs before you read any result.
  • Win rate is a frequency. Judge a system on expectancy, with its sample size.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • Chapter Metrics and their limits (metrics and their limits), for further reading

All numbers in this chapter are made up for practice.

Drawdown, recovery and compounding

Hook

A hypothetical Rs 50,000 account falls 50%, to Rs 25,000. A 50% gain on Rs 25,000 brings it to Rs 37,500. That is not back to Rs 50,000. To return to Rs 50,000 from Rs 25,000, the account needs a 100% gain. The hole is deeper than the drop that made it.

The idea

Drawdown is the fall from a peak to the lowest point after it. That lowest point is called the trough. Drawdown is shown as a share of the peak. Equity means the value of your account at a point in time. In words: drawdown = (peak equity - trough equity) / peak equity.

Recovery gain is the rise needed from the trough to get back to the peak. It is measured from the trough, not from the peak. If the drawdown is d (as a fraction of the peak), the recovery gain is d / (1 - d). In rupees, that is (peak - trough) / trough.

Asymmetry. Losses and gains do not work the same way. Each loss takes a share of a smaller balance. Each recovery must then be measured from that smaller balance. A 50% loss needs a 100% gain.

Compounding. Each percentage move applies to the balance at that time. Equal up and down percentages do not cancel. Rs 50,000 gains 10%, to Rs 55,000. Then it loses 10% of Rs 55,000 (Rs 5,500), to Rs 49,500. The net result is minus 1%. The order of the moves does not change this.

Risk of ruin is the probability, over many trades, that equity falls to a level from which the plan cannot continue. It is not a single bad trade. It depends on the edge (the plan’s average result per trade), the risk per trade and the streaks (Consecutive losses and losing streaks). The book works it out by simulation in Lab 4. That means many repeated test runs. It does not use a shortcut here. For the same sequence of results, a smaller risk per trade lowers the chance of a deep drawdown.

See it

Move the loss slider and watch how fast the gain needed grows. Start at 30%, then go to 50%. Read the recovery gain each time. The curve is steeper at large losses. So each extra point of loss costs more to recover.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Account values over time: Rs 50,000, then Rs 55,000, then Rs 48,000, then Rs 52,000.

StepWorkingResult
Peakhighest value before the troughRs 55,000
Troughlowest value after the peakRs 48,000
Drawdown(55,000 - 48,000) / 55,00012.7%
Recovery gain from trough(55,000 - 48,000) / 48,00014.6%

Compounding check, with each move applied to the balance at the time:

StepWorkingResult
StartRs 50,000
Gain 10%50,000 x 1.10Rs 55,000
Loss 10%55,000 x 0.90Rs 49,500
Net49,500 - 50,000minus Rs 500, which is minus 1%

Loss and gain needed to recover. The formula is recovery gain = loss / (1 - loss). Worked row: a 20% loss takes Rs 50,000 to Rs 40,000. Getting back to Rs 50,000 needs Rs 10,000 / Rs 40,000 = 25%.

LossGain needed to recover
10%11.1%
20%25%
30%42.9%
50%100%
75%300%

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: Account values over time: Rs 50,000, then Rs 45,000, then Rs 39,000, then Rs 44,000 (hypothetical). Find the peak, the trough, the drawdown and the recovery gain from the trough.

Hints:

  1. Find the highest point before the trough. That is the peak.
  2. Drawdown = (peak - trough) / peak.
  3. Recovery gain = (peak - trough) / trough.

Answer: Peak Rs 50,000. Trough Rs 39,000. Drawdown = 11,000 / 50,000 = 22.0%. Recovery gain from the trough = 11,000 / 39,000 = 28.2%.

Independent exercise

Task: Capital is Rs 50,000. Your drawdown limit is 15%. The account falls 20% and then recovers. Does the fall go past the limit? What gain is needed from the trough?

Check your answer

Yes. A 20% fall is more than the 15% limit. The trough is Rs 40,000 (Rs 50,000 minus Rs 10,000). Recovery to Rs 50,000 needs Rs 10,000 / Rs 40,000 = 25% from the trough.

Quiz

Six questions cover recovery arithmetic, compounding, the meaning of risk of ruin, and reading a drawdown.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in Lab 3 (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Take the ten-row hypothetical table for Trader A from Why money math comes before charts.
  2. Enter it in Lab 3 as hypothetical rows, or work it on paper with the balance after each trade.
  3. Record the maximum drawdown in rupees and as a percentage, measured from the peak. Lab 3 shows the drawdown curve and the maximum drawdown.
  4. Compare your result with the 4% worked out in Why money math comes before charts. Note which peak the figure is measured from.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Drawdown depends on the order of the results. The same trades in a different order give a different path.
  • Recovery arithmetic does not say how long a recovery will take, or whether it will happen.
  • Risk of ruin is not worked out here. Lab 4 estimates it by simulation.
  • You set a drawdown limit before you trade. This book does not allow withdrawals during a drawdown (Scaling and withdrawal policy).
  • The examples are hypothetical. They are not a forecast of any account.

Remember

  • Drawdown = (peak - trough) / peak.
  • Recovery gain = drawdown / (1 - drawdown), measured from the trough.
  • Equal percentage up and down moves do not cancel. A 50% loss needs a 100% gain.
  • Risk of ruin is a probability over many trades, not one bad trade.
  • A smaller risk per trade lowers the chance of deep drawdowns for the same sequence.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • Standard drawdown arithmetic, computed in this chapter

All numbers in this chapter are made up for practice.

Consecutive losses and losing streaks

Hook

Imagine your plan won six of its first ten trades. Then you lose five trades in a row. Your gut says a win is due. This chapter shows the arithmetic behind streaks. It also shows why “due” is the wrong word for it.

The idea

Start with one trade. Let p be the win rate. The chance of a loss on one trade is 1 - p.

Streak probability. If each trade is independent of the others, the chance of k losses in a row starting at a given trade is (1 - p) raised to the power k. Independent means each trade’s result does not depend on the others. In words: multiply the loss chance by itself k times.

Independence is an assumption. The formula assumes each trade’s result does not depend on the previous one. Real markets may cluster, with losses coming together. Lab 4 tests this assumption with a Monte Carlo test of trade order. That means many random shuffles of the same trades. Monte Carlo of trade order explains it. Do not treat the formula as settled for a real plan.

Across many trades, streaks appear. The chance of a streak starting at one particular trade is small. The chance that a streak of that length appears somewhere in a long sequence is much larger, because there are many starting points.

Fixed risk and shrinking losses. If each trade risks a fixed percentage of current capital, each loss is a smaller rupee amount as capital falls. Capital after k losses is capital x (1 - risk)^k. So the total lost is capital minus that figure.

Streak rules are your choice. Examples: pause after N losses, reduce risk after N losses, or review the plan. Write the rule before a streak starts. The rule that matters most is that you never increase size after losses. Doubling after losses, to recover faster, is a martingale-style method. That is a betting method that doubles after each loss. It is dangerous and is covered in Why scalping, option buying and martingale are dangerous.

Why streaks feel wrong. A low win rate or a long run of losses is common in the arithmetic. But it feels like a broken system. A streak alone is not evidence that a system has stopped working.

See it

Look at the longest losing streak in each grid. Look at how often streaks of 3 or more appear. Then compare the 40% grid with the 60% grid. Change the risk setting and see how the rupee loss per streak changes. The streak lengths stay the same.

Worked example

Hypothetical.

Win rate 60%. Loss chance = 0.4.

Losses in a rowWorkingProbability, starting at a given trade
30.4 x 0.4 x 0.40.064, about 6.4%
50.4 x 0.4 x 0.4 x 0.4 x 0.40.01024, about 1.0%

Win rate 40%. Loss chance = 0.6. For 5 losses: 0.6^5 = 0.07776, about 7.8%.

Capital lost at 1% risk on Rs 50,000. Each loss is 1% of the capital at that time.

Losses in a rowCapital left (approx.)Lost (approx.)
550,000 x 0.99^5 = Rs 47,550Rs 2,450
1050,000 x 0.99^10 = Rs 45,219Rs 4,781
1550,000 x 0.99^15 = Rs 43,003Rs 6,997

Capital lost at 2% risk on Rs 50,000.

Losses in a rowCapital left (approx.)Lost (approx.)
550,000 x 0.98^5 = Rs 45,196Rs 4,804
1050,000 x 0.98^10 = Rs 40,854Rs 9,146
1550,000 x 0.98^15 = Rs 36,928Rs 13,072

Rs 10,000 at 1% risk. Five losses cost about Rs 490. Ten cost about Rs 956. Fifteen cost about Rs 1,399.

Hand check for the first row: a 1% loss on Rs 50,000 is Rs 500. The next loss is 1% of Rs 49,500, which is Rs 495. Each later loss is a little smaller. So five losses total slightly less than Rs 2,500.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task: Win rate 45% (hypothetical). What is the probability of three losses in a row, starting at a given trade?

Hints:

  1. Loss chance = 1 - 0.45.
  2. Raise the loss chance to the power 3.
  3. Convert the result to a percentage.

Answer: Loss chance = 0.55. 0.55^3 = 0.166375, about 16.6%.

Independent exercise

Task: Capital is Rs 50,000. Risk is 1% per trade, and each loss is 1% of the capital at that time. You take seven losses in a row. What capital remains, and how much is lost in total?

Check your answer

Capital remaining = 50,000 x 0.99^7 = 50,000 x 0.932065 = about Rs 46,603. Total lost = 50,000 - 46,603 = about Rs 3,397.

Quiz

Six questions cover streak probability, the “due” misconception, the streak rule, reading a streak, and a loss-amount calculation.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in Lab 3 (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Before you start, write your streak rule in one line. For example: pause after N losses. Do not change it during the streak.
  2. Record a hypothetical streak of seven losses at 1% risk on Rs 50,000. Use the emotion_before field, and the capital_at_plan field for the capital at each step.
  3. Work out the capital after each of the seven losses. The seventh should be about Rs 46,603.
  4. Write one sentence on what the rule says to do after the streak ends. Note whether you would have kept to it.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The formula assumes each trade is independent. If results cluster in a real plan, the chance of a streak is higher than the formula gives.
  • The figures describe chances. They do not predict when the next streak will start.
  • A streak is not, on its own, evidence that a system is broken. Equally, a streak is not evidence that it still works.
  • The Monte Carlo test in Lab 4 comes later. Monte Carlo of trade order covers the independence test.
  • Doubling size after losses is not a recovery method in this book. It breaks the capital-first rules.

Remember

  • Probability of k losses in a row, from a given trade = (1 - p)^k, if trades are independent.
  • A losing streak is expected at some frequency. It does not mean the next trade is more likely to win.
  • At a fixed percentage risk, each loss is a smaller rupee amount as capital falls.
  • Write your streak rule before the streak starts.
  • Never increase size after losses.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • Chapter Monte Carlo of trade order (independence and resampling tests)
  • Chapter Living through a losing streak (streak-sim with feelings log) (living through streaks)

All numbers in this chapter are made up for practice.

Risk limits

Hook

Hypothetical: an account has Rs 50,000. You plan to risk Rs 500 on each trade. The first trade of the morning loses Rs 500, as planned. You feel you must win the money back. So you take a second trade that is bigger than planned, and it loses Rs 1,500. The day ends at minus Rs 2,000. That is four times the Rs 500 you planned for one trade. No limit was set for the day, so nothing stopped the second trade.

The idea

Per-trade risk controls one trade. See Risk per trade and stop-loss distance and Position sizing step by step. A limit controls a period. It is the most rupees you allow yourself to lose over a set time. You write it down before trading starts.

LimitWhat it capsExample size
Per tradePlanned loss on one trade1% of capital
DailyAll losses in one day2% of capital
WeeklyAll losses in one week4% of capital
MonthlyAll losses in one month8% of capital
Max trades per dayNumber of trades2

The sizes are examples. You set every number.

  • Base. Each percentage needs a stated base. The base is your starting capital or your current equity (what the account is worth now). Write the base on the limit card. A limit that moves with your results is not a fixed rule.
  • Rupee limit = base x limit percentage.
  • Remaining limit = rupee limit minus the losses already used in that period.
  • Stop rule. When a limit is reached, the rule says what to do. If the daily limit is reached, you make no more trades that day. If the weekly limit is reached, you make no more trades that week. No revenge trades, which are trades made to win back a loss. No recovery attempts. A limit that the next trade can lift is not a limit.
  • Reduce exposure. A rule set in advance that lowers your risk after a drop in the account. For example, you halve your per-trade risk percentage once the month’s drawdown (the fall from the account’s high point) reaches a set level. It only ever moves risk down. You write it before trading.
  • Two different figures. Your Rs 2,000-3,000 figure is a daily profit goal. The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check tests it against the arithmetic. It is not a loss limit. Do not use it as one.

A limit is a rule you set and follow yourself. Limits cannot stop a gap, where the price jumps past your stop (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk). Brokers also have risk controls. They are different from the limits in this book.

  • For intraday (MIS) positions, Zerodha closes open positions automatically at a set time. For shares it is 3:25 PM for most shares and 3:12 PM for shares in the closing auction session. For futures and options it is 3:26 PM. Zerodha charges Rs 50 plus 18% GST for each order it closes this way.
  • Zerodha’s risk team can change these times, and can reduce or remove intraday leverage during the day.
  • Zerodha says you are responsible for closing your own intraday positions. It is not bound to do it for you.

(As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on auto square-off timings, and the Zerodha announcement of 26 Dec 2025 that moved the share time from 3:20 PM to 3:25 PM. Rules change, so check Zerodha support.)

See it

Read the ladder from the smallest limit upward: per trade, then daily, weekly and monthly. Each loss fills one step. The first step that fills decides your next action. The stop state shows that no more trades are allowed. Compare how fast the daily step fills with how fast the per-trade step fills.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Limits: per trade 1%, daily 2%, weekly 4%, monthly 8%, two trades a day.

LimitCalculationRupees
Per trade50,000 x 1% = 50,000 x 0.01500
Daily50,000 x 2% = 50,000 x 0.021,000
Weekly50,000 x 4% = 50,000 x 0.042,000
Monthly50,000 x 8% = 50,000 x 0.084,000

Day 1

  1. Trade 1 loses Rs 500. Daily used 500. Remaining 1,000 - 500 = Rs 500.
  2. Trade 2 loses Rs 500. Daily used 1,000. Remaining 1,000 - 1,000 = Rs 0.
  3. The daily limit is reached. Stop for the day. Trade 3 is not allowed.

Day 2

  1. The day loses Rs 1,000. Weekly used = 1,000 (day 1) + 1,000 (day 2) = Rs 2,000. That equals the weekly limit. Stop for the week.

Reduce exposure

  1. The month’s drawdown reaches 10%: 10% x 50,000 = Rs 5,000.
  2. The rule set in advance halves per-trade risk to 0.5%. On the starting-capital base: 0.5% x 50,000 = Rs 250.
  3. On a current-equity base: equity is 50,000 - 5,000 = Rs 45,000, and 0.5% x 45,000 = Rs 225.

Steps 6 and 7 use the same rule but give different answers. That is why you must write the base down.

Try it

Use the simulator with the guided exercise inputs:

Guided exercise

Task. Capital is Rs 30,000. Per-trade 1%, daily 2%, weekly 4% and monthly 8%. Work out each limit in rupees. Then two losses of Rs 300 happen on the same day. How much of the daily limit is left?

Hints:

  1. Multiply capital by each percentage. First change the percentage to a decimal (1% = 0.01).
  2. Add the day’s losses first. Then take them away from the daily limit.
  3. Check that the per-trade limit is smaller than the daily limit.

Answer: Per trade Rs 300; daily Rs 600; weekly Rs 1,200; monthly Rs 2,400. Two losses of Rs 300 use Rs 600, so the daily limit left is 600 - 600 = Rs 0. The daily stop applies.

Independent exercise

Task. Capital Rs 20,000. Daily limit 3%, weekly limit 6%, monthly limit 10%. Each trade loses Rs 150. How many losing trades until the daily limit is reached? Then state the stop rule in one sentence.

Check your answer

Daily limit = 20,000 x 3% = Rs 600. Each loss is Rs 150, so 600 / 150 = 4 trades. Check: 4 x 150 = 600.

Stop rule (sample wording): once the daily limit is reached, no more trades that day, whatever the setup looks like.

For reference: the weekly limit is Rs 1,200 and the monthly limit is Rs 2,000.

Quiz

Six questions check limit math, the stop rule, the reduce-exposure rule and the base. Each wrong answer has an explanation.

Paper task

Mode: learning note (Lab 3). No orders are placed.

  1. Write a one-page limit card. Include capital, base, per-trade, daily, weekly and monthly limits, max trades per day, and the stop rule.
  2. Work out each rupee limit from your made-up capital. Show each multiplication.
  3. Write it in your journal. If you do not have the journal app yet, use paper. Keep a “remaining daily limit” line for five made-up days.
  4. For each day, note the losses used and the amount left. Mark any day on which a limit would have stopped trading.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Limits do not stop gaps. A stop can fill well below the stop price. So the loss can be bigger than planned (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk).
  • The percentages are examples, not advice. You set every number before trading.
  • A broker’s risk controls are not your limits. The broker can close intraday positions at its set time (3:25 PM for most shares as of 11 Nov 2026) and can cut leverage. Check Zerodha support for the current rules.
  • Limits cannot fix a plan that does not work. They only cap the damage in each period.
  • A limit card works only if you follow the stop rule. The common mistake is trading again after the stop.

Remember

  • A limit caps your losses over a period. It is set in rupees before trading.
  • Write the base (starting or current capital) on the card.
  • When a limit is reached, the stop rule applies. No more trades in that period.
  • Reduce exposure is a rule set in advance. It lowers risk after a drawdown. It never raises risk.
  • Limits cannot stop a gap loss that is bigger than the planned amount.

Sources and verification

Sources, read on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Zerodha support: “Intraday auto square-off timings”.
  • Zerodha Z-Connect: “Changes to the auto square-off timings for equity and F&O” (26 Dec 2025).
  • Zerodha support: “How much margin and leverage does Zerodha provide”.

Overtrading and why daily profit targets encourage it

Hook

Hypothetical: a trader sets a daily profit goal of Rs 2,000. By mid-morning the day is down Rs 300. No setup from the checklist has appeared. The trader takes a trade “to get back on track”. The trade is not on the checklist, and it loses. The target did not cause the first loss. It changed the second decision. That is the pattern this chapter measures.

The idea

Expectancy is the average result per trade over many trades. It counts wins and losses together. Measured in R (the rupee amount risked on one trade), an expectancy of 0.1R means the average trade makes one tenth of one R.

Costs are paid on every trade. So they lower that average:

  • Net expectancy per trade = expectancy - cost per round trip. A round trip is one buy and one sell.

Trades needed to reach a target, on average:

  • Trades needed = target / net expectancy per trade.
  • Short form: N = T / E_net, where N is trades, T is the target and E_net is net expectancy.

Three things follow:

  1. Small expectancy means many trades. A small average needs a large number of trades to add up to a daily target.
  2. Costs change the count. Each rupee of cost you remove from the average cuts the number of trades needed.
  3. No positive net expectancy means no count works. If E_net is zero or below, no number of trades reaches the target on average. More trades only add more of a negative average.

A daily target also works as pressure. It makes a no-trade day feel like a loss. That pushes a trader to take trades when no setup exists. More trades mean more exposure to random price moves (noise), more costs and more chances to break a rule. Costs are covered in Costs on a trade.

Signs of overtrading in a journal:

  • Trades placed outside the checklist.
  • Trade count above the daily limit.
  • Size increased on a later trade.
  • A trade placed to recover an earlier loss.

Your Rs 2,000-3,000 figure is tested in The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check. It is not a goal for any strategy.

See it

This is a static table. The target is fixed at Rs 2,000 a day. Each row shows how the number of trades rises as net expectancy falls.

Net expectancy per trade (Rs)Trades needed per dayNote
5040Before costs in the worked example
2580
20100
10200After the dummy cost in the worked example
0 or belowNo count reaches the target on average

Read down the table. When net expectancy halves, the trade count doubles. The 200-trade row is where the worked example lands once costs are included.

Worked example

Hypothetical. For arithmetic only, not an estimate: R = Rs 500 (1% of Rs 50,000). Expectancy = 0.1 x 500 = Rs 50 per trade. Target = Rs 2,000 a day.

StepCalculationResult
1. Trades needed before costs2,000 / 5040 trades a day
2. Over 250 trading days (assumed)40 x 25010,000 trades a year
3. Dummy cost per round tripRs 40 assumedNet expectancy = 50 - 40 = Rs 10
4. Trades needed after costs2,000 / 10200 trades a day
5. Over 250 trading days (assumed)200 x 25050,000 trades a year

The cost assumption turns a 40-trade day into a 200-trade day. The Rs 40 figure is a DUMMY value, used for arithmetic only. It is a round number for practice, not a real broker fee or government charge. As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists zero brokerage on delivery trades. On intraday trades it lists 0.03% of the trade value or Rs 20 per order, whichever is lower, plus taxes and exchange fees. The full table is in Costs on a trade. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page.

Try it

Work each exercise by hand first. Then use the demo under it to check your answer. Change the cost and see how the number of trades moves.

Guided exercise

Task. Target Rs 1,000 a day. Expectancy Rs 25 per trade. DUMMY cost Rs 15 per trade. Work out the trades needed before costs and after costs.

Hints:

  1. Work out the net expectancy first: expectancy minus cost.
  2. Divide the target by the expectancy, then by the net expectancy.
  3. Check: the after-cost count should be larger than the before-cost count.

Answer: Before costs, 1,000 / 25 = 40 trades. Net expectancy 25 - 15 = Rs 10. After costs, 1,000 / 10 = 100 trades.

Independent exercise

Task. Target Rs 3,000 a day. R = Rs 400. Expectancy is 0.15R. DUMMY cost Rs 30 per trade. Work out the net expectancy, the trades per day and the trades per week over five trading days.

Check your answer

Expectancy = 0.15 x 400 = Rs 60 per trade.

Net expectancy = 60 - 30 = Rs 30 per trade.

Trades per day = 3,000 / 30 = 100.

Trades per week = 100 x 5 = 500. Check: 500 / 5 = 100 per day.

Quiz

Six questions cover the trades-needed math, the effect of costs, the wrong idea that more trades even out an average, the overtrading signs and the no-trade day.

Paper task

Mode: learning note (Lab 3). No orders are placed.

  1. Log the trade count for each day of one week. Use made-up or learning entries only.
  2. Add a “checklist passed (Y/N)” column for each trade.
  3. Record one no-trade day and the reason: no setup passed the checklist.
  4. Count the trades that failed the checklist. Note any trade placed to recover a loss.

Write it in your journal. If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Expectancy is an average of past or assumed trades. It does not predict any single day.
  • The costs here are DUMMY values. Real costs are in Costs on a trade and Lab 2.
  • A lower trade count does not promise a profit. It cuts the exposure to costs and rule breaks.
  • The maximum trades per day is your choice. This chapter does not set one.
  • A no-trade day is a valid result. It is not proof of skill either.

Remember

  • Trades needed = target / net expectancy per trade.
  • Costs apply to every trade, so they raise the count.
  • A daily target can make a no-trade day feel like a failure. That pushes trades with no setup.
  • Set a maximum trades per day in advance. Count a no-trade day as a valid result.
  • Watch four journal signs: trades outside the checklist, trade count above the limit, larger size, and trades placed to recover a loss.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • Discipline (discipline) and Loss aversion and revenge trading (revenge trading), for the behaviour links.

The costs in this chapter are round numbers for practice. The real charges, with dates and sources, are in Costs on a trade.

Leverage and margin

Hook

Hypothetical: your own money is Rs 50,000. A 10% fall in a Rs 50,000 holding costs Rs 5,000. That is 10% of your own money. Now take a Rs 1,00,000 position, where half is borrowed. A 10% fall costs Rs 10,000. That is 20% of your own money. The borrowed Rs 50,000 is still owed. The price moved by the same amount in both cases. Only the size of the position changed.

The idea

Leverage means controlling a position that is bigger than your own money. You do this with borrowed money or margin.

  • Leverage ratio = position value / own capital. A Rs 1,00,000 position with Rs 50,000 of your own money has a ratio of 2 (2x).

  • Gains and losses are both multiplied by the ratio. Measured as a percentage of your own capital, a price move of m percent changes your capital by ratio x m percent. A fall is a loss.

  • Wipe-out move = 1 / ratio, for a long position (you own the asset and gain when it rises). It is the price fall that takes the position value down to the borrowed amount. That uses up all your own money. This ignores costs and financing, which make the real position worse.

  • Margin is money or securities you deposit or borrow to support a position. The broker and the regulator set the rules. Check the rules for each product.

    • Since 1 September 2021, SEBI’s rules say a broker must collect the full margin up front for positions in the stock market. This is why the old very high intraday leverage is gone. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI circulars as summarised by Zerodha and others. Rules change, so check the SEBI site.)
    • Zerodha says its intraday leverage depends on the share. It can be cut or removed during the day. Shares you pledge as collateral are valued at less than their market price. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support. Rules change, so check Zerodha support.)

  • Margin calls and forced sales. When the position falls far enough, the broker can sell it to cover the shortfall. A forced sale happens at the price available at that time. That price is often the worst one.

  • Financing costs. Borrowed money usually has a cost. For example, Zerodha’s margin trading facility (MTF) charges interest of 0.04% a day on the amount it funds. That is about Rs 40 a day for every Rs 1 lakh funded, or roughly 14.6% a year. It runs every day you hold the position. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on MTF interest. Rules change, so check Zerodha support.)

  • Intraday leverage has auto square-off rules. The broker can close your positions at a set time. At Zerodha, open intraday share positions are closed at about 3:25 PM, and futures and options at about 3:26 PM. Each order closed this way costs Rs 50 plus 18% GST. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support. Rules change, so check Zerodha support.)

This book does not practise leverage. The leverage demo is theory only. F&O (futures and options) is covered as theory in Futures and why they are leveraged.

See it

Set the ratio to 1x, then to 4x, and keep the same 10% price fall. The loss on your own capital grows from 10% to 40%. The wipe-out line marks the price fall that uses up all your own money. The sliders change only the arithmetic. There are no trading controls.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Own capital Rs 50,000. A 10% fall in the price.

RatioPosition valueLoss on a 10% fallLoss as % of own capitalWipe-out move (1 / ratio)
1x50,0005,00010%100%
2x1,00,00010,00020%50%
3x1,50,00015,00030%33.3%
4x2,00,00020,00040%25%

Each row uses the same steps. Position value x 10% = loss. Then loss / Rs 50,000 = percent of own capital.

2x, step by step (hypothetical; this is only arithmetic, not an offer or a product).

  1. Own money Rs 50,000. Borrowed Rs 50,000. Position Rs 1,00,000.
  2. Price rises 10%: gain = 1,00,000 x 10% = Rs 10,000, which is 10,000 / 50,000 = 20% of own money.
  3. Price falls 10%: loss = Rs 10,000, which is 20% of own money.
  4. Wipe-out: 1 / 2 = 50%. Price falls 50%: position value = 50,000. That equals the borrowed Rs 50,000, so your own money is gone.

Try it

Use the demo with the guided exercise inputs (theory mode):

Guided exercise

Task. Own capital Rs 20,000. Position Rs 60,000 (3x). The price falls 15%. Work out the loss in rupees and as a percentage of own capital.

Hints:

  1. Find the loss on the position first: position value x fall.
  2. Divide that loss by your own capital.
  3. Check: a 3x position with a 15% fall should give 3 x 15% = 45% of own capital.

Answer: Loss on the position = 60,000 x 15% = Rs 9,000. As a share of own capital, 9,000 / 20,000 = 45%.

Independent exercise

Task. Own capital Rs 25,000 at 4x leverage. Work out the price fall that wipes out the own capital, and the loss at a 10% fall.

Check your answer

Position = 4 x 25,000 = Rs 1,00,000.

Wipe-out move = 1 / 4 = 25%. Check: a 25% fall on Rs 1,00,000 is Rs 25,000, which equals the own capital.

Loss at a 10% fall = 1,00,000 x 10% = Rs 10,000. As a share of own capital, 10,000 / 25,000 = 40%.

Quiz

Six questions cover the leverage ratio, the bigger loss, the wipe-out move, margin and the limits of a stop.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. No orders are placed, and the Kite demo is not used for this task.

  1. Draw a table with four rows (1x, 2x, 3x, 4x). Add columns for position value, loss on a 10% fall, and loss as a percentage of own capital.
  2. Use own capital of Rs 50,000 and a 10% fall in every row.
  3. Add a column for the wipe-out move (1 / ratio). Check each row against the worked example.
  4. Write it in your journal. If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

The Kite demo has no practice path for leverage. Do not look for leverage features in it.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This table leaves out financing costs, charges and margin rules. Those matter in real life. Check them with the broker and the regulator.
  • The wipe-out formula is for a long position. It ignores costs. A gap can take the price past the wipe-out line (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk).
  • Leverage does not improve a plan. It multiplies whatever the plan does, in both directions.
  • A stop does not protect you from margin calls or forced sales. The quiz explains why.
  • The 2x example is arithmetic only. It is not an offer and not a product.

Remember

  • Leverage ratio = position value / own capital.
  • Gains and losses are both multiplied by the ratio.
  • Wipe-out move = 1 / ratio, for a long position, ignoring costs.
  • Margin can bring financing costs and forced sales at a bad price.
  • The book does not teach leverage in practice. Leverage is not a route to a target.

Sources and verification

Sources, read on 11 Nov 2026:

  • SEBI circulars on peak margin and upfront margin (sebi.gov.in), as summarised in public broker guides. The 1 September 2021 date is from those summaries, so read the SEBI circular itself too.
  • Zerodha support: margin trading facility (MTF) interest and FAQs.
  • Zerodha support: intraday auto square-off timings and charges.
  • Zerodha bulletin on intraday (MIS) leverage, which says leverage can be reduced during the day.

Portfolio exposure and concentration

Hook

Hypothetical: a trader holds five stocks and says, “I am diversified.” Three of the five are in the same sector. A bad week for that sector moves all three in the same direction, on the same day. The account feels like five separate bets. In practice, it behaved like one large bet.

The idea

Four terms to know:

  • Exposure is the rupee value of your positions. It is often written as a percentage of capital. Exposure is not risk. A position can be large and still carry a small planned loss.
  • Open risk is the planned loss if every stop fills at its stop price.
    • Open risk = add up (entry - stop) x shares for each position.
    • This is the planned loss, not the maximum loss. Gaps and circuits (daily price limits set by the exchange) can make the real loss bigger (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk).
  • Concentration is how much of your capital sits in one stock, one sector or one theme.
  • Correlation means positions that tend to move together. Stocks in the same sector often do. Five stocks that move together are closer to one bet than to five bets.

Other points:

  • Cash = capital - deployed value. Deployed value is the total of the position values.
  • Diversification reduces the risk from one company or one event. It does not remove risk that hits the whole market.
  • Limits. You set a maximum per position, a maximum per sector and a maximum total open risk. Lab 1 uses a 25% per-position cap as a starting value. You choose the sector limits.
  • Adding to a losing position to lower the average cost raises concentration in a failing idea. This is called averaging down. The book’s capital-first rules ban it (Position sizing step by step). Check the limits before you add.
  • Sector labels come from your data. Check their source against the NSE sectoral index definitions. NSE Indices sorts each company into four levels: macro-economic sector, sector, industry and basic industry. Other data providers may use different labels, so two lists can put the same company in different groups. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Indices industry classification. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

See it

Pie chart of the worked example, with values in rupees (hypothetical):

pie showData
    title Capital split, Rs 50,000 (hypothetical)
    "Sector X (positions A and B)" : 22500
    "Sector Y (position C)" : 7500
    "Cash" : 20000

The chart shows that Sector X is the largest slice of capital. Sector X is 45% of capital, and cash is 40%. The table in the worked example also shows the open risk and the sector limit. A pie chart cannot show those.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Holdings:

PositionSectorValue% of capitalPlanned loss at stop
AX10,00020%500
BX12,50025%500
CY7,50015%300
  1. Deployed: 10,000 + 12,500 + 7,500 = Rs 30,000. As a share of capital: 30,000 / 50,000 = 60%.
  2. Cash: 50,000 - 30,000 = Rs 20,000, which is 40%.
  3. Open risk: 500 + 500 + 300 = Rs 1,300. As a share of capital: 1,300 / 50,000 = 2.6%.
  4. Sector X: 10,000 + 12,500 = Rs 22,500. As a share of capital: 22,500 / 50,000 = 45%. This is inside the example sector limit of 50%.

Adding a new position. Position D is Rs 6,000 in Sector X, with a planned loss of Rs 300.

  1. Sector X becomes 22,500 + 6,000 = Rs 28,500. As a share of capital: 28,500 / 50,000 = 57%.
  2. Open risk becomes 1,300 + 300 = Rs 1,600. As a share of capital: 1,600 / 50,000 = 3.2%.
  3. The example sector limit is 50% of capital. Before D, Sector X is at 45%, which is inside the limit. After D, it is 57%, which is above 50%, so the limit is breached. The new position is rejected. The sector limit is the rule that causes the rejection.

Try it

Work each exercise by hand first. Then use a demo to check your answer. The demo under See it opens on the three positions of the worked example, so it fits the guided exercise. The independent exercise has its own demo below it.

Guided exercise

Task. Using the holdings in the worked example, work out the deployed percentage, the open risk percentage and the Sector Y share.

Hints:

  1. Add the position values first, then divide by capital.
  2. Add the planned losses, then divide by capital.
  3. Sector Y has one position only.

Answer: Deployed 30,000 / 50,000 = 60%. Open risk 1,300 / 50,000 = 2.6%. Sector Y 7,500 / 50,000 = 15%.

Independent exercise

Task. Four positions, each at 25% of capital, and each with a planned loss of 1% of capital. Work out the total open risk and the deployed percentage. Then explain why this is a concentration problem if all four are in one sector.

Check your answer

Each position is 25% of capital, so deployed = 4 x 25% = 100%. Cash = 0.

Each planned loss is 1% of capital, so open risk = 4 x 1% = 4%.

If all four are in one sector, a single sector move can hit all four stops at once. The four positions are closer to one large bet on that sector than to four separate bets. So the sector share (100% of capital) is the concentration problem.

Quiz

Six questions cover open risk, sector share, the difference between exposure and risk, correlation, and a spot-the-mistake on sector totals.

Paper task

Mode: learning note (or paper). No orders are placed.

  1. Write a weekly portfolio snapshot in your journal. Use made-up holdings only.
  2. For each position, note the value, the sector, the percentage of capital and the planned loss at the stop.
  3. Add up the totals: deployed, cash, open risk and each sector’s share.
  4. Compare each total with your own limits. Write one line on whether any limit is breached.

If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Exposure and open risk are not forecasts. Open risk assumes every stop fills at its stop price. Gaps and circuits can break that assumption (Slippage, gaps and planned versus actual risk).
  • Sector labels depend on the data source. Check the source before use.
  • Diversification does not remove market-wide risk. A broad fall can hit every sector.
  • The 50% sector limit is an example. You set every limit.
  • Adding to a losing position to lower the average cost raises concentration. It is not a fix.

Remember

  • Exposure is value. Open risk is the planned loss. They are different numbers.
  • Open risk = total of (entry - stop) x shares across positions.
  • Positions in one sector often move together. Count them as one larger bet.
  • Update sector totals every time you add a position.
  • Check every limit before you add a position, not after.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • NSE sectoral index methodology documents.

The NSE Indices four-level industry classification was read on 11 Nov 2026 from the NSE Indices site.

Capital allocation

Hook

Hypothetical: your savings are Rs 4,00,000, and you had set Rs 50,000 as your trading money. After a good month, you move Rs 2,00,000 into trading. A 40% fall on Rs 2,00,000 is Rs 80,000. That loss now reaches money that was meant for the household rent. The amount was never fixed in rupees, so nothing stopped the move.

The idea

Split your money into three layers:

  1. Household needs and emergency reserve. Never traded. You choose the size of the reserve. The book does not set it.
  2. Trading capital. A fixed rupee amount, set by you before trading. It must be an amount you can afford to lose in full.
  3. Per-trade risk. A percentage of your current trading capital. It comes from trading capital, not from total savings.

Rules that follow:

  • Set in rupees. Trading capital is a rupee amount you set before trading. It is not a percentage of growing savings.
  • Cut rule. A drawdown level, for example 10% of trading capital, that lowers the risk percentage or the allocation.
  • Stop rule. A deeper level, for example 20%, that pauses trading for review. The pause starts when the level is reached, not after a recovery. The stop rule links to When to stop trading altogether (suspension conditions).
  • State the base. The worked examples measure drawdown from the starting allocation of Rs 50,000. Per-trade risk uses the current trading capital.
  • Extra money comes only from spare income. Household savings are never trading capital. They are never used to top up trading capital after losses.
  • Scale-up and withdrawal are covered in Scaling and withdrawal policy. This chapter does not set them.

See it

Read the bar from left to right. The untouched amount is the largest part. The trading capital is a small segment. The cut line marks a 10% drop from the Rs 50,000 starting allocation. The stop line marks a 20% drop. The untouched amount includes the emergency reserve, which the bar does not split out.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Savings Rs 4,00,000.

ItemCalculationRupees or %
Trading capitalSet by youRs 50,000
Trading share of savings50,000 / 4,00,00012.5%
Untouched amount4,00,000 - 50,000Rs 3,50,000

The untouched amount includes the emergency reserve. The book does not set the reserve.

Cut at 10% drawdown.

  1. Cut level = 10% x 50,000 = Rs 5,000.
  2. Trading capital falls to 50,000 - 5,000 = Rs 45,000.
  3. Per-trade risk at 1% = 1% x 45,000 = Rs 450. The lower capital gives a lower risk.

Stop review at 20% drawdown.

  1. Stop level = 20% x 50,000 = Rs 10,000.
  2. Trading capital falls to 50,000 - 10,000 = Rs 40,000.
  3. Trading pauses for review. If trading restarts after the review, per-trade risk at 1% would be 1% x 40,000 = Rs 400. No trade is taken during the pause.

Try it

Use the planner with the guided exercise inputs:

Guided exercise

Task. Savings Rs 6,00,000. Trading capital Rs 30,000. Work out the trading share of savings (5%), the untouched amount, and the per-trade risk at 1%. Then, at a 15% drawdown, work out the capital and the per-trade risk at 1%.

Hints:

  1. Trading share = trading capital / total savings.
  2. Untouched = total savings - trading capital.
  3. After a drawdown, take percentages of the trading capital after the drawdown, not of savings.

Answer: Trading share = 30,000 / 6,00,000 = 5%. Untouched = 6,00,000 - 30,000 = Rs 5,70,000. Per-trade risk = 1% x 30,000 = Rs 300. At a 15% drawdown, the loss is 15% x 30,000 = Rs 4,500, so the capital is 30,000 - 4,500 = Rs 25,500. Per-trade risk = 1% x 25,500 = Rs 255.

Independent exercise

Task. Write your own allocation with five numbers: total savings, emergency reserve, trading capital, cut threshold and stop threshold. Work out the per-trade risk at 1%, and the capital at each threshold.

Check your answer

Sample numbers (yours will differ): savings Rs 3,00,000; emergency reserve Rs 1,00,000; trading capital Rs 40,000; cut threshold 10%; stop threshold 20%.

  • Trading share of savings = 40,000 / 3,00,000 = 13.3%.
  • Per-trade risk at 1% of Rs 40,000 = Rs 400.
  • Cut: 10% x 40,000 = Rs 4,000. Capital = 36,000. Per-trade risk = 1% x 36,000 = Rs 360.
  • Stop: 20% x 40,000 = Rs 8,000. Capital = 32,000. Trading pauses for review. The 1% figure would be Rs 320 if trading restarted.

The reserve is not used in any of these calculations. It stays untouched.

Quiz

Six questions cover the trading share, the capital after a drawdown, the top-up rule, the base of the risk percentage and the timing of the stop rule.

Paper task

Mode: learning note (Lab 3). Planned fields only.

  1. Make a one-page allocation sheet in your journal. Use made-up numbers.
  2. Record the five numbers: total savings, emergency reserve, trading capital, cut threshold and stop threshold.
  3. Build a threshold table with three rows: starting, cut and stop. For each row, record the capital and the per-trade risk at 1%.
  4. Mark the sheet as a learning note. It is a planning step only.

If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The numbers are your decisions. The book does not recommend an amount, a reserve or a threshold.
  • The 10% and 20% thresholds are examples.
  • A fixed trading amount does not make any plan profitable. It only limits how much you can lose.
  • The sheet does not cover scale-up or withdrawal. Those rules are in Scaling and withdrawal policy.
  • This is not personal financial advice. Household planning may need a qualified professional.

Remember

  • Set trading capital in rupees before trading. It is not a share of growing savings.
  • Household needs and the emergency reserve are never traded.
  • Per-trade risk comes from the current trading capital.
  • The cut lowers risk. The stop pauses trading at its threshold, not after a recovery.
  • Household money is never used to top up trading capital.

Sources and verification

Sources (names only):

  • The book’s capital framework (PLAN section 2 and section 4.6).

This chapter uses only your own numbers and example thresholds. It states no outside facts.

The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check

Hook

A hypothetical trader with Rs 50,000 writes one number into her plan: Rs 2,000 a day. Over 250 trading days that is Rs 5,00,000. That is ten times her capital. Her plan does not explain how any strategy would make that every day. This chapter does the arithmetic she skipped. The result is not a strategy problem to solve. It is a number that shows the target does not fit this capital.

The idea

Start with the target in rupees. Then ask what capital and what return it would need.

Terms

  • Daily target: the profit you want in one trading day, in rupees.
  • Trading days per year: the number of market sessions in a year. This chapter uses 250 as a round number for the arithmetic. The real count is a little lower. In 2026 the NSE calendar has about 245 normal trading days, after weekends and market holidays. Using 245 instead of 250 changes the totals by about 2%, and the point of the chapter stays the same. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE holiday list for 2026. The count changes each year, so check the NSE site.)
  • Annual return: the yearly profit as a percentage of your capital.
  • Required daily return: the daily target as a percentage of your capital.

Capital needed. Daily target times trading days per year, divided by the annual return.

In symbols: C = (T x D) / r, where T is the daily target, D is the number of trading days, r is the annual return as a decimal, and C is the capital.

Required daily return. Daily target divided by capital.

In symbols: g = T / C.

Sensitivity. You cannot control the annual return. So the table shows three values: 10%, 20% and 50%. You can treat 20% a year as a very good result. That is a reference point, not a forecast. The 50% row shows only the scale of the problem. It is not an expectation.

Risk and the number of trades. At Rs 50,000 with 1% risk per trade, one R is Rs 500. A Rs 2,000 day is 4R before costs. That is four wins of 1R each, with no losing trade. Every losing trade takes away from that figure. The more trades a target needs, the more chances costs and mistakes have to add up (Overtrading and why daily profit targets encourage it).

Costs. Every trade pays costs (Costs on a trade). This chapter uses the dummy cost model from Costs on a trade: a Rs 190 round trip on Rs 50,000. Dummy figures are for arithmetic only. They are round numbers for practice, not the real charges. As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists intraday brokerage as 0.03% of the trade value or Rs 20 per order, whichever is lower, plus STT, exchange fees, SEBI fee, stamp duty and GST. The table in Costs on a trade has every line. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page. Gross profit must cover costs before any of it counts toward the target.

Streaks. Losing days come in runs (Consecutive losses and losing streaks). The worked example shows the capital lost over 5, 10 and 15 losing days. It assumes each losing day loses the risk percentage of the capital at that moment. So each loss is a smaller rupee amount than the one before.

Consistency. A daily target assumes a steady daily return. Real results vary from one day to the next. A few good days say nothing about the next day. Consistent daily returns cannot be guaranteed.

Compounding check. 1.04 raised to the power 250 is about 18,000. Put another way, Rs 1 would grow to about Rs 18,000 if it earned 4% on each of 250 days. No responsible plan can assume that. The 4% a day figure is shown here only to make the scale visible.

Conclusion. At Rs 10,000 to Rs 50,000, a target of Rs 2,000 to Rs 3,000 a day is unrealistic. This book does not work backwards from a target to invent a strategy that makes it look possible. The target must never drive a trade decision.

See it

Look at the capital lost after five losing days. Then change the streak to 10 and 15. The loss grows a little more slowly than a straight line, because each loss is 1% of a smaller balance. Then change the risk to 2% and compare the same streak lengths.

Worked example

All figures are hypothetical and use the arithmetic of this chapter. The 250-day count is a round number for practice. The 2026 NSE calendar has about 245 normal trading days.

Step 1: capital needed for Rs 2,000 a day at 20% a year

StepCalculationResult
Yearly target2,000 x 250Rs 5,00,000
Capital needed5,00,000 / 0.20Rs 25,00,000 (Rs 25 lakh)

Check: 25,00,000 x 0.20 = 5,00,000.

For Rs 3,000 a day: 3,000 x 250 = Rs 7,50,000, and 7,50,000 / 0.20 = Rs 37,50,000 (Rs 37.5 lakh).

Step 2: the effect of the annual return

Daily target10% a year20% a year50% a year (scale only)
Rs 2,000Rs 50,00,000Rs 25,00,000Rs 10,00,000
Rs 3,000Rs 75,00,000Rs 37,50,000Rs 15,00,000

Each cell is the yearly target divided by the return. Example: 5,00,000 / 0.10 = 50,00,000, and 7,50,000 / 0.50 = 15,00,000.

Step 3: required daily return

CapitalRs 2,000 a dayRs 3,000 a day
Rs 10,00020% a day30% a day
Rs 50,0004% a day6% a day
Rs 10,00,0000.2% a day0.3% a day

Example: 2,000 / 10,000 = 0.20, which is 20%. And 3,000 / 50,000 = 0.06, which is 6%.

Step 4: losing streaks at fixed risk

Each loss is the risk percentage of the capital at that moment. Example at 1% on Rs 10,000:

DayCapital before the lossLoss (1%)Capital after
1Rs 10,000Rs 100Rs 9,900
2Rs 9,900Rs 99Rs 9,801
3Rs 9,801Rs 98.01Rs 9,702.99
4Rs 9,702.99Rs 97.03Rs 9,605.96
5Rs 9,605.96Rs 96.06Rs 9,509.90

After five losses the account is about Rs 9,510, so about Rs 490 has been lost.

Capital lost, rounded:

CapitalRisk per day5 losing days10 losing days15 losing days
Rs 10,0001%Rs 490Rs 956Rs 1,399
Rs 50,0001%Rs 2,450Rs 4,781Rs 6,997
Rs 10,0002%Rs 961Rs 1,829Rs 2,614
Rs 50,0002%Rs 4,804Rs 9,146Rs 13,072

Step 5: cost drag on the target

Dummy round trip: Rs 190 on Rs 50,000 (from the dummy model in Costs on a trade).

  • Four round trips a day: 4 x Rs 190 = Rs 760 (dummy).
  • To keep Rs 2,000 after these dummy costs, gross profit must be Rs 2,760 (2,000 + 760).

Step 6: the compounding check

1.04 raised to the power 250 is about 18,000. Rs 1 at 4% a day becomes about Rs 18,000 after 250 days. That is the scale the target implies, and it is why it is not a sustainable path.

Try it

Change the capital and the risk. Check that your readings match the table in Step 4.

Guided exercise

Task: Work out the capital needed for a daily target of Rs 1,500 at 20% a year over 250 trading days.

Hints:

  1. Multiply the daily target by 250 to get the yearly target.
  2. Divide the yearly target by 0.20, the 20% return written as a decimal.
  3. Check your answer: multiply it by 0.20 and you should get the yearly target back.

Answer: 1,500 x 250 = Rs 3,75,000. Then 3,75,000 / 0.20 = Rs 18,75,000 (Rs 18.75 lakh). Check: 18,75,000 x 0.20 = 3,75,000.

Independent exercise

Task: Your capital is Rs 50,000. Work out (a) the required daily return for a target of Rs 2,500 a day, and (b) the capital needed for Rs 2,500 a day at 15% a year over 250 trading days.

Check your answer

(a) 2,500 / 50,000 = 0.05, which is 5% a day.

(b) 2,500 x 250 = Rs 6,25,000. Then 6,25,000 / 0.15 = Rs 41,66,667, about Rs 41.7 lakh.

Check: 41,66,667 x 0.15 is about 6,25,000.

Quiz

Seven questions cover capital needed, required daily return, cost drag, streaks and the reality check.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. No trades and no live account. Write it in your journal. If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write your daily target in rupees. Use a made-up or blank figure for your capital.
  2. Work out the capital needed at a 20% annual return over 250 days, or over your own checked trading-day count.
  3. Work out the required daily return: target divided by capital.
  4. Using your own cut rule from Capital allocation, count how many losing days at your chosen risk you could take before the cut applies.
  5. Record the three results in one note, with today’s date.

Limits and common mistakes

  • It does not predict any day’s result. The arithmetic assumes a steady return, and real trading does not give one.
  • The 250-day count and the dummy costs are round numbers for practice. The 2026 NSE calendar has about 245 normal trading days, and the real charges are in Costs on a trade. Check both before you rely on any figure.
  • It does not show that any strategy can reach the target. It shows only what the target would need.
  • 20% a year is a yardstick for a very good year. It is not an expectation. The 50% row is for scale only.
  • The streak figures assume one fixed risk percentage and losses that do not cluster unusually. Real streaks can be longer or shorter.

Remember

  • Capital needed = daily target x trading days / annual return.
  • At Rs 10,000, Rs 2,000 a day needs a 20% return each day. At Rs 50,000, it needs 4% a day.
  • Costs are added on top of the target. Losing streaks take capital away from the base that must work.
  • A few good days say nothing about the next one. Consistent daily returns cannot be guaranteed.
  • The target must never drive a trade. At this capital it is unrealistic.

Sources and verification

Sources, read on 11 Nov 2026:

  • NSE holiday list and trading calendar for 2026, for the trading-day count (about 245 normal days).
  • Zerodha charges page, cross-referenced from Costs on a trade. The Rs 190 round trip cost in this chapter is a round number for practice, not Zerodha’s real charge. The real charges are in the table in Costs on a trade.

Scaling and withdrawal policy

Hook

A hypothetical account starts at Rs 50,000 and climbs to Rs 57,000. The trader feels like taking money out now. Later the account falls to Rs 53,000. The trader thinks about topping it up from household savings to get back faster. Both ideas feel reasonable on the day. The second breaks the rule that household money never becomes trading capital. This chapter explains why the first needs a written rule before any withdrawal is made.

The idea

Two rules come first. Reinvest profits before you take any money out. Never withdraw while the account is in a drawdown.

Terms

  • High-water mark (HWM): the highest equity the account has reached so far. It only moves up.
  • Drawdown from the HWM: how far equity sits below the HWM, as a share of the HWM.

In symbols: drawdown = (H - E) / H, where H is the high-water mark and E is the current equity.

  • Withdrawal threshold: a rule with numbers you set in advance. The example in this chapter is: withdraw only when equity is at a new HWM and above starting capital plus Rs 10,000. The numbers are examples. You choose the real ones. A threshold may also need a stated period of positive results (for example, a set number of months).

Why no withdrawal in a drawdown. A withdrawal lowers the base the account must recover from. The gap to the HWM does not shrink. In percentage terms, it grows. From Rs 53,000, with an HWM of Rs 57,000, the account needs a gain of 4,000 / 53,000 = 7.5% to return to the peak. If Rs 5,000 is withdrawn first, the account is at Rs 48,000. Then the gain needed becomes 9,000 / 48,000 = 18.8%.

Scale-up. Trading capital may increase only after gates G6 and G7 are passed, and only with a recorded result behind it. Never increase risk after losses. Scale-up that comes from a losing run is a rule break, not a plan.

Scale-down. The cut and stop rules from Capital allocation reduce the trading capital or pause trading. Scale-down follows the rules, not feelings about the last week.

Records. Every capital change is logged in the journal, in the capital_at_plan field (Lab 3). A change that is not recorded cannot be reviewed.

Method. Withdrawals also change the base used to measure drawdown. State the method before the first withdrawal. This chapter measures drawdown against the high-water mark using the actual account value after the withdrawal. So a withdrawal shows up as a lower account value. Some people lower the high-water mark by the amount withdrawn instead. Either way works, if you pick one, write it down, and do not change it after a bad month.

See it

Read the path against two lines: the high-water mark and the Rs 60,000 threshold. A withdrawal is allowed only when equity is at a new high and above the threshold. Change the last point to Rs 61,000 and check whether the rule now allows a withdrawal.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Start at Rs 50,000. The rule in this example: withdraw only when equity is at a new HWM and above Rs 60,000 (starting capital of Rs 50,000 plus Rs 10,000).

StepEquity (Rs)HWM after step (Rs)Drawdown from HWMWithdrawal allowed?
Start50,00050,0000%No. Not above Rs 60,000
154,00054,0000%No. Below Rs 60,000
251,00054,000(54,000 - 51,000) / 54,000 = 5.6%No. In drawdown
357,00057,0000%No. Below Rs 60,000
453,00057,000(57,000 - 53,000) / 57,000 = 7.0%No. In drawdown, and below Rs 60,000

At step 4 the threshold is not met at Rs 53,000, so no withdrawal is allowed. The account is in drawdown.

Recovery gap, in percent:

  • From Rs 53,000 with no withdrawal: 4,000 / 53,000 = 7.5% gain needed.
  • After withdrawing Rs 5,000: the account is at Rs 48,000, and the gap to Rs 57,000 is 9,000. The gain needed is 9,000 / 48,000 = 18.8%.

Try it

Add a Rs 5,000 withdrawal at Rs 53,000 and compare the gain needed to get back to the high-water mark, with and without the withdrawal.

Guided exercise

Task: For the equity path Rs 50,000, 54,000, 51,000, 57,000, 53,000, work out the high-water mark after each step and the drawdown at the end.

Hints:

  1. The HWM is the highest equity so far. Carry the largest value forward.
  2. Drawdown = (HWM - current equity) / HWM.
  3. Use the HWM after the last step, not the starting value.

Answer: HWM after each step: 50,000; 54,000; 54,000; 57,000; 57,000. Final drawdown: (57,000 - 53,000) / 57,000 = 4,000 / 57,000 = 7.0%.

Independent exercise

Task: Write a withdrawal policy with three numbers: a threshold above starting capital, a positive-period length in months, and a maximum withdrawal as a percentage of the gain. Test it on the path Rs 50,000, 62,000, 58,000, 66,000. State the first step on which a withdrawal is allowed under your rule.

Check your answer

The numbers are yours, so this is one example. Threshold: Rs 10,000 above starting capital (Rs 60,000). Positive-period length: none. Maximum withdrawal: 50% of the gain.

  • Step 1 (Rs 50,000): no. Not above Rs 60,000.
  • Step 2 (Rs 62,000): yes. It is a new HWM and above Rs 60,000. If your policy has a waiting period, the first allowed step moves later.
  • Step 3 (Rs 58,000): no. Drawdown from Rs 62,000 is 4,000 / 62,000 = 6.5%.

So under this example, the first allowed step is Rs 62,000.

Quiz

Five questions cover the withdrawal rule, withdrawals in a drawdown, the top-up mistake, high-water mark arithmetic and scale-up after losses.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal. If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write your withdrawal policy on one page: threshold above starting capital, positive-period length and maximum withdrawal percentage.
  2. Write the scale-up condition next to it: gates G6 and G7 and a recorded result. Add the scale-down triggers from Capital allocation.
  3. Test the policy on the path Rs 50,000, 62,000, 58,000, 66,000. Note the first step on which a withdrawal is allowed.
  4. Add a line saying every capital change will be logged in the journal, in the capital_at_plan field.
  5. Date the page with today’s date. Do not record a live account.

Limits and common mistakes
