  • The numbers are yours. The threshold in this chapter is an example, not a recommendation.
  • A withdrawal rule does not show that the account will grow. Every equity path here is hypothetical.
  • Tax on withdrawals and gains is not covered here. Tax depends on your gains and your own situation, not on this chapter’s rules. See Taxes on trading profits and ask a qualified tax professional.
  • A policy written after a good month is a judgement made in good conditions. Write the policy before any trade, not after a result.
  • Scale-up after losses is not allowed, whatever the recent result.

Remember

  • The high-water mark only moves up. Drawdown = (HWM - current equity) / HWM.
  • Never withdraw during a drawdown. A withdrawal makes the gain needed to recover bigger.
  • A withdrawal needs both the threshold and the high-water condition to be met.
  • Scale up only after G6 and G7 and a recorded result. Scale down with the Capital allocation cut and stop rules.
  • Record every capital change in the journal.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • Taxes on trading profits, for tax treatment (read on 11 Nov 2026).
  • The withdrawal method and thresholds in this chapter are your own choices. This chapter states no outside facts about them.

Money-math assessment

Hook

A hypothetical trader has Rs 50,000, risks 1% per trade and plans to enter at Rs 400 with a stop at Rs 380. She divides her capital by the entry price and buys 125 shares. Her planned loss at the stop is 125 x 20 = Rs 2,500. That is 5% of her capital, five times the Rs 500 she set as the limit. This assessment checks whether the number of shares follows from the risk rule, not from the cash in the account.

The idea

This assessment covers the money math in this part of the book. It is the check for gate G2. To pass, you must get the cost, position-size and expectancy questions right within the allowed margin, on a fresh set of questions. You must also complete the reality-check chapter (The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check).

What it checks

  • Costs: fixed charges (per order) versus proportional charges (a percentage of turnover). Fixed charges weigh more on small trades (Costs on a trade). The practice questions use round numbers. The real charges, with dates and sources, are in the table in Costs on a trade.
  • Position size: the lowest of three limits, not the largest one.
  • Break-even win rate: the win rate at which wins pay for losses, before costs.
  • Expectancy and profit factor: the average result per trade, and the ratio of gains to losses.
  • Drawdown recovery: the gain needed to return to a starting value after a loss.
  • Daily-target capital: the capital a daily target needs (The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check).

Position size. Work out three limits, then take the lowest:

  • Risk budget = capital x risk percentage.
  • Risk per share = entry - stop.
  • Raw shares = risk budget / risk per share, rounded down.
  • Cash cap = floor(capital / entry).
  • Concentration cap = floor(capital x maximum position percentage / entry). The Lab 1 starting value is 25%.
  • Shares = the lowest of raw shares, cash cap and concentration cap.

Break-even win rate. Before costs, with a reward of R times the risk (R is the reward-to-risk ratio), break-even win rate = 1 / (1 + R). Equivalently, win rate x R = (1 - win rate) x 1.

Expectancy. Per trade, in R: (win rate x average win in R) - (loss rate x average loss in R). In rupees, multiply the R result by the rupee value of one R. Expectancy describes an average over many trades. It is not a forecast of the next trade.

Profit factor. Total gross wins divided by total gross losses. A value above 1 means the wins outweighed the losses in the record. It says nothing about how many trades the figure is based on.

Drawdown recovery. A loss of L (as a fraction of the value before it) needs a gain of L / (1 - L) on the smaller base. A 30% loss needs 0.3 / 0.7 = 42.9%, not 30%.

Question types. Number answers (with a small allowed margin), multiple choice, scenario, spot-the-mistake and order of steps.

Fresh sets. The gate uses a new set of questions each time, made by a computer with checks. For example, a long’s stop must be below its entry, capital stays in a sensible range, and quantities are whole shares. Each number answer is worked out by a tested calculation. The sample set in this chapter is fixed.

Margin for percentages. Rupee answers must be exact to the paisa (one hundredth of a rupee). Percentages are accepted within a small margin.

Cost questions and out-of-date facts. Cost questions depend on the checked facts file. If a cost fact is out of date, the assessment shows a warning and does not count those questions.

Retakes. You may retake the assessment at any time. Each retake gets a new set of questions. There is no timer, and your attempt history is kept.

Common wrong answers to watch for

  • Sizing with capital instead of risk per share.
  • Ignoring the lowest of the caps.
  • Forgetting the loss on a fixed charge.
  • Treating expectancy as what the next trade will do.

See it

The gate screen says: educational competence is not proof of profitability, and there is no pressure to go live. Read that screen before you read your score.

Worked example

Three questions, each worked in full. Hypothetical throughout.

Question 1: position size with both caps

Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1%. Entry Rs 400. Stop Rs 380.

StepWorkingResult
Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
Risk per share400 - 380Rs 20
Raw shares500 / 20, rounded down25
Cash capfloor(50,000 / 400)125
Concentration cap (25% of capital)floor(50,000 x 0.25 / 400) = floor(31.25)31
Shareslowest of 25, 125 and 3125 (the risk budget sets the size)

Check: 25 x 20 = Rs 500, which is the risk budget. And 25 x 400 = Rs 10,000, which is within the Rs 12,500 concentration limit.

Question 2: expectancy

Hypothetical record: win rate 40%, average win 2R, average loss 1R, and R = Rs 1,000.

StepWorkingResult
Win part0.40 x 2R0.80R
Loss part0.60 x 1R0.60R
Expectancy0.80R - 0.60R0.20R
In rupees0.20 x Rs 1,000Rs 200 per trade

Check: in 10 hypothetical trades, 4 wins of Rs 2,000 give Rs 8,000, and 6 losses of Rs 1,000 give Rs 6,000. The net is Rs 2,000, and Rs 2,000 / 10 = Rs 200 a trade. This describes that record only. It is not a forecast.

Profit factor on the same record: 0.80R / 0.60R = 1.3.

Question 3: daily-target capital

Target Rs 2,000 a day, 250 trading days (a round number for practice; the 2026 NSE calendar has about 245 normal trading days), 20% a year.

  • Yearly target: 2,000 x 250 = Rs 5,00,000.
  • Capital: 5,00,000 / 0.20 = Rs 25,00,000.

Check: 25,00,000 x 0.20 = 5,00,000.

Try it

Move the stop to Rs 390 and then to Rs 370. At Rs 390 the risk budget allows 50 shares, but the concentration cap of 31 is lower, so 31 is the size. At Rs 370 the risk budget allows 16 shares, which is the lowest, so 16 is the size. Note that the cash and concentration caps depend on entry price and capital, not on the stop.

Guided exercise

Task: Answer three sample questions.

  1. Capital Rs 50,000, risk 1%, entry Rs 400, stop Rs 380. How many whole shares, applying both caps?
  2. What win rate breaks even before costs when the reward is R = 3 (win 3R, lose 1R)? Give a percentage.
  3. An account falls 30%. What gain, in percent, brings it back to its starting value?

Hints:

  1. For question 1, work out the risk budget and the risk per share, then the raw shares. Compare the raw shares with both caps and take the lowest.
  2. For question 2, use W = 1 / (1 + R). Check: 25 wins of 3R and 75 losses of 1R give 75R won and 75R lost.
  3. For question 3, the gain is loss / (1 - loss), applied to the smaller account. Do not use 30% of the starting value.

Answer: Question 1: 25 shares (risk budget Rs 500, risk per share Rs 20, raw 25; caps 125 and 31). Question 2: 25%. Question 3: 42.9%.

Independent exercise

Task: Answer a ten-question set on paper with no hints. Then enter your answers in the app. Record your score and the concepts you missed in the learning log.

Check your answer

There is no single answer key for a set you write or draw yourself. Check each result against these tests:

  • A share count is never above the lowest of the three limits.
  • A break-even win rate satisfies W x R = (1 - W) x 1 for a 1R loss.
  • Expectancy in R equals (win rate x average win) minus (loss rate x average loss).
  • A drawdown recovery uses loss / (1 - loss), applied to the smaller base.

Missed questions are scheduled for review after the reviewAfterDays value.

Quiz

Eight sample questions cover the areas above. The gate uses a fresh set of questions, not this one.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal. If the journal app is not ready, use paper.

  1. Use ten questions covering the areas above. Use the app’s set once it is ready, or write ten yourself.
  2. Answer all ten on paper, with no notes and no hints.
  3. Check each answer against the working in this chapter.
  4. Enter your answers in the app once it is ready.
  5. Record your score and the missed concepts in the learning log, with the date.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Passing G2 unlocks no live trading. The gate screen says so.
  • Number success does not show that a strategy works. The expectancy in this chapter is a worked example, not a forecast.
  • Cost answers are only as good as the checked cost facts. If a fact is out of date, those questions are not counted.
  • A pass on a small set is a small sample. The gate’s set of questions is also a sample.
  • Money-math skill does not replace the risk rules. They apply before every trade.

Remember

  • Position size is the lowest of the risk, cash and concentration limits.
  • Break-even win rate = 1 / (1 + R), before costs.
  • Expectancy = (win rate x average win) - (loss rate x average loss), in R.
  • Recovering from a 30% loss needs a 42.9% gain, not 30%.
  • Educational competence is not proof of profitability.

Sources and verification

Sources, read on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Costs on a trade, for the real Zerodha charges that sit beside the practice numbers.
  • NSE holiday list and trading calendar for 2026, for the trading-day count (about 245 normal days).

3. Chart reading

  1. Candlesticks
  2. Timeframes and how the same price looks different
  3. Support and resistance

Candlesticks

Hook

Hypothetical. A stock opens the day at Rs 500. It closes the day at Rs 509. The chart shows that whole day as one box. A trader looks at the box. The trader thinks, “The price went up, so it will keep going up.” That thought is the mistake this chapter addresses. The box is a summary of one day. It holds four numbers. It does not say what the next day will bring.

The idea

A price is one trade. Many trades happen in a day, so many prices appear. A candle squeezes all the prices for one period into four numbers.

The four numbers are:

  • Open: the first traded price in the period.
  • High: the highest traded price in the period.
  • Low: the lowest traded price in the period.
  • Close: the last traded price in the period.

A period can be one minute, one day or any other length. The length is covered in Timeframes and how the same price looks different.

The candle has three parts that come from those four numbers:

  • Body: the thick part. It is the gap between open and close, never a negative number. Body = close minus open, ignoring the sign.
  • Shadows (also called wicks): the thin lines above and below the body. The upper shadow runs from the top of the body up to the high. The lower shadow runs from the bottom of the body down to the low.
  • Range: the full height of the candle. Range = high minus low.

Two labels describe the candle:

  • A bullish candle has a close above its open. The price finished higher than it started.
  • A bearish candle has a close below its open. The price finished lower than it started.

Colours differ between charting tools. One tool may draw bullish candles in green. Another may use white. Read the legend, the small key on the chart, before you rely on colour. The rule is the comparison of close with open, not the colour. Kite’s default candles are green and red. Zerodha says a setting called “Colour bars based on previous close” can change how the colours are chosen, so read the legend on your own chart. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on candle colours. Settings change, so check Kite.)

A candle is built from many trades. Each trade is one tick. The candle keeps the first, highest, lowest and last tick of the period.

The open of a daily candle on NSE comes from the pre-open phase. NSE sets the day’s open price from the price found in pre-open, which runs from 9:00 am to 9:15 am. If no price is found there, the first trade of the normal market at 9:15 am is the open. The session times are covered in Trading sessions, timings and holidays. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE pre-open page. Rules change, so check the NSE site.) Daily candles on Kite use the exchange’s end-of-day data, while minute candles are built from the live price feed, so the open on a minute chart can differ a little from the daily open.

A candle does not show everything. It does not show the order of trades inside the period. It does not show how many shares were traded. It says nothing certain about the next candle.

See it

Use Step to add one tick at a time. The open stays fixed at the first tick. The high and low move only when a new extreme arrives. The close is always the last tick. Use Play, Pause, Step and Reset. Check each number against the text table beside the picture.

Worked example

Hypothetical. One daily candle: open Rs 500, high Rs 512, low Rs 497, close Rs 509.

PartCalculationResult
Body509 minus 500Rs 9
Upper shadow512 minus 509Rs 3
Lower shadow500 minus 497Rs 3
Range512 minus 497Rs 15

Check: body plus both shadows must equal the range. 9 + 3 + 3 = 15.

The close is above the open, so the candle is bullish.

Cost check, before charges: 100 shares at Rs 509 cost 100 x 509 = Rs 50,900. Check: 100 x 500 = Rs 50,000, and 100 x 9 = Rs 900, which adds to Rs 50,900.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Five ticks arrive in this order: Rs 501, Rs 499, Rs 510, Rs 506 and Rs 504. Build the candle. Give the open, high, low and close. Say whether it is bullish or bearish.

Hints:

  1. The open is the first tick.
  2. The high is the largest tick.
  3. The low is the smallest tick.
  4. The close is the last tick.

Answer: Open Rs 501. High Rs 510. Low Rs 499. Close Rs 504. The candle is bullish, because 504 is above 501.

  • Body: 504 minus 501 = Rs 3.
  • Upper shadow: 510 minus 504 = Rs 6.
  • Lower shadow: 501 minus 499 = Rs 2.
  • Range: 510 minus 499 = Rs 11. Check: 3 + 6 + 2 = 11.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. A candle has open Rs 300, high Rs 308, low Rs 296 and close Rs 297. Work out the body, the upper shadow, the lower shadow and the range. Say whether the candle is bullish or bearish. Name one thing this candle does not show.

Check your answer
  • Body: 300 minus 297 = Rs 3.
  • Upper shadow: the top of the body is the open here, because the close is lower. 308 minus 300 = Rs 8.
  • Lower shadow: 297 minus 296 = Rs 1.
  • Range: 308 minus 296 = Rs 12. Check: 3 + 8 + 1 = 12.
  • The candle is bearish, because 297 is below 300.
  • One thing it does not show: the order of the trades inside the day. It also does not show the volume. Either answer is correct.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the four prices, the body and shadow arithmetic, and what a single candle cannot tell you.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Pick one stock and a chart you are allowed to view. Write the result in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Choose one stock. Use a chart you are allowed to view.
  2. For 10 daily candles, write the open, high, low and close.
  3. For each candle, work out the body, the upper shadow, the lower shadow and the range.
  4. Mark each candle as bullish or bearish.
  5. If you use the Kite demo instead, write the mode as kite_demo. Say that the demo is for interface practice only.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A long lower shadow does not mean the price will bounce. It shows only that the price fell during the period and then recovered part of the fall.
  • Colour does not define a candle. Check the legend first. Then compare the close with the open.
  • The order of moves inside a candle is unknown. The shape cannot show whether the high or the low came first.
  • A candle does not show volume. Two candles with the same shape can have very different numbers of shares traded. Volume is covered in Volume and what it adds.
  • A candle is a record of one period. Calling the last candle strong or weak does not predict the next one.

Remember

  • A candle squeezes one period into four numbers: open, high, low and close.
  • Body = close minus open, ignoring the sign. Range = high minus low.
  • Bullish means close above open. Bearish means close below open.
  • Colour is a display setting. Read the legend before you rely on it.
  • A candle describes one past period. It is not a forecast.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, candlestick module (reference only)
  • NSE India, market data definitions
  • Kite chart help pages

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Kite’s default candle colours are green and red, and a setting can change them. This comes from Zerodha’s support site.
  • NSE sets the day’s open from the pre-open price, or from the first normal-market trade if pre-open finds none. This comes from NSE’s pre-open page.
  • Daily candles use exchange end-of-day data, and minute and hourly candles use the live feed. This comes from Zerodha’s support site.

Timeframes and how the same price looks different

Hook

Hypothetical. A trader opens a stock on the one-minute chart. The last few minutes look weak, so the trader decides the stock is falling. Then the trader opens the daily chart and sees a stock that rose over the past weeks. Both charts show the same stock at the same moment. Which one is right? The honest answer is that both are right. They show different periods. The mistake is to pick the chart that agrees with the mood of the moment.

The idea

A timeframe is the length of one candle. A one-minute candle covers one minute of trading. A daily candle covers one whole day. The same trades give different candles at different timeframes.

When you join shorter candles into one longer candle, use four rules:

  • Open comes from the first short candle.
  • Close comes from the last short candle.
  • High is the largest high of all the short candles.
  • Low is the smallest low of all the short candles.

Shorter timeframes show more small moves. Many of those moves are noise. Noise means small price movement that does not change your plan. Longer timeframes show fewer moves. They give fewer, slower decisions.

For an end-of-day plan, the daily candle is the decision frame. Shorter timeframes help with later questions about timing. Those questions are covered in Multiple timeframe analysis.

Three points to keep in mind:

  • A stop set from a five-minute wick is not a daily decision. Write the timeframe in your plan.
  • Switching timeframes until a pattern appears is hindsight shopping. Choose the timeframe before you look at the chart.
  • A longer timeframe is not safer by default. It shows fewer moves, but a gap can still carry the price past your stop.

See it

Switch between the one-minute, five-minute and daily settings. The same tick data redraws at each setting. Compare the candle you see at each setting with the table in the worked example. Notice that the close and the open can change the label.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Five one-minute candles, each shown as open, high, low and close:

MinuteOpenHighLowClose
1500501499500.5
2500.5503500502
3502504501501
4501502498499
5499500497498.5

Join them into one five-minute candle:

PartRuleResult
Openopen of minute 1500
Highlargest high (minute 3)504
Lowsmallest low (minute 5)497
Closeclose of minute 5498.5

Range = 504 minus 497 = Rs 7. Check: the range is also the highest high minus the lowest low, so no number is missed.

The close of 498.5 is below the open of 500. The five-minute candle is bearish. Minute 2 closed up, but the five-minute candle still compares the first open with the last close.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Join the five one-minute candles in the worked example into one five-minute candle. Give the open, high, low and close. Give the range. Say whether the candle is bullish or bearish.

Hints:

  1. The open is the open of the first minute.
  2. List all five highs. Take the largest.
  3. List all five lows. Take the smallest.
  4. The close is the close of the last minute.

Answer: Open 500. High 504. Low 497. Close 498.5. Range Rs 7 (504 minus 497). The candle is bearish, because 498.5 is below 500.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Four one-hour candles follow. Each shows open, high, low and close: (200, 204, 199, 203), (203, 206, 202, 205), (205, 205, 200, 201) and (201, 203, 199, 202). Join them into one four-hour candle. State whether it is bullish or bearish.

Check your answer
  • Open: the first candle’s open, 200.
  • High: the largest of 204, 206, 205 and 203, which is 206.
  • Low: the smallest of 199, 202, 200 and 199, which is 199.
  • Close: the last candle’s close, 202.
  • Range: 206 minus 199 = Rs 7.
  • The candle is bullish, because 202 is above 200.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the aggregation rules, the range, and why a choice of timeframe must come before you look.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write the result in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Pick one stock.
  2. On the same day, look at its five-minute, one-hour and daily charts.
  3. For each chart, write whether the candles look up, down or sideways.
  4. Write which timeframe your plan uses, and write it before you look at the charts.
  5. Note any day where the labels disagree. Keep that note for later use in your study.

If you do this in the Kite demo, write the mode as kite_demo. The Kite demo is for looking at screens only. Write any practice trade as paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A longer timeframe does not remove risk. A daily candle can still open far below your stop after a gap (Gaps and what they mean for stops).
  • Changing the timeframe until a pattern appears gives false confidence. Choose the timeframe first.
  • A stop placed on a five-minute wick can be hit by normal noise. A daily plan would ignore that noise. The planned loss changes with the timeframe, so recheck the numbers.
  • Intervals and how far back the history goes depend on the platform. Check the current limits before you plan. Zerodha says Kite keeps 5-second, 10-second, 15-second, 30-second and 45-second data for only the last two trading days. For equities, 1-minute and longer candles go back to 2015. Daily NSE data goes back to 2003 and BSE data to 2008. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support page on historical data on Kite charts. Limits change, so check Kite.)

Remember

  • A timeframe is the length of one candle.
  • Joining candles: open from the first, close from the last, high is the largest high, low is the smallest low.
  • The same stock can look bullish on one timeframe and bearish on another.
  • Choose the timeframe and write it in your plan before you look.
  • A stop must match the timeframe of the plan it belongs to.

Sources and verification

  • Kite chart help pages
  • Zerodha Varsity, timeframe module (reference only)
  • Zerodha support, intraday data pages

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • How far back each Kite candle length goes comes from Zerodha’s support page on historical data on Kite charts. The list of every interval Kite offers is not copied here, so check the chart menu in Kite.

Support and resistance

Hook

Hypothetical. A stock has reached about Rs 251 three times. Each time, the price turned back down. A trader says, “Rs 251 is a wall. It will hold a fourth time.” The trader then places a stop exactly at Rs 251. The next small move down takes the stock to Rs 250.5 and the stop is hit. The trader lost money on a level that was never a wall. The level was only a place where price had turned before.

The idea

Support is an area where price has often stopped falling and turned up. Resistance is an area where price has often stopped rising and turned down. Both come from the past. They describe where buyers or sellers showed up before. They do not promise what will happen next.

Use a zone, not a single line. A zone is a band of prices where the turns clustered. A single line hides the spread. Two people can draw different zones on the same chart. Your zone is a judgement, and you should write it down.

More touches are not proof. Price reaching a zone many times does not give a chance of a bounce. Touch count is a description of the past.

Role reversal means a broken level can change its job. A support that breaks can later act as resistance. A resistance that breaks can later act as support. Price may come back to the old level and turn there. That is also a description, not a promise.

Round numbers, such as Rs 250 or Rs 500, are watched by many traders. Some people treat them as levels. Their value as predictors is not established. Treat them as a hypothesis that you can test.

Stops go beyond the zone, not on it. Normal noise often touches the edges of a zone. A stop on the edge can be hit by that noise. A stop beyond the zone is still a planned loss. The planned loss must fit your risk budget (Position sizing step by step).

Support is a hypothesis. Never buy more of a falling share because price is “at support.” That is called averaging down. It means buying more of a falling share to lower your average cost. It is covered in Why scalping, option buying and martingale are dangerous.

See it

Three swing highs, each one rejected, clustered close together:

Swing highPriceWhat happened next
High 1Rs 251Rejected
High 2Rs 252Rejected
High 3Rs 250.5Rejected

The three highs sit between Rs 250.5 and Rs 252. That band is the zone. Look at the spread of the cluster, not at one price.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Swing highs at Rs 251, Rs 252 and Rs 250.5 were each rejected. Price later closed above them. Assume the old resistance now acts as support (role reversal). The support zone runs from Rs 249 to Rs 252. Assume an entry at Rs 252, the top of the zone. Assume a stop at Rs 247, which is Rs 2 below the bottom of the zone. Capital is Rs 50,000. Risk per trade is 1 percent.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
2. Risk per share252 minus 247Rs 5
3. Raw sharesfloor(500 / 5)100
4. Cash capfloor(50,000 / 252) = floor(198.4)198
5. Concentration cap (25%)floor(12,500 / 252) = floor(49.6)49
Sharesminimum of 100, 198 and 4949

Binding constraint: the concentration cap. The planned loss is 49 x Rs 5 = Rs 245. That is inside the Rs 500 budget. The concentration cap is the smallest limit, so it sets the size.

The steps are the same as in Position sizing step by step.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Use the three swing highs in the See it table. Draw one zone that includes all three. Write the top edge and the bottom edge of your zone.

Hints:

  1. Find the swing highs first. Here they are already given.
  2. Look for a cluster, not a single point.
  3. Draw the zone to include all the clustered highs.
  4. Any stop you set goes beyond the zone, not on its edge.

Answer: A zone that includes all three highs runs from Rs 250.5 (bottom) to Rs 252 (top). The worked example uses a wider zone of Rs 249 to Rs 252. Both are judgements. Your zone is correct if it holds all three highs and you can explain why you drew it there.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. A support zone runs from Rs 380 to Rs 384. Entry Rs 384. Stop Rs 377. Capital Rs 40,000. Risk 1 percent. Concentration cap 25 percent. Work out the risk per share, the raw shares, the cash cap, the concentration cap and the final shares. Name the binding constraint.

Check your answer
  • Risk budget: 40,000 x 1% = Rs 400.
  • Risk per share: 384 minus 377 = Rs 7.
  • Raw shares: floor(400 / 7) = floor(57.1) = 57.
  • Cash cap: floor(40,000 / 384) = floor(104.2) = 104.
  • Concentration cap: floor(40,000 x 25% / 384) = floor(10,000 / 384) = floor(26.04) = 26.
  • Final shares: minimum of 57, 104 and 26 = 26.
  • Binding constraint: concentration cap.
  • Planned loss: 26 x 7 = Rs 182.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks zones against lines, role reversal, and why a stop goes beyond a zone.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready). Lab 3 is the place for these notes.

  1. Pick 10 charts, from any chart you are allowed to view.
  2. On each chart, draw one support zone or one resistance zone.
  3. Write down the zone before you look at what happened next.
  4. Later, mark each zone as held, broke, or not tested.
  5. Record the notes as learning notes. Do not treat them as proof.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Support and resistance describe past turns. A level that held before may not hold again.
  • Your zone is a judgement. Another trader may draw a different zone on the same chart.
  • Touch count is not a probability. Three touches do not make a fourth touch more likely to hold.
  • A stop beyond the zone is still a planned loss. Check it against your risk budget with the steps in Position sizing step by step.
  • Never average down because price is at support. Averaging down adds to a losing position, and the plan must not allow it.

Remember

  • A zone is a band of prices. A line is one price.
  • Support and resistance describe past turns. They do not promise future turns.
  • A broken level can change its job. This is role reversal.
  • Put stops beyond the zone, never exactly on it.
  • A stop is only safe if its planned loss fits the risk budget.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, support and resistance module (reference only)
  • Peer-reviewed reviews of technical trading rules
  • Dow theory overview, from standard technical-analysis texts

Trend and market structure

Hook

Hypothetical. A stock rises from Rs 200 to Rs 230. It falls to Rs 215, then rises to Rs 245 and falls to Rs 222. A trader sees one strong up day in the middle of that path. The trader says, “The trend is up, so I will buy.” Yet the trader has not set a swing definition, a stop or a rule. The chart has been drawn, but the trend has not been defined. This chapter shows how to read the path step by step.

The idea

A trend is the direction of successive swing points. It is not the direction of one candle. One up candle does not make an uptrend.

A swing high is a high with lower highs on both sides. A swing low is a low with higher lows on both sides. The number of candles on each side is your choice. Call it N. A change to N changes which points count as swings. A change to N can change the label too. Write N in your notes.

Three labels describe the structure:

  • Uptrend: higher highs and higher lows.
  • Downtrend: lower highs and lower lows.
  • Range: neither pattern. The highs and lows move sideways.

Compare each swing with the previous swing of the same type. Compare highs with highs and lows with lows.

A structure break is the level where the current pattern fails. In an uptrend, a close below the most recent swing low means the last higher low has failed. The structure is no longer making higher lows.

Structure labels are retrospective. They are updated as new candles arrive. A label that looked clear last week can change this week.

A trend is a description that can end. Using a trend to decide a trade is a strategy. That strategy is covered in Trend following in depth.

See it

The swing points from the worked example, sorted by type:

Swing highsSwing lows
Rs 230Rs 200
Rs 245Rs 215
Rs 222

Read down each column. Each highs value is above the one before it. Each lows value is above the one before it. Those two facts describe an uptrend.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Swing sequence in time order: low Rs 200, high Rs 230, low Rs 215, high Rs 245, low Rs 222.

SwingPriceCompared with the previous swing of the same type
LowRs 200none yet
HighRs 230none yet
LowRs 215higher than Rs 200
HighRs 245higher than Rs 230
LowRs 222higher than Rs 215

The lows 200, 215 and 222 are rising. The highs 230 and 245 are rising. The structure is an uptrend.

The structure-break level is Rs 222. A close below Rs 222 means the most recent higher low has failed. That close would change the label. Check: 222 is the most recent swing low, so it is the level to watch.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Swing sequence in time order: low Rs 100, high Rs 120, low Rs 110, high Rs 125, low Rs 115. Label each swing as a higher or lower point. Classify the structure. Name the structure-break level.

Hints:

  1. Compare each swing with the previous swing of the same type.
  2. Compare the highs with the highs. Compare the lows with the lows.
  3. Check whether both the highs and the lows are rising.
  4. The structure-break level is the most recent swing low in an uptrend.

Answer: Lows: 110 is higher than 100, and 115 is higher than 110. Highs: 125 is higher than 120. The structure is an uptrend. The structure-break level is Rs 115, the most recent swing low.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Swing sequence in time order: high Rs 260, low Rs 240, high Rs 255, low Rs 238. Label each swing. Classify the structure. For a downtrend view, state the structure-break level.

Check your answer
  • The high of Rs 255 is lower than the high of Rs 260. It is a lower high.
  • The low of Rs 238 is lower than the low of Rs 240. It is a lower low.
  • The structure is a downtrend: lower highs and lower lows.
  • For a downtrend view, the structure-break level is the most recent swing high, Rs 255. A close above Rs 255 means the last lower high has failed.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks higher highs and higher lows, range identification, and why the swing definition must be fixed before you look.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write the result in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Pick one stock.
  2. Each week for 8 weeks, label the structure on the weekly chart as uptrend, downtrend or range.
  3. Write the swing definition you used, including N. Keep it the same for all 8 weeks.
  4. Note any week where your label changed after new data arrived.
  5. Write down what changed, and why, without drawing a conclusion from the change.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The label depends on the swing definition. A different N can give different swings and a different label.
  • Labels update as new candles arrive. A week labelled as an uptrend can become a range the next week.
  • One up candle is not an uptrend. A new high on one day is not enough to make a trend.
  • Writing “the trend is up, so I buy” without a rule and a stop is the mistake. A label alone is not a plan.

Remember

  • A trend is the direction of successive swing points, not one candle.
  • Uptrend: higher highs and higher lows. Downtrend: lower highs and lower lows. Range: neither.
  • Write down your swing definition, including N, before you label the chart.
  • The structure-break level is the most recent swing point that the trend depends on.
  • Labels are retrospective. Expect them to change as new candles arrive.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, trend module (reference only)
  • Dow theory overview, from standard technical-analysis texts

Breakouts

Hook

Hypothetical. A stock stays between Rs 300 and Rs 310 for 10 days. One morning it trades at Rs 311 for a minute. It then closes at Rs 308, inside the range. A trader says, “It broke out, so I bought.” The price did trade above the top. The close did not. This chapter explains why the close matters and how to test a breakout rule.

The idea

A range has a top and a bottom. A breakout is a move above the top of the range. A breakdown is a move below the bottom of the range.

Define the breakout before you look at the chart:

  • Close-based: the candle closes beyond the level.
  • Touch-based: price trades beyond the level, even for a moment.

Close-based is easier to test. A close is one fixed number for the period. A touch can happen and reverse within seconds. A plan that uses touches needs more rules to say what counts.

A false breakout is a move beyond the level that fails to hold. Price closes back inside the range. The move was real, but it did not last.

Volume is supporting context. A high-volume breakout is more active, but it is not proof. Volume is covered in Volume and what it adds.

A breakout rule has an edge only if the rule, the costs and the number of trades all work together. That idea is covered in What a trading edge is. The book gives no failure rate for breakouts. Test your own rules in Lab 4 before you trust them.

Breakouts attract fear of missing out. That is the urge to buy because others seem to be buying. Define the rule before the candle closes. Do not chase a move that has already gone far beyond the level.

See it

Use the candle builder with the range drawn as a band. The band shows the top and the bottom of the range. Watch whether the close lands above the top edge or inside the band.

Then use predict-next. Guess the candle after a close beyond the range. The point is to see how little one close tells you about the next candle.

Worked example

Hypothetical. A 10-day range runs from Rs 300 to Rs 310.

DayCloseVolumeResult
11Rs 3121,80,000 shares (20-day average 1,00,000)Close above Rs 310: close-based breakout
12Rs 307Not used hereBack inside the range: false breakout

Relative volume on day 11 = 1,80,000 divided by 1,00,000 = 1.8. Check: 1.8 times the average is 1,80,000.

A hypothetical entry is at Rs 312. A hypothetical stop is at Rs 308. Risk per share = 312 minus 308 = Rs 4.

On day 12 the close is Rs 307. That is below the stop of Rs 308. Under the next-open fill rule used in Lab 4, the exit would happen at the open of day 13. The exit price is not known until that open is known. Check: 307 is below 308, so the close is through the stop.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. A range runs from Rs 500 to Rs 520. A day closes at Rs 522. The planned stop is Rs 516. The planned size is 40 shares. Work out the distance of the close beyond the range top, the stop distance from the entry, and the planned loss.

Hints:

  1. The breakout distance is measured from the range top. Compute 522 minus 520.
  2. The stop distance is the entry minus the stop. Compute 522 minus 516.
  3. The planned loss is the shares times the stop distance. Compute 40 times that distance.

Answer: The close is Rs 2 above the range top (522 minus 520). The stop is Rs 6 below the entry (522 minus 516). The planned loss is 40 x 6 = Rs 240. Check: 40 x (522 minus 516) = 40 x 6 = 240.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. A range runs from Rs 150 to Rs 158. A day closes at Rs 160 on 1.5 times the average volume. Entry Rs 160. Stop Rs 154. Size 30 shares. Compute the planned loss. State whether the close-based breakout rule was met.

Check your answer
  • Risk per share: 160 minus 154 = Rs 6.
  • Planned loss: 30 x 6 = Rs 180. Check: 30 x 6 = 180.
  • Close-based rule: the close of Rs 160 is above the range top of Rs 158. The rule is met.
  • The volume of 1.5 times average is context only. It does not prove the breakout.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks close versus touch, false breakouts, and the planned loss on a breakout trade.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Log it in your journal, or on paper if the journal is not ready.

  1. Choose 5 breakouts from chart history on any chart you are allowed to view.
  2. For each one, write the breakout definition you used before you look at what happened next.
  3. Record the close-based result after 5 days.
  4. Mark whether each breakout was a false breakout.
  5. Label the whole log “hindsight study, not evidence”.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A touch is not a breakout under a close-based rule. A trade above the top that closes inside the range has not broken out.
  • Volume is context, not proof. A high-volume false breakout is still a false breakout.
  • Chasing a move that has already moved far past the level adds risk. Your planned loss may grow far beyond the plan.
  • Circuit limits can stop trading beyond a price band, and that can affect a breakout (Circuit limits and halts). NSE sets daily bands of 2%, 5%, 10% or 20% for most stocks. Stocks with futures and options use a flexible 10% range instead. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE FAQs and SEBI’s master circular. Check the NSE site for changes.)
  • Work out the planned loss before you enter. Share count multiplies the risk per share (Position sizing step by step).

Remember

  • A breakout is a move beyond the range top. A breakdown is a move beyond the range bottom.
  • Define close-based or touch-based before the candle closes.
  • A false breakout closes back inside the range.
  • Volume supports the context. It does not prove the breakout.
  • Breakout rules must be tested, not assumed.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, breakout material (reference only)
  • NSE India, circuit-limit pages
  • SEBI circulars on price bands

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Band percentages come from NSE’s Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market. The flexible range for stocks with futures and options comes from SEBI’s master circular on trading. The full detail is in Circuit limits and halts.

Gaps and what they mean for stops

Hook

Hypothetical. A trader buys 100 shares at Rs 400, with a stop at Rs 390. The planned loss is Rs 1,000. The next morning the stock opens at Rs 380, below the stop. The stop does not get filled at Rs 390. The loss is about Rs 2,000, which is twice the plan. This chapter explains why the stop did not protect the planned amount.

The idea

A gap is an open that differs from the previous close. No trading happens between the two prices. The gap is the empty space on the chart between the old close and the new open.

Overnight news and events are common causes of gaps. This book does not predict gaps. It explains what a gap does to a stop.

A stop order is a trigger. When the market reaches the trigger price, the stop becomes a market order or a limit order. From that point, the fill depends on the market at that moment. A trigger price is not a guaranteed fill price.

If the open is already beyond the stop, the fill is near the open, not at the stop price. This is called a gap through stop. The stop did not limit the loss to the planned amount.

Planned risk is entry minus stop, times shares. Actual risk is entry minus the real fill, times shares. Gaps make the two different.

Two more points:

  • Circuit limits set how far a price may move in one session. A circuit limit can stop trading beyond a band, so an exit may not happen at all. NSE sets daily bands of 2%, 5%, 10% or 20% for most stocks. Stocks with futures and options use a flexible 10% range. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE FAQs and SEBI’s master circular.) Circuit limits are covered in Circuit limits and halts.
  • Size your position against a gap you can afford, not only against the stop distance. This is covered in Slippage.

Gap percent = (open minus previous close) divided by previous close, times 100. A negative gap percent means the open was below the previous close.

See it

The sl-trigger-sim shows the trigger, the open and the fill side by side. It runs in gap-through-stop mode.

Look at the trigger price first. Then look at the open. The fill sits near the open, not at the trigger. Compare the planned loss with the actual loss.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Previous close Rs 400. Entry Rs 400. Stop Rs 390. Size 100 shares. The next day opens at Rs 380.

StepCalculationResult
Planned loss100 x (400 minus 390)Rs 1,000
Gap percent(380 minus 400) divided by 400minus 5%
Fill (hypothetical)near the openabout Rs 380
Actual loss100 x (400 minus 380)Rs 2,000
Ratio2,000 divided by 1,0002 times the plan

The stop triggered, and the fill was near the open of Rs 380. The actual loss was twice the planned loss. All figures are illustrative.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. The same trade as the worked example: previous close Rs 400, entry Rs 400, stop Rs 390, 100 shares. This time the open is Rs 385. Compute the gap percent, the actual loss and the ratio of actual loss to planned loss.

Hints:

  1. The gap percent uses the previous close, which is Rs 400.
  2. The actual loss per share is the entry minus the fill. The fill is the open here.
  3. Divide the actual loss by the planned loss.

Answer: Gap percent = (385 minus 400) divided by 400 = minus 15 divided by 400 = minus 3.75%. Actual loss = 100 x (400 minus 385) = 100 x 15 = Rs 1,500. Planned loss = Rs 1,000. Ratio = 1,500 divided by 1,000 = 1.5.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Previous close Rs 250. Assume the entry is at Rs 250. Stop Rs 240. Size 50 shares. The open is Rs 228. Compute the planned loss, the actual loss and the ratio.

Check your answer
  • Planned loss: 50 x (250 minus 240) = 50 x 10 = Rs 500.
  • Actual loss: 50 x (250 minus 228) = 50 x 22 = Rs 1,100. Check: 50 x 20 = 1,000, and 50 x 2 = 100, which adds to 1,100.
  • Ratio: 1,100 divided by 500 = 2.2.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks gap through stop, the planned and actual loss, and sizing against a gap.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Log it in your journal, or on paper if the journal is not ready.

  1. Build a watchlist of stocks you are allowed to view.
  2. For one month, log every gap above 2 percent on the watchlist.
  3. For each gap, write the size and the direction.
  4. Write whether a stop at a distance you chose would have been passed.
  5. Make no outcome predictions. Record only what happened.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A stop is a trigger, not insurance. It does not promise the fill price.
  • A gap through stop can make the loss several times the planned loss. The planned loss is not a limit.
  • Gap percent is not rupees. A 5 percent gap on Rs 400 is Rs 20 per share. Keep the two measures apart.
  • Circuit limits can prevent an exit beyond a price band. Check the current rules before you rely on any exit (Circuit limits and halts).
  • Compare the planned loss with the largest gap you consider plausible. Keep that loss inside your risk budget (Slippage).

Remember

  • A gap is an open that differs from the previous close, with no trading between.
  • A stop becomes an order at its trigger. The fill depends on the market then.
  • In a gap through stop, the fill is near the open, not at the stop price.
  • Planned loss is not the same as actual loss when gaps occur.
  • Size against the gap you can afford, not only the stop distance.

Sources and verification

  • NSE India, market timings and circuit-limit pages
  • SEBI circulars on price bands
  • Zerodha support, stop-order articles

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • Band percentages come from NSE’s Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market and SEBI’s master circular on trading.
  • The cash market opens at 9:15 am after pre-open. It closes at 3:30 pm, or at 3:15 pm for stocks with futures and options. This comes from NSE circulars and Zerodha’s market timings page.
  • Zerodha explains that a stop-loss market (SL-M) order becomes a market order when the trigger is hit, so it fills at whatever price is available. A stop-loss (SL) order becomes a limit order, so if the price jumps past your limit price after the trigger, it can stay unfilled. A stop-loss order lasts only for the trading day. This comes from Zerodha’s support pages on stop-loss orders. Some order types are blocked for some products, so check Kite.

Volume and what it adds

Hook

A stock moves above a price level on a big day. The candle looks strong. Many traders say, “Big volume means the move is real.” Is that true? This chapter shows what volume measures. It also shows what volume cannot tell you. You will compare today’s volume with a normal day. You will also turn shares into rupees, so you can compare the trade with your planned size.

The idea

  • Volume is the number of shares traded in a period. The period can be a day, an hour or one candle. Volume is only a count. It does not say whether buyers or sellers were stronger.

  • Average volume is the usual amount of trading for the stock. A common choice is the last 20 days. Add up the volume for those days, then divide by 20.

  • Relative volume compares today with the usual amount.

    Relative volume = today’s volume divided by the average volume.

    A value of 2.0 means twice the usual volume. A value of 0.5 means half the usual volume.

  • High relative volume means more people traded than usual. It does not show which way they wanted the price to go. Every trade has a buyer and a seller. Volume alone does not say which side was stronger.

  • Traded value is the rupee amount that changed hands.

    Traded value = volume multiplied by price.

    Shares alone can hide the size of a trade. Rupees show it more clearly.

  • Liquidity means how easily you can buy or sell without moving the price much. You met this in Order matching, spread and liquidity. A low traded value can make it hard to sell a planned size. Selling is called exiting a trade.

  • Delivery volume is a different measure from traded volume. It refers to shares that were taken as delivery, not bought and sold again the same day. NSE publishes a report called “Security-wise Delivery Positions”, and NSE reads a higher share of delivered quantity as more buyers holding on to shares. Check NSE’s own definition before you use it. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE reports page. Definitions can change, so check the NSE site.)

  • Volume can rise for reasons that have nothing to do with the trend. News, index changes and corporate actions can all add volume. See Corporate actions.

See it

The bars show daily volume. The line shows relative volume. The data is made up. Find the breakout day and read its bar. Then read the line on that day. Change the lookback from 20 days to 10 days. Notice how the average changes and how the line moves.

Worked example

Hypothetical. The stock trades at Rs 312 on the breakout day.

StepCalculationResult
1. Average volume, last 20 daysgiven1,00,000 shares
2. Breakout day volumegiven1,80,000 shares
3. Relative volume1,80,000 divided by 1,00,0001.8
4. Traded value on the day1,80,000 multiplied by Rs 312Rs 5,61,60,000
5. Your planned 50 shares, as a share of the day50 divided by 1,80,000about 0.03%

Check step 4 another way: Rs 5,61,60,000 divided by Rs 312 is 1,80,000 shares. One crore is Rs 1,00,00,000. So the day traded about Rs 5.616 crore.

Check step 5 another way: 1,80,000 divided by 50 is 3,600. And 100 divided by 3,600 is about 0.028%, which rounds to 0.03%.

What it shows. The breakout day had 1.8 times the usual volume. Your planned 50 shares are a tiny part of that day. In this example, the stock is easy to trade. A thin stock can look very different.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Volumes for the last four days are 80,000, 120,000, 100,000 and 200,000 shares. Find the average of the first three days. Then find the relative volume on the last day.

Hints:

  1. Add the first three volumes. Then divide the total by 3.
  2. Divide the last day’s volume by that average.

Answer:

  • Total of the first three days: 80,000 + 120,000 + 100,000 = 3,00,000 shares.
  • Average of the first three days: 3,00,000 divided by 3 = 1,00,000 shares.
  • Relative volume on the last day: 2,00,000 divided by 1,00,000 = 2.0.
  • Check: 2.0 multiplied by 1,00,000 = 2,00,000. This matches the last day.

The last day had twice the usual volume. The numbers do not show which way the price moved.

Independent exercise

Task. A stock has an average volume of 2,50,000 shares. Its price falls 2 percent on a day with 90,000 shares traded. Work out the relative volume. Then write one sentence on what it shows and one sentence on what it does not show.

Check your answer
  • Relative volume: 90,000 divided by 2,50,000 = 0.36.
  • Check: 2,50,000 multiplied by 0.36 = 90,000.
  • What it shows: the day had much less trading than usual. Fewer people traded than on a normal day.
  • What it does not show: the volume does not tell you the direction of the price next. It also does not prove what caused the fall.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the relative volume formula, traded value in rupees, and what a high or low volume day does and does not show.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Pick 10 breakouts that you logged in Breakouts.
  2. For each breakout, write the average volume for the 20 days before it.
  3. Divide the breakout day’s volume by that average. Record the relative volume.
  4. Put the 10 results in one table. Compare them with the notes you wrote in Breakouts.
  5. Write that 10 samples are too few to draw a conclusion.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Volume does not show direction. A down day can have low volume, as the independent exercise shows. A down day can also have high volume.
  • A total volume figure means little on its own. Compare it with the same stock’s own average. A number that is large for one stock can be small for another.
  • Volume confirmation is a common idea. It is not shown to work in this chapter. Test a rule before you rely on it (Backtesting basics and the realism contract).
  • A thin stock can move on small volume. Small volume is a liquidity risk for your planned position.

Remember

  • Volume is the number of shares traded in a period.
  • Relative volume = today’s volume divided by the average volume.
  • High volume shows more trading. It does not show direction.
  • Traded value = volume multiplied by price. It shows the rupee size of the trading.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • NSE India, market data glossary
  • Zerodha Varsity, volume module (reference only, original wording here)

Facts to check:

Facts checked on 11 Nov 2026:

  • NSE lists a “Security-wise Delivery Positions” report on its reports page and reads a higher deliverable share as buyers holding on to shares. Read the exact NSE definition on the NSE site before you rely on it.
  • How Kite draws volume on charts was not checked here. Look at the volume setting on your own chart.

Candlestick formations and price action

Hook

A friend shows you two candles on a chart. The friend says, “This is a bearish engulfing pattern. The price will fall now.” The name sounds certain. This chapter shows what a candlestick pattern really is. It is a shape rule with a name. You will check two shape rules by hand. You will also learn why a name alone cannot show what the price does next.

The idea

  • A candlestick pattern is a shape rule. The rule says what the candles must look like. A name is only a label for that rule.
  • Authors do not always agree on the rule. The same name can mean different shapes in different books. Always write down the exact rule you use.
  • Bullish and bearish describe the candle’s direction. A green candle closes above its open. A red candle closes below its open.
  • Context means where the shape appears. Compare it with Support and resistance and Trend and market structure. The same shape can mean something different in a rising market than in a falling one.
  • Base rate is how often a shape is followed by the move its name suggests. This is an open research question. This book states no figure for any formation.
  • Price action means reading the price and its candles directly. It is still only a description. It becomes a method only after you test its rules.
  • Many shapes appear by chance. Naming a shape after the price has already moved is hindsight. Hindsight looks clear only when you look back.
  • Confirmation rules change the result. For example, you may wait for the next candle to close before you act. State the rule before you test it.

See it

Each pair shows two candles. Choose the formation, or choose none. Then check the bodies. Ask whether the second body covers the first body. Ask also whether the second candle has the colour the rule needs.

Worked example

Hypothetical. A bearish engulfing pattern. The rule used here has three checks. The second candle is red. Its body covers the body of the first candle. Its close is below the first candle’s open.

CandleOpenCloseColourBody
Day 1Rs 105Rs 110GreenRs 105 to Rs 110
Day 2Rs 111Rs 102RedRs 102 to Rs 111

Check the rule:

  • Day 2 body runs from Rs 102 to Rs 111. Day 1 body runs from Rs 105 to Rs 110. Day 2 covers Day 1 on both ends. The rule is met.
  • Day 2 closes at Rs 102. Day 1 opened at Rs 105. Rs 102 is below Rs 105. The rule is met.

Hypothetical. A hammer-shaped candle. One version of the rule says the lower shadow is at least twice the body, and the upper shadow is small. The shadow is the thin line above or below the body. Other authors use other rules.

StepCalculationResult
1. Bodyopen Rs 200, close Rs 199. 200 minus 199Rs 1
2. Lower shadowclose Rs 199 minus low Rs 192Rs 7
3. Lower shadow against body7 divided by 17 times the body
4. Upper shadowhigh Rs 201 minus open Rs 200Rs 1

Check step 3: 1 multiplied by 7 is 7. The lower shadow is at least twice the body, so this rule is met.

Lesson. Both shapes meet their rules. Neither shape shows what the price did next. These checks test the shape only.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Three pairs of candles are below. The values are hypothetical. For each pair, say whether the bearish or the bullish engulfing rule is met. Show the body comparison.

  • Pair A. Day 1: open Rs 100, close Rs 104, green. Day 2: open Rs 105, close Rs 98, red.
  • Pair B. Day 1: open Rs 100, close Rs 104, green. Day 2: open Rs 103, close Rs 107, green.
  • Pair C. Day 1: open Rs 110, close Rs 104, red. Day 2: open Rs 103, close Rs 112, green.

Hints:

  1. Compare the bodies, not the wicks.
  2. Check whether the second body covers the first body.
  3. Check the colour of the second candle. Red is needed for bearish. Green is needed for bullish.

Answer:

  • Pair A: the bearish engulfing rule is met. Day 1 body is Rs 100 to Rs 104. Day 2 body is Rs 98 to Rs 105. Day 2 covers Day 1, and Day 2 is red. Day 2 closes at Rs 98, which is below Rs 100.
  • Pair B: the rule is not met. Day 2 is green, so it cannot be bearish. Its body starts at Rs 103, which is above Rs 100. It does not cover the start of Day 1’s body. It closes at Rs 107, above Rs 100, so it is not bearish.
  • Pair C: the bullish engulfing rule is met. Day 1 body is Rs 104 to Rs 110. Day 2 body is Rs 103 to Rs 112. Day 2 covers Day 1, and Day 2 is green. Day 2 closes at Rs 112, which is above Rs 110.

Independent exercise

Task. Write your own definition of one formation in four lines or fewer. State the context you require. List three things you would need before you could test the rule.

Check your answer

A sample answer:

  • Definition: a bearish engulfing pattern is a red candle whose body covers the body of the green candle before it.
  • Context: the shape appears after a rise, near a level you have already marked.
  • Needed before testing: an exact definition of the context. A fixed test period. A fixed rule for when to record the outcome.

Any answer is acceptable when the rule is exact, the context is stated and the test steps are written before any outcome is seen.

Quiz

The quiz checks engulfing and hammer rules, the limits of a pattern name, and what a test needs.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Choose one formation. Write its definition in four lines or fewer.
  2. Write the context you require, such as the trend before the shape.
  3. Find 10 occurrences on charts you have seen. Note each date, the candles and the context.
  4. Record the outcome only after the number of days you set before you began. Do not change that number later.
  5. Record the results as learning notes in Lab 3. Do not treat any result as a trading signal.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A named pattern is not a promise. The book makes no claim that any formation predicts profit.
  • A pattern named after the move is hindsight. The shape can look clear now. It was not clear when the price was moving.
  • A rule with no stated context and no fixed test period cannot be checked.
  • Ten or twenty examples are too few to show that a formation works or fails.
  • Changing the rule after you see the result makes the test unfair to the rule.

Remember

  • A candlestick pattern is a shape rule. Write the exact rule before you use it.
  • Context and confirmation rules change what a shape means.
  • A name does not show what the price does next.
  • Test a rule before you trust it (Backtesting basics and the realism contract).

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • Zerodha Varsity, candlestick patterns module (reference only, original wording here)
  • Peer-reviewed studies of candlestick patterns. No results from these studies are stated in this chapter.

Facts to check:

  • Any base rate for a named formation, if a study is cited later. No base rate is stated here, because no study has been checked for this book.

Chart-reading assessment

Hook

A zone on a chart runs from Rs 249 to Rs 252. You plan to buy at Rs 252 and place a stop at Rs 247. You have Rs 50,000 and you risk 1 percent. Your planned loss looks small. Then the market opens at Rs 240, below your stop. The loss is bigger than the plan. This assessment checks whether you can work through numbers like these. It also checks whether you can read a chart you have not seen before.

The idea

This assessment has three parts.

  1. Calculation. You read open, high, low and close values. You work out the body, the shadow and the range. You turn a stop into a number of shares and a planned loss.
  2. Identification. You look at a chart you have not seen. You name the structure, any breakout and any gap.
  3. Scenario. You work through one trade with a stop and a gap.

Some terms used in the items:

  • Range is the high minus the low.
  • Body is the gap between the open and the close.
  • Shadow is the thin line above or below the body.
  • Gap is a jump in price between one trade and the next. The price opens away from the last close.
  • Risk per share is the entry price minus the stop price, for a buy.

Feedback follows every answer. Each wrong answer shows the rule that applies.

The pass mark is 80 percent for this chapter. With 6 questions, that means at least 5 correct. You can retake the assessment as often as you like. There is no timer.

The result screen says the same thing each time. Passing this chapter does not show that any chart method works. It shows only that you can read the chart items correctly.

See it

For the identification items, look at the chart first. Mark the highs and lows. Then name the structure. Check the gap, if any, before you answer. Do not look at the answer until you have written yours.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Zone item. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1 percent. Zone Rs 249 to Rs 252. Entry Rs 252. Stop Rs 247. The stop sits below the zone.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget50,000 multiplied by 1%Rs 500
2. Risk per share252 minus 247Rs 5
3. Shares allowed by the risk budget500 divided by 5100
4. Shares allowed by cash50,000 divided by 252, rounded down198
5. Shares allowed by the 25 percent cap12,500 divided by 252, rounded down49
6. Sharesthe smallest of 100, 198 and 4949
7. Planned loss49 multiplied by Rs 5Rs 245

The 25 percent cap comes from Position sizing step by step. The concentration cap is the binding limit here.

Gap case. The market opens at Rs 240. You leave at the open price, which is Rs 240. The loss per share is 252 minus 240, which is Rs 12. Actual loss = 49 multiplied by Rs 12 = Rs 588.

Check the gap loss another way: 50 multiplied by 12 is 600. Take away 1 share at Rs 12 and you get Rs 588.

Lesson. The planned loss was Rs 245. The gap made the loss Rs 588. That is more than the Rs 500 risk budget. A stop is a trigger, not a guarantee of the price you get.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1 percent. Zone Rs 500 to Rs 506. Entry Rs 506. Stop Rs 497. Work out the planned shares and the planned loss.

Hints:

  1. Identify the zone. Check that the stop sits outside it, and write the reason for the stop.
  2. Risk per share = entry minus stop.
  3. Take the smallest of the three share limits. Round each one down.

Answer:

  • Zone: Rs 500 to Rs 506. The stop at Rs 497 is below the zone.
  • Risk per share: 506 minus 497 = Rs 9.
  • Risk budget: Rs 500. Shares allowed: 500 divided by 9, rounded down, is 55.
  • Cash: 50,000 divided by 506, rounded down, is 98.
  • 25 percent cap: 12,500 divided by 506, rounded down, is 24.
  • Shares: the smallest is 24.
  • Planned loss: 24 multiplied by Rs 9 = Rs 216.
  • Check: 20 multiplied by 9 is 180. 4 multiplied by 9 is 36. 180 plus 36 is 216.

Independent exercise

Task. Work through this item with no hints. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1 percent. Zone Rs 600 to Rs 604. Entry Rs 604. Stop Rs 597. Find the planned shares, the planned loss and the loss if the market opens at Rs 590.

Check your answer
  • Risk per share: 604 minus 597 = Rs 7.
  • Risk budget: Rs 500. Shares allowed: 500 divided by 7, rounded down, is 71.
  • Cash: 50,000 divided by 604, rounded down, is 82.
  • 25 percent cap: 12,500 divided by 604, rounded down, is 20.
  • Shares: the smallest is 20.
  • Planned loss: 20 multiplied by Rs 7 = Rs 140.
  • Gap loss: risk per share at the open is 604 minus 590 = Rs 14. Loss = 20 multiplied by Rs 14 = Rs 280.
  • Check: 20 multiplied by 14 = 280. Rs 280 is below the Rs 500 risk budget.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz covers the calculation, the gap scenario, the stop reason, and the limits of a passing score.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Choose 20 charts you can find. Write your labels for each one before you read any answer key. The labels are structure, breakouts and gaps.
  2. Compare your labels with the answer key.
  3. Record each difference. Write the rule that explains it.
  4. Record the results as learning notes in Lab 3.
  5. Write one paragraph on what your results do and do not show about chart reading.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A passing score does not show that any chart method works. It shows only that you read the items correctly.
  • A stop is a trigger. A gap can open the price beyond your stop, so the loss can be bigger than planned.
  • Using the entry price instead of the stop price for risk per share gives the wrong loss. The stop sets the risk per share.
  • A stop placed inside a zone needs a stated reason. Without one, the stop is a guess.
  • Fast answers on sample items can feel like skill. Check your reasons, not only your score.

Remember

  • Risk per share = entry minus stop, for a buy.
  • Shares = the smallest of the risk limit, the cash limit and the cap. Round each one down.
  • A gap can make the actual loss bigger than the planned loss.
  • A passing score is not proof that any chart method works.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • The sources listed for the earlier chart-reading chapters on candles, timeframes, support and resistance, trend, breakouts, gaps, volume and formations.

Facts to check:

  • None of the time-sensitive facts apply to this assessment.

  9. A complete demo walkthrough
