Kite web and mobile tour

Hook

A trading app shows many buttons, numbers and labels. Some of them look urgent. One wrong tap in an order window can send a real order. This chapter gives you a map of the main areas. You will learn what the demo is and what it is not. You will not place any order in this chapter.

The idea

Kite is the trading platform from Zerodha. It runs on the web and on mobile. This book does not depend on either version. The labels in your own app may differ from the labels here. Check your app.

The main areas are these. Look for each name in your own app.

  • User ID menu. Click your user ID at the top right. Look for My profile, Settings and the tour.
  • Marketwatch. Your lists of symbols you follow. On the web you can keep up to 7 lists, numbered 1 to 7.
  • Chart. A picture of past prices for one symbol.
  • Order window. Opens when you click Buy or Sell on a row. Reading it does not place an order. On the phone app you place an order by swiping a button, not by tapping it.
  • Top bar (web). Look for Dashboard, Orders, Holdings, Positions, Funds, Bids and Apps. These pages show your orders, the shares you hold, your open trades and your money. The exact order of the names may differ in your app.
  • Terminal Mode (web). A layout you can arrange yourself, with your own set of windows.

Console is a separate back-office website, at console.zerodha.com. A back office keeps records, reports and statements. Console holds items such as contract notes and ledgers. It is not where you look at live prices or enter trades.

The demo is a practice version of Kite that uses dummy data. Dummy data is made-up data, not real market data. Paper trading means practising with pretend money and keeping a written record of your decisions. Zerodha says it does not offer demo accounts for paper trading. It does offer a demo version of Kite with dummy data. You can reach it at kite-demo.zerodha.com/dashboard, or with the Try demo button on zerodha.com/products/kite.

The demo is view-only. You can open every screen and click through them to read them. You cannot place orders, create GTTs or set alerts in it. Real orders need a funded account. This book never asks you to place a real order.

If you share a screenshot of a real account, crop out names, client IDs and balances first. Private details are easy to miss.

See it

Use the toggle to switch between the web and mobile versions. Find the same areas in both. Note any area that has a different label. The screens are recreations. Check your own Kite for the current layout.

Worked example

Hypothetical illustration. Suppose you enter a buy order for 10 shares at Rs 250 in the order window. You do not submit it. The order value is what you read on the screen before charges.

StepCalculationResult
1. Sharesgiven10
2. PricegivenRs 250
3. Order value before charges10 multiplied by Rs 250Rs 2,500
4. Chargesshown separatelyLook for them near the margin figures in the order window

Check step 3: Rs 2,500 divided by 10 shares is Rs 250 a share.

Charges are not in the Rs 2,500. The demo is view-only, so you cannot open an order window there. In a live account, the order window shows the margin figures and the charges near them. Read them before you submit anything.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. In your own Kite demo, find each named area from the tour. Write down the label your app uses for each one.

Hints:

  1. Use the app’s own search if you cannot find an area.
  2. If a label differs from this chapter, write both labels and check Zerodha support.
  3. Do not place any order in this exercise.

Answer: There is no single correct list of labels. Your answer is complete when each area has a label from your own app. Any difference from this chapter is written next to the label. No order is placed.

Independent exercise

Task. Draw a one-page map of your Kite layout. Use labels from your own app. Do not use screenshots of your real account.

Check your answer

A good map has these parts:

  • The user ID menu, Marketwatch, the chart, the order window, and the orders, holdings, positions and funds pages.
  • Console, shown as a separate website.
  • A note that the demo uses dummy data.
  • No names, client IDs or balances.

If your map shows a name, a client ID or a balance, remove it before you keep or share the map.

Quiz

The quiz checks the demo, the role of Console, the order value and the rule on sharing screenshots.

Paper task

Mode: kite demo. You will read screens only. Do not place any order.

  1. Complete the tour checklist: user ID menu, Marketwatch, chart, order window, orders, holdings, positions, funds and Console.
  2. Write the label your app uses for each area.
  3. Write a short reflection on what you did not understand.
  4. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The tour places no orders. It also does not teach you how to choose a trade.
  • Being familiar with the demo screens is not evidence of trading skill.
  • Labels, screens and rules change. Check your own app before you rely on this map.
  • Never share a screenshot before you remove names, client IDs and balances.

Remember

  • Kite is the trading platform on web and mobile. This book does not depend on either.
  • The demo uses dummy data. It is for practice with the screens only.
  • Console is a back-office area for reports and records. It is not for live prices.
  • The order value shown in the order window is before charges.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • Zerodha support: “Does Zerodha offer demo accounts for paper trading?” (support.zerodha.com)
  • The Kite user manual (kite.trade/docs/kite)
  • Zerodha support pages on Console

Facts to check:

  • Current Kite and mobile labels. Check your own app.
  • What Console holds. Check Zerodha’s Console help.

Watchlists, search and price alerts

Hook

A stock rises sharply during the day. You add it to a list. You then set an alert at a higher price. The list and the alert can feel like a plan. They are not a plan. This chapter shows what a watchlist and an alert do. It also shows what they do not do.

The idea

  • A watchlist is a list of symbols you keep in view. Its job is to hold your attention. Write your rules before you add a symbol. Useful rules include liquidity, sector and data availability. You met liquidity in Order matching, spread and liquidity.
  • A watchlist is not a list of recommendations. Being on it says nothing about whether a trade is right.
  • On the Kite web, your watchlist is called Marketwatch. You can keep up to 7 lists, numbered 1 to 7. Each list can hold up to 50 symbols. Kite saves your lists on its own.
  • To add a symbol on the web, press ~ (the tilde key) to open search. Type a name or code. Click Add next to the result you want. Hover over a row to see its buttons: Buy, Sell, Chart, Market depth, Delete and More. Look for the exact icons in your app. The More menu holds options such as Create GTT, Create alert and Add to basket.
  • Symbol search finds a stock by its code or name. Similar names and codes exist. Check the exchange and the symbol before you act.
  • The same company can have separate listings on NSE and BSE. Kite shows the exchange next to each row. Check which exchange your symbol belongs to.
  • An alert is a notification. It tells you that a price has reached a level you chose. A plain alert only notifies you. Kite also has ATO (Alert Triggers Order). With ATO, an alert can place a basket of up to 20 orders on its own when it fires. This book does not use alerts to trade. Treat an alert as a reminder to review.
  • To set an alert on the web, hover over the row, click More, then Create alert. Enter a name, choose the property, the operator and the value, then click Create. On the phone app, open the stock and tap Set alert. Kite limits how many alerts and ATO alerts you can have, and an alert lasts 365 days.
  • Alerts create urgency. Use them as review prompts, not as triggers to act. Your stop and your size should be decided before any alert fires.

See it

Look at the watchlist first. Then look at the search result. Find the exchange label next to each symbol. Then look at the alert dialog. Notice the words for the alert, and check whether an ATO option is switched on. Check the layout in your own Kite.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Suppose you follow 10 symbols. Each one trades at Rs 250. Buying one share of each would cost Rs 2,500 before charges.

StepCalculationResult
1. Symbolsgiven10
2. Price of eachgivenRs 250
3. One share of each, before charges10 multiplied by Rs 250Rs 2,500

Check step 3: Rs 2,500 divided by 10 symbols is Rs 250 each.

The watchlist itself costs nothing. Your attention is the real cost.

Now suppose you set an alert at Rs 252. That alert is a prompt to review the chart. Your stop and your size must already be written down. They must not be chosen because the alert fired.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Build a five-symbol watchlist on paper. Write one rule for each symbol. In the Kite demo, find where the alert button sits. Then write one alert as a sentence on paper. Write down what you will do when it fires.

Hints:

  1. Write the rule for each symbol before you add it.
  2. Check the exchange for each symbol.
  3. For the alert, write a review step. For example: look at the chart, check your stop and size, and do nothing more until your plan allows it.

Answer: Your answer is complete when each symbol has a written rule, each exchange has been checked, and the alert note says what you will review first. No order is placed in this exercise.

Independent exercise

Task. Write four selection rules for one hypothetical stock. Then write an alert plan for the same stock. The plan must say what the alert is for and what you will do before any order.

Check your answer

A sample answer, for a hypothetical stock:

  • Rule 1: the stock trades enough shares each day that a planned size can be sold.
  • Rule 2: the stock belongs to a sector that you have already studied.
  • Rule 3: the data needed for your chart is available from your source.
  • Rule 4: the stock is not on the list only because it rose sharply today.
  • Alert plan: the alert is for a review of the chart at a set level. Before any order, you check the stop, the size, the cost and your written plan. An alert does not decide anything by itself.

All details are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the difference between an alert and an order, what a watchlist is, and a common mistake after an alert.

Paper task

Mode: paper. The Kite demo is view-only. Use it only to find the alert button and read its labels. Write the list and each alert on paper.

  1. Write the rules for your watchlist on paper.
  2. Check the exchange for each symbol on the list.
  3. Write each alert as a sentence on paper, with its purpose.
  4. Write the review steps you will take before any order.
  5. Record this as a learning note in Lab 3. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A watchlist is not a buy list. Being on it does not make a trade right.
  • A stock that rose most today is not a reason to act. Check your written rules first.
  • A plain alert is not an order. Seeing it does not change your plan. But an alert with ATO switched on can place orders. Check the alert settings before you set one.
  • Alerts and lists can drive fear of missing out. A rising price on a list can feel urgent. Slow down and check your rules.

Remember

  • A watchlist controls your attention. It is not a recommendation list.
  • Check the exchange and the symbol before you act.
  • A plain alert is a notification. An ATO alert can also place orders. Either way, check your plan first.
  • Write your stop and size before any alert fires.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • Zerodha support pages on watchlists, alerts and ATO (Alert Triggers Order). Check the current limits there.
  • NSE and BSE symbol directories

Facts to check:

  • Alert types, notification channels, and the ATO rules, including limits on alerts and orders. Check Zerodha’s alert pages.
  • Symbol and series conventions on the NSE and BSE symbol pages.

Market depth and charts in Kite

Hook

A stock shows a price of Rs 250 on the screen. You plan to buy 100 shares at the market price. The price you pay can be a little higher than Rs 250. The reason is the order book, the list of buy and sell prices waiting at this moment. This chapter shows how Kite shows that list. It also shows how to set up a daily chart.

The idea

  • Market depth is a view of the buy and sell prices waiting in the order book. On Kite it shows the best 5 bids and the best 5 offers by default. A “View 20 depth” option shows up to 20 on each side for NSE stocks.

  • Best bid is the highest price a buyer is waiting to pay.

  • Best ask is the lowest price a seller is waiting to take. Kite calls this the Offer, not the ask.

  • Spread is the gap between them.

    Spread = best ask minus best bid.

  • Mid-price is the point halfway between the best bid and the best ask.

    Mid-price = (best bid plus best ask) divided by 2.

  • Crossing the spread means buying at the ask or selling at the bid. A market buy takes the ask. You pay about half the spread per share more than the mid-price.

  • On Kite’s depth view, each price shows the quantity waiting there (Qty) and the number of orders at that price (Orders). The view also shows the day’s Open, High, Low and Previous Close, the Volume, the Avg. Price (the average price traded so far today), the last traded quantity and time, the circuit limits (the lowest and highest price the stock may trade at today) and, for F&O, the open interest (OI).

  • To open the depth view on the web, go to Marketwatch, hover over a row and click Market depth. You can also select the row and press D. Clicking a price in the depth view fills in an order at that price.

  • Depth is a snapshot. Orders can be placed and cancelled at any time, so depth changes quickly. Some orders are hidden. An iceberg order shows only a small part of its full size. You cannot see the rest. On Kite, an Iceberg is also an order type you can place yourself. Kite sends it in smaller legs, and each leg goes to the exchange only after the one before it fills.

  • Chart setup means the timeframe, the chart type and any indicators you add. You learned about timeframes in Timeframes and how the same price looks different. Kite offers two chart engines, ChartIQ (the default) and TradingView. You can change the default in your profile settings at kite.zerodha.com/profile. Indicators are called studies. Kite lists over 100 studies and over 30 drawing tools. You can also place orders from the chart (Trade from charts).

  • Depth matters less for end-of-day decisions. It shows one moment. Liquidity decides whether a planned size can be sold. See Order matching, spread and liquidity.

See it

Watch the bids and asks change as orders arrive. Find the spread in each moment. Then look at the depth panel in the Kite illustration. Note which settings you can change on the chart. Check the layout in your own Kite.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Best bid Rs 249.90. Best ask Rs 250.10.

ItemCalculationResult
Spread250.10 minus 249.90Rs 0.20
Mid-price(249.90 plus 250.10) divided by 2Rs 250.00
Cost above mid for 100 shares(250.10 minus 250.00) multiplied by 100Rs 10

Check the cost above mid another way: 100 shares at Rs 250.10 cost Rs 25,010. 100 shares at Rs 250.00 cost Rs 25,000. The difference is Rs 10.

The buy costs Rs 10 more than the mid-price. This is not a fee from the broker. It comes from the gap between the bid and the ask.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. This is a hypothetical depth table.

SidePriceQuantity
BidRs 180.40400
BidRs 180.35600
AskRs 180.60300
AskRs 180.65500

Find the best bid, the best ask, the spread and the mid-price. Then work out the cost above mid for a market buy of 200 shares.

Hints:

  1. The best bid is the highest price on the buy side.
  2. A market buy takes the lowest ask first. The ask quantity of 300 is enough for 200 shares.
  3. Cost above mid = (ask minus mid) multiplied by quantity.

Answer:

  • Best bid: Rs 180.40.
  • Best ask: Rs 180.60.
  • Spread: 180.60 minus 180.40 = Rs 0.20.
  • Mid-price: (180.40 plus 180.60) divided by 2 = Rs 180.50.
  • Cost above mid for 200 shares: (180.60 minus 180.50) multiplied by 200 = Rs 20.
  • Check: 200 shares at Rs 180.60 cost Rs 36,120. 200 shares at Rs 180.50 cost Rs 36,100. The difference is Rs 20.

Independent exercise

Task. The spread is Rs 0.50 on a stock priced at Rs 400. You buy 200 shares at the ask. Work out the spread as a percent of the price. Then work out the cost above mid.

Check your answer
  • Spread as a percent of price: 0.50 divided by 400 = 0.00125. Multiply by 100 to get 0.125 percent.
  • Check: 400 multiplied by 0.125 percent = 400 multiplied by 0.00125 = Rs 0.50.
  • Cost above mid: half the spread is Rs 0.25 a share. Multiply by 200 shares to get Rs 50.
  • Check: 200 shares at Rs 400.25 cost Rs 80,050. 200 shares at Rs 400.00 cost Rs 80,000. The difference is Rs 50.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the spread, the mid-price, the cost above mid, and what depth can and cannot show.

Paper task

Mode: kite demo. Watch the screens only. Do not place any order.

  1. Choose one symbol. Record its spread at three times on one trading day.
  2. Write the chart settings you used, such as the timeframe and any indicators.
  3. Check the market session times on NSE’s own website first.
  4. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Depth can mislead. A large bid can disappear within seconds.
  • Depth is not a basis for end-of-day decisions. It shows one moment only.
  • Liquidity decides whether a planned size can be sold. A tight spread does not replace a check of liquidity.
  • The spread is not a fee from the broker. It is the gap between the best bid and the best ask. Its cost is paid when you cross it.
  • Colours can change. Zerodha’s older guide used blue for bids and red for offers. Look at the colours in your own app.

Remember

  • Spread = best ask minus best bid.
  • Mid-price = (best bid plus best ask) divided by 2.
  • Buying at the ask costs about half the spread per share above the mid-price.
  • Depth changes. Hidden orders are not visible.

Sources and verification

Sources:

  • NSE India, market depth and order book pages
  • Zerodha support pages on market depth, charts and iceberg orders. Check the current options there.

Facts to check:

  • How many depth levels Kite shows, and the “View 20 depth” option. Check Kite’s market depth help.
  • Chart intervals and how much history Kite holds. Check Kite’s chart help.
  • Available chart settings. Check the chart in your own Kite.
  • Session times for the paper task. Check NSE’s website.

Order types

Hook

You hold 50 shares bought at Rs 250. You want a stop-loss that sells if the price falls to Rs 240, and you want that stop to stay in place overnight. The next morning the price opens at Rs 232, below your trigger. Will your stop sell? The answer depends on the order type you chose, and on how long Kite keeps that order alive. This chapter explains four order types: market, limit, SL and SL-M.

The idea

An order is an instruction to buy or sell. Some orders have a condition. A condition is a price rule that must be met first. Each order type handles price in its own way.

Market order. You buy or sell at the best price available now. You do not set a price. You get speed, but you do not get a price guarantee. In a fast move, the price you get can be far from the last price you saw.

Limit order. You set the worst price you accept. A buy limit fills at the limit price or lower. A sell limit fills at the limit price or higher. If the price never reaches your limit, the order may never fill.

Trigger price. Some orders wait for a trigger price. The trigger is the price that activates the order. Until the price reaches the trigger, nothing happens.

SL (stop-loss limit). An SL order has two prices: a trigger and a limit. When the trigger is reached, the order becomes a limit order at the limit price. It fills only at the limit price or better.

SL-M (stop-loss market). An SL-M order has a trigger only. When the trigger is reached, the order becomes a market order. It sells at the best price available then. That fill can be well away from the trigger.

“Long” means you own the shares and want the price to rise. For a long holding, a stop-loss sell has its trigger below your entry price. Its limit is at or below the trigger.

A gap is a jump in price with no trading in between. Gaps often happen at the market open. A gap can make a stop act differently from what you expect. See Gaps and what they mean for stops.

Order typeWhat you setWhen it becomes activePrice you may getMain risk
MarketNothingNowBest available nowThe price can be far from the last price you saw
LimitLimit priceNowLimit price or betterIt may never fill
SLTrigger and limitWhen the price reaches the triggerLimit price or betterIt may not fill in a gap
SL-MTriggerWhen the price reaches the triggerBest available thenThe fill can be far from the trigger

Read the SL row again. In a gap, the SL order may stay open while the price keeps falling. You still hold the shares.

Validity is how long an order stays live. Kite offers Day (the default), Immediate or Cancel (IOC, which fills what it can at once and cancels the rest) and Minutes (from 1 to 120 minutes). A normal SL or SL-M order is valid for one trading day only. If it has not triggered by the end of the session, it expires. For a stop that must last longer, use a GTT (good till triggered). See GTT orders and their limits.

Market protection. On the web, click Advanced, then tick Market protection. A market order then fills only within a set range of the last price. Any part that cannot fill in that range becomes a limit order at the protection price, and it stays open.

Blocked orders. Kite blocks market orders for stock options and for some index option contracts. It blocks SL-M for index options and for all BSE instruments. On BSE, Zerodha suggests SL instead.

Other order varieties (Kite’s word for the kinds of order you can place):

  • Cover (CO). A market or limit entry with a built-in stop-loss, for intraday trades on NSE equity only. Once the first part fills, the second part cannot be cancelled.
  • Iceberg. A large order that Kite sends to the exchange in smaller legs. See Market depth and charts in Kite.
  • Disclosed quantity. For equity orders only. Only part of the order is shown to the market. Where it sits in the order window can change, so look for it in your own app.
  • AMO (After Market Order). An order placed after market hours. Kite sends it to the exchange when the market opens. Until then, the order book shows AMO REQ RECEIVED. That is not yet an exchange order. AMO is not allowed for Cover or Iceberg orders. Zerodha does not guarantee that pre-market or AMO orders will execute.

An order travels in steps. It goes from you to your broker. The broker sends it to the exchange. The exchange matches it with an opposite order. The visual below shows this path.

See it

Start in normal mode. Compare what the SL order does with what the SL-M order does. Then switch to gap mode and compare again. Note the fill price in each case. The order flow panel shows the path of one order.

Worked example

Hypothetical. You are long 50 shares bought at Rs 250. Your stop has a trigger at Rs 240 and a limit at Rs 238. It must still be live the next morning. A normal SL would have expired at the end of the day, so this stop is a GTT.

Planned loss at the trigger = 50 x (250 - 240) = 50 x 10 = Rs 500.

ScenarioWhat happensFillLoss
A. Orderly fallThe price falls through Rs 240, then Rs 238Rs 23850 x (250 - 238) = 50 x 12 = Rs 600
B. Gap to Rs 232, with a limit stop (SL, or a GTT with a limit price)The trigger is passed. A sell at Rs 238 or better cannot fill at Rs 232No fill. The order stays openNot sold yet. The loss is still open and can grow
C. Gap to Rs 232, with a market stop (SL-M, or a GTT set to Market)The trigger is passed. The order sells at the best price thenAbout Rs 23250 x (250 - 232) = 50 x 18 = Rs 900

Scenario A loses Rs 100 more than planned (Rs 600 minus Rs 500). Scenario C loses Rs 400 more than planned (Rs 900 minus Rs 500). In scenario B, no sale has happened, so the loss stays open.

On Kite, a market stop can carry market protection. If it does, part of the order can stay open at the protection price instead of filling at Rs 232.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. You are long 50 shares bought at Rs 250. Your SL trigger is Rs 240 and your limit is Rs 236. The price opens at Rs 235. Decide whether the SL order fills. Then work out what an SL-M order would do.

Hints:

  1. A sell limit fills only at its price or higher.
  2. An opening price below the limit does not fill the order.
  3. An SL-M order fills near the opening price.

Answer:

  • The SL does not fill. The price is Rs 235, which is below the limit of Rs 236.
  • The SL-M fills near Rs 235.
  • SL-M loss: 50 x (250 - 235) = 50 x 15 = Rs 750.

Independent exercise

Task. You are long 100 shares bought at Rs 300. The SL trigger is Rs 290 and the limit is Rs 287. The price opens at Rs 284. Work out the planned loss at the trigger, the SL-M loss, and whether the SL fills.

Check your answer
  • Planned loss at the trigger: 100 x (300 - 290) = 100 x 10 = Rs 1,000.
  • SL-M loss: 100 x (300 - 284) = 100 x 16 = Rs 1,600.
  • SL fill: no. The open at Rs 284 is below the limit of Rs 287.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the difference between SL and SL-M, and what a gap does to a stop.

Paper task

Mode: paper for the written work. Use kite_demo only to read the screen. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Open the Kite demo and find the order window. Look for the label your own app uses for the order type.
  2. Write on paper the trigger and the limit you plan to use.
  3. Write the SL order’s trigger and limit prices on paper. Do not place any real order.
  4. Write the field names you saw and what the order view showed.
  5. Write one sentence: what would this order do in a gap?

Limits and common mistakes

  • A stop does not guarantee a sale at the trigger. An SL order can stay open after the price passes it.
  • SL-M is not the same as SL. SL-M sells at the best price available once triggered. SL sells only at its limit price or better.
  • A stop does not cap your loss. A gap can make the loss bigger than planned (Slippage).
  • A normal SL or SL-M order lasts one trading day only. A stop that must last longer needs a GTT.
  • SL-M is blocked for index options and BSE instruments. Check the current rules before you rely on either order.
  • Do not buy more shares of a losing holding to offset a stop problem. That is averaging down, and this book forbids it.

Remember

  • A market order fills now at the best price available. It gives no price guarantee.
  • A limit order sets the worst price you accept. It may never fill.
  • SL uses a trigger and a limit. SL-M uses a trigger only.
  • For a long holding, a stop-loss sell has its limit at or below its trigger.
  • In a gap, an SL may not fill, and an SL-M may fill far from the trigger.
  • A normal SL or SL-M order lasts one trading day only. Use a GTT for a longer stop.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support articles on stop-loss orders, market protection and order validity. Zerodha changes these rules, so confirm them on its support pages before you rely on them.
  • NSE India, order types and trading rules

Product types

Hook

Two traders buy 10 shares at Rs 250. Both plan to hold for three weeks. One of them picks the product type meant for a same-day trade. A product type is one setting on the order screen. It tells the broker how long the trade is meant to last. This chapter shows how to read that setting before you submit an order.

The idea

A product type tells the broker how long a trade is meant to last. It also tells the broker how the trade is settled. Settlement is the step where shares and money change hands after a trade. See Demat accounts and settlement.

Kite names its products like this. Look for these names, or the same choices, in your own app.

CNC (Longterm, delivery). CNC stands for cash and carry. You pay the full price for the shares. There is no leverage, which means no borrowed money. Kite does not close a CNC holding for you. A CNC buy shows in Positions on the day you buy. From the next trading day it shows in Holdings. A CNC holding can stay overnight, for weeks or longer. If you buy and sell the same share on the same day under CNC, Zerodha treats it as an intraday trade for brokerage.

MIS (Intraday, margin). MIS stands for margin intraday square-off. The position is meant to close within the same trading day. It uses margin, which is borrowed money that lets you control more shares than your cash alone would buy. That is leverage. See Leverage and margin. This book explains MIS as theory only. It does not teach MIS practice.

NRML (Overnight). NRML is used for futures and options (F&O). You can hold these positions until expiry. There is no intraday leverage.

MTF (Margin Trading Facility). MTF is a separate product where Zerodha funds part of a share purchase. Kite lists it, but this book does not teach it.

Cover (CO) is an order type, not a product. It is covered in Order types.

On the phone app, the product is a tick box named Intraday. Tick it for MIS. Untick it for delivery (CNC).

Auto square-off. Zerodha closes open MIS positions at a set time, without any action from you. Kite’s support page gives these times:

  • Stocks in the closing auction session (CAS): 3:12 PM.
  • Other equity: 3:25 PM.
  • F&O: 3:26 PM.
  • MCX commodities: 10 minutes before the close.

These times changed on 26 December 2025. They can change at the risk team’s discretion. Zerodha charges a fee for each order it squares off. At last check it was Rs 50 plus 18% GST. Zerodha can change this, so check the Zerodha support pages before you rely on it. After the cut-off you cannot place a new MIS order. You also cannot convert a CNC or NRML position to MIS. Zerodha does not have to close your intraday position for you, so you must plan to close it yourself.

Convert. In Positions, open the options menu for a position and choose Convert. You can change MIS to NRML or CNC, and back. Cover orders cannot be converted. You can convert only before the auto square-off time.

Margin and leverage. Zerodha’s support article has said that equity MIS allows up to 5 times leverage, which means 20 percent margin. The risk team can reduce or remove leverage during the day. Zerodha can change this. Check the Zerodha support pages before you rely on it.

Charges. The product type can change the charges. Check the cost preview for the product you chose. Do not assume one product is cheaper.

Why this book uses CNC. The method in this book looks at prices at the end of each day. It holds trades for days or weeks. That style uses CNC. Intraday trading is a higher-risk style. It is not a shortcut to more money.

Worked example

Hypothetical. You buy 10 shares at Rs 250.

StepCalculationResult
Order value10 x 250Rs 2,500 before charges
Adverse move1% of Rs 2,500Rs 25 before charges

An adverse move means the price moves against you. A 1% fall on the order costs Rs 25 before charges.

  • CNC: the holding can continue overnight.
  • MIS: the position is meant to close the same day. If it stays open, Zerodha’s auto square-off applies at the time shown above.

See it

Look at the product selector and the list of consequences beside it. Change the product and see which consequences change. Note which product matches a holding that lasts for weeks.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Classify three planned trades. Choose a product for each one and write the reason.

  • (a) Hold for weeks.
  • (b) Close today.
  • (c) Carry overnight if the stop is not hit.

Hints:

  1. The holding period decides the product.
  2. If the plan says “carry overnight”, MIS is wrong.
  3. Write one reason for each choice.

Answer:

  • (a) CNC. The plan holds for weeks.
  • (b) This is an intraday plan. The matching product is MIS. This book does not practise intraday trading, so write “not for this book” as the reason and do not plan it here.
  • (c) CNC. The plan carries overnight, so MIS is wrong.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a product-error scenario. Explain the consequence, and explain how you would catch the error before you submit.

Check your answer

Sample answer:

  • Plan: buy 20 shares and hold them for three weeks. Product: CNC.
  • Error: MIS is selected by mistake.
  • Consequence: the position is meant to close the same day, and Zerodha’s auto square-off may close it at the cut-off time.
  • Catch: before you submit, compare the product field with the holding period written in the plan.

Quiz

The quiz checks how the product type follows the holding period, and why this book uses CNC.

Paper task

Mode: paper for the written work. Use kite_demo only to read the screen. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Find where the product selector sits in the Kite app flow shown above. Use the chapter’s illustration if you do not open the Kite demo.
  2. List the product choices you see. Note which one is selected first.
  3. Write that choice in your journal, with today’s date.
  4. Write one sentence: which product would you choose for a three-week plan, and why.
  5. Do not submit any order for this task.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A product type does not make a trade safer. It only sets how long the trade is meant to last.
  • MIS rules can close an open position for you. The times above can change. Confirm them on Zerodha’s support pages.
  • CNC does not remove risk. A holding for weeks can still fall. Set a stop (Risk per trade and stop-loss distance).
  • Margin in MIS is leverage. This book covers it as theory only, and you should not use margin to size a position in this book.

Remember

  • The product type sets how long the trade is meant to last.
  • CNC fits a holding that stays overnight.
  • MIS is meant to close the same day. Zerodha closes any MIS position still open at the auto square-off time.
  • Check the product field against your plan before you submit.
  • Do not pick a product because you think it costs less. Check the cost preview.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support articles on what CNC, MIS and NRML mean, and on intraday auto square-off timings. Zerodha changes these times and charges, so confirm them there before you rely on them.
  • SEBI and NSE circulars on margin and intraday rules. Search their websites for the current circulars.

GTT orders and their limits

Hook

You buy 100 shares at Rs 250. You set a GTT to sell at Rs 240, and then you forget it. Months later you sell the shares in another way. The old GTT is still waiting. A forgotten GTT is a hidden risk. It keeps waiting, and it can act when the price reaches its trigger.

The idea

GTT stands for good till triggered. A GTT stores an order that waits for a trigger price. When the price reaches the trigger, Kite places the stored order on the exchange. The stored order is a limit order or a market order. A market order carries price protection (see Order types).

A GTT lasts up to one year. The form has no field where you choose the end date. Zerodha sets the period, and you can have only a limited number of active GTTs at one time. Zerodha can change that limit, so check the Zerodha support pages. A GTT fires only once. You do not need the cash or the shares in place when you create it. Kite checks your funds and holdings when the trigger is hit.

A GTT is available for CNC, NRML and MTF orders.

A GTT is not a guaranteed fill. Once the trigger is reached, the stored order follows the rules of its type. The gap and limit rules from Order types still apply.

The quantity in a GTT must match your holding. A GTT that asks to sell more shares than you hold is a mistake. Check the quantity every time.

Check every GTT after each trade. Check it after each Corporate actions event, such as bonus shares or a split, because these change share counts and prices. Check it again after any change in your holdings. Some corporate actions can disable a GTT, and a large one can cancel it. If that happens, you must place a fresh GTT.

Where to find GTTs. Go to Orders, then the GTT tab. There you can view, change or cancel them.

How to create one (web). Go to Marketwatch, Holdings or Positions. Hover over the instrument and click More (the three dots). Choose Create GTT. On the phone app, tap the instrument, tap Create GTT, enter the details and slide the Create GTT button.

The form (web). Look for these fields in your app:

  • Buy or Sell, at the top left.
  • Single or OCO, at the top right.
  • Trigger price. For Single, one trigger price. For OCO, two: a stoploss trigger and a target trigger.
  • Quantity.
  • Limit or Market. If you choose Limit, enter the limit price. For a sell GTT, set the limit below the trigger, or use Market, to help the order fill.
  • Trailing (in the Stoploss part). A tick box and a trailing point value. This is on the web only, from June 2026.
  • Place.

The form has no expiry field.

Types.

  • Single: one trigger. It can start a buy or end a holding.
  • OCO (one cancels the other): a stoploss and a target. When one triggers, its order is placed and the other is cancelled. Sell OCO works for shares and F&O. Buy OCO is for F&O only.
  • Trailing stoploss: the trigger moves up with the price by the amount you set. For shares, it works on sell orders only.

Statuses. Kite uses seven statuses: Active, Triggered, Disabled, Expired, Cancelled, Rejected and Deleted. Your app may show them with different capital letters or colours. Look for them on your own screen.

  • Disabled: needs your action. For example, a bonus or a split can disable it. So can a trigger set too close to the current price (within 0.25 percent when placed).
  • Cancelled or Rejected: Kite stopped it. For example, the stock was delisted or suspended, or a large corporate action took place.
  • Expired: one year passed without a trigger, or the futures or options contract ended.
  • Deleted: you deleted it.

If a GTT is disabled or expired, you must place a fresh one.

Trigger hours. A GTT can trigger only while trading continues. The cut-off times differ: F&O stocks until 3:15 PM, other stocks until 3:30 PM, and F&O contracts until 3:40 PM. Zerodha can change these times. Check the Zerodha support pages before you rely on them.

Worked example

Hypothetical. You bought 100 shares at Rs 250 (CNC). Your GTT has a trigger at Rs 240 and sells 100 shares.

ItemCalculationResult
HoldingGiven100 shares
Loss per share250 - 240Rs 10
Planned loss if it fills at the trigger100 x 10Rs 1,000

A fill at the trigger is not guaranteed. A fill below the trigger can cost more than the planned Rs 1,000.

If the GTT is disabled, cancelled or expires before your review date, the position has no stop after that date. This is an illustration only.

See it

Look at the trigger price, the quantity and the Buy or Sell choice on the form. Notice that the form has no expiry field. Then open Orders, then GTT, and look at the list. Compare the quantity of any GTT there with your holding. A mismatch is a warning sign.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. You hold 100 shares bought at Rs 250. Plan a GTT with a trigger at Rs 240. Then compare the planned loss with a trigger at Rs 245.

Hints:

  1. Loss = (entry - trigger) x quantity.
  2. Check the quantity against your holding.
  3. Write your review date.

Answer:

  • Trigger Rs 240: 100 x (250 - 240) = 100 x 10 = Rs 1,000.
  • Trigger Rs 245: 100 x (250 - 245) = 100 x 5 = Rs 500.
  • Quantity: 100, the same as your holding.
  • Review date: write one date in your journal.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. You hold 200 shares bought at Rs 400. Write a GTT plan with the trigger, the quantity, the planned loss, an expiry check date and a weekly review step.

Check your answer

Sample answer:

  • Trigger: Rs 380 (one example).
  • Quantity: 200, the same as your holding.
  • Planned loss: 200 x (400 - 380) = 200 x 20 = Rs 4,000.
  • Expiry check date: the date one year after you create the GTT.
  • Weekly review step: check the GTT status and its quantity against your holdings.

Check the planned loss against your risk budget before you save the GTT. All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the planned loss of a GTT, the quantity rule, and the limits of a GTT.

Paper task

Mode: paper for the written work. Use kite_demo only to read the GTT form. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write the GTT trigger and limit on paper only.
  2. In the Kite demo, find the GTT form and note the fields. Note any warning the screen gives. Check that the form has no expiry field.
  3. Write one sentence on the quantity check: how did you confirm it matched the holding?

Limits and common mistakes

  • A GTT is not a risk limit. It does not replace your plan.
  • A GTT is not a guaranteed exit. A gap can move the fill away from the trigger (Gaps and what they mean for stops).
  • A GTT can be forgotten. Put a review date in your journal for each GTT you create.
  • A GTT that is larger than your holding is a mistake. Fix the quantity before you rely on it.
  • After you sell shares in another way, check for any GTT on the same stock and cancel it if it is no longer part of your plan.
  • Selling delivery shares needs CDSL TPIN authorisation, unless you have a DDPI or POA. Without it, a GTT sell can fail when it triggers (see Order book).

Remember

  • A GTT waits for a trigger price, then places the stored order.
  • A GTT lasts up to one year, and you can have a limited number of active GTTs.
  • A GTT is not a guaranteed fill. The gap and limit rules still apply.
  • The quantity must match your holding.
  • Check every GTT after each trade and after each corporate action.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support articles on GTT: what it is, disabled, cancelled and expired GTTs, and why a GTT did not execute. Zerodha changes these limits, so confirm them there.
  • NSE India corporate action notices for the stock

Order book

Hook

You place an order to buy 10 shares. The screen shows 6 shares done, and you think the order is finished. So you place the order again. Your first order still has 4 shares open, and the second order may now buy up to 10 more. You may end up with more shares than your plan allowed. Kite shows one order in several views. Reading the views together stops this mistake.

The idea

Kite shows an order in several places. Each place shows one part of the story.

Order book. A list of each instruction you gave, with its status. On the web, the end statuses are OPEN, COMPLETE, REJECTED and CANCELLED. Interim statuses include TRIGGER PENDING and AMO REQ RECEIVED. “Executed” is the name of a phone tab, not a status.

Where to find these. On the web, the Orders page has tabs such as Orders, GTT, Baskets, SIPs and Alerts. On the phone app, the Orders area has Open, Executed and GTT tabs, and you swipe to change them. Look for the exact names in your own app.

Rejected orders. When Kite rejects an order, it gives a reason. On the web, look for Info in the order’s options menu.

Baskets. A basket is a group of orders placed together. A basket can hold up to 20 orders, and you can have up to 50 baskets. Execute places the orders in the order they are listed. That order changes the margin needed, not the final amount blocked.

Trade book. A list of each execution, also called a fill. Each row shows a quantity and a price. Look for a trades view in your own app. Its name on the web can differ from one screen to the next.

Holdings. The shares in your demat account after settlement. See Demat accounts and settlement. A CNC buy shows in Positions on the day you buy it. From the next trading day it shows in Holdings. Until the shares are delivered to your demat account, they show as T1 holdings.

Positions. Trades that are still open for the day or carried forward. A position shows the product (such as CNC, MIS or NRML), the instrument, the quantity, the average price, the last price and the profit or loss. Which trades show here depends on the product type.

Funds. On the web, press F to open Funds. Look for the available margin (the money you can use for new trades), the used margin (the money blocked in trades and open orders) and the available cash. The Add funds and Withdraw buttons are here too.

Cost preview. The estimated charges shown before you submit an order. On the web, the order window shows the margin needed and your available margin. Charges may appear between them, but Zerodha can change the layout. Final charges are in Console, in the contract note and the ledger.

Selling delivery shares. If you do not have a POA or DDPI, Kite asks for a CDSL TPIN authorisation before a delivery sell. A POA or DDPI is a permission that lets your broker sell from your demat account. The TPIN comes by email and SMS. You enter it with an OTP. An authorisation lasts one day. On the web, look for the Authorise button on the Holdings page.

Partly filled means some shares are done and some are still pending. A partly filled order is not finished. It stays OPEN, with the filled part shown. Do not treat it as complete.

Reconciliation means checking that the views agree with each other. The order quantity equals the sum of the fills. That sum equals the change in holdings or positions. If the numbers do not agree, stop and find out why before you act.

Worked example

Hypothetical. You place a buy order for 10 shares with a limit at Rs 250 (CNC). Two fills happen.

FillQuantityPriceValue
16Rs 249.806 x 249.80 = Rs 1,498.80
24Rs 250.004 x 250.00 = Rs 1,000.00
Total10Rs 2,498.80

The trade book shows two rows. Together they total 10 shares.

Average price = total value divided by total quantity = 2,498.80 divided by 10 = Rs 249.88.

See it

Trace this one order from the order book to the trade book, then to holdings and positions. Watch which numbers match across the views. Look for the quantity that is still pending.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Reconcile the worked example above. Find the total quantity, the total value and the average price.

Hints:

  1. Add the fill quantities.
  2. Multiply each fill by its price.
  3. Divide the total value by the total quantity.

Answer:

  • Total quantity: 6 + 4 = 10 shares.
  • Total value: Rs 1,498.80 + Rs 1,000.00 = Rs 2,498.80.
  • Average price: Rs 2,498.80 / 10 = Rs 249.88.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. You place an order for 20 shares. 15 shares fill at Rs 402. The other 5 are still pending. Compute the value filled and the average price. Then write what each view should show at this moment.

Check your answer
  • Value filled: 15 x 402 = Rs 6,030.
  • Average price: 6,030 / 15 = Rs 402.00.
  • Order book: partly filled, with 5 shares still pending. The order stays OPEN.
  • Trade book: fills totalling 15 shares.
  • Holdings or positions: no more than 15 shares, because only 15 have filled. Check your app for when new shares appear.
  • Cost preview: an estimate. It is not a final charge.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks fill arithmetic, what each view shows, and why a partly filled order is not finished.

Paper task

Mode: paper for the written work. Use kite_demo only to read the screens. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write the limit price for one small order on paper first.
  2. Write down what the order would show in Orders: its status, quantity and price. Do not place it.
  3. Write how it would appear in the order book, the trade book, holdings and positions.
  4. Write the cost preview you expect to see before you submit.
  5. Compare your written preview with the final charges. The final charges for a real order are in Console.
  6. Write one line: did the four views agree?

Limits and common mistakes

  • A partly filled order is not finished. Check the order book before you act again.
  • Do not average down on a pending order. Buying more of a falling holding to lower your average price is a mistake (Why scalping, option buying and martingale are dangerous).
  • The cost preview is an estimate. Do not use it as your final cost.
  • Demo data is dummy data. The demo is view-only, so it has no real fills. It cannot show how a live fill would look.
  • A sell of delivery shares can fail without the TPIN authorisation or a DDPI or POA. Check this before you sell.

Remember

  • The order book shows each instruction and its status.
  • The trade book shows each fill, with its quantity and price.
  • Holdings are shares after settlement. Positions depend on the product type.
  • A partly filled order is still open. Check the pending quantity.
  • Reconcile the views. If the numbers differ, find out why before you act.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support pages on order status, the trade book, holdings and CDSL TPIN authorisation. Check the current labels there.
  • Zerodha charges page (check the current rates there)

Common execution mistakes

Hook

You plan to buy 10 shares at Rs 250, which is Rs 2,500. You type 100 instead. The order value is Rs 25,000. On a Rs 50,000 account, that is half your capital in one trade. The mistake took one extra zero. This chapter shows the common order mistakes and how to catch them before you submit.

The idea

Most order mistakes fall into a few families.

  1. Wrong product. The product type does not match your plan. See Product types.
  2. A stop that never triggers. The stop is placed, but it cannot act.
  3. A price typing error. This is also called a fat-finger error. It is a typing mistake, such as an extra zero or a misplaced decimal point.
  4. Wrong quantity. The number of shares does not match your plan.
  5. Wrong order type or validity. For example, SL-M is blocked on BSE stocks. A stop set for the Day validity ends at the end of the session, so it is gone the next morning. An AMO placed after hours stays as AMO REQ RECEIVED until it reaches the exchange, so it is not yet a live order.
  6. A fill far from the price you saw. A market order can fill well away from the last price, especially in a fast move.

A stop can fail to act for four common reasons:

  • Wrong side. A sell stop was placed as a buy stop, or the reverse.
  • Trigger never reached. The trigger is set where the price does not go.
  • Order inactive or cancelled. The stop is no longer live.
  • GTT not active or expired. A stop kept as a GTT has stopped working. See GTT orders and their limits.

On the web, Kite sends an order when you click Buy or Sell, or press Enter. Zerodha’s manual describes no extra “are you sure?” step. Kite does reject some orders and shows a message. Examples:

  • A quantity above the freeze limit gets a nudge. Kite then splits the order into slices. The nudge asks you to check the quantity. It is a warning, not a block.
  • An order outside the day’s circuit band is rejected by Kite’s own risk check.
  • An order without enough margin is rejected. The message reads like “Insufficient funds. Required margin is Rs X but available margin is Rs Y”.

Read these messages. Do not rely on them to catch your own typing errors.

A checklist written before the session is better than a fix made during it. Write the checklist when you are calm, and use it every time.

After a mistake, reduce your exposure by your rules. Do not add shares to fix it. Adding to a losing position is covered in Why scalping, option buying and martingale are dangerous.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Your plan is 10 shares at Rs 250. You type 100 by mistake. The stop is Rs 10 below the entry, at Rs 240.

ItemIntendedTyped
Shares10100
PriceRs 250Rs 250
Order value10 x 250 = Rs 2,500100 x 250 = Rs 25,000
Planned loss at Rs 24010 x 10 = Rs 100100 x 10 = Rs 1,000
  • The typed order is ten times the intended size.
  • On a Rs 50,000 account, Rs 25,000 is half of your capital in one trade.
  • The planned loss on 100 shares is Rs 1,000. That is 2% of Rs 50,000 (1,000 / 50,000 = 2%).
  • Your risk budget at 1% of Rs 50,000 is Rs 500. The typed loss is twice that budget.

See it

Compare each preview with its plan. Find the one error in each. Write the correction before you open the answer.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Three orders are described below. Each one has one error. Find the error and state the correction. Check side, product, quantity and price against the plan.

  1. Plan: a sell stop for 100 shares you hold, with a trigger at Rs 240. Entered: a buy stop for 100 shares, with a trigger at Rs 240.
  2. Plan: buy 25 shares at Rs 300. Entered: buy 250 shares at Rs 300.
  3. Plan: CNC, because you will hold for three weeks. Entered: MIS.

Hints:

  1. A stop for a holding is a sell order.
  2. Count the digits of the quantity against the plan.
  3. The product follows the holding period.

Answer:

  1. Wrong side. The stop must be a sell stop. Correction: change the side to sell.
  2. Wrong quantity. The order is ten times the plan. Correction: 25 shares.
  3. Wrong product. The holding is for three weeks, so the product is CNC. Correction: change the product to CNC.

Independent exercise

Task. Write your own eight-item pre-submission checklist. Then test it against three errors you were not shown.

Check your answer

A sample checklist:

  1. Side (buy or sell) matches the plan.
  2. Product matches the holding period in the plan.
  3. Quantity matches the plan, and the digit count is right.
  4. Price matches the plan, with no typing error.
  5. Order type (limit, market, SL or SL-M) matches the plan, and the validity is right.
  6. A stop trigger sits on the correct side of the entry price for the holding.
  7. A stop quantity equals the number of shares you hold.
  8. Any GTT on the same stock is checked and has a review date.

All examples are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the cost of a typing error, the rules for stops that never trigger, and the check before submitting.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write one hypothetical plan on paper, with a quantity and a price.
  2. Write the wrong order on paper, with the wrong quantity. Do not place it.
  3. Note where you would catch the error: before you clicked Buy or Sell, or not at all.
  4. Log the mistake in your journal with a rule-break tag.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The book cannot check your practice. Your reflection is your own record.
  • A checklist reduces errors. It does not remove them. Keep it current.
  • Kite’s messages are not a safety net. They may not catch a wrong quantity or a wrong side. Check the order yourself before you click Buy or Sell.
  • A wrong quantity on a stop also changes the planned loss. Check the stop as well as the entry order.
  • An order set to Day validity ends at the end of the session. Check the validity before you rely on an order over several days.

Remember

  • Most order mistakes are wrong product, a stop that never triggers, a typing error, a wrong quantity, or a wrong order type or validity.
  • A stop can fail to act when it is on the wrong side or cancelled, or when its trigger is never reached.
  • Ten times the intended size can turn a small plan into half of your capital.
  • Check side, product, quantity, price and validity against the written plan before you submit.
  • After a mistake, reduce exposure by your rules. Do not add to the position.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support pages on order rejections and Kite error messages. Check the current wording there.
  • NSE India, trading rules and price band notices. Search the NSE website for the current circulars.

A complete demo walkthrough

Hook

A trader writes her plan on paper first. The plan has the entry, the stop, the number of shares and the planned loss. Only then does she open the Kite demo to see where each field sits. The demo uses dummy data, so it cannot show whether the plan would have paid. Its value is in following the steps every time.

The idea

This chapter is a paper walkthrough with the Kite click path. It puts the steps from the earlier order chapters together. Use the same steps for every paper walkthrough.

  1. Plan before you open Kite. Write the setup, the entry, the stop, the number of shares, the planned loss, the product and the review date. For a holding you plan to keep for weeks, the product is CNC.
  2. Size the trade. Use the stop distance, the risk budget and the caps. See Position sizing step by step.
  3. Write the stop as its own order. The stop is a separate order from the entry. For a holding you plan to keep for weeks, use a GTT. A normal SL is valid for one trading day only. Then check the stop’s side, type, trigger, limit and quantity.
  4. Check the sell rules. Before a delivery sell, check that you have a CDSL TPIN authorisation, or a DDPI or POA. See Order book.
  5. Record on paper. Write the plan and the field labels you found, then compare the two.
  6. Label the record. Write paper in the journal. Never write “live”.

On the phone app, a real order is sent by swiping the button, not by tapping it. In the demo, only find the button and read its label.

Three terms from the sizing steps:

  • Risk budget: the most you accept to lose on one trade. It is your capital times your risk percent.
  • Cash cap: the most shares your cash can buy.
  • Concentration cap: the most shares that keep one position within a set share of your capital. See Portfolio exposure and concentration.

The number of shares is the smallest of the raw risk figure, the cash cap and the concentration cap. Round each down. If two limits tie, say so.

The demo’s dummy data gives no real profit or loss. A demo result, of any size, does not prove that your plan works.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1%, so the risk budget is Rs 500. Entry Rs 250. Stop Rs 240, below a chart level. The risk per share is Rs 10.

StepCalculationResult
Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
Risk per share250 - 240Rs 10
Raw sharesfloor(500 / 10)50
Cash capfloor(50,000 / 250)200
Concentration cap25% of 50,000 = 12,500, then floor(12,500 / 250)50
Sharesminimum of 50, 200 and 5050
  • Position value: 50 x 250 = Rs 12,500. That is exactly the 25% cap.
  • Planned loss: 50 x 10 = Rs 500.
  • Binding constraint: the risk budget and the concentration cap tie at 50 shares.
  • Stop order: a GTT (Sell, Single) with a trigger at Rs 240 and a limit at Rs 238. A GTT is needed because the holding is for weeks. Write this stop order on paper.

A limit stop with the limit below the trigger may not fill in a gap. See Order types.

See it

Fill in the plan form first, using the example numbers. Then find the entry and stop fields in the Kite demo and note where each one sits. Fill in the execution form on paper after the walkthrough.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Complete the plan form for the worked example above.

Hints:

  1. Work out the risk per share first.
  2. Raw shares = risk budget divided by risk per share, rounded down.
  3. Check both caps before you choose the number of shares.

Answer:

  • Risk budget: Rs 500.
  • Risk per share: Rs 10.
  • Raw shares: 50.
  • Cash cap: 200. Concentration cap: 50.
  • Shares: 50.
  • Planned loss: Rs 500.
  • Binding constraint: a tie between the risk limit and the concentration cap, both 50 shares.
  • Stop: GTT, Sell, Single, trigger Rs 240, limit Rs 238.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Entry Rs 300. Stop Rs 290. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1%. Compute the final number of shares, the planned loss and the binding constraint.

Check your answer
  • Risk budget: Rs 500.
  • Risk per share: 300 - 290 = Rs 10.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 10) = 50.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 300) = 166.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 300) = 41.
  • Shares: minimum of 50, 166 and 41 = 41.
  • Planned loss: 41 x 10 = Rs 410.
  • Binding constraint: concentration cap.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the sizing steps, the planned loss, and the rule for labelling a paper record.

Paper task

Mode: paper for the full cycle. Use kite_demo only to find the fields. Complete the full cycle for one hypothetical trade. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Plan. Write the setup, entry, stop, shares, planned loss, product and review date.
  2. Size. Use the sizing steps to check the number of shares.
  3. Find the entry fields. In the Kite demo, find the entry fields on the order screen and read their labels. Do not place any order.
  4. Find the stop fields. In the Kite demo, find the stop fields and read their labels. For this holding, the stop is a GTT. Do not set it.
  5. Record on paper. Write what you planned and which fields you found. Compare the two. Write a short reflection. Label the record paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A paper walkthrough is practice and is not live evidence. Do not record it as live.
  • The demo shows no real fill. Do not treat any number on screen as a fill.
  • A stop is not a risk limit by itself. A gap can make the loss bigger than planned (Slippage).
  • The demo is view-only. It cannot place, change or cancel an order, create a GTT or set an alert. Do the order steps on paper.
  • Finishing this walkthrough is not permission to trade with real money. Live use is a separate decision, made much later, after the checks at the end of this book.

Remember

  • Plan first, then size, then find the entry and stop fields, then record on paper.
  • Shares = the smallest of the raw risk figure, the cash cap and the concentration cap.
  • Planned loss = shares x risk per share.
  • Compare your written walkthrough with the plan, and write what differed.
  • Label every walkthrough record paper, never “live”.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support article on the Kite demo. The demo uses dummy data and is at kite-demo.zerodha.com/dashboard. Check the current wording there.
  • The book’s own sizing and journal pages

Kite readiness assessment

Hook

Picture a learner who has read every Kite chapter and feels ready. She asks herself one question: what does her stop order do if the price jumps down overnight? She cannot answer it clearly. This chapter is a check on the basics of order mechanics. It asks you to show what you know, in writing and in a short quiz. It does not give you permission to trade real money.

The idea

This is the third checkpoint in the book, called gate G3. A gate is a check you pass before you move on. G3 checks two things. The first is that you understand how orders work. The second is that you have done the demo steps from the Kite chapters.

A pass means you understand order mechanics and your demo checklist. It does not mean you can trade profitably.

G3 has three parts. You must pass all three.

  • Checklist. You tick each item from the demo checklist in the nine Kite chapters before this one. You write one line of reflection for each item.
  • Quiz. You answer a set of questions about common execution mistakes. You need 90 percent or more correct.
  • Paper trade statement. For one paper trade from the walkthrough chapter, you state three things. These are the planned loss, the binding constraint and the stop order type, with how that stop behaves on a gap.

Some words in this chapter:

  • Binding constraint. The sizing limit that gives the smallest number of shares. It sets your size.
  • Gap. A jump in price with no trading in between. A stop can fill far from its trigger after a gap.

You may retake the quiz as often as you like. Wrong answers show their explanation before you retake. There is no timer.

The screen states this notice, and the pass screen shows it too:

This checks that you understand order mechanics and your demo checklist. It is not proof that you can trade profitably, and it is not a recommendation to place live orders. There is no pressure to go live.

Your checklist ticks, reflections, quiz answers, times and gate status stay on your device. Nothing is sent anywhere. They are included in your backup file.

See it

PartWhat you doWhat counts as a pass
ChecklistTick every demo item from the nine Kite chapters. Write one line for each.Every item ticked, each with a reflection. You tick the items yourself.
QuizAnswer all the quiz questions.90 percent or more correct. With 8 questions, that means all 8. With 10 questions, it means 9.
Paper trade statementState the planned loss, the binding constraint and the stop order type with its gap behaviour.All three stated correctly, for one paper trade.

Read the last column first. Each part has a clear pass rule. The checklist depends on your honesty, because the book cannot see what you did in the demo.

Worked example

Hypothetical. This example uses the paper trade from A complete demo walkthrough. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1 percent. Entry Rs 250. Stop trigger Rs 240.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
2. Risk per share250 - 240Rs 10
3. Raw sharesfloor(500 / 10)50
4. Cash capfloor(50,000 / 250)200
5. Concentration capfloor(12,500 / 250)50
Sharesminimum of 50, 200, 5050

For part 3 of G3, a full answer has three lines:

  • Planned loss: 50 x Rs 10 = Rs 500.
  • Binding constraint: the risk budget and the concentration cap tie at 50 shares. Say so.
  • Stop order: a GTT (Sell, Single) with trigger Rs 240 and limit Rs 238. A normal stop-loss order lasts one trading day only, and this holding is for weeks. If the price gaps down below Rs 238, the order may not fill at all. The loss can then be bigger than Rs 500.

For the quiz, count the score this way. Ten questions with 9 correct is 9 divided by 10, which is 90 percent. That is a pass. Ten questions with 8 correct is 80 percent. That is not a pass, so you retake.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Using the worked example above, write the three lines for part 3 of G3. State the planned loss, the binding constraint, and the stop order type with its gap behaviour.

Hints:

  1. Multiply the shares by the risk per share, not by the entry price.
  2. Check the risk limit and the concentration cap. Do they tie, or does one give fewer shares?
  3. Ask what happens to the order if the price opens below the limit price.

Answer:

  • Planned loss: Rs 500 (50 x Rs 10).
  • Binding constraint: the risk budget and the concentration cap tie at 50 shares.
  • Stop order: a GTT (Sell, Single), trigger Rs 240, limit Rs 238. A gap below Rs 238 can leave the order unfilled, so the loss can be larger than Rs 500.

Independent exercise

Task. Entry Rs 300. Stop Rs 290. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1 percent. Work out the final shares, the planned loss and the binding constraint.

Check your answer
  • Risk budget: 50,000 x 1% = Rs 500.
  • Risk per share: 300 - 290 = Rs 10.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 10) = 50.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 300) = floor(166.67) = 166.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 300) = floor(41.67) = 41.
  • Shares: minimum of 50, 166, 41 = 41.
  • Planned loss: 41 x Rs 10 = Rs 410.
  • Binding constraint: concentration cap.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks order mechanics. It covers sizing, stop fills after a gap, order types, product types and GTT limits. It uses the same numbers and rules you met in the earlier Kite chapters.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Copy the nine Kite chapters into a list. Use their titles, from Kite web and mobile tour to A complete demo walkthrough.
  2. For each chapter, tick the item only if you did the demo step. Do not tick an item you skipped.
  3. Write one line of reflection under each tick.
  4. Write the three lines for one paper trade from A complete demo walkthrough. Include the planned loss, the binding constraint and the stop order type with its gap behaviour.
  5. Take the quiz until you reach 90 percent. Write down each question you missed, and the rule it tested.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A pass shows that you know the mechanics. It does not show that you can make money. The quiz tests understanding, not skill with real prices.
  • The checklist is marked by you. The book cannot check whether you did a demo step.
  • The demo uses dummy money and dummy prices. A good demo result says nothing about real results.
  • Do not rush the quiz to pass. You can retake it as often as you like, so a fast pass teaches little.
  • Passing G3 does not mean you are ready for real money. The final review in Gate G7 live-readiness review and the one-page trading plan decides that.

Remember

  • G3 has three parts: the checklist, the quiz at 90 percent or more, and the paper trade statement.
  • A stop order can fill far from its trigger after a gap.
  • Name the binding constraint every time. If two limits tie, say so.
  • A demo pass is not a live result. Do not treat it as one.

Facts to check before you use this chapter:

  • The checklist items. These come from your own demo steps, not from Zerodha.
  • The product and GTT labels shown in the demo, if they differ from this book.

5. Indicators
