What indicators are and are not

Hook

A trader named Meena (hypothetical) sees a line on her chart cross above another line. She is confident the price will rise tomorrow. She has not checked what the lines are made from. Both lines are only averages of past prices. Neither line knows tomorrow. This chapter explains what an indicator is, and what it cannot do.

The idea

An indicator is a formula. It takes past prices, and sometimes volume, over a fixed window of days. Then it gives one number, or a line, for each day.

An indicator adds no outside information. It only rearranges the price data you already have. Its output describes the past and the present. It is not a forecast.

Three words matter here:

  • Window. The number of days the formula looks back over. A five-day window uses the last five days.
  • Parameter. A setting you choose for the formula, such as the window length. A five-day average and a twenty-day average use different parameters.
  • Lagging. An indicator that averages past data reacts after the price has already moved. It is late by design.

Three points follow from this.

  1. Indicators lag. Most trend indicators average past data, so they react after a turn in price. They cannot react to a turn before it happens.
  2. Many indicators are related. Most indicators are built from the same closing prices. Two of them often agree because they use the same data. Using several of them does not give you several independent opinions.
  3. Changing a parameter changes the answer. A five-day line and a twenty-day line can give opposite readings on the same day. You must fix the parameters before you test them. You will see this in Indicator playground and when indicators disagree.

Indicators do not replace the other rules in this book. A trade still needs a stop and a size. The stop and size come from rules you set first, such as those in Position sizing step by step.

Here are three things an indicator cannot tell you:

  • It cannot tell you tomorrow’s close.
  • It cannot tell you whether a crossover will work this time.
  • It cannot tell you how much you can lose on a trade.

See it

Change the window length and watch the line. A longer window makes the line smoother and slower. A shorter window makes it follow the price more closely. Notice that the line always trails the price after a turn. Try the first turn in the chart, then the second.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Closes for five days: Rs 100, Rs 102, Rs 104, Rs 103 and Rs 105.

StepCalculationResult
1. Add the five closes100 + 102 + 104 + 103 + 105Rs 514
2. Divide by 5514 / 5Rs 102.80
3. Compare with the latest close105 - 102.80Rs 2.20 above the average

The average of Rs 102.80 summarises the last five days. It does not predict day six. The latest close is above the average because the price rose. That is all the number tells you.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Closes on three days are Rs 100, Rs 102 and Rs 104. Work out the three-day average.

Hints:

  1. Add the three closes.
  2. Divide the total by 3.

Answer: Rs 102.00. The total is Rs 306, and 306 / 3 = Rs 102.00.

Independent exercise

Task. Explain in three sentences why an indicator built only from closing prices cannot know tomorrow’s close.

Check your answer

A good answer has three points. The indicator uses only data up to today. Tomorrow’s close is not in that data. A formula cannot create information that the data does not hold.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the arithmetic of a simple average. It also checks that you know indicators lag and cannot forecast.

Paper task

Mode: kite_demo. Demo only. Do not place any order.

  1. Open the demo chart for one stock.
  2. List every indicator on the chart. Write its name and its parameter, such as the window length.
  3. Record the default parameter for each indicator. Note the setting exactly as the demo shows it. Zerodha’s charting manual does not list default settings, so read them on your own screen in the indicator settings.
  4. For each indicator, write one sentence on what it describes about the past.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • An indicator does not tell you that a trade is good. It only summarises past prices.
  • A crossover of two lines is an observation. It is not a signal you can rely on until you have tested it.
  • Stacking several indicators adds more lines, not more evidence. Many of them use the same closing prices.
  • A longer window is not always more accurate. It is only smoother and slower.
  • An indicator does not reduce the need for a stop and a size rule.

Remember

  • An indicator is a formula applied to past prices or volume over a window.
  • Most trend indicators lag, because they average past data.
  • Indicators describe the past and the present. They are not forecasts.
  • Related indicators agree often, so agreement is not separate proof.
  • Fix the parameters before you test. Changing them after you see results is a common self-deception.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, technical indicators overview (reference only).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It says Kite has over 100 indicators, and that indicators apply to the closing price by default. (As of 11 Nov 2026. Check the manual for changes.)
  • J. Welles Wilder, New Concepts in Technical Trading Systems (1978), the original source of RSI and ATR.
  • StockCharts ChartSchool, a free reference on the standard indicator settings.

Moving averages

Hook

Ana (hypothetical) keeps the closing price of a stock for five days. She adds them up and divides by five. Then she does the same on the next day. Her numbers move, but they trail the price. She wonders whether a different kind of average would react faster. This chapter shows two common averages, the simple moving average and the exponential moving average, and how each one is calculated.

The idea

A moving average is the average of closing prices over a window of days. The window moves forward each day, so the average moves too. There are two common kinds.

Simple moving average (SMA). Every day in the window counts the same.

  • Add the last N closing prices.
  • Divide the total by N.

In symbols, SMA = (sum of the last N closes) / N.

To update the SMA without adding all N values again, use this rule:

  • New SMA = old SMA + (today’s close minus the close N days ago) / N.

The price that drops out of the window is the close N days ago. The new price comes in on the other side.

Exponential moving average (EMA). Recent days count more than older days. The EMA uses a smoothing factor, written k, that sets how much weight the newest close gets.

  • k = 2 / (N + 1).
  • Today’s EMA = k x today’s close + (1 - k) x yesterday’s EMA.

In words, the EMA moves a fraction k of the way from yesterday’s EMA towards today’s close. A bigger k means a faster reaction.

Three points to keep in mind.

  1. The EMA reacts faster than an SMA of the same length. Its recent weight is larger.
  2. The first EMA needs a starting value. A common choice is the first SMA. This is a convention, and it changes the early values. It does not change the later logic.
  3. Both averages lag. An N-day SMA lags a steady trend by about (N - 1) / 2 days. This is a rough guide, not an exact law.

Crossovers are a popular use of moving averages. A crossover happens when a short average moves above or below a long average. Some traders treat it as a sign of trend. Whether such signals help is an open question. The book tests these rules later, in Lab 4.

See it

Look at both lines on the same made-up prices. The EMA bends sooner after a turn in the price. Then switch the starting value of the EMA and see how the first few days change. The later values move closer together.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Five closes: Rs 100, Rs 102, Rs 104, Rs 103 and Rs 105.

Part 1. Simple moving average.

StepCalculationResult
1. Sum of five closes100 + 102 + 104 + 103 + 105Rs 514
2. Divide by 5514 / 5Rs 102.80

Next close is Rs 107. The close that drops out is Rs 100.

MethodCalculationResult
Rolling update102.80 + (107 - 100) / 5 = 102.80 + 1.40Rs 104.20
Direct check102 + 104 + 103 + 105 + 107 = 521, then 521 / 5Rs 104.20

Both methods give the same answer. The rolling update is quicker to do by hand.

Part 2. Exponential moving average with N = 5.

StepCalculationResult
1. Smoothing factor2 / (5 + 1)0.3333
2. Yesterday’s EMAStarting value, set equal to the first SMARs 102.80
3. Today’s closeGivenRs 110
4. Change from yesterday’s EMA110 - 102.80Rs 7.20
5. Weighted change0.3333 x 7.20Rs 2.40
6. Today’s EMA102.80 + 2.40Rs 105.20

The EMA moved Rs 2.40 towards the new close. On the same close of Rs 110, the SMA would have moved Rs 2.00, to Rs 104.80. Check: 102.80 + (110 - 100) / 5 = 104.80. The EMA gives today’s close more weight, so it moves further.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Closes on three days are Rs 50, Rs 51 and Rs 53. Find the first three-day SMA. The next close is Rs 56. Use the rolling update to find the new SMA.

Hints:

  1. The first SMA is (50 + 51 + 53) / 3.
  2. The new SMA is the old SMA + (56 - 50) / 3. The close that drops out is Rs 50.

Answer: The first SMA is Rs 154 / 3 = Rs 51.33, rounded. The updated SMA is Rs 51.33 + Rs 2.00 = Rs 53.33, rounded. Check: (51 + 53 + 56) / 3 = 160 / 3 = Rs 53.33.

Independent exercise

Task. Find the EMA for a nine-day window, where k = 0.2. Yesterday’s EMA is Rs 500. Today’s close is Rs 510. Find today’s EMA.

Check your answer
  • Change from yesterday’s EMA: 510 - 500 = Rs 10.
  • Weighted change: 0.2 x 10 = Rs 2.
  • Today’s EMA: 500 + 2 = Rs 502.00.

Check: 2 / (9 + 1) = 0.2, which matches k.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the two formulas, the lag, and the smoothing factor. It also checks that a crossover is not a sure signal.

Paper task

Mode: kite_demo. Demo only. Do not place any order.

  1. On the daily chart of one stock, draw a 20-period SMA and a 20-period EMA. Use the same closing price for both.
  2. Find two turning points in the price, one high and one low.
  3. At each turning point, note which average changed direction first.
  4. Write the result as a learning note. Do not draw a conclusion from two turning points.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • In a sideways market, a crossover can switch back and forth many times. Each switch can cost money through costs and can hit a stop. See Costs on a trade.
  • A crossover is a rule to test, not a profit method. This book does not present it as one.
  • The EMA reacts sooner, but it can also react to a single unusual day more strongly.
  • The starting value of the EMA changes the early numbers. Compare two charts only when they use the same starting value.
  • Both averages lag. They show a trend after it has begun, and they do not warn you before it starts.

Remember

  • SMA = sum of the last N closes / N. Every day counts the same.
  • EMA uses k = 2 / (N + 1). Today’s EMA = k x today’s close + (1 - k) x yesterday’s EMA.
  • The EMA reacts faster than the SMA of the same length.
  • Both averages lag a steady trend.
  • A crossover is a hypothesis to test, not a sure signal.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, moving averages module (reference only).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It lists 11 moving-average types and says indicators apply to the closing price by default. It does not state default settings, so read the setting on your own screen. (As of 11 Nov 2026. Check the manual for changes.)
  • StockCharts ChartSchool, a free reference on moving averages.

ATR and volatility, used for stops

Hook

Vikram (hypothetical) wants a stop that fits how much a stock usually moves. He sets his stop Rs 2 below his entry. The stock often moves Rs 8 in a day. His stop is hit by normal movement, not by bad news. He has used a stop that is too tight for the stock. This chapter shows one way to measure normal movement, called the Average True Range, or ATR.

The idea

Volatility means how much a price moves. A stock that moves Rs 1 a day is less volatile than one that moves Rs 10 a day.

True range (TR) measures how far one day moved. It is the largest of three values for a day.

  1. High minus low for the day.
  2. The absolute value of high minus yesterday’s close.
  3. The absolute value of low minus yesterday’s close.

The second and third values count gaps. A gap is a jump in price with no trading in between. If the stock opens far above yesterday’s close, the true range picks up that jump. Simple high minus low would miss it.

Average True Range (ATR) is the average of TR over N bars. A common setting is N = 14. The bar can be a day, an hour or any other period.

ATR uses Wilder smoothing, a method that gives recent bars more weight.

  • The first ATR is the simple average of the first N true ranges. This is a common convention.
  • Each later ATR = (yesterday’s ATR x (N - 1) + today’s TR) / N.

Some points to keep in mind.

  • ATR is in rupees per share. To compare two stocks, convert it to a percent of the price.
  • A stop distance can be a multiple, k, of ATR. For example, a stop 2 times ATR below entry. The multiple is a design choice that you test later. See The fully specified swing strategy for how such choices are fixed and tested in this book.
  • A wider ATR gives a wider stop. For the same risk budget, a wider stop gives fewer shares.
  • ATR cannot cover unusual gaps beyond normal volatility. A gap can still be much larger than ATR. See Gaps and what they mean for stops.

Position size still comes from the rules in Position sizing step by step. ATR sets the stop distance. The budget and the caps set the number of shares.

See it

The three bars show the three limits. The shortest bar sets the size. Here the concentration cap is the shortest bar. Change the stop and watch how the risk bar grows. Notice that the concentration bar does not move, because the entry price does not change.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Five bars. Each row gives the high, the low and the previous close.

BarHighLowPrevious closeHigh - lowHigh - previous closeLow - previous closeTR
Day 21061021034314
Day 31081031045415
Day 41071041073033
Day 51101061054515

Each TR is the largest value in its row. For example, Day 4 has TR 3, because high minus low is 3 and the two gap values are 0 and 3.

Seed ATR for N = 3. (4 + 5 + 3) / 3 = 4.00.

Wilder ATR on Day 5. (4.00 x 2 + 5) / 3 = (8 + 5) / 3 = 13 / 3 = 4.33, rounded.

Stop and size. Assume capital Rs 50,000, risk 1 percent and the 25 percent concentration setting. Entry is Rs 250.

StepCalculationResult
1. Stop distance2 x 4.33Rs 8.66
2. Stop price250 - 8.66Rs 241.34
3. Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
4. Raw sharesfloor(500 / 8.66) = floor(57.7)57
5. Cash capfloor(50,000 / 250)200
6. Concentration capfloor(12,500 / 250)50
Sharesminimum of 57, 200, 5050

Binding constraint: the concentration cap. The planned position is 50 x Rs 250 = Rs 12,500, which is 25 percent of capital. The planned loss is 50 x Rs 8.66 = Rs 433, which is less than the Rs 500 budget. The cap stopped the size before the budget was used up.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. A bar has high Rs 300, low Rs 292 and previous close Rs 305. Find the true range.

Hints:

  1. High minus low = 300 - 292 = 8.
  2. High minus previous close = 300 - 305 = -5, so the absolute value is 5.
  3. Low minus previous close = 292 - 305 = -13, so the absolute value is 13.

Answer: The true range is Rs 13, the largest of 8, 5 and 13. The gap from the previous close sets the answer here.

Independent exercise

Task. ATR is Rs 6.00 on a Rs 400 stock. The stop multiple is 2.5. Capital is Rs 50,000, risk is 1 percent and the concentration setting is 25 percent. Find the stop distance, the stop price and the shares. Name the binding constraint.

Check your answer
  • Stop distance: 2.5 x Rs 6.00 = Rs 15.
  • Stop price: 400 - 15 = Rs 385.
  • Risk budget: 50,000 x 1% = Rs 500.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 15) = floor(33.3) = 33.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 400) = 125.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 400) = floor(31.25) = 31.
  • Shares: minimum of 33, 125, 31 = 31.
  • Binding constraint: concentration cap.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks true range with a gap, the stop distance from ATR, and the link between the stop and the size. It also checks that ATR does not cover gaps.

Paper task

Mode: paper. No account and no order is needed.

  1. Pick five stocks. For each one, copy a sample of 20 bars by hand from a chart or a printed table. Write the high, low and close for each bar.
  2. Work out the true range for each bar that has a previous close.
  3. Find the ATR for the first 14 bars by taking their simple average. Then apply Wilder smoothing to the bars that follow.
  4. Compare your ATR with the ATR shown by the platform. Note the platform setting as shown on your screen. Zerodha’s charting manual does not list a default ATR setting.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • An ATR stop can be too tight for normal noise. It can also be too wide to fit the budget.
  • The risk budget still decides the size. A wider stop does not give you more shares. It gives fewer.
  • ATR cannot protect you from a gap that is larger than normal movement.
  • ATR is in rupees, not in percent. Converting it to percent is a separate step.
  • A tighter stop raises the raw share count. The caps still limit the final size, as shown in Position sizing step by step.

Remember

  • True range is the largest of high minus low, and the two gap distances.
  • ATR is an average of true range. Wilder smoothing gives recent bars more weight.
  • Stop distance = a multiple of ATR. The multiple is a choice that you test later.
  • Shares are still the minimum of the risk limit, the cash cap and the concentration cap.
  • ATR does not cover gaps beyond normal movement.

Sources and verification

  • J. Welles Wilder, New Concepts in Technical Trading Systems (1978). This is where ATR and Wilder smoothing come from.
  • Zerodha Varsity, volatility module (reference only).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It gives no default ATR setting, so read it on your own screen.
  • StockCharts ChartSchool, ATR page. It gives 14 periods and Wilder smoothing. The first true range is high minus low, and the first ATR is the average of 14 true ranges. (As of 11 Nov 2026. This is a common convention, not an exchange rule.)

RSI (Relative Strength Index)

Hook

Sana (hypothetical) opens a chart and sees a line marked RSI at 74. A friend tells her the stock is overbought, so she should sell now. Sana does not know what the number means or how it was made. The stock has risen for weeks. This chapter shows how the RSI is calculated, why the 70 level is only a convention, and why a high reading can last a long time in a strong trend.

The idea

RSI is a momentum indicator. It compares the size of recent gains with the size of recent losses. It gives a number from 0 to 100.

Build it in four steps.

  1. Change. Change = today’s close minus yesterday’s close. A rise is a gain. A fall is a loss, and you write its size as a positive number.
  2. Average gain and average loss. Add the gains over N periods and divide by N. Do the same for the losses. N = 14 is a common convention. The first averages use simple averages. After that, Wilder smoothing is used, as in ATR and volatility, used for stops.
  3. RS. RS = average gain / average loss. RS is the ratio of the two averages.
  4. RSI. RSI = 100 - 100 / (1 + RS).

Some words to know:

  • Momentum. How strongly the price has been moving up or down over a window.
  • Overbought. A label people use for a high RSI. It is a description of the recent gains, not an order to sell.

Some points to keep in mind.

  • 70 and 30 are conventional levels. Many charts draw lines at 70 and 30. These are reference levels. They are not signals.
  • Divergence is a guess. A divergence is when price and RSI move in different directions. Traders watch it. Its value has not been established.
  • Strong trends keep RSI high or low. In a strong uptrend, RSI can stay above 70 for a long time. A high reading does not mean the trend is over.

A rule such as “buy when RSI is below 30” is a hypothesis. It must be tested with costs and with data the rule was not built on. The book tests such rules later, in Lab 4. It does not present such a rule as a fact.

See it

Switch the reference levels on and off. Watch how RSI behaves during a strong rise. It can sit above 70 for many days. Then watch a sideways period. The reading moves up and down between the levels.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Four-period RSI. Closes: Rs 100, Rs 102, Rs 103, Rs 100 and Rs 102.

PeriodCloseChangeGainLoss
1102102 - 100 = +220
2103103 - 102 = +110
3100100 - 103 = -303
4102102 - 100 = +220
Total53
StepCalculationResult
1. Average gain5 / 41.25
2. Average loss3 / 40.75
3. RS1.25 / 0.751.6667
4. RSI100 - 100 / (1 + 1.6667) = 100 - 100 / 2.6667 = 100 - 37.562.5

For a four-period example, each average is a simple average of the four changes. A real RSI uses N = 14, and it uses Wilder smoothing after the first average.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Four changes are +1, -1, +2 and -2. Find the RSI for this four-period window.

Hints:

  1. Gains are +1 and +2, total 3. Average gain = 3 / 4 = 0.75.
  2. Losses are 1 and 2, total 3. Average loss = 3 / 4 = 0.75.
  3. RS = 0.75 / 0.75 = 1.

Answer: RSI = 100 - 100 / (1 + 1) = 100 - 50 = 50.

Independent exercise

Task. Four changes are +3, +1, -1 and -2. Find the average gain, the average loss, RS and RSI. Round RS to four decimals and RSI to two decimals.

Check your answer
  • Gains: 3 + 1 = 4. Average gain = 4 / 4 = 1.00.
  • Losses: 1 + 2 = 3. Average loss = 3 / 4 = 0.75.
  • RS = 1.00 / 0.75 = 1.3333.
  • RSI = 100 - 100 / (1 + 1.3333) = 100 - 100 / 2.3333 = 100 - 42.86 = 57.14.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the calculation of RS and RSI. It also checks that 70 and 30 are conventions, and that a high RSI in a strong trend is not an automatic sell.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Calculation practice only. Do not place any order.

  1. Choose ten stocks. Write their names in your journal.
  2. Once a week, at the same time of day, record the RSI for each stock from the chart. Note the time you used.
  3. Work out one RSI by hand each week from the closes you copied, to check the chart value.
  4. Do not draw conclusions from the weekly readings. They are for learning the calculation.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • A reading above 70 does not tell you to sell. In a strong uptrend, it can stay above 70 for a long time.
  • A reading below 30 does not tell you to buy. In a strong downtrend, it can stay below 30.
  • RSI measures momentum over a window. It does not measure whether the price is high or low in general.
  • A threshold rule needs a stop and a size rule. It does not replace them.
  • The chart may use a different period or a different smoothing method. Check the setting before you compare two charts. Wilder’s original RSI uses 14 periods. A chart tool may show another number, so read the setting on your screen.

Remember

  • Change = today’s close minus yesterday’s close. Gains and losses are kept apart.
  • RS = average gain / average loss. RSI = 100 - 100 / (1 + RS).
  • The result always falls between 0 and 100.
  • 70 and 30 are conventional reference levels, not signals.
  • A high RSI in a strong trend can last for a long time.

Sources and verification

  • J. Welles Wilder (1978), where RSI and Wilder smoothing come from.
  • Zerodha Varsity, RSI module (reference only).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It gives no default RSI setting, so read it on your own screen.
  • StockCharts ChartSchool, RSI page. It gives 14 periods and Wilder smoothing (the earlier average times 13, plus the new value, divided by 14). (As of 11 Nov 2026. This is a common convention, not an exchange rule.)

MACD

Hook

Tomas (hypothetical) sees the MACD line above zero on his chart. He takes this to mean the stock is in an uptrend, so he buys without a stop. Two days later the line falls below zero, and he has a loss he did not plan for. He never checked how the MACD is built or how often it crosses in a sideways market. This chapter shows how MACD is calculated, what a crossover means, and why it lags.

The idea

MACD stands for moving average convergence divergence. It is built from two exponential moving averages (EMAs) of the closing price. It has three lines.

  1. MACD line. MACD line = EMA(12) - EMA(26). The 12 and 26 are conventional periods.
  2. Signal line. Signal line = EMA(9) of the MACD line. This is a smoothed copy of the MACD line.
  3. Histogram. Histogram = MACD line - signal line.

A positive histogram means the MACD line is above the signal line. A negative histogram means it is below.

A crossover is a change of sign in the histogram. The histogram moves from positive to negative, or from negative to positive. Traders watch crossovers as possible signs of a change in trend. Their value has not been established, so treat them as a rule to test.

Some points to keep in mind.

  • MACD inherits lag. It sits on two EMAs, so it reacts after the price has already moved. See Moving averages for why EMAs lag.
  • Ranges cause whipsaw. A whipsaw is a string of false signals. In a sideways market, the lines cross often, and each crossing looks like a signal. Most of them lead nowhere.
  • Zero is not a command. MACD above zero describes the recent averages. It does not tell you to buy.

Each crossover is a possible trade. Every trade has costs and needs a stop and a size rule. A crossover does not remove those needs. See Costs on a trade and Position sizing step by step.

See it

Watch the histogram bars as they move above and below zero. Each sign change is a crossover. Then look at a sideways period. Count how many crossovers happen in a short stretch. The count shows why crossovers need a test before anyone relies on them.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Illustrative EMA values. The EMAs below are made up. In practice, a reader computes the real ones with a spreadsheet, using the EMA method from Moving averages.

StepCalculationResult
1. MACD lineEMA(12) - EMA(26) = 105.00 - 102.003.00
2. Signal lineEMA(9) of the MACD line, given as2.40
3. Histogram3.00 - 2.400.60, positive

Yesterday, the MACD line was 2.20 and the signal line was 2.30. The histogram was 2.20 - 2.30 = -0.10, negative.

Today the histogram is positive. Its sign changed, so there is a crossover. Remember that this is a sign change in a made-up example. It is not a forecast. It is also the kind of event that happens often in a sideways market.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. EMA(12) is Rs 98.40 and EMA(26) is Rs 100.10. The signal line is Rs -1.20. Find the MACD line and the histogram.

Hints:

  1. MACD = 98.40 - 100.10.
  2. Histogram = MACD - signal.

Answer: MACD = 98.40 - 100.10 = -1.70. Histogram = -1.70 - (-1.20) = -1.70 + 1.20 = -0.50.

Independent exercise

Task. Yesterday the MACD line was 1.10 and the signal line was 1.30. Today the MACD line is 1.60 and the signal line is 1.40. Find the histogram each day and say whether the sign changed.

Check your answer
  • Yesterday: histogram = 1.10 - 1.30 = -0.20.
  • Today: histogram = 1.60 - 1.40 = +0.20.
  • The sign changed from negative to positive, so there is a crossover.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the MACD line, the histogram and the meaning of a crossover. It also checks that zero is not a buy command.

Paper task

Mode: paper. A learning log only. Do not place any order.

  1. Pick five stocks. On each, find five crossovers on the daily chart.
  2. For each crossover, write the date and the price move over the next five days.
  3. Label the log as hindsight. You are looking back at results you can now see.
  4. Do not work out a success rate from five events. Five events are too few to mean anything.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • A crossover in a sideways market can reverse soon after. Each reversal can cost money and can hit a stop.
  • MACD above zero is a description, not an instruction to buy.
  • The histogram is the difference between two lines. It is not the MACD line itself.
  • Changing the periods from 12, 26 and 9 changes every reading. Fix them before you test a rule.
  • A crossover does not make a trade safer. The stop and the size still decide the loss.

Remember

  • MACD line = EMA(12) - EMA(26). Signal line = EMA(9) of the MACD line.
  • Histogram = MACD line - signal line. A sign change is a crossover.
  • MACD lags because it uses two EMAs.
  • In a range, the lines cross often, and most crossings are noise.
  • Each crossover still needs a stop and a size rule.

Sources and verification

  • Gerald Appel, the originator of MACD, as recorded in standard technical-analysis texts.
  • Zerodha Varsity, MACD module (reference only).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It gives no default MACD setting, so read it on your own screen.
  • StockCharts ChartSchool, MACD page. It gives a 12-day EMA minus a 26-day EMA, with a 9-day EMA signal line, on closing prices. (As of 11 Nov 2026. This is a common convention, not an exchange rule.)

Bollinger Bands

Hook

A reader sees a close above the upper line on a chart. The reader thinks, “The price is too high, so it must fall.” The reader sells the next morning. The price keeps rising for two more weeks. This chapter shows what the bands measure, how to calculate them by hand, and why touching a band is not a signal on its own.

The idea

Bollinger Bands are three lines drawn around the price. Each line has a job.

  • Middle band. The average of the last N closes. A common choice is N = 20.
  • Standard deviation (SD). A number that shows how spread out those same closes are. Small SD means the closes sit close together. Large SD means they are spread wide.
  • Upper band. The middle band plus k times the SD.
  • Lower band. The middle band minus k times the SD. A common choice is k = 2.

In words first: upper band = average + (k times SD). In symbols: upper = average + k x SD.

There are two ways to calculate SD:

  • Population SD divides the total of the squared differences by N.
  • Sample SD divides the same total by N minus 1.

Tools can use either one. John Bollinger’s own site says it uses the population calculation. Zerodha’s charting manual does not say which one Kite uses, so check before you compare numbers from two tools. (As of 11 Nov 2026, bollingerbands.com. This is a common convention, not an exchange rule.)

Some points to keep in mind:

  • The bands measure spread around an average. They do not measure what the price will do next.
  • When the price moves more, the SD rises and the bands get wider. When the price calms down, the bands narrow.
  • A close above the upper band tells you where the price sits compared with recent closes. It does not say the price will turn.

See it

Watch the middle line first. Then look at how far the upper and lower lines sit from it. Switch from population to sample SD and see how the bands move a little. Change k from 2 to 1 and see how much narrower the bands become.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Five closes: Rs 100, Rs 102, Rs 104, Rs 103 and Rs 105. We use N = 5 so the sum is easy to check by hand. We use k = 2 and population SD.

StepCalculationResult
1. Middle band (average)(100 + 102 + 104 + 103 + 105) / 5Rs 102.80
2. Differences from the average100 - 102.80, and so on-2.80, -0.80, +1.20, +0.20, +2.20
3. Squared differenceseach difference times itself7.84, 0.64, 1.44, 0.04, 4.84
4. Total of squares7.84 + 0.64 + 1.44 + 0.04 + 4.8414.80
5. Population variance14.80 / 52.96
6. Population SDsquare root of 2.96Rs 1.72
7. Band distance2 x 1.72, using the unrounded SDRs 3.44
8. Upper band102.80 + 3.44Rs 106.24
9. Lower band102.80 - 3.44Rs 99.36

Keep the extra decimals while you work. Round only at the end.

Hypothetical. Same closes, sample SD. Total of squares is still 14.80. Divide by N minus 1 = 4. Sample variance = 14.80 / 4 = 3.70. Sample SD = square root of 3.70 = Rs 1.92 (rounded). Band distance = 2 x 1.9235 = Rs 3.85 (rounded). Upper band = Rs 106.65. Lower band = Rs 98.95.

The sample version gives slightly wider bands here. The difference comes only from the divisor.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Closes Rs 10, Rs 12 and Rs 14. N = 3, population SD, k = 2. Find the middle band, the SD and the upper and lower bands.

Hints:

  1. The average of 10, 12 and 14 is the middle band.
  2. Square each difference from the average. Add the squares.
  3. Divide the total by 3 to get the variance. Then take the square root.

Answer:

  • Middle band: (10 + 12 + 14) / 3 = Rs 12.
  • Differences: -2, 0, +2. Squares: 4, 0, 4. Total: 8.
  • Variance: 8 / 3 = 2.667.
  • SD: square root of 2.667 = 1.633, which rounds to Rs 1.63.
  • Band distance: 2 x 1.633 = 3.266.
  • Upper band: 12 + 3.266 = Rs 15.27.
  • Lower band: 12 - 3.266 = Rs 8.73.

Independent exercise

Task. Closes Rs 200, Rs 204, Rs 196 and Rs 200. N = 4, population SD, k = 2. Find the upper and lower bands.

Check your answer
  • Middle band: (200 + 204 + 196 + 200) / 4 = Rs 200.
  • Differences: 0, +4, -4, 0. Squares: 0, 16, 16, 0. Total: 32.
  • Population variance: 32 / 4 = 8.
  • SD: square root of 8 = 2.828, which rounds to Rs 2.83.
  • Band distance: 2 x 2.828 = 5.657, which rounds to Rs 5.66.
  • Upper band: 200 + 5.66 = Rs 205.66.
  • Lower band: 200 - 5.66 = Rs 194.34.

All prices are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the arithmetic of the bands, the difference between the two SD methods, and why a band touch is not a signal alone.

Paper task

Mode: paper. This is a learning log, not a record of trades. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Choose five stocks from any chart you are allowed to view.
  2. Each week, write the close and whether it was outside the upper or lower band.
  3. Each week, write the band width. Band width = upper band minus lower band.
  4. Read the log at the end of the month. Write what surprised you.
  5. Do not use the log as proof that any band rule works.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The bands use only past closes. They say nothing certain about the next close.
  • In a strong trend, price can stay near one band for a long time. A touch can be the start of a trend, not a turn.
  • The result depends on the window and the SD method. Two tools can draw different bands from the same closes.
  • A band touch is not a signal alone. A rule needs a stated entry, a stop and a test before anyone uses it (Backtesting basics and the realism contract explains how a test is set up).

Remember

  • The middle band is an average. The upper and lower bands add and subtract k times the SD.
  • SD measures spread. A narrow band means the closes were close together.
  • Population SD divides by N. Sample SD divides by N minus 1. Check which one your tool uses.
  • A close outside a band is an observation. It is not a forecast.

Sources and verification

  • John Bollinger, Bollinger on Bollinger Bands (the originator’s book).
  • Zerodha Varsity, Bollinger Bands module (reference only, original wording).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It gives no default Bollinger setting, so read it on your own screen.
  • StockCharts ChartSchool, Bollinger Bands page. It gives a 20-period simple average, with bands 2 standard deviations away. (As of 11 Nov 2026. This is a common convention, not an exchange rule.)

Fibonacci retracement and the evidence debate

Hook

A reader draws Fibonacci lines on a chart after a fall. The price pauses near one line. The reader writes, “This level guarantees a bounce.” Then the price falls through the line and keeps going. This chapter shows how the levels are calculated, why the swing points you choose change them, and why the evidence that they predict price is still contested.

The idea

A retracement is the part of a move that the price gives back. After a rise, the price often falls back for a while before it moves again. Fibonacci retracement lines mark how far the fall might go, using fixed ratios.

For an uptrend, the steps are:

  1. Pick a swing low (the start of the rise) and a swing high (the top of the rise).
  2. Find the range = swing high minus swing low.
  3. For each ratio, a level = swing high minus (ratio times range).

In words first: level = swing high minus the ratio times the range. In symbols: level = high - (ratio x range).

The ratios used most often are:

  • 23.6 percent
  • 38.2 percent
  • 50 percent
  • 61.8 percent
  • 78.6 percent

Some points to keep in mind:

  • The 50 percent level is not a Fibonacci ratio in the strict sense. It is half the range, and it is a convention to include it. Label it that way.
  • The other ratios come loosely from the Fibonacci number sequence. That link is a pattern in numbers. It is not proof that prices follow the pattern.
  • Choosing the swing low and the swing high is a judgement. Two careful readers can pick different points and get different levels.
  • Many traders may watch the same levels. That is a plausible explanation for any reaction, but it is a hypothesis, not a fact.
  • The evidence that these levels predict price is contested. Treat each level as a zone, and check it against support and resistance (Support and resistance).

See it

Picture each level as a horizontal line drawn across the chart, between the swing high and the swing low. Read the range first. Then find each line. Notice that the 61.8 percent line sits lower than the 50 percent line. The worked example below gives the exact prices for one swing.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Swing low Rs 200. Swing high Rs 260. Range = 260 - 200 = Rs 60.

RatioRatio x rangeLevel = 260 - (ratio x 60)
23.6%0.236 x 60 = 14.16260 - 14.16 = Rs 245.84
38.2%0.382 x 60 = 22.92260 - 22.92 = Rs 237.08
50% (convention)0.50 x 60 = 30.00260 - 30.00 = Rs 230.00
61.8%0.618 x 60 = 37.08260 - 37.08 = Rs 222.92
78.6%0.786 x 60 = 47.16260 - 47.16 = Rs 212.84

Check: the 78.6 percent level is the lowest. Its distance from the swing high is the largest. It is 47.16, which is less than the full range of 60. So the level stays above the swing low.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Swing low Rs 150. Swing high Rs 180. Find the 23.6 percent level and the 61.8 percent level.

Hints:

  1. The range is 180 minus 150.
  2. Multiply the range by each ratio.
  3. Subtract each result from the swing high.

Answer:

  • Range: 180 - 150 = Rs 30.
  • 23.6 percent: 0.236 x 30 = 7.08. Level = 180 - 7.08 = Rs 172.92.
  • 61.8 percent: 0.618 x 30 = 18.54. Level = 180 - 18.54 = Rs 161.46.

Independent exercise

Task. Swing low Rs 200. Swing high Rs 240. Find the 38.2 percent level. Then say in one sentence how a different swing low would move this level.

Check your answer
  • Range: 240 - 200 = Rs 40.
  • 38.2 percent: 0.382 x 40 = 15.28.
  • Level: 240 - 15.28 = Rs 224.72.
  • Sentence: a lower swing low makes the range bigger, so the level moves down. For example, a swing low of Rs 190 gives a range of Rs 50 and a 38.2 percent level of Rs 220.90.

All prices are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the level formula, the difference between a convention and a ratio, and why a level is not a guarantee.

Paper task

Mode: paper. This is a learning log only. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Pick five charts with one clear rise and fall each.
  2. On each chart, choose the swing low and swing high, and write them down before you look at later prices.
  3. Calculate the 38.2 percent and 61.8 percent levels. Write them down.
  4. Later, record whether the price reacted near each level, and how.
  5. Write one sentence on how much your choice of swing points changed the levels.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The levels depend on the swing points you pick. Different choices give different levels.
  • The ratios are conventions with loose links to a number pattern. The evidence that they predict prices is contested.
  • A level that price respected once is not evidence that it will work again.
  • A Fibonacci level is not an exact stop. A stop needs a stated rule, a tested reason and a planned loss that fits your risk budget (Risk per trade and stop-loss distance).

Remember

  • Level = swing high minus (ratio times range). Range = swing high minus swing low.
  • The 50 percent level is a convention, not a Fibonacci ratio.
  • Swing points are a judgement. Write them down before you look at later prices.
  • Treat levels as zones and cross-check them with other evidence.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, Fibonacci module (reference only, original wording).
  • This book names no study on Fibonacci levels. Researchers have reached mixed findings on technical trading rules in general, so treat any single claim with care.
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting). It says Kite has more than 30 drawing tools. Look in the tools menu on your own screen to see whether a Fibonacci tool is there.

Volume indicators and VWAP

Hook

A stock rises on a day with heavy trading. One reader says, “The big volume proves the rise is real.” Another reader says, “Volume tells me nothing.” Both claim too much. This chapter shows how two volume tools are built, what each one adds up, and what neither one can show.

The idea

Volume is the number of shares traded in a period (Volume and what it adds). Two indicators use volume in different ways.

On-balance volume (OBV) is a running total. Each day, it adds or subtracts that day’s volume:

  • If today’s close is higher than yesterday’s close, add today’s volume.
  • If today’s close is lower than yesterday’s close, subtract today’s volume.
  • If the close is the same, leave the total unchanged.

The total starts at zero, or at any starting number you choose. The starting number only moves the whole line up or down. It does not change the moves.

Volume-weighted average price (VWAP) is an average price. Each price counts more when more shares trade at it. The steps are:

  1. Typical price = (high + low + close) divided by 3.
  2. Multiply each typical price by that bar’s volume.
  3. Add these products for the whole session. Add the volumes too.
  4. VWAP = total of products divided by total volume.

VWAP starts again at the start of each session. Some charts show VWAP on daily bars. That is a different measure. Zerodha’s charting manual does not give its own VWAP definition, so check the indicator on your screen before you rely on it.

NSE also uses VWAP for other jobs. Its daily settlement price for futures is based on the VWAP of the last 30 minutes of the day. That is a different window from the whole-session VWAP above. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Clearing settlement page. Check the NSE Clearing site.)

Some points to keep in mind:

  • OBV adds whole days of volume. It does not show how much of that volume came from buyers and how much from sellers.
  • VWAP is a reference line. Traders watch it, but its predictive value is not established.
  • This book uses VWAP only in an optional intraday module, on paper. It is not used for swing decisions.

See it

Look at the OBV line as you step through the days. It goes up on a day with a higher close and down on a day with a lower close. Turn VWAP on and watch it reset when a new session starts. Then compare the line with the price. Ask what the line adds, and what it leaves out.

Worked example

Hypothetical. OBV. Closes Rs 100, Rs 103 and Rs 101. Volumes 10,000, 20,000 and 5,000 shares. Start at zero.

DayCloseVolumeCompared with the day beforeOBV
1Rs 10010,000no earlier close0
2Rs 10320,000higher, so add0 + 20,000 = 20,000
3Rs 1015,000lower, so subtract20,000 - 5,000 = 15,000

Hypothetical. VWAP for three bars. The typical prices are rounded in the table. Keep the full value while you work. Here, the second and third typical prices are each 307 / 3.

BarTypical priceVolumeTypical price x volume (rounded)
1Rs 100.0010,00010,00,000
2Rs 102.33 (307 / 3)20,00020,46,667
3Rs 102.33 (307 / 3)5,0005,11,667
Total35,00035,58,333 (full precision)

VWAP = 35,58,333 / 35,000 = Rs 101.67.

The rounded column adds to a number one unit higher than the full-precision total. That small gap comes from rounding. It does not change the answer.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Two bars in one session. Bar 1: typical price Rs 200, volume 1,000 shares. Bar 2: typical price Rs 206, volume 3,000 shares. Find the VWAP.

Hints:

  1. Multiply each typical price by its volume.
  2. Add the two products.
  3. Divide by the total volume.

Answer:

  • Bar 1: 200 x 1,000 = 2,00,000.
  • Bar 2: 206 x 3,000 = 6,18,000.
  • Total of products: 2,00,000 + 6,18,000 = 8,18,000.
  • Total volume: 1,000 + 3,000 = 4,000.
  • VWAP: 8,18,000 / 4,000 = Rs 204.50.

Independent exercise

Task. OBV starts at 50,000. Day 1: close higher than the day before, volume 10,000. Day 2: close lower, volume 4,000. Day 3: close the same as the day before, volume 8,000. Find the OBV after day 3.

Check your answer
  • Start: 50,000.
  • Day 1, higher close: 50,000 + 10,000 = 60,000.
  • Day 2, lower close: 60,000 - 4,000 = 56,000.
  • Day 3, same close: no change, so 56,000.
  • OBV after day 3: 56,000.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the OBV rules, the VWAP formula and the reset rule, and why volume does not show direction.

Paper task

Mode: paper. This is a learning log only. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. For one stock, write the closes and volumes for 10 sessions from a chart you are allowed to view.
  2. Calculate the OBV for each day, starting from zero. Write each step.
  3. Record the relative volume (Volume and what it adds) for the same days.
  4. In the demo chart, look for VWAP in the indicator list; if it is there, note the time it resets, as your screen shows it.
  5. Write one sentence on what the OBV line did not show you.

Limits and common mistakes

  • OBV counts whole days of volume. It does not show whether buyers or sellers drove the volume.
  • The starting value of OBV moves the line. It does not change the moves.
  • VWAP resets each session. Compare it only inside one session, and only with the same definition.
  • VWAP’s predictive value is not established. The book uses it only in an optional intraday module, on paper.
  • Volume figures can differ between data sources and chart settings. Check what your chart counts.

Remember

  • OBV adds volume on up closes, subtracts on down closes, and changes nothing on equal closes.
  • VWAP = total of (typical price x volume) divided by total volume, from the start of the session.
  • Typical price = (high + low + close) divided by 3.
  • Volume shows how much traded. It does not show which way the price will go.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, volume indicators module (reference only, original wording).
  • NSE India, market timings and trading system pages, for session start and end. NSE says shares without the closing auction trade continuously from 9:15 am to 3:30 pm, and the closing auction for some shares runs after 3:15 pm. (As of 11 Nov 2026. NSE has announced changes, so check the NSE site.)
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting), for the indicators Kite offers.

Multiple timeframe analysis

Hook

A trader looks at a daily chart and sees a small dip. The trader then checks the 5-minute chart, the hourly chart and the weekly chart. Each one tells a slightly different story. After enough checking, the trader finds one chart where the picture looks clear. The trader then trades on that chart alone. This chapter shows a simple way to use two timeframes, and why adding more can fool you.

The idea

Multiple timeframe analysis means looking at the same stock on more than one timeframe (Timeframes and how the same price looks different). Keep the method simple.

  • The higher timeframe is a filter. It decides whether trades are allowed at all. For example, a weekly rule.
  • The lower timeframe is the trigger. It decides when, within the allowed trades, to act. For example, a daily rule.

You can build a higher-timeframe candle from lower-timeframe candles. The rules from Timeframes and how the same price looks different apply:

  • Open comes from the first candle in the period.
  • Close comes from the last candle in the period.
  • High is the largest high. Low is the smallest low.

Some points to keep in mind:

  • Pick two timeframes before you test anything. Write them down.
  • Each extra timeframe adds settings. More settings make it easier to fit a rule to past data (Overfitting and the number of things you tried).
  • Holiday weeks have fewer daily candles. A weekly candle built from a short week covers fewer days. The days when the market is shut are on the NSE trading holidays page.
  • A filter that passes is not a forecast. It only says the rule allows a look.

See it

Start with the five daily candles. Switch to weekly and see one candle replace them. Check that the weekly open is the Monday open and the weekly close is the Friday close. Then switch back and compare.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Five daily candles for one week. Each row shows open, high, low and close in rupees.

DayOpenHighLowClose
MonRs 290Rs 296Rs 288Rs 294
TueRs 294Rs 301Rs 293Rs 299
WedRs 299Rs 300Rs 292Rs 293
ThuRs 293Rs 297Rs 290Rs 291
FriRs 291Rs 298Rs 289Rs 296

Build the weekly candle:

PartRuleCheckWeekly value
Openopen of the first dayMon openRs 290
Highlargest of the five highs296, 301, 300, 297, 298Rs 301
Lowsmallest of the five lows288, 293, 292, 290, 289Rs 288
Closeclose of the last dayFri closeRs 296

The weekly candle is bullish, because the close (Rs 296) is above the open (Rs 290).

Hypothetical filter. Suppose the rule says: the weekly close must be above a 10-week average of Rs 285. This number is illustrative. Rs 296 is above Rs 285, so the filter passes.

Hypothetical trigger. Suppose the daily rule looks for a pullback to about Rs 291, near a 20-day average of Rs 290. This number is also illustrative. The trigger is read only after the filter passes.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Build one weekly candle from the five daily candles in the worked example. Confirm that the weekly candle is bullish.

Hints:

  1. The weekly open is the first day’s open.
  2. Find the largest of the five highs.
  3. Find the smallest of the five lows.
  4. The weekly close is the last day’s close.

Answer:

  • Open: Rs 290 (Monday).
  • High: Rs 301 (Tuesday’s high is the largest).
  • Low: Rs 288 (Monday’s low is the smallest).
  • Close: Rs 296 (Friday).
  • Bullish, because Rs 296 is above Rs 290.

Independent exercise

Task. A hypothetical week ends with a weekly close of Rs 280. The illustrative 10-week average is Rs 285. Does the filter pass? What does the framework say to do with the daily pullback that week?

Check your answer
  • The filter asks whether the weekly close is above Rs 285.
  • Rs 280 is below Rs 285, so the filter does not pass.
  • The framework does not allow trades for that direction this week.
  • So the daily pullback is not used. The rule does not read it, whatever the daily chart looks like.

All numbers are hypothetical and illustrative.

Quiz

The quiz checks how a weekly candle is built, what a filter does, and why more timeframes add risk.

Paper task

Mode: paper. This is a learning log only. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Choose five stocks from a chart you are allowed to view.
  2. Write down your two timeframes before you look at any chart.
  3. Each week, record the higher-timeframe filter result: pass or not.
  4. If the filter passes, record what the lower-timeframe rule says.
  5. After four weeks, write one sentence on whether you changed either timeframe during the test.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Two timeframes do not make a trade safer. A stop and a position size are still needed.
  • Each added timeframe adds settings. More settings make overfitting easier.
  • A weekly candle needs its full set of days. A holiday week can give a candle built from fewer days.
  • A filter that passes is not a forecast. The filter only allows a look at the trigger.

Remember

  • The higher timeframe is a filter. The lower timeframe is the trigger.
  • A weekly candle has the first day’s open, the highest high, the lowest low and the last day’s close.
  • Fix your two timeframes before you test. Do not add more after you see results.
  • Agreement across timeframes is not proof that a trade will work.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, multiple timeframe module (reference only, original wording).
  • Zerodha, Kite charting manual (kite.trade/docs/kite/charting), for the chart intervals Kite offers. It says intraday data is available from 2015.
  • NSE India, trading holidays page.

Indicator playground and when indicators disagree

Hook

A reader puts five indicators on one chart. Four say the price is rising. One says it is overbought. The reader feels confident and buys. Later, the reader changes the period of one indicator again and again, until an old trade looks like a winner. This chapter shows why that is self-deception. It also shows how to set a tie-break rule before you look at the data.

The idea

Indicators are built from a small set of inputs, mostly the closes, the volume and the high and low. Many indicators use the same closes. So many of them give the same answer.

Some points to keep in mind:

  • Indicators are often redundant. Two momentum indicators built from the same closes will agree most of the time for that reason. Count the inputs, not the names.
  • Disagreement is normal. Decide before you look which indicator, if any, decides.
  • Changing settings after you see results is the most common self-deception. A setting that looks good on past data may not work on new data (Overfitting and the number of things you tried).
  • The playground is for understanding. It shows how a formula reacts when a setting changes. It is not for finding the best setting.
  • Stops and position size still apply. They apply even when every indicator agrees.

The short rule: write the tie-break rule first, then look at the data.

See it

Move the period slider from 10 to 50. Watch how the average changes shape as the period grows. Use the predict mode before you reveal each answer. Write down your guess, then check it. Notice how often two averages with different periods give different answers on the same date.

Worked example

Hypothetical and illustrative only. One date. Close Rs 250. The readings below come from four indicators.

ReadingValueWhat it says
20-day averageRs 248Price is above the average, so the reading leans up
RSI72Above the common 70 reference, so it reads overbought
MACD histogramPositiveMomentum is leaning up
Upper Bollinger BandRs 255Price is below the upper band, so the band is not touched

Most readings point up. One points to overbought. Counting readings does not decide the trade. A rule written before the day decides.

Suppose the rule uses only the breakout test from The fully specified swing strategy. Then the RSI reading is not used at all. The breakout test decides, and the count of indicators makes no difference.

Parameter sensitivity. Same date, same close of Rs 250. Now change only the period of the average:

AverageValueAbove or below the close of Rs 250
10-dayRs 252Above
20-dayRs 248Below
50-dayRs 240Below

The 20-day and 50-day averages sit below the close. The 10-day average sits above it. The same date gives a different reading when only the period changes. That is why you fix the periods before you test.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. The close rises sharply for three days in a row. Predict whether a 10-day average will sit above or below the latest close. Then check your guess in the playground.

Hints:

  1. A shorter average reacts faster than a longer one.
  2. After a sharp rise, the short average moves up quickly.
  3. The average still includes older, lower closes. Ask how far the latest close sits above the average.

Answer: The 10-day average moves up quickly after the rise. It usually stays below the latest close, because it still includes older and lower closes. Check this on the playground, and try a different number of rising days.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a tie-break rule in one paragraph. Use at most two indicators. State what you do when they disagree, and what you do when they agree. Write it before you look at any chart.

Check your answer

A good answer has these parts:

  • The two named indicators, with their periods fixed.
  • The rule for agreement, for example: both lean up, so the rule allows the trade and the stop and size rules apply.
  • The rule for disagreement, for example: one leans up and one leans down, so the rule allows no trade that day.
  • A note that the rule was written before the data was read.

The rule should not change after a result. If it changes, write the change as a new rule and test that rule separately.

Quiz

The quiz checks how indicators agree, how a period changes a reading, and why settings must be fixed before a test.

Paper task

Mode: paper. This is a learning log only. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Choose two indicators from this track. Fix their periods and write them down.
  2. Write a tie-break rule for disagreement. Date it before you look at the data.
  3. On 10 days where the two indicators disagree, record the rule’s answer.
  4. Record the outcome later, after a fixed number of days. Write the outcome as a learning note.
  5. Write one sentence on whether any period change would have changed your answer.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Changing periods after you see results is the main way to fool yourself. Fix the settings before you test.
  • Agreement is not proof. Two indicators built from the same closes can give the same answer twice.
  • A disagreement rule written after a loss is hindsight. It is not a rule.
  • The playground shows how a formula behaves. It does not show which setting works.

Remember

  • Count the inputs, not the names. Many indicators use the same closes.
  • Decide the tie-break rule before you look at the data.
  • Fix the periods before you test. Record every setting you try.
  • Stops and position size still apply when all indicators agree.

Sources and verification

  • The sources listed in the earlier chapters of this track.
  • Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado and Zhu (2014), “Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism: The Effects of Backtest Overfitting on Out-of-Sample Performance”, Notices of the American Mathematical Society, 61(5), 458-471.
  • Zerodha’s Kite charting manual does not list default indicator settings. Read each setting on your own screen.
