Investing and trading styles

Hook

Two people each put Rs 50,000 into the market. One buys shares in a business and plans to hold them for years. The other watches prices all day and hopes to earn Rs 2,500 every day. Both people use the word “trading,” but they are doing very different things. This chapter sorts the words, compares the main styles, and shows which style this book focuses on.

The idea

Three words are often mixed up. They differ by purpose and holding period.

  • Investing means owning part of a business for a long time. The result depends on how the business and the wider market do over years.
  • Trading means holding for a shorter time, with written rules for entry, exit and risk. A trade can last a day or several weeks.
  • Speculation means betting on a price move without a rule you can test. The result depends on luck or on an event you cannot analyse well. The line between trading and speculation is blurry. A label does not prove that a plan has an edge.

Common trading styles include:

  • Intraday. Open and close within the same session.
  • Swing. Hold for days to a few weeks.
  • Positional. Hold for weeks to months.
  • Momentum. Buy what has risen and hold while it keeps rising.
  • Breakout. Enter when the price moves beyond a defined range.
  • Trend following. Stay in while the trend continues, and exit when it ends.
  • Reversal. Bet that a falling price will turn up, or a rising price will turn down.
  • Price action. Read the candles and structure directly, without many indicators.

The book sets seven criteria for a beginner. A style should fit all of them as far as possible:

  1. Protect capital first.
  2. No daily profit target as a goal.
  3. No need to watch prices all day.
  4. A fixed loss per trade.
  5. No leverage, which means no borrowed money to take bigger positions.
  6. Results that hold across different kinds of market.
  7. A clear downside before you enter.

Some points to keep in mind:

  • The scores below are the book’s judgement against these seven criteria. They are not evidence about how any style performs.
  • A style is a method, not a result. No style is shown here as profitable.
  • Round trip means one buy and one sell of the same shares. Every round trip has costs (Costs on a trade).

See it

StyleUsual holding periodWatching neededFit with the seven criteria
IntradaySame sessionHigh, all dayPoor fit. Needs watching all day. Often uses margin, which is borrowed money, so it conflicts with criterion 5.
Swing (end of day)Days to a few weeksCheck once after the closeFits well. Decisions are made from the daily candle, with a fixed loss set before entry.
PositionalWeeks to monthsLow, with a regular reviewFits well. Fewer decisions. Results are slow to show, so records take longer to build.
MomentumDays to monthsMedium to highMixed. Fits only with written rules, a fixed stop and a clear downside.
BreakoutDays to weeksMedium, around the breakoutMixed. Fits if the breakout is defined by a close, which can be checked at the end of the day.
Trend followingWeeks to monthsLow to mediumFits. Uses a fixed loss and a trailing stop. Long periods of small losses must be expected.
ReversalDays to weeksMedium to highPoor fit. Often tempts traders to add to a falling position (averaging down), which the book forbids.
Price actionVariesHigh, because reading is judgementMixed. Hard to test, so it is hard to check the fit with criterion 6.

The table describes common usage. It does not describe a rule for any style. Read each cell as a judgement with a reason, not as a fact.

Worked example

Dummy numbers. Not real charges. Suppose a round trip costs Rs 50 in total. The position is Rs 12,500. This Rs 50 is a made-up number to show the arithmetic. It is not a Zerodha charge and it is not a rate to use.

StepCalculationResult
Cost of one round trip as a share of the position50 / 12,5000.4%
Cost of ten round trips10 x Rs 50Rs 500
Ten round trips as a share of the account500 / 50,0001%

Ten such trades cost Rs 500, which is 1 percent of the Rs 50,000 account. This happens before any profit or loss. A swing style with one round trip every two weeks makes far fewer trades. Its total cost is much smaller for the same account.

Check: 0.4% of Rs 12,500 is Rs 50, and 10 x 0.4% of Rs 12,500 is Rs 500. The figures agree.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Classify these five examples as investing, trading or speculation. Give one reason for each.

  1. Buy shares in a business and hold them for years.
  2. Buy shares before a results date and sell the next day.
  3. Hold shares for about three weeks after a defined breakout.
  4. Place a bet each day that the price will move by a daily target amount.
  5. Put a fixed share of savings into a mix of businesses for many years.

Hints:

  1. Look at the holding period first.
  2. Ask whether the plan has a rule you can test.
  3. Ask whether the outcome depends on one event or a long business result.

Answer:

  1. Investing. The holding period is long and the result depends on the business over years.
  2. Speculation. The outcome depends on one event, the results, and no tested rule is stated.
  3. Trading. The holding period is defined and the entry rule is written.
  4. Speculation. The aim is a daily amount, not a rule. The result depends on luck, not a tested method.
  5. Investing. The horizon is long and the result depends on the businesses over time.

Independent exercise

Task. Score three styles against the seven criteria in this chapter: swing, intraday and trend following. Write one line of reason for each cell. Then state which style does not fit, and why.

Check your answer

A model answer:

  • Swing. Fits criteria 1, 3, 4, 5 and 7 well, because the decision is made after the close and the loss is set before entry. Criterion 6 needs a test. Criterion 2 is met if no daily target is set.
  • Intraday. Fails criterion 3, because it needs watching all day. Often conflicts with criterion 5, because margin is borrowed money. Its many round trips also add cost (see the worked example).
  • Trend following. Fits criteria 4 and 7, because it uses a fixed loss and a trailing stop. It needs long periods of small losses to be accepted, which tests criterion 1 (capital first).
  • Does not fit: intraday. It conflicts with criterion 3 and criterion 5 directly, and the book does not teach it as practice.

Quiz

The quiz checks the three words, the book’s focus, and why a daily goal is not a plan.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write down the style you would consider first, and why.
  2. Write down the holding period you expect, in days or weeks.
  3. Write down the seven criteria and mark each one as met, not met or not known yet.
  4. List three things that would make you reject that style.
  5. Do not record a live account or a real order in this note.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The scores in this chapter are the book’s judgement. They are not market evidence about any style’s results.
  • No style is shown as profitable. The table describes methods and fit, not results.
  • The line between trading and speculation is blurry. A label does not prove that a plan has an edge.
  • Investing is not risk-free. A business can lose value over many years.
  • Tax and charge treatment can differ by style. As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists zero brokerage on delivery trades, and 0.03% or Rs 20 per order (whichever is lower) on intraday trades. The law treats a trade that is not settled by delivery as speculative, with different loss rules from delivery gains. The details are in Costs on a trade and Taxes on trading profits. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page and incometax.gov.in, and ask a qualified tax professional.

Remember

  • Investing is long-term ownership. Trading uses written rules for a shorter stay. Speculation has no rule you can test.
  • Compare styles on holding period, watching needed and fit with the criteria.
  • A daily profit target is a goal, not a method. This book does not use one (The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check).
  • The book focuses on end-of-day swing and positional trading. These fit its criteria best.

Sources and verification

  • SEBI investor education pages, for investor awareness material.
  • Zerodha Varsity, trading styles module (reference only, original wording).
  • Zerodha charges page, read on 11 Nov 2026, for brokerage by product.
  • Taxes on trading profits, for the tax rules, with their own sources.

What a trading edge is

Hook

A friend says, “My method wins eight out of ten trades, so I have an edge.” That sounds strong. Here is a hypothetical example. Each win gains Rs 100. Each loss costs Rs 900. Eight wins give Rs 800. Two losses cost Rs 1,800. Overall, the method loses Rs 1,000. Winning often does not prove that a method works. This chapter shows how to measure an edge properly.

The idea

An edge is a reason to expect a positive result from a method. In this book, an edge means an average result per trade that stays positive after costs. Costs are the charges you pay on each trade, such as brokerage, exchange charges and taxes.

An edge is only a hypothesis until you measure it. A hypothesis is a clear statement that a test could prove wrong. Write it down before you test it. Use this form:

If condition X occurs, the average trade result will be positive over N trades. The result is measured in R. The test uses data that was not used to design the rule.

R is the planned loss on one trade. If your plan risks Rs 500 on a trade, then 1R is Rs 500. A winner of Rs 1,000 is +2R. A loser of Rs 500 is -1R. Measuring in R lets you compare trades of different sizes.

Every method must answer four decisions:

  • Entry: when do you open the trade?
  • Exit: when do you close it? This includes the stop, which is the price where you accept a loss.
  • Sizing: how many shares do you buy? See Position sizing step by step.
  • Filters: which signals do you skip, even when the entry rule is met?

Expectancy is the average result per trade, measured in R. In words, it is the win rate times the average win, minus the loss rate times the average loss. The loss rate is one minus the win rate. In symbols, E = (W x AW) - ((1 - W) x AL). W is the win rate. AW is the average win in R. AL is the average loss in R. See Expectancy and profit factor for more.

Costs reduce expectancy. A cost of Rs 50 on a trade with 1R of Rs 500 is 0.1R. That is small for one trade. Over many trades, costs add up.

Sample size matters. A few trades can look strong by luck. Imagine a coin that lands heads six times in ten tosses. That does not prove the coin is unfair. Ten trades show very little. Many more are needed before a result says much.

Testing a rule on the same data you used to build it is a common error. The result looks better than it really is. Keep some data aside and test on that. The chapter Overfitting and the number of things you tried explains why.

This book does not assume you have an edge. Many ideas that look promising show no edge when they are measured.

See it

Press Step to move the simulator one bar at a time. At each bar, check three things. Is the entry rule met? Where is the stop? Has the exit rule triggered? The step log shows the answer for each bar in a row. Read the log from top to bottom, the same way the price moved.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Win rate 40 percent. Average win 2R. Average loss 1R. R = Rs 500. Assumed cost Rs 50 per trade.

StepCalculationResult
1. Win part0.40 x 2R0.80R
2. Loss part0.60 x 1R0.60R
3. Expectancy0.80R - 0.60R0.20R per trade
4. In rupees0.20 x Rs 500Rs 100 per trade
5. After costsRs 100 - Rs 50Rs 50 per trade

The Rs 50 cost is a round number for practice, not the real charge. As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists zero brokerage on delivery trades, and 0.03% or Rs 20 per order (whichever is lower) on intraday trades, plus taxes and exchange fees. The full table is in Costs on a trade. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page. Taxes on gains are not included here.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Win rate 30 percent. Average win 3R. Average loss 1R. Compute the expectancy in R.

Hints:

  1. Multiply the win rate by the average win.
  2. Multiply the loss rate by the average loss. The loss rate is 1 minus 0.30.
  3. Subtract the second result from the first.

Answer: 0.30 x 3 = 0.90R. The loss rate is 0.70. 0.70 x 1 = 0.70R. 0.90R - 0.70R = 0.20R per trade.

Independent exercise

Task. Write one hypothesis for a breakout idea. Then write four rule definitions: entry, exit (including the stop), sizing and filters. Do not write any expected profit.

Check your answer

A sample answer, with example numbers only:

  • Hypothesis: If price closes above the highest high of the previous 20 days, the average trade result will be positive over 50 trades. The result is measured in R, on data not used to design the rule.
  • Entry: buy at the next day’s open after the signal close.
  • Exit: close when price falls to the trailing stop.
  • Stop: set 2 ATR below the entry price at the time of entry.
  • Sizing: risk 1 percent of capital, and apply the cash cap and the concentration cap.
  • Filters: skip signals when the daily loss limit has been reached.

Check that the answer has no profit figure. The numbers above are examples, not results.

Quiz

The quiz checks the expectancy formula, the form of a hypothesis, and why a streak of wins is not proof.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write one hypothesis in the form shown in The idea.
  2. Write the four decisions: entry, exit (including the stop), sizing and filters.
  3. Write the test that would show you are wrong. Name the data you will use, and say it was not used to design the rule.
  4. Do not write any profit target.
  5. Mark the page as hypothetical.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A high win rate alone does not show a positive expectancy. The size of the wins and the losses matters too.
  • Costs matter at any account size. A Rs 50 cost on a Rs 500 planned loss takes 0.1R from every trade.
  • Eight wins in ten trades is a small sample. Luck can produce it.
  • A rule built and tested on the same charts looks better than it is.
  • Naming the test before you run it helps you. It does not prove that an edge exists.

Remember

  • An edge is a hypothesis until it is measured after costs.
  • Write the hypothesis, the rules and the test before you look at results.
  • Expectancy = (win rate x average win in R) minus (loss rate x average loss in R).
  • A high win rate alone does not show positive expectancy.
  • Ten or twenty trades cannot separate skill from luck.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, material on expectancy and trading systems (reference only).
  • Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado and Zhu (2014), “Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism: The Effects of Backtest Overfitting on Out-of-Sample Performance”, Notices of the American Mathematical Society, 61(5), 458-471. On trying many rules on the same past data.
  • Harvey, Liu and Zhu (2016), “… and the Cross-Section of Expected Returns”, Review of Financial Studies, 29(1), 5-68. On how many tests make a lucky result likely.
  • Zerodha charges page, read on 11 Nov 2026, for the real charges next to the Rs 50 example.

Trend following in depth

Hook

A trader buys a share that has been rising. Her plan allows a planned loss of Rs 500 per trade. After eight losing trades in a row, she drops the plan and trades by feel. Hypothetical example: the next signal the plan would have taken was a large winner. She missed it, because she had left the rules. This chapter explains why a losing run is part of the design, not a sign to abandon it.

The idea

Trend following means you enter when price moves up strongly and stays strong. You hold while the move continues. You exit when the move ends. See Trend and market structure.

The method accepts many small losses. It aims for fewer, larger wins. In this style there is no fixed profit cap. You do not set a profit target in advance. Every trade still has a loss cap.

Fixed loss per trade. Each trade risks the same rupee amount. This is called 1R. For example, 1R may be Rs 500. A losing trade then costs about 1R. Each loss has the same size, so you can count the results in R.

Trailing stop. A trailing stop moves up as price rises. It never moves down. It is an exit rule, not a profit target. When price falls back to the stop, the trade closes. The distance of the stop can be set using ATR. See ATR and volatility, used for stops.

Low win rate. A method can win only 35 percent of its trades and still have a positive expectancy. This happens when the average win is much larger than the average loss. Expectancy is explained in What a trading edge is. A low win rate is a hypothesis to test. It is not a fact.

Dry spells. A dry spell is a long run with no winners, or many small losses in a row. Dry spells are normal in this design. They are also the most dangerous time to change the rules or add money. Write the rule for a dry spell before one starts. See Living through a losing streak.

This method does not add money after losses.

Sample size. A few dozen trades cannot show whether the method works. Results from a small sample can come from luck.

See it

Use the trailing-stop view first. Watch the stop rise with each new high close, and watch it stay where it is when price falls. Then use the streak view. Look at how often long losing runs appear, even at a fixed win rate.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Twenty trades. 1R = Rs 500. Seven trades are wins, and each win is +3R, which is Rs 1,500. Thirteen trades are losses, and each loss is -1R, which is Rs 500.

ItemCalculationResult
Total from wins7 x 3R = 21R, then 21 x Rs 500Rs 10,500
Total from losses13 x 1R = 13R, then 13 x Rs 500minus Rs 6,500
Net gross21R - 13R = 8R, then 8 x Rs 500Rs 4,000
Win rate7 / 2035 percent
Expectancy in R0.35 x 3 - 0.65 x 1 = 1.05 - 0.650.40R per trade
Expectancy in rupees0.40 x Rs 500Rs 200 per trade
A run of 10 losses10 x Rs 500minus Rs 5,000

These figures are before costs and taxes. Real charges and the rules on tax are in Costs on a trade and Taxes on trading profits. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists zero brokerage on delivery trades. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page.)

The losing run is the hard part. The run of 10 losses costs Rs 5,000 with no winner in between. The plan must allow for it before it starts.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Five trades are wins at +2R. Ten trades are losses at -1R. R = Rs 500. Compute the net result in R and in rupees.

Hints:

  1. The five wins total 10R.
  2. The ten losses total 10R.
  3. Subtract the losses from the wins.

Answer: Wins: 5 x 2R = 10R. Losses: 10 x 1R = 10R. Net: 10R - 10R = 0R, which is Rs 0 gross.

Independent exercise

Task. Forty trades. Win rate 30 percent. Average win 3R. Average loss 1R. R = Rs 500. Compute the expectancy in R, the expectancy in rupees per trade, and the total gross result.

Check your answer
  • Expectancy: 0.30 x 3 - 0.70 x 1 = 0.90 - 0.70 = 0.20R.
  • In rupees: 0.20 x Rs 500 = Rs 100 per trade.
  • Total gross: 40 x Rs 100 = Rs 4,000.
  • Check by count: 12 wins x 3R = 36R. 28 losses x 1R = 28R. 36R - 28R = 8R. 8 x Rs 500 = Rs 4,000.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the low win rate, the trailing stop and the rules for losing runs.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write the rules for entry, initial stop, trailing stop and the dry-spell rule before you look at any chart.
  2. From past chart history, pick ten trades that the rules would have taken. Use hypothetical trades only.
  3. Record the result of each trade in R.
  4. Add up the net R. Work out the expectancy in R.
  5. Label the whole set hypothetical.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A trailing stop does not stop every loss. A price gap can jump past the stop. See Gaps and what they mean for stops.
  • No profit cap does not mean no loss cap. Each trade still has a planned loss.
  • A low win rate is hard to live with. Plan for dry spells before they start.
  • Ten trades show almost nothing. A few dozen trades cannot show whether the method works either.
  • The figures here are before costs and taxes. See Costs on a trade and Taxes on trading profits.

Remember

  • Each trade risks the same planned amount, called 1R.
  • A trailing stop moves only up. It is an exit rule, not a profit target.
  • A 35 percent win rate can still give a positive expectancy, if the average win is large enough. This is a hypothesis to test.
  • Dry spells are normal in this design. Keep the rules unchanged during them.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha Varsity, material on trend following (reference only).
  • Costs on a trade and Taxes on trading profits, each with its own dated sources (read on 11 Nov 2026).

The fully specified swing strategy

Hook

A trader buys a share after a breakout. He has no written stop. The price falls, so he sets a stop after the fall, at a lower level. The loss grows far beyond what he planned. Hypothetical example. A complete rule set prevents this. Every rule is written before the trade, and every number is fixed in advance. This chapter goes through one example rule set, line by line. It is an illustration, not a recommendation.

The idea

This is a rule set to test. It is not advice. It is an example of how a strategy can be written in full. It carries no evidence of results. The book never recommends it, or any stock.

A complete rule set answers every question a trade can raise. When to look. When to buy. Where to stop. When to sell. How many shares. Each rule has a name and a number. The table below shows the rules of this example.

RuleWhat it saysNumbers used
SetupToday’s close is above the highest high of the previous 55 bars, not counting today. Today’s close is also above the 50-day simple moving average.55 bars, 50 days
EntryBuy at the next day’s open after the setup close.Next open
Initial stopEntry price minus 2.5 times ATR(14).2.5, 14
ExitHighest close since entry minus 3 times ATR(14). The trailing stop only moves up.3, 14
SizingRisk 1 percent of capital per trade. Apply the cash cap and the 25 percent concentration cap.1 percent, 25 percent
FiltersMinimum price Rs 50. Minimum 20-day average traded value Rs 5 crore. At most 4 open positions.Rs 50, Rs 5 crore, 4
Loss limitsNo new entries after the daily or weekly loss limit is reached.Set in the plan
Leverage and averagingNo leverage. No averaging down.None

Some points about these rules:

  • The setup uses the close, not the high. The highest high is only the level to beat. The close must beat it. See Moving averages for the 50-day average.
  • Entry is the next open. The signal is known only after the close. A trade cannot be filled at a price that has already passed. This is the realism rule. It also avoids Look-ahead bias.
  • ATR measures typical daily movement. The stop is set as a multiple of ATR, so it is wider when price moves more. See ATR and volatility, used for stops.
  • The exit trails. The trailing stop follows the highest close since entry. It never moves down. A bar that opens below the stop exits at the open. A bar that trades below the stop, but opens above it, exits at the stop price.
  • Sizing uses three limits. The risk budget, the cash cap and the concentration cap are all checked. The smallest one decides the size. See Position sizing step by step.
  • The filter values are choices. The price, traded value and position count are design choices for this example. They are not market facts.
  • Costs and taxes are included in any result. A result without them is not complete.

Changing a number after you see the results is a common trap. It fits the rule to the past. See Overfitting and the number of things you tried.

See it

Load the example rule set. Step through the bars. For each bar, read the setup check, the entry check, the stop and the exit check. The rule results show why the simulator acted, or did not act, on each bar.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Signal close Rs 255. The setup is assumed to be met. Next day’s open is Rs 257, so the entry is Rs 257. ATR(14) is Rs 4.00.

StepCalculationResult
1. Initial stop257 - (2.5 x 4.00) = 257 - 10.00Rs 247
2. Risk per share257 - 247Rs 10
3. Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
4. Raw sharesfloor(500 / 10)50
5. Cash capfloor(50,000 / 257)194
6. Concentration capfloor(50,000 x 25% / 257) = floor(12,500 / 257)48
7. Sharesminimum of 50, 194 and 4848
8. Planned loss48 x Rs 10Rs 480, which is 0.96 percent of capital

The binding constraint is the concentration cap, at 48 shares.

Later, the highest close since entry is Rs 280. ATR(14) is Rs 5.00.

StepCalculationResult
9. Trailing stop280 - (3 x 5.00) = 280 - 15Rs 265
10. Exit at the stopPrice falls to Rs 265Exit at Rs 265
11. Gross gain(265 - 257) x 48 = 8 x 48Rs 384
12. Gain in R384 / 4800.8R

The gain is Rs 384, before costs and taxes. The result is hypothetical. The real charges, such as Zerodha’s zero brokerage on delivery trades as of 11 Nov 2026, are in Costs on a trade, and the tax rules are in Taxes on trading profits. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Entry Rs 300. ATR(14) Rs 6.00. Capital Rs 50,000. Compute the initial stop, the risk per share, the shares and the planned loss.

Hints:

  1. The stop is 2.5 times ATR below entry. That is 2.5 x Rs 6.00 = Rs 15.
  2. The raw shares are floor(Rs 500 / risk per share).
  3. Check the cash cap and the concentration cap. Then take the smallest.

Answer:

  • Initial stop: 300 - 15 = Rs 285.
  • Risk per share: Rs 15.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 15) = floor(33.3) = 33.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 300) = 166.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 300) = floor(41.7) = 41.
  • Shares: minimum of 33, 166 and 41 = 33. The binding constraint is the risk budget.
  • Planned loss: 33 x Rs 15 = Rs 495.

Independent exercise

Task. The highest close since entry is Rs 320. ATR(14) is Rs 4.00. Compute the trailing stop. Then state whether the trailing stop can move down on a later bar.

Check your answer
  • Trailing stop: 320 - (3 x 4.00) = 320 - 12 = Rs 308.
  • It cannot move down. The trailing stop only moves up, so it stays at Rs 308 or rises on a later bar.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks how to read the rules, the trailing stop, and the setup check that uses the bar before today.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Pick one week of daily bars from your chart history.
  2. Apply the rules in the table above, one bar at a time. Write the decision for each bar.
  3. For each planned trade, record the planned fields: entry, initial stop, shares, planned loss and the reason.
  4. Mark every entry hypothetical.
  5. Do not write any profit figure.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This specification is not evidence of profit. A rule set that passes a backtest has not shown that it works live.
  • Forward testing and live trading are different. Prices, fills and emotions change when real money is at risk.
  • The filter values and the 55-bar and 50-day settings are choices. Changing them after you see results fits the past.
  • A gap can move the exit far from the stop. See Gaps and what they mean for stops. NSE puts a daily price band of 2%, 5%, 10% or 20% on most shares, measured from the previous close. If a share is locked at its lower band, there may be no buyers, and you may not be able to exit at all. Shares with futures and options use a flexible 10% range instead. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market and SEBI’s master circular on trading. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)
  • Costs, taxes and holidays are not in the arithmetic above. The exchange holiday list for 2026 is on the NSE site (16 weekday holidays, as shown on Zerodha’s market holiday calendar on 11 Nov 2026, which includes 15 Jan, added after NSE’s first list of 12 Dec 2025). Bars are only for days the market is open, so check the list before you count days.

Remember

  • A complete rule set states setup, entry, stop, exit, sizing and filters, with numbers.
  • Entry is the next open after the setup close. The setup uses the close.
  • The initial stop is entry minus 2.5 times ATR(14). The trailing stop is highest close minus 3 times ATR(14), and only moves up.
  • Shares are the smallest of the risk limit, the cash cap and the concentration cap.
  • A written example is not a recommendation and carries no record of results.

Sources and verification

  • Book chapters: ATR and volatility, used for stops, and Position sizing step by step.
  • The testing rules for realism, in Backtesting basics and the realism contract.
  • Costs on a trade and Taxes on trading profits, each with its own dated sources (read on 11 Nov 2026).
  • NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market (price bands) and NSE holiday list for 2026, read on 11 Nov 2026.

Why scalping and martingale are dangerous

Hook

A trader buys 100 shares at Rs 100. He aims to sell for Rs 0.10 more per share, a gain of Rs 10. He assumes a cost of Rs 50 for the round trip. Hypothetical example. The Rs 50 cost is larger than the Rs 10 gain, so the trade loses Rs 40 even when it works as planned. The Rs 50 is a round number for practice, not the real charge. The real charges are in Costs on a trade. This chapter shows the arithmetic behind five approaches that feel quick or clever, and why each one is dangerous.

The idea

This chapter covers five approaches. The book treats them as failure modes to recognise. They are not methods to practise.

Scalping. A scalper makes many trades, each aiming for a small gain. Every trade pays a cost. A fixed cost takes a large share of a small gain. The break-even move is the cost divided by the number of shares. It is the price move needed just to cover the cost.

Intraday trading. Intraday trades open and close within the same day. Some products used for intraday trades use borrowed money, called margin. Borrowed money makes gains and losses larger. See Leverage and margin. Intraday positions must be closed the same day. At Zerodha, open intraday share positions are closed for you at about 3:25 PM, and futures and options at about 3:26 PM, and each order closed this way costs Rs 50 plus 18% GST. Zerodha can also cut intraday leverage during the day. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support. Rules change, so check Zerodha support.) SEBI’s July 2024 study of individual intraday traders in the equity cash segment found that 71% of them lost money after costs in FY23. This book does not encourage intraday trading. Any later practice would be on paper only.

Averaging down. Averaging down means buying more of a share that has fallen. The average price falls. But the money at risk on the same idea grows. A fall in price needs a larger gain to recover. The bigger the loss, the bigger the gain needed. See Drawdown and recovery math.

Martingale. Martingale means doubling the stake after each loss. The stakes grow very fast. One long run of losses can cost more than the whole account. A high win rate does not remove this risk. A single long run is enough.

Options, in theory only. An option buyer pays a price called the premium. The buyer’s loss is limited to the premium paid. An option seller receives the premium. The seller’s gain is limited to the premium received. The seller’s loss can be far larger. Premiums, decay and margin are not covered here. See Options basics and Greeks.

None of these approaches is a shortcut to a daily profit target. See The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check.

See it

Start with the recovery view. Watch how the gain needed grows as the fall gets deeper. Then watch the stake view. Each loss doubles the next stake. The streak view shows how often a long losing run can appear.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Scalping. Buy 100 shares at Rs 100 (Rs 10,000).

StepCalculationResult
Target gross gain100 x Rs 0.10Rs 10
Assumed round-trip costPlaceholder for this exampleRs 50
Net resultRs 10 - Rs 50minus Rs 40
Break-even move per shareRs 50 / 100 sharesRs 0.50, which is 0.5 percent of Rs 100

Hypothetical. Averaging down. Buy 100 shares at Rs 100 (Rs 10,000). The price falls to Rs 80.

StepCalculationResult
Loss at Rs 80(80 - 100) x 100minus Rs 2,000, which is 20 percent
Gain needed to get back to Rs 100100 / 80 - 125 percent
Second buy100 shares at Rs 80Rs 8,000
New average price(10,000 + 8,000) / 200Rs 90
Loss at Rs 60 on 200 shares(60 - 90) x 200minus Rs 6,000
Comparison: one lot of 100 shares at Rs 60(60 - 100) x 100minus Rs 4,000

The average price fell from Rs 100 to Rs 90. But the number of shares doubled, and the loss at Rs 60 is larger. Averaging down did not lower the risk. It raised it.

Hypothetical. Martingale. Base risk Rs 500. Each loss doubles the next stake.

TradeStakeTotal lost if this trade and all before it lose
1Rs 500Rs 500
2Rs 1,000Rs 1,500
3Rs 2,000Rs 3,500
4Rs 4,000Rs 7,500
5Rs 8,000Rs 15,500
6Rs 16,000Rs 31,500

After five losses, the stake is 500 x 2 to the power 5, which is Rs 16,000. That stake is larger than a Rs 10,000 account. It is 32 percent of a Rs 50,000 account. The total lost after five losses is 500 x (1 + 2 + 4 + 8 + 16) = Rs 15,500.

Recovery math. A 40 percent loss leaves 60 percent of the value. The gain needed is 1 / 0.6 - 1 = 66.7 percent.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. A position falls 40 percent. What gain is needed to recover to the starting value?

Hints:

  1. After a 40 percent loss, 60 percent of the value remains.
  2. The gain needed is 1 divided by 0.6, minus 1.
  3. Give the answer as a percent to one decimal place.

Answer: 1 / 0.6 = 1.667. 1.667 - 1 = 0.667. The gain needed is 66.7 percent.

Independent exercise

Task. A scalp trade uses 200 shares at Rs 100. The assumed round-trip cost is Rs 60. Compute the break-even move per share, and the break-even move as a percent of Rs 100.

Check your answer
  • Break-even move per share: Rs 60 / 200 shares = Rs 0.30.
  • As a percent of Rs 100: 0.30 / 100 = 0.3 percent.
  • Check: 200 shares x Rs 0.30 = Rs 60, which equals the cost.

The Rs 60 cost is an assumed figure for this example only. All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the break-even move, the recovery math, the stake doubling and the risk of averaging down.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write one learning note for each of the five approaches in this chapter.
  2. In each note, copy the arithmetic from this chapter.
  3. Check each calculation again with a calculator.
  4. For each approach, write the single event that would cause the largest loss.
  5. Mark every note as hypothetical.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The break-even figures use a round-number cost for practice. The real charges are in Costs on a trade.
  • Averaging down does not lower risk. It increases the money at risk on the same idea.
  • Option figures here are theory only. Premiums, decay and margin rules are not covered in this chapter. See Options basics and Greeks.
  • A stake that doubles can exceed your account before the losing run ends.
  • The prices in these examples are invented. They do not predict any real share.
  • Intraday and option charges, margins and rules change. Check the Zerodha charges page, Zerodha support and the NSE site before you rely on any figure.

Remember

  • A fixed cost per trade is a large share of a small gain.
  • The break-even move is the cost divided by the number of shares.
  • After a fall, the gain needed to recover grows faster than the fall.
  • Averaging down raises the money at risk on the same idea.
  • Martingale can lose more than the whole account, even with a high win rate.

Sources and verification

  • SEBI, “Analysis of Intraday Trading by Individuals in Equity Cash Segment” (July 2024). It reports that 71% of individual intraday traders in the equity cash segment made net losses in FY23.
  • SEBI study of individual traders in equity derivatives for FY25 and FY26 (August 2026). It reports that 87.7% of individuals lost money in FY26.
  • Zerodha Varsity, material on risk and position sizing (reference only).
  • Zerodha charges page and Zerodha support on auto square-off (read on 11 Nov 2026).

Strategy simulator

Hook

A trader sets a stop at Rs 247. She expects to sell at Rs 247 if the price falls that far. The next morning, the stock opens at Rs 240, because of news overnight. Hypothetical example. She does not sell at Rs 247. The first price available is near Rs 240. Her loss is larger than the plan allowed. A simulator shows this step by step, so you can see the fill before you trade with real money.

The idea

A strategy simulator runs a set of rules over a price history, one bar at a time. A bar is one period of prices, such as one day. The simulator writes down what each rule says at each bar. It then shows the trade it would have made.

A simulator run follows the rules you wrote. It is not a record of real trading. The data can be real or synthetic. Synthetic data is made up for learning. Any total on screen is for learning, and it carries a hypothetical label.

The simulator follows four realism rules:

  • The decision is made at the close of day t. The rules read the close of the bar that just ended.
  • The fill is at the open of day t plus 1. The trade is filled at the next day’s opening price. You cannot trade at a price that has already passed. See Look-ahead bias.
  • The trailing stop updates at the close and applies from the next bar. A stop does not change in the middle of a bar.
  • A gap through a stop fills near the open, not at the stop price. If the bar opens below the stop, the exit is at the open. See Gaps and what they mean for stops. Circuit limits can also affect the fill. NSE puts a daily price band of 2%, 5%, 10% or 20% on most shares, measured from the previous close. If a share is locked at its lower band, there may be no buyers, and an exit may not be possible. Shares with futures and options use a flexible 10% range instead. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market and SEBI’s master circular on trading. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

The step log is the main teaching tool. Each row is one bar. It shows the bar’s prices, the result of each rule and the action taken. Reading the log is more useful than reading the final total.

See it

Press Step to move one bar. Read the decision on the close of the bar. Then press Step again and check the fill on the next bar’s open. Use Reset to start again. The step log shows each bar in a row. Check that every entry uses the next open, not the signal close.

Worked example

Hypothetical. The signal is at the close of day t. The close is Rs 255. The setup rules are met.

DayPricesRule resultAction
Day tClose Rs 255Setup metPlan an entry at the next open
Day t plus 1Open Rs 257Entry check passedBuy 48 shares at Rs 257. Set the initial stop at Rs 247.
Day t plus 2Open Rs 240Open is below the stop of Rs 247Exit at about Rs 240

The entry fill. The entry is Rs 257, not Rs 255. The difference is Rs 2 per share. With 48 shares, the difference is 2 x 48 = Rs 96.

The stopped trade. The planned loss is 48 x (257 - 247) = 48 x Rs 10 = Rs 480. The actual loss is (257 - 240) x 48 = Rs 17 x 48 = Rs 816. The ratio of actual loss to planned loss is 816 / 480 = 1.7.

The stop was at Rs 247, but the price was already at Rs 240 when the bar opened. The fill is at the open. The loss is larger than planned.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Use the same signal as the worked example. The day t plus 2 bar opens at Rs 250, not Rs 240. During that day, price trades down to Rs 246. The stop is Rs 247 and the position is 48 shares. Find the exit price and the actual loss.

Hints:

  1. Is the open below the stop? Compare Rs 250 with Rs 247.
  2. Did the bar trade below the stop during the day? Compare Rs 246 with Rs 247.
  3. If the open is above the stop, the exit is at the stop price.

Answer:

  • The open of Rs 250 is above the stop of Rs 247, so the open does not trigger an exit at the open.
  • The bar trades down to Rs 246, which is below Rs 247. The exit is at the stop, Rs 247.
  • Actual loss: (257 - 247) x 48 = 10 x 48 = Rs 480. This equals the planned loss.

Independent exercise

Task. Make your own three-bar example with these inputs. Signal close Rs 200 on day t. The entry is the next day’s open of Rs 202. ATR(14) is Rs 2.00. Capital is Rs 50,000. Risk is 1 percent of capital. The concentration cap is 25 percent. The following day opens at Rs 193. Write the step log. Find the shares, the planned loss, the exit price, the actual loss, and the ratio of actual loss to planned loss.

Check your answer
  • Initial stop: 202 - (2.5 x 2.00) = 202 - 5 = Rs 197.
  • Risk per share: Rs 5. Risk budget: Rs 500.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 5) = 100.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 202) = 247.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 202) = 61.
  • Shares: minimum of 100, 247 and 61 = 61.
  • Planned loss: 61 x Rs 5 = Rs 305.
  • The open of Rs 193 is below the stop of Rs 197, so the exit is at the open, Rs 193.
  • Actual loss: (202 - 193) x 61 = 9 x 61 = Rs 549.
  • Ratio: 549 / 305 = 1.8.
  • Check: 61 x 202 = Rs 12,322, which is below Rs 12,500.

All figures are hypothetical and made up for learning.

Quiz

The quiz checks the fill timing, the gap through a stop, and the rules for updating the trailing stop.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Choose a simple made-up three-bar price list. Label it synthetic.
  2. Run the rules from The idea on that list, bar by bar. Write one row per bar in a step log.
  3. Mark each decision as made on a close or filled on an open.
  4. If a stop is hit, write the planned loss and the actual loss.
  5. Label the whole log hypothetical. Do not state a profit or a result.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A simulator result is a set of rules applied to data. It is not the strategy’s performance.
  • Synthetic data does not show how real prices move. Real gaps and circuit limits can be harder.
  • The fill rules are assumptions. Real fills depend on the market at that moment, and on price bands.
  • A simulator makes it simple to try many settings. Trying many settings can fit the past without showing an edge. See Overfitting and the number of things you tried.
  • Costs are not in the examples above. The real charges, with dates and sources, are in Costs on a trade. As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha lists zero brokerage on delivery trades. Rules change, so check the Zerodha charges page.

Remember

  • The decision is made at the close. The fill is at the next open.
  • The trailing stop updates at the close and applies from the next bar.
  • A gap through a stop fills near the open, not at the stop price.
  • The step log shows the reason for each action. Read it row by row.
  • Every simulator result is hypothetical.

Sources and verification

  • The realism rules in Backtesting basics and the realism contract.
  • Gaps and what they mean for stops, for gap fills.
  • NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market (price bands) and Zerodha charges page, read on 11 Nov 2026.

Choosing no-trade

Hook

A trader loses Rs 500 on one trade and another Rs 500 on the next. The day’s loss is now Rs 1,000. Hypothetical example. He feels behind and wants to win the money back. A third setup appears. It does not meet all the rules, but he takes it anyway. This chapter shows how to decide, before the session, when not to trade. It also shows why a day with no trade can be a correct day.

The idea

No-trade conditions are written in advance, as a list with rules. They are not decided during the session, when emotions run high.

A no-trade decision can come from five groups of conditions.

Market conditions. There is no setup under the rules. The market is too thin to trade at a sensible price. See Order matching, spread and liquidity. There is a circuit or event risk. See Circuit limits and halts. Check each event date on an official source. For example, NSE publishes its holiday list, and company results dates are posted on the NSE and BSE sites. (As of 11 Nov 2026. Dates change, so check the exchange sites.)

Account conditions. The daily or weekly loss limit has been reached. A consecutive-loss rule has been reached. See Risk limits and Consecutive losses and losing streaks. The capital is below the minimum that the plan allows.

Data conditions. The prices are stale or the feed has failed. The symbol is unclear. A corporate action is unclear. See Corporate actions.

Personal conditions. You are short of sleep, distracted, or in a strong emotion after a loss. Revenge trading is excluded. It is a trade taken to win back money lost. See Loss aversion and revenge trading.

Plan conditions. The rule for the trade is not met. The plan does not allow the trade.

A no-trade decision is not missing out. Earning nothing on a day can be the correct result. A no-trade day has a result of Rs 0 and still follows the plan.

A rule that stops trading after a limit breach must be fixed in advance. When the limit is reached, trading stops for the day. Do not make an exception because the next trade looks good.

See it

Enter two losing trades of Rs 500 each. Watch the running total. When the total reaches the limit, the stop-trading state appears. Then try a day with no trades. The day’s result stays at Rs 0.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Example daily loss limit: Rs 1,000 for a Rs 50,000 account. This is 2 percent of capital. It is an example of the arithmetic, not a recommended limit.

EventTrade resultRunning total for the day
Trade 1minus Rs 500 (1R)minus Rs 500
Trade 2minus Rs 500 (1R)minus Rs 1,000
Limit checkLimit of minus Rs 1,000 is reachedNo more trades today

The day’s result is minus Rs 1,000, which is the planned maximum loss for the day.

A day with no setup. The rules find no setup. There are no trades. The result is Rs 0. The day is compliant with the plan.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Classify six scenarios as trade or no-trade. For each scenario, name the rule that decides.

Hints:

  1. Is there a setup under the rules?
  2. Has a loss limit or a consecutive-loss rule been reached?
  3. Is the data reliable?
  4. Is your personal state fit for trading?

Scenarios:

  1. No setup appears under the rules, so you wait.
  2. The daily loss limit was reached this morning. A new setup appears.
  3. The price feed shows yesterday’s last price with an old timestamp.
  4. You slept badly and feel angry after a loss.
  5. A setup meets every rule. The data is clean. The limit is not reached. You are calm.
  6. A company has announced a corporate action, and the terms are unclear.

Answer:

  1. No-trade. Market rule: no setup.
  2. No-trade. Account rule: loss limit reached.
  3. No-trade. Data rule: the price is stale.
  4. No-trade. Personal rule: your state is not fit.
  5. Trade. All conditions are met.
  6. No-trade. Data rule: the corporate action is unclear.

Independent exercise

Task. Write your own six-item no-trade list. Each item needs a rule and a category. Use the five categories from The idea.

Check your answer

A sample list:

  1. No setup under the rules. Category: market.
  2. Daily loss limit reached. Category: account.
  3. Two consecutive losses, as set in the plan. Category: account.
  4. Stale or missing prices. Category: data.
  5. An unclear symbol or corporate action. Category: data.
  6. Strong emotion after a loss, or poor sleep. Category: personal.

Each item has a rule and a category. The check is that every item can be decided before the session begins.

Quiz

The quiz checks the categories of no-trade, the loss-limit arithmetic, and the rule that stops trading after a breach.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Journal five no-trade days, one for each day.
  2. For each day, write the rule that decided the no-trade.
  3. Write the category of that rule: market, account, data, personal or plan.
  4. Record the day’s result, which is Rs 0 when no trade was taken.
  5. Mark the entries hypothetical unless they come from your own record.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The Rs 1,000 limit in this chapter is an illustration of the arithmetic. It is not a recommended limit.
  • No-trade rules must be written before the session. Writing them during the day invites excuses.
  • A no-trade rule does not close a position you already hold. Open positions follow their exit rules.
  • Waiting does not remove all risk. A stop can still be hit, and a gap can still cause a loss.
  • The calendar of holidays and events must be checked from an official source. NSE’s 2026 list has 16 holidays that fall on weekdays. One of them, 15 Jan, was added after NSE’s first list of 12 Dec 2025. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha’s market holiday calendar. Check the NSE site for changes.)

Remember

  • No-trade conditions are written before the session, in five categories.
  • A limit that is reached ends trading for the day. Do not make an exception.
  • A day with no trade has a result of Rs 0 and can be a compliant day.
  • Missing a trade is not a failure of the plan.
  • A no-trade rule does not close open positions. Exit rules still apply.

Sources and verification

  • NSE India, holiday list page for 2026 (read on 11 Nov 2026).
  • SEBI investor education material on trading risks.
  • NSE Basic FAQs on the Secondary Market, for price bands (read on 11 Nov 2026). Most shares have a daily band of 2%, 5%, 10% or 20%. Shares with futures and options use a flexible 10% range.
  • For each event you use in a rule, write down the official page that gives its date.

From idea to hypothesis to test plan

Hook

Imagine you have this idea: stocks that make new highs keep going up. You test it on old prices. You try three stop levels and keep the one that shows the biggest gain. The gain looks good. But you picked the stop after you saw the results. That choice makes the test unfair. The result can tell you very little. This chapter shows how to write an idea so that a test can really check it.

The idea

An idea is a feeling about the market. “Prices feel strong lately” is an idea. No one can test a feeling as it stands. No result would prove it wrong.

A hypothesis is a rule that can be wrong. It has a clear entry, a clear exit and a clear size. Each part is a number or a plain condition. Then a test can show that the rule failed. A trading edge is a rule with a real, repeatable advantage (What a trading edge is). A hypothesis is how you check whether an edge exists.

Falsifiable means you can say which result would make you drop the idea. A plan that says “I drop the idea if the average result after costs is not above zero” is falsifiable. If no result could prove you wrong, the idea is not ready to test.

Fix the pass or fail rule before you look at the data. Changing the rule after you see results is the most common way to fool yourself. The test then becomes a fit to the past. It stops being a check of the idea.

A test plan lists these fields before the run:

  • Universe: the stocks you will test.
  • Data source: where the prices come from. Read its terms.
  • Period: the dates the test covers.
  • Split: how the data is divided. One part is for building the rule. The other part is kept back for the final check (Out-of-sample and walk-forward testing).
  • Entry, stop and exit: the rules that decide when you enter, when you cut a loss and when you leave.
  • Sizing: how many shares each trade uses (Position sizing step by step).
  • Costs: the charges and slippage the test adds (Costs on a trade).
  • Metrics: the numbers you will read, such as expectancy (Expectancy and profit factor).
  • Pass or fail rule: the result that decides the outcome.
  • Number of variants tried: every version you test, including the ones you drop.

A test has three possible results:

  • Supported: the idea passed this test under its rules.
  • Not supported: the idea failed under its rules. Drop it or write a new idea. Do not move the rules to save it.
  • Inconclusive: the test did not decide. For example, it had too few trades to say much.

A passed test is weak evidence. It means the idea survived one test. It does not prove the idea works.

See it

flowchart TD
    A["Idea: a feeling about the market"] --> B["Hypothesis: a rule that can be wrong"]
    B --> C["Test plan: data, period, costs and pass or fail rule written first"]
    C --> D["Run one test on the agreed data"]
    D --> E{"Result"}
    E -->|"Supported"| F["Idea survived this test only"]
    E -->|"Not supported"| G["Drop the idea or write a new one"]
    E -->|"Inconclusive"| H["Too little evidence to decide"]

Follow the arrows from top to bottom. The test plan must exist before the run. Each of the three results leads to a different next step. No box in this flow says trade.

Worked example

Hypothetical. The idea is: stocks that make new highs keep going up. Here is one testable hypothesis written from that idea.

PartRule
EntryBuy at the next day’s opening price, after a day closes above the highest price of the previous 55 days.
Stop2.5 times ATR(14) below the entry price. ATR is the average true range, a measure of how far a price usually moves in one day. The 14 is the number of days used.
ExitTrail the stop at 3 times ATR. A trailing stop moves up as the price rises. It does not move down.
SizeRisk 1% of Rs 50,000 = Rs 500 per trade.
CostsThe costs from the cost settings in Lab 2.
Pass ruleExpectancy after costs must be above zero over the held-out period. Expectancy is the average result per trade. The held-out period is the part of the data kept back for the final check.

Now change the pass rule after a result. Say the first run gives an expectancy of minus Rs 20 per trade. You change the pass rule to “above minus Rs 50 per trade”. The idea now passes. But the new rule is a different, untested idea. The first test had already said not supported.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Turn each vague idea into a hypothesis. For each one, name the entry trigger, the exit, and the one number that defines “works”.

  1. Buy strong stocks.
  2. Sell when the market feels weak.
  3. Buy stocks that look cheap.

Hints:

  1. Name the entry trigger in a measurable way. Words such as “strong” or “cheap” need a number.
  2. Name the exit as a condition you can check on a price chart.
  3. Pick one number that decides “works”. For example, expectancy after costs above zero.

Answer:

  1. Entry: buy at the next day’s open, after a close above the highest close of the previous 20 days. Exit: sell at the next day’s open, after a close below the lowest close of the previous 10 days. Works: expectancy after costs is above zero in the held-out period.
  2. “Feels weak” is an exit idea for shares you already hold. Exit: sell at the next day’s open when a chosen market index closes below its 50-day average close. Works: the average result per trade after costs is above zero in the held-out period.
  3. Entry: buy at the next day’s open, after a close at least 10% below the 60-day average close. Exit: sell at the next day’s open when the close returns to the 60-day average, or after 20 trading days, whichever comes first. Works: expectancy after costs is above zero in the held-out period.

Add a stop and a size to each one, as in the worked example.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a complete test plan for a breakout idea of your own choosing. Include every field listed in The idea.

Check your answer

A sample plan. All numbers are examples set for this exercise.

  • Universe: 30 listed stocks, chosen by a written rule before the test starts.
  • Data source: a named free source, whose terms you have read. Many free sources allow personal use only. See Historical data.
  • Period: 2016 to 2022.
  • Split: 2016 to 2019 for building the rule. 2020 to 2022 kept back for the final check. Nothing is chosen using 2020 to 2022.
  • Entry: buy at the next day’s open, after a close above the highest close of the previous 20 days.
  • Stop: 2 times ATR(14) below the entry price.
  • Exit: the stop, or the next day’s open after a close below the lowest close of the previous 10 days, whichever comes first.
  • Sizing: risk 1% of a test capital of Rs 50,000, which is Rs 500 per trade, with the caps from Position sizing step by step.
  • Costs: the cost settings from Lab 2. Add slippage only if you set it.
  • Metrics: expectancy after costs, profit factor, and the largest fall from a peak in the test account (Drawdown and recovery math).
  • Pass or fail rule: expectancy after costs is above zero in the held-out period, with at least 30 trades. The 30 is an example number, set before the test.
  • Number of variants tried: 1, this plan. Any other version you try is logged too.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks falsifiable rules, pass rules set after results, what a passed test shows, and how to answer a teammate’s chart.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This is not a trade row.

  1. Write one idea of your own in one sentence.
  2. Turn it into a hypothesis with an entry, a stop, an exit and a size.
  3. Fill in every field from The idea list.
  4. Write the pass or fail rule and date it. Do not change it after any run.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • A test plan does not show that the idea will work. It only shows how the idea was checked.
  • A test plan is not a trading plan. Nothing in this chapter allows real money to be used.
  • Writing a rule down does not make it a system. A system needs tested rules, costs, sizing and a record of results over time.
  • Changing the pass rule after you see results is the most common error. Treat any change as a new idea that needs a new test (Overfitting and the number of things you tried).

Remember

  • A hypothesis is a rule that can be wrong.
  • Write the pass or fail rule before you see any result.
  • A passed test is weak evidence, not proof.
  • A changed rule is a new, untested idea.
  • A test plan is not a trading plan.

Sources and verification

  • Robert Pardo, The Evaluation and Optimization of Trading Strategies, 2nd edition (Wiley, 2008), on how to test a trading idea.

Historical data

Hook

Imagine you test an idea on a file of old prices. The file shows a share falling 50% in one day. Did the share really fall that much, or is the file wrong? A stock split can cause this kind of change. A bad row can cause it too. If you do not check the file first, the test can give a false answer. This chapter explains what price data holds, what it leaves out, and how to check it before a test.

The idea

Historical data is a file of past prices. Most free files have one row for each trading day. Each row holds five numbers. The five numbers are called OHLCV:

  • Open: the price at the start of the day.
  • High: the highest price reached during the day.
  • Low: the lowest price reached during the day.
  • Close: the price at the end of the day.
  • Volume: the number of shares that changed hands during the day.

OHLCV leaves things out. It does not show the bid and the ask, which are the prices buyers and sellers offer at one moment (Order matching, spread and liquidity). It also does not show the order-by-order path of trades inside the day. So a test on daily rows cannot know the order of events inside the day.

Corporate actions are company events that change the share price or share count. A split is one example. After a split, you hold more shares, each at a lower price. The total value stays the same. Other examples are bonus issues and dividends (Corporate actions).

Corporate actions create two kinds of price series:

  • Unadjusted: the prices as they traded on each day. The past stays as it was.
  • Adjusted: the past prices are rewritten to match the new share count. The old prices change in the file.

An adjusted series can contain information that was known only later. The adjustment is worked out after the event. If a test uses that number as if it were known on the day, it looks into the future. This is a form of look-ahead bias (Look-ahead bias).

Before a test, check the file. The import checks used in Lab 4 are these:

CheckWhat it looks forWhat to do
Sorted datesEach date comes after the one before itFix the order against the source. Do not guess.
Duplicate datesTwo rows for the same dayFind out why before you keep either row.
GapsA trading day missing from the datesCompare with the NSE trading holidays page. Some gaps are holidays.
Zero or negative pricesA price of zero or belowTreat it as an error to check.
OHLC consistencyThe high must be at or above the open and close. The low must be at or below the open and close.Check the row against the source.
Jumps over 20%A large move with no flag for a splitAsk the question: is there a flagged split? Do not delete the row on sight.
Zero volumeNo shares traded that dayCheck the day with the source before you use it.

Free data is not always complete or correct. The checks find some problems, not all of them.

Licence. A data source sets its own terms. Many free sources allow personal use only. Read the terms at the source. Do not assume you may share, sell or publish a file. Two real examples show why you must read the terms.

  • Yahoo Finance. Yahoo’s terms of service say you may not collect its data with automated tools such as robots or scrapers without permission. They also say you may not reuse the service for any commercial purpose, and you may not copy, sell or hand out its content without written permission. The terms do not describe the file columns or the adjustment method. So open your own file and read its header. Files often carry a column for an adjusted close, but check yours. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Yahoo terms of service. Terms change, so read them at the source.)
  • NSE. NSE’s data usage and sharing policy treats end-of-day and historical data as market data. NSE shares it through agreements and licences. So passing NSE data on to others needs NSE’s permission. NSE stopped its old daily “bhavcopy” file on 8 July 2024 and replaced it with a new common bhavcopy format (NSE circular of 12 June 2024). Check the NSE site for the file layout and the terms for each file. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE data usage and sharing policy. Terms change, so read them at the source.)

The bundled practice data in this book is made up. If you import real data, it stays on your own device. The source’s terms still apply to that data.

See it

StepWhat happens
1. ReadThe file is opened.
2. Sniff the headerThe row with the column names is found.
3. Map columnsEach column is matched to Date, Open, High, Low, Close and Volume.
4. ValidateEach row is checked against the rules in The idea.
5. PreviewYou see which rows pass and which rows fail.
6. Accept or excludeYou decide which rows to keep.

The import in Lab 4 follows these six steps. Read them in order. A row must pass the checks before it reaches a test.

Worked example

Hypothetical. A split. One share is priced at Rs 1,000. The company splits it 1 for 2. Each old share becomes 2 shares. The price becomes Rs 500.

Before the splitAfter the split
Your holding10 shares at Rs 1,00020 shares at Rs 500
Value10 x 1,000 = Rs 10,00020 x 500 = Rs 10,000

Your value did not change. But the unadjusted price series falls from Rs 1,000 to Rs 500. That looks like a fall of 50%: (500 - 1,000) / 1,000 = -50%. No one lost value because of the split. A test that uses the unadjusted series will still see a loss.

Hypothetical. A bad row. A second file has six rows. Each row is one trading day.

RowDateOpen (Rs)High (Rs)Low (Rs)Close (Rs)Volume
12026-03-022502542482521,20,000
22026-03-0325225625025598,000
32026-03-042552572532541,10,000
42026-03-052552582522511,05,000
52026-03-062512532492521,01,000
62026-03-0925225525125499,000

Row 4 has a low of Rs 252 and a close of Rs 251. The low is above the close. A day’s low cannot be above its close. So row 4 breaks the OHLC rule. The other five rows pass every check.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Check this six-row hypothetical file. Name each problem, the row it sits in, and what you would do next.

RowDateOpen (Rs)High (Rs)Low (Rs)Close (Rs)Volume
12026-03-021001029910150,000
22026-03-0310110310010260,000
32026-03-0310210410110355,000
42026-03-041031041011020
52026-03-0513013212813190,000
62026-03-0613113012913285,000

Hints:

  1. Check the date order first, from row 1 to row 6.
  2. Then check for duplicate dates.
  3. Then check each row’s OHLC rule, one row at a time.
  4. A jump over 20% is a question to answer. It is not an automatic deletion.

Answer:

  • Date order: passes. The dates run from 2 March to 6 March.
  • Duplicate date: rows 2 and 3 both show 3 March. Find out why before you keep either row. Do not keep both.
  • Zero volume: row 4 shows no shares traded. Check the source before you use that day.
  • Jump over 20%: row 5 closes at Rs 131. Row 4 closed at Rs 102. The rise is 131 - 102 = Rs 29. 29 / 102 = 28.4%. There is no split flag in the file. Ask the source. Do not delete the row without a reason.
  • OHLC rule: row 6 fails. Its high is Rs 130. Its open of Rs 131 and its close of Rs 132 are both above the high. Check the row against the source, or exclude it.
  • Clean rows: row 1 passes every check. Row 5 passes the OHLC rule, but its jump needs an answer.

Independent exercise

Task. In writing, explain what goes wrong if adjusted and unadjusted price series are mixed in one backtest.

Check your answer

A backtest that mixes the two series can see the same share at two different prices. Take the split from the worked example. Before the split, the series shows Rs 1,000. After the split, the adjusted series shows Rs 500 for the same past price, or the unadjusted series shows Rs 500 for the new price. Either way, the test sees a fall of 50% that did not happen to the holder. A stop or a signal can then fire on that fake fall. The test reports trades and results that did not happen. The result looks wrong, and the cause is the mixed data, not the idea.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks adjusted and unadjusted prices, the OHLC rule, and the jump check.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Use Lab 4 to import the bundled practice file and write a validation summary. If the lab is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet, using the six rows in the worked example.

  1. Write the row count of the file.
  2. List each row that fails a check, with the reason.
  3. Write what you chose to do with each failed row, and why.
  4. Record the summary in a learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • Free data can be incomplete or wrong. A file that passes the checks can still contain errors the checks cannot find.
  • More history does not always make a test better. Old prices may come from a time with different market rules. Read the source’s notes on the years you use.
  • A clean price file is not full market data. It has no bid and ask prices and no order-by-order detail.
  • Use data only as the source’s terms allow. Do not share, sell or publish the files.

Remember

  • OHLCV is one row per day: open, high, low, close and volume.
  • A split changes the price and the share count. The total value stays the same.
  • Unadjusted and adjusted series must not be mixed in one test.
  • A jump over 20% is a question. Check it with the source.
  • A row that breaks the OHLC rule is a row to check, not a typo to ignore.

Sources and verification

  • Yahoo terms of service (legal.yahoo.com).
  • NSE Data Usage and Data Sharing Policy (nseindia.com).
  • NSE circular of 12 June 2024 on the new common bhavcopy.
  • NSE trading holidays page.

Backtesting basics and the realism contract

Hook

Imagine a test shows a trade that exits at the stop price of Rs 490. In real life, the market opened at Rs 480 that day. The test says the loss is Rs 336. The loss from the real open is Rs 576. The test was too kind by Rs 240. A set of timing and fill rules prevents this kind of error. This chapter explains those rules. The book calls them the realism contract.

The idea

A backtest replays past prices and applies a fixed set of rules to them. It shows what the rules would have done on that past data. Every result of a backtest is hypothetical. The label on every result reads: Hypothetical backtest. Not live results.

The realism contract is a set of rules that makes the backtest act more like a real trade. These are the main rules:

  • Timing. A signal uses data up to and including the close of day t. The order fills at the open of day t+1. Day t is the day you see the signal, after the close. The earliest you can act is the next day’s open.
  • No same-bar fills. A fill at the same close that made the signal is not possible in real trading. You see the close only after the market shuts.
  • Stops, long trade. The stop is checked against the day’s low. If the open is already below the stop, the fill is at the open. If not, and the low reaches the stop, the fill is at the stop price.
  • Trailing stops. A trailing stop moves up as the price rises. In this contract, it updates at the close. The new level applies from the next day.
  • Costs. Every fill passes through the cost settings in Lab 2. The default is CNC, the delivery type, where you buy shares and hold them (Product types). Costs are covered in Costs on a trade.
  • Slippage. Slippage is a fill worse than the planned price. It is optional. You set it for each side of a trade (Slippage).
  • Sizing. The trade size follows the rules in Position sizing step by step. No leverage is used. Leverage means borrowed money, and this track uses none (Leverage and margin).
  • Same inputs, same result. The same data, the same settings and the same seed give the same result. A seed is the starting number for any random step. Each run saves a record of its settings and data. This record is called a manifest.

Profit and loss (P&L) is what a trade gains or loses. The net P&L is the result after costs and slippage. A backtest that leaves out costs shows a higher P&L than the trader would really get.

See it

The picture shows one day. The stop is at Rs 490. The day opens at Rs 480, below the stop. Move the open above the stop and look at where the fill moves. Then move the open below the stop again and compare.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1% per trade, which is Rs 500. The signal comes at the close. The next open is Rs 504. The stop is Rs 490.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget50,000 x 1%Rs 500
2. Risk per share504 - 490Rs 14
3. Raw sharesfloor(500 / 14) = floor(35.7)35
4. Cash capfloor(50,000 / 504) = floor(99.2)99
5. Concentration cap at 25%floor(12,500 / 504) = floor(24.8)24
Sharesminimum of 35, 99 and 2424

The concentration cap is the binding constraint. It is the smallest limit, so it sets the size.

Planned loss. 24 x 14 = Rs 336.

Gap through the stop. The next day opens at Rs 480, below the stop of Rs 490. The stop fills at the open, Rs 480. The loss is:

24 x (504 - 480) = 24 x 24 = Rs 576, before costs.

That is Rs 240 more than planned: 576 - 336 = 240.

The stop price did not protect the trade from the gap. The fill followed the open. Costs are not added in this example. They come from the cost settings in Lab 2.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Recompute the worked example with the stop at Rs 492. Find the shares, the binding constraint, the planned loss and the position value.

Hints:

  1. Risk per share changes. Recompute it first.
  2. Then redo the raw, cash and concentration caps, in that order.
  3. State which cap binds, and why.

Answer:

  • Risk per share: 504 - 492 = Rs 12.
  • Raw shares: floor(500 / 12) = floor(41.7) = 41.
  • Cash cap: floor(50,000 / 504) = 99. This does not change.
  • Concentration cap: floor(12,500 / 504) = floor(24.8) = 24. This does not change.
  • Shares: minimum of 41, 99 and 24 = 24.
  • Binding constraint: the concentration cap. It is the smallest limit.
  • Planned loss: 24 x 12 = Rs 288.
  • Position value: 24 x 504 = Rs 12,096, which is 24.2% of capital.

A closer stop gives a bigger raw figure. The concentration cap still sets the size at 24 shares.

Independent exercise

Task. The open is Rs 495. The day’s low is Rs 488. The stop is Rs 490. At what price does the stop fill, and why?

Check your answer

The stop fills at Rs 490.

The open is Rs 495, which is above the stop. There is no gap through the stop at the open. The day’s low is Rs 488, which is below the stop. So the low crosses the stop during the day. In this model, the fill is at the stop price, Rs 490.

Real fills can be worse than this model. See the limits below. How a stop order fills depends on the market on that day and on the order type you use. Read Zerodha’s support pages on order types, and the NSE price band pages, before you assume any fill price.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks same-bar fills, gap fills, cost placement, repeatable results and a spot-the-mistake on entry timing.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Use Lab 4 for a practice run. If the lab is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

  1. Make a made-up 20-day price series. Include one day that opens below your stop.
  2. Make the same series rise, so a trailing stop moves up and then exits on a later day.
  3. Before you run anything, write the expected planned loss, the expected fill on the gap day, and the expected P&L before costs.
  4. Run the series in Lab 4, or work it out on paper or in a spreadsheet.
  5. Compare the result with your expected figures. Write down each difference and its cause.
  6. Log the note in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • The realism contract is a minimum standard. It is not a promise that live fills will match the backtest.
  • The gap rule fills at the open. Real fills can be worse than the open, even when the low only crosses the stop.
  • The costs in this chapter are not calculated here. A backtest that leaves them out shows a result too high for a real trader.
  • A backtest that follows every rule can still be wrong about the future. Look-ahead, survivorship and overfitting are covered in the next chapters (Look-ahead bias, Survivorship bias, Overfitting and the number of things you tried).

Remember

  • A signal uses the close of day t. The order fills at the open of day t+1.
  • A fill at the signal’s own close is not realistic.
  • A stop that gaps below the open fills at the open, not at the stop price.
  • Costs enter every fill. The net P&L is the result after costs.
  • Every backtest result is hypothetical, and it is not a live result.

Sources and verification

  • NSE price band pages. Price bands can stop a share from trading beyond a set move in a day, which can change a fill. This book states no band.
  • Zerodha charges page (zerodha.com/charges), for the cost settings. The rates are not restated in this chapter, because they change.

Look-ahead bias

Hook

Imagine a rule buys when today’s close is the highest close of the last 20 days. A test fills the trade at that same close, Rs 300. But you only know the close after the market shuts. At Rs 300 you could never have bought that trade. The test gave you the price you wanted, not the price you could get. This mistake is called look-ahead bias. It makes a result look better than it could have been.

The idea

Look-ahead bias means a decision uses information that did not exist at the time of the decision. The test sees the future, even by a little.

It has three common forms:

  1. Same-close fill. The trade fills at the same close that produced the signal. The close was not known until the day ended.
  2. Full-sample statistic. A rule uses a number calculated over all the data, including years after the signal. For example, a percentile or a median across all years.
  3. Later data used as if known. Data that was adjusted or revised later is used as if it were known at the time. The adjustment factor is worked out after the event (Historical data).

Each form makes a result look better than it could have been traded. The effect usually points the same way. The test looks more profitable than the real trade would have been.

The lab’s peek switch shows the same rules twice. The peek run uses the leaking method. The honest run does not. Putting the two results side by side shows how much the leak helped.

See it

Result on the same rules (hypothetical)Peek runHonest run
Fill priceRs 300, the same closeRs 303, the next open
Cost of 10 shares10 x 300 = Rs 3,00010 x 303 = Rs 3,030
Shown resultLooks betterLower, and realistic

Read the two rows that matter. The peek run is cheaper by Rs 30 for each trade. That small gap is the leak. The honest run is the result you could have traded.

Worked example

Hypothetical. The rule: buy when the close is the highest close of the last 20 days.

  • Peek fill: at the same close, Rs 300.
  • Honest fill: at the next open, Rs 303.
  • Shares: 10 in each trade.

Per trade:

  • Peek cost: 10 x 300 = Rs 3,000.
  • Honest cost: 10 x 303 = Rs 3,030.
  • Difference: 3,030 - 3,000 = Rs 30 per trade.

Over 40 hypothetical trades:

  • 40 x 30 = Rs 1,200.
  • Check: Rs 1,200 / 40 = Rs 30 per trade.

The size of the gap depends on the rule and the market. The lesson is the direction. In this example, the peek run looks better.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. The four lines below form a rule set. One line leaks. Find it, and say which form of look-ahead it uses.

  1. Calculate the 20-day average close, using closes up to and including today.
  2. Calculate the median close across all years in the data, and use it as the level to beat.
  3. Buy at the next day’s open when today’s close is above the 20-day average close.
  4. Sell at the next day’s open when today’s close is below the 20-day average close.

Hints:

  1. For each line, ask whether the number was known at the close of the day it is used.
  2. Check any line that uses the words percentile, median, average over the whole dataset or adjusted.
  3. Compare the data each line can see on the day of the decision.

Answer:

  • Line 2 leaks. It uses a median across all years, including years after the signal. This is form 2, a full-sample statistic.
  • Lines 1, 3 and 4 do not leak. Line 1 uses closes up to today. Lines 3 and 4 fill at the next open.
  • A fix for line 2: use the median of all closes up to and including today, recalculated each day.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a rule that leaks through a full-sample median. Then write the fix.

Check your answer

Leaking rule: Buy when today’s close is above the median close of the whole data set. The median uses years after the signal, so the rule uses future information.

Fixed rule: Buy at the next day’s open when today’s close is above the median of all closes up to and including today. Recalculate the median each day, using only the data available that day.

All figures in this example are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the three forms of look-ahead, same-close fills, full-sample statistics and what a peek result means.

Paper task
