Mode: learning note. Use the peek switch in Lab 4 to run both versions of one rule. If the lab is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

  1. Choose one simple rule, such as a 20-day high breakout.
  2. Run the honest version and record its result.
  3. Run the peek version and record its result.
  4. Write the difference in a learning note, and the form of look-ahead that caused it.
  5. Log the note in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • Removing look-ahead does not prove an edge. The honest run can still lose money.
  • The Rs 30 gap in the worked example is one made-up case. The size of the leak depends on the rule. The direction is the lesson.
  • Adjusted data can leak in ways a simple check cannot find. A rule that uses adjusted prices needs a check of when each adjustment was known. Read your data source’s notes on how and when it changes past prices.
  • A peek run shows where the leak is. Do not record a peek result as the result of the rule.

Remember

  • Look-ahead bias uses information that did not exist when the decision was made.
  • The three forms are a same-close fill, a full-sample statistic and later-revised data.
  • A fill at the signal’s own close is not realistic.
  • A peek run that looks better is a warning, not a success.

Sources and verification

  • Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado and Zhu (2014), “Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism: The Effects of Backtest Overfitting on Out-of-Sample Performance”, Notices of the American Mathematical Society, 61(5), 458-471.
  • Robert Pardo, The Evaluation and Optimization of Trading Strategies, 2nd edition (Wiley, 2008).

Survivorship bias

Hook

Imagine you test a rule on today’s list of companies, going back to 2015. The test looks strong. But the companies that failed, merged or stopped trading are not on today’s list. The test only saw the companies that survived. So the result looks better than a trader could have got in 2015. This mistake is called survivorship bias.

The idea

Survivorship bias means a test includes only the things that lasted. The companies that fell out of the market are missing.

A company can leave the list in three common ways:

  • It is delisted. Its shares are removed from the exchange and no longer trade.
  • It merges into another company.
  • It stops trading for a long period, or its shares become worthless.

Companies that left in these ways drop out of today’s lists. A test built on those lists never sees them. The missing companies are often the ones that did badly. So the bias pushes results toward looking better, not worse.

The test universe must be the stocks that existed and could be traded on each past date. This is called a point-in-time universe. A test that uses it includes a company in each past year when it was really part of the market, even if it is gone today.

Lab 4 includes delisted synthetic tickers. It can also book a delisted stock’s loss at its last traded price. That loss is a setting you can change. It is not a real exit price.

Free data sources often lack the price history of delisted companies. That is a data limit. It must be stated in the test, not hidden.

See it

Stock (hypothetical)Two-year resultIn the survivors-only test?In the full test?
A+50%YesYes
B+20%YesYes
C-100%, delisted and worthlessNoYes
D-40%, trading stopped and it left today’s listNoYes

Look at the two columns on the right. The survivors-only test leaves out C and D. The full test keeps them. The result of the two tests differs by a lot.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Four stocks over two years:

  • A: +50%
  • B: +20%
  • C: -100% (delisted and worthless)
  • D: -40% (trading stopped and it left today’s list)

Survivors-only test (A and B):

  • Average: (50 + 20) / 2 = +35%.

Full test (A, B, C and D):

  • Average: (50 + 20 - 100 - 40) / 4 = -70 / 4 = -17.5%.

For a simple comparison, apply each average to Rs 10,000:

  • Survivors-only: 35% of Rs 10,000 = Rs 3,500 gain.
  • Full test: -17.5% of Rs 10,000 = Rs 1,750 loss.

The gap between the two results is 35 - (-17.5) = 52.5 percentage points. The survivors-only test shows a gain. The full test shows a loss. Both use the same four stocks. Only the list differs.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Add a fifth stock, E, which rose 10%. Recompute both averages. Then compare the gap between the two averages before and after adding E.

Hints:

  1. The survivors are now A, B and E. The full universe has five stocks.
  2. Work out each average to one decimal place.
  3. Compare the gap before and after E is added.

Answer:

  • Survivors-only: (50 + 20 + 10) / 3 = 80 / 3 = +26.7%.
  • Full test: (50 + 20 - 100 - 40 + 10) / 5 = -60 / 5 = -12.0%.
  • Gap before E: 35.0 - (-17.5) = 52.5 points.
  • Gap after E: 26.7 - (-12.0) = 38.7 points.

Adding E narrows the gap but does not remove it. C and D are still missing from the survivors-only test.

Independent exercise

Task. Name the data you would need for a point-in-time test of a strategy that trades only index members. For each item, say whether you could get it free.

Check your answer

You would need:

  • Past index membership lists, with the date each company joined and the date each company left. Without these, you cannot know who was in the index on each past date.
  • Price history for companies that have left the index, including companies that were delisted or merged. Without these, the test is still biased.
  • Corporate action history for each company, such as splits, bonus issues and mergers (Corporate actions). Without these, prices cannot be adjusted correctly (Historical data).

Whether you can get each item free depends on the source. Check each source’s own help pages and terms. Many free sources do not have all three. If you cannot get them, say so in the test plan. Do not hide the gap.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the definition of survivorship bias, the direction of its effect, and how to choose a universe for a past-date test.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Use Lab 4 to run the survivors-only test and the full-universe test on the same rules. If the lab is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet, using the four-stock example.

  1. Write the survivors-only average and the full-universe average.
  2. Write the gap between them.
  3. Write which stocks each test used, and why.
  4. Log the note in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • The four-stock example is made up. Real losses from delisting vary. Do not apply the Rs 1,750 figure to a real test.
  • Free sources often lack the price history of delisted companies. A missing history is still a bias, even when the reason is practical.
  • A point-in-time list fixes the universe. It does not fix the rules. Look-ahead can still enter the rules (Look-ahead bias).
  • A large list of stocks is not a fix on its own. A large list of today’s companies is still a survivors-only list.

Remember

  • Survivorship bias comes from testing only the companies that lasted.
  • Delisted, merged and suspended companies drop out of today’s lists.
  • The effect pushes results toward looking better.
  • The test universe must be the stocks that could be traded on each past date.
  • A data limit must be stated, not hidden.

Sources and verification

  • NSE Indices methodology and constituent pages (niftyindices.com and nseindia.com), for how an index is chosen and changed.
  • The help page of the data source you choose, for what it says about delisted shares.

Overfitting and the number of things you tried

Hook

Imagine a trader tries 20 settings and keeps the best one. On the old data, that setting shows a profit of Rs 12,000 on Rs 50,000. The trader is pleased. Then the same setting is tested on new prices and loses Rs 3,000. The trader did not find an edge. The trader found the best of 20 tries, which was partly luck. This is overfitting. This chapter shows how to count the tries honestly.

The idea

A parameter is a choice inside a rule. Examples are the number of days in an average, the width of a stop, or an extra filter. Each choice you try is a chance to find a lucky result.

A grid search tests every combination of the parameter choices. Each combination is a separate test. Count every one, including the ones you never report.

A lucky result is a good-looking result that appeared by chance. Suppose each test has a 5% chance of looking good by luck. Then 100 tests produce about 5 lucky results: 100 x 0.05 = 5. The numbers are simple, but the lesson is clear. The more tests you run, the more lucky results you will see.

Informal tweaking counts too. A variant you tried “just to see” is part of the number. Changes you did not record still count.

The in-sample data is the data you use to choose settings. The out-of-sample data is kept back and used only to check the chosen setting. The best in-sample result is biased upward. It was picked because it looked best on that data.

A heat map shows the result of every grid cell in one picture. The lab’s overfitting demo shows the heat map for the in-sample data. It also shows the result of the chosen cell on the out-of-sample data.

See it

Result (hypothetical)In sample (used to choose)Out of sample (kept back)
Combinations tried20Not tested again
Chosen setting, resultRs 12,000 profit on Rs 50,000 (24%)Rs -3,000 on the same capital (-6%)

The chosen setting looks strong on the data used to choose it. The same setting looks weak on the data kept back. The table shows the collapse. The heat maps below show all 20 cells, not only the chosen one.

Worked example

Hypothetical. The grid has 5 lookbacks and 4 ATR multiples.

  • Combinations: 5 x 4 = 20.
  • Best in-sample result: Rs 12,000 profit on Rs 50,000. That is 12,000 / 50,000 = 24%.
  • Out-of-sample result for the same setting: Rs -3,000. That is -3,000 / 50,000 = -6%.

The collapse is the lesson. The figures are made up. Counting the 20 combinations is part of the honest result. A report that leaves out the count hides the size of the search.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. A grid has 6 lookbacks, 5 ATR multiples and 3 filters. Count the combinations. Then state how many lucky results to expect if each test has a 5% chance of looking good by luck.

Hints:

  1. Multiply the three counts.
  2. Multiply the number of combinations by 0.05.
  3. Round the answer to the nearest whole number, or give a range.

Answer:

  • Combinations: 6 x 5 x 3 = 90.
  • Lucky results: 90 x 0.05 = 4.5. So about 4 or 5 lucky results.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a one-row-per-variant trial log template. Include the fields you need to count every variant honestly.

Check your answer

A sample template. Each row is one variant, including the ones you dropped.

FieldWhat to write
Trial numberA number that goes up by one each time. Never skip a number.
DateThe date you ran the variant.
Rule name and versionThe name of the rule and a version number.
ParametersEach setting used, such as lookback, ATR multiple and filter.
Data usedThe name of the data set and whether it is in sample or out of sample.
Result after costsThe net result, in rupees and in percent.
MetricsThe other numbers you read, such as expectancy and the largest fall from a peak.
Kept, dropped or changedOne word.
ReasonOne sentence on why the variant was kept, dropped or changed.

Keep every row, even when a variant was dropped. The number of rows is the number of tries.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the count of trials, the chance of lucky results, the best in-sample setting and what a report should show.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Start a trial log in your journal learning note. Record every variant you test in Lab 4 from now on, including the ones you discard. If the lab is not ready, keep the log on paper or in a spreadsheet.

  1. Create the log with the fields from the independent exercise.
  2. Add one row for each variant you test, in order.
  3. Add a row for each discarded variant too.
  4. At the end of the week, count the rows and write the total in the note.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A trial log is only as honest as the rows you write. A variant you did not record is still a trial.
  • Out-of-sample data can be spent. Once you use it to choose a setting, it becomes part of the in-sample data (Out-of-sample and walk-forward testing).
  • More testing is not always better. Each extra variant adds another chance of a lucky best result.
  • The 5% chance in this chapter is a simple example. It is not a measured figure for any real strategy.

Remember

  • Every combination in a grid is a test, even if it is never reported.
  • The best in-sample result is biased upward.
  • Informal tweaks count as trials.
  • Report the number of variants tried, with the result.
  • A chosen setting must be checked on data it was not chosen with.

Sources and verification

  • Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado and Zhu (2014), “Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism: The Effects of Backtest Overfitting on Out-of-Sample Performance”, Notices of the American Mathematical Society, 61(5), 458-471.
  • Campbell R. Harvey and Yan Liu (2015), “Backtesting”, The Journal of Portfolio Management, 42(1), 13-28.

Out-of-sample and walk-forward testing

Hook

Ana tests a trading rule on ten years of daily prices. This is a hypothetical example. She tries many settings and keeps the one that scores best. The score looks strong. But she chose the settings using the same years she then scored them on. That is like writing an exam and marking it with your own answer key. This chapter shows how to keep some data aside, so the test is honest.

The idea

Split the data by time, never at random. Part of the data is in-sample. You may use it to choose settings. The rest is out-of-sample. You may not use it to choose anything. The out-of-sample part is the test. It shows how the rule behaves on prices that did not help you choose it.

Why keep the order of the dates? Markets change over the years. If you shuffle the days at random, some later days land in the training part. The training then learns from prices that come after the test days. This leaks the future into the past. A random shuffle does not make the test fairer. It makes the test less honest.

The default split in the backtest lab is 70 percent in-sample and 30 percent out-of-sample, cut by time. The first 70 percent of the dates is for choosing. The last 30 percent is for testing.

One split tests the rule once. Walk-forward testing repeats the test many times:

  1. Choose settings on a training window.
  2. Test those settings on the next window. The choice did not use that window.
  3. Roll both windows forward by one step.
  4. Join the test results in date order.

The lab’s default windows are 3 years of training and 1 year of testing. They roll forward by 1 year at a time.

A test period stays honest only while nobody uses it to make choices. Suppose you look at 2023, change one stop, and test again on 2023. Now 2023 has been used to choose. It is in-sample again, and the second result does not test the rule. A test period that you look at once, with no changes, is still a test period. The same period, seen after tuning, is not.

A passed out-of-sample test is one more piece of evidence. It is not proof. Walk-forward testing does not prove a rule works either. It shows how the rule’s choices held up across several separate test years.

See it

RunTraining yearsTest year
12016 to 20182019
22017 to 20192020
32018 to 20202021
42019 to 20212022
52020 to 20222023
62021 to 20232024
72022 to 20242025

Read each row from left to right. The training years come first and the test year comes after them. The test year is never used to pick that row’s settings. Look down the last column: the test years run from 2019 to 2025 with no gaps.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Ten years of daily prices, 2016 to 2025.

Step 1. The 70/30 split by time.

PartYearsLengthUse
In-sample2016 to 20227 yearsChoose settings
Out-of-sample2023 to 20253 yearsTest once

Seven years is 70 percent of ten years. Three years is 30 percent.

Step 2. Walk-forward with 3 years of training and 1 year of testing, rolling by 1 year.

  • The first window trains on 2016 to 2018 and tests 2019.
  • The next window trains on 2017 to 2019 and tests 2020.
  • The last window trains on 2022 to 2024 and tests 2025.

Count the test years: 2019 to 2025 is 7 years. So there are 7 windows.

The joined test results cover 2019 to 2025 only. The years 2016 to 2018 were training data for the first window. They never appear in the joined test results.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Use data for 2019 to 2024, which is six years. Train on 2 years, test on 1 year, and roll forward by 1 year. List every window.

Hints:

  1. The first test year is the third year of data.
  2. The answer has 4 windows.
  3. Each window uses 3 years in total: 2 for training and 1 for testing.

Answer:

  • Window 1: train 2019 to 2020, test 2021.
  • Window 2: train 2020 to 2021, test 2022.
  • Window 3: train 2021 to 2022, test 2023.
  • Window 4: train 2022 to 2023, test 2024.

Four windows. The joined test results cover 2021 to 2024.

Independent exercise

Task. Explain why “settings optimised on all ten years, then tested on the last three” is not an out-of-sample result.

Check your answer

The settings were chosen using all ten years. That includes the last three years. Those years were already used to make choices, so they are in-sample. Testing them afterwards checks data that the choice was built from. It does not check data the choice has never seen.

Quiz

The quiz checks time order, counting windows, reuse of the test period, and what walk-forward does and does not show.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. If the backtest lab is not ready, do this task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

  1. Choose one set of settings and one set of daily prices. Use at least ten years, if you have them.
  2. Run one split: 70 percent in-sample and 30 percent out-of-sample, cut by time. Write down the last in-sample date and the first out-of-sample date.
  3. Run one walk-forward with 3 years of training and 1 year of testing, rolling by 1 year. Write down the first window and the last window.
  4. Use the same settings in both runs. Change nothing between them.
  5. Log the note in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

Limits and common mistakes

  • A good out-of-sample result is not a promise. It does not mean the rule will work with real money.
  • Three test years may not include every kind of market. A rule that passes in one kind of year may fail in another.
  • Walk-forward testing does not remove look-ahead bias or survivorship bias. Those need their own checks (Look-ahead bias and Survivorship bias).
  • Each change made after you look at a test period turns that period into in-sample data. Keep a count of how often you looked (Overfitting and the number of things you tried).
  • The joined test curve leaves out the early training years. In the worked example, 2016 to 2018 never appear in it.

Remember

  • Split by time. Never shuffle the dates before you split.
  • In-sample data may be used to choose. Out-of-sample data may not.
  • One 70/30 split tests once. Walk-forward testing tests in many windows.
  • Looking at a test period and then changing the rule turns it into in-sample data.
  • A passed test is one piece of evidence, not validation.

Sources and verification

  • Robert Pardo, The Evaluation and Optimization of Trading Strategies, 2nd edition (Wiley, 2008).
  • The backtest lab’s notes on split and walk-forward testing.

Metrics and their limits

Hook

A test report shows a win rate of 50 percent, a profit factor of 2.2 and a maximum drawdown of Rs 1,000. These are hypothetical figures. A reader may think the rule is both strong and safe. But the report is built from only six trades. Six trades cannot show whether a rule works. This chapter explains each number, shows how to work it out by hand, and says what each number cannot tell you.

The idea

Start with a list of trades. Each trade has a result in rupees. A winning trade has a positive number. A losing trade has a negative number. Keep the signs clear.

  • Win rate is the number of winning trades divided by the total number of trades. It is the share of trades that made money.
  • Average win is the total of the winning trades divided by the number of wins.
  • Average loss is the total of the losing trades divided by the number of losses. Show it as a positive number.
  • Expectancy in rupees is (win rate x average win) minus (loss rate x average loss). The loss rate is 1 minus the win rate. It is the average result per trade in this sample. It does not promise the next trade.
  • Expectancy in R is the expectancy in rupees divided by the rupee risk of one R. One R is your planned loss on one trade (see Risk per trade and stop-loss distance).
  • Profit factor is gross profit divided by gross loss. Gross profit is the total of the wins. Gross loss is the total of the losses, shown as a positive number. If there are no losses, the profit factor cannot be worked out.
  • Equity curve is the running total of trade results. It starts at zero.
  • Maximum drawdown is the largest fall on the equity curve. It is measured from a high point to a later low point. It is not measured from the start.
  • Sharpe ratio is the average return above a safe rate, divided by how much the returns move up and down. It assumes returns are spread in a fairly regular way around the average. Trading returns often do not follow that pattern. The result also changes with the safe rate you pick. The backtest lab uses 0 as the default safe rate and labels the metric as weak.

The lab shows a warning under 30 trades: too few trades to conclude anything. No single metric captures risk. A high win rate or a high profit factor does not mean low risk.

See it

Find the highest point before the largest fall. Then find the lowest point after it. The gap between those two points is the maximum drawdown in the example. The win rate and the other metrics are worked out from the trade list, not from the picture.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Six trades, in rupees: +1,000, -500, +1,500, -500, -500, +800. Planned risk per trade is Rs 500, so 1R = Rs 500.

TradeResult (Rs)Equity after trade (Rs)
1+1,0001,000
2-500500
3+1,5002,000
4-5001,500
5-5001,000
6+8001,800

Step 1. Wins and losses. Three wins total Rs 3,300 (1,000 + 1,500 + 800). Three losses total Rs 1,500 (500 + 500 + 500).

Step 2. Win rate. 3 / 6 = 50%.

Step 3. Averages. Average win = 3,300 / 3 = Rs 1,100. Average loss = 1,500 / 3 = Rs 500.

Step 4. Expectancy in rupees. (0.5 x 1,100) - (0.5 x 500) = 550 - 250 = Rs 300 per trade.

Step 5. Expectancy in R. 300 / 500 = 0.6R.

Step 6. Profit factor. 3,300 / 1,500 = 2.2.

Step 7. Maximum drawdown. The highest point is Rs 2,000, after trade 3. The lowest point after it is Rs 1,000, after trade 5. The fall is 2,000 - 1,000 = Rs 1,000.

Check. Net result = 3,300 - 1,500 = Rs 1,800. This matches the last equity figure.

Six trades is far too few to conclude anything. The numbers describe these six trades only. This example does not work out the Sharpe ratio. That needs a longer list of returns and a chosen safe rate.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Recompute the expectancy with these trades: +600, -300, +900, -300 (in Rs). Then express the result in R, using a rupee risk of Rs 300 per trade.

Hints:

  1. Find the wins and losses first, then the averages.
  2. Express the result in R by dividing by the rupee risk of Rs 300.

Answer:

  • Wins: 600 and 900, total Rs 1,500, from 2 trades. Losses: 300 and 300, total Rs 600, from 2 trades.
  • Win rate = 2 / 4 = 50%.
  • Average win = 1,500 / 2 = Rs 750. Average loss = 600 / 2 = Rs 300.
  • Expectancy = (0.5 x 750) - (0.5 x 300) = 375 - 150 = Rs 225 per trade.
  • Check: net result = 1,500 - 600 = 900. 900 / 4 = 225.
  • In R: 225 / 300 = 0.75R.

Independent exercise

Task. Use these ten hypothetical trades, in rupees: +800, -400, +1,200, -400, -400, +600, -400, +1,000, -400, +500. Planned risk per trade is Rs 400, so 1R = Rs 400. Work out the win rate, the average win, the average loss, the expectancy in rupees and in R, the profit factor and the maximum drawdown. Then write one sentence on what this sample can and cannot show.

Check your answer
  • Win rate: 5 wins out of 10 trades = 50%.
  • Total wins: 800 + 1,200 + 600 + 1,000 + 500 = Rs 4,100. Average win = 4,100 / 5 = Rs 820.
  • Total losses: five trades of Rs 400 = Rs 2,000. Average loss = 2,000 / 5 = Rs 400.
  • Expectancy = (0.5 x 820) - (0.5 x 400) = Rs 210 per trade. Check: net result 4,100 - 2,000 = 2,100, and 2,100 / 10 = 210.
  • Expectancy in R: 210 / 400 = 0.525R.
  • Profit factor: 4,100 / 2,000 = 2.05.
  • Maximum drawdown: the equity curve goes 800, 400, 1,600, 1,200, 800, 1,400, 1,000, 2,000, 1,600, 2,100. The high point is Rs 1,600 after trade 3. The low point after it is Rs 800 after trade 5. The fall is Rs 800.
  • Sample sentence: Ten trades net Rs 2,100. That is far below 30, so the numbers cannot show whether the rule works.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the expectancy formula, profit factor with no losses, maximum drawdown, the limits of Sharpe, and reading metrics from a small sample.

Paper task

Mode: backtest, which means the trades come from past data.

  1. Write the six worked-example trades in your journal, in order, as mode backtest. Use Lab 3 for this if it is ready. If it is not, use paper or a spreadsheet.
  2. Read the figures the journal or spreadsheet gives you. Check them against the worked example: win rate 50 percent, expectancy Rs 300 per trade, profit factor 2.2, maximum drawdown Rs 1,000.
  3. If any figure differs, find the error before you go on.
  4. Write one sentence: six trades are too few to conclude anything.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Each number describes the trades in the list. It does not describe the next trade.
  • A small sample can make any metric look strong or weak by chance. The lab warns when there are under 30 trades.
  • A high win rate does not mean low risk. A rule can win often and still lose a lot on its few large losses.
  • Sharpe ratio depends on the safe rate you pick. Trading returns often do not follow the pattern it assumes.
  • Costs are not in these figures unless the trade list already includes them (Costs on a trade).

Remember

  • Expectancy = (win rate x average win) - (loss rate x average loss).
  • Profit factor = gross profit divided by gross loss. With no losses, it cannot be worked out.
  • Maximum drawdown is measured from a high point to a later low point, not from the start.
  • Sharpe ratio depends on the safe rate and on how returns are spread.
  • Under 30 trades, no metric concludes anything.

Sources and verification

  • William F. Sharpe (1966), “Mutual Fund Performance”, The Journal of Business, 39(1), 119-138.
  • The backtest lab’s notes on the dashboard and its metric list.

Monte Carlo of trade order

Hook

Two traders use the same ten trades. This is a hypothetical example. Trader A meets the winning trades first, and the account climbs. Trader B meets the same losses in a bunch, and the account falls by Rs 4,000 before it recovers. Both traders end with the same total. Only the path differs. A single backtest shows one path only. This chapter shows how to test many paths for the same trades.

The idea

Reordering trades does not change the expectancy. The total and the average stay the same. But the path changes. The maximum drawdown and the longest losing streak can change a lot.

A Monte Carlo test works like this:

  1. Take the list of trade results.
  2. Shuffle the order, or resample the list, to make a new path.
  3. Record the maximum drawdown and the longest losing streak for that path.
  4. Repeat 1,000 times or more.

Read the results as percentiles. The 5th percentile is a low value. The 50th is the middle. The 95th is a high value. For drawdown, the 95th percentile means that 95 runs in every 100 had a drawdown no bigger than that figure. Five runs in every 100 had a bigger drawdown. Do not read only the average.

The test has one big assumption. It treats each trade as independent. That means one trade does not depend on the one before it. Real losses often cluster in hard periods of the market. So this test can understate the streaks you may meet.

Probability of ruin is the chance that your account falls to a level you chose before the run. For example, a hypothetical level of Rs 25,000 on Rs 50,000 of capital. The level must be written down before the run. Choosing it after you see the results is a form of hindsight.

The output is a range of possible paths for the same trades. It is not a forecast of your next drawdown.

See it

Use this table after a run. Fill it in from your own results. Read the 50th row first, then the 95th row.

PercentileMax drawdown (Rs)Longest losing streak (trades)
5th
50th
95th

Worked example

Hypothetical system. Win rate 35 percent. Average win 2R. Average loss 1R. Capital Rs 50,000. Risk 1 percent of capital, which is Rs 500 per trade.

Step 1. Expectancy in R. (0.35 x 2) - (0.65 x 1) = 0.70 - 0.65 = +0.05R per trade.

Step 2. Expectancy in rupees. 0.05 x Rs 500 = Rs 25 per trade, before costs. This is a thin edge.

Step 3. A specific run of 5 losses. The chance of five losses in a row, starting at a given trade, is 0.65 to the power of 5. That means 0.65 x 0.65 x 0.65 x 0.65 x 0.65. The result is about 0.116, or 11.6 percent.

Step 4. Rupee cost of that run. 5 x Rs 500 = Rs 2,500. That is 2,500 / 50,000 = 5 percent of capital.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Work out 0.65 to the power of 3. Then work out the rupee loss of a 3-trade losing run at 1R = Rs 500.

Hints:

  1. Multiply 0.65 by itself three times.
  2. A 3-trade losing run costs 3 x Rs 500.

Answer:

  • 0.65 x 0.65 = 0.4225. Then 0.4225 x 0.65 = 0.274625, which is about 27.5 percent.
  • Rupee loss: 3 x Rs 500 = Rs 1,500.

Independent exercise

Task. Your Monte Carlo 95th-percentile maximum drawdown is Rs 9,000 on capital of Rs 50,000. Explain what this number does and does not tell you. Then say what you would check before you trust it.

Check your answer
  • The figure is 9,000 / 50,000 = 18 percent of capital.
  • What it tells you: in 95 runs out of every 100, the drawdown was Rs 9,000 or less. This is for the same trades, shuffled, under the test’s assumptions.
  • What it does not tell you: it is not the worst case. About 1 run in 20 had a bigger drawdown. It is not a forecast of your next drawdown.
  • What to check first:
    • The trade list is complete and includes costs and realistic fills.
    • The run used 1,000 or more shuffles.
    • The risk per trade is the one you would really use.
    • The assumption that trades are independent is noted as a limit.
    • Any probability of ruin uses a level written down before the run.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks why reordering changes the path but not the expectancy. It also checks percentiles and the independence assumption.

Paper task

Mode: backtest, which means the trades come from past data.

  1. Take your own trade list from a backtest run. Use Lab 4, experiment 7, if the lab is ready.
  2. If the lab is not ready, copy your trade list into a spreadsheet and shuffle the order. Run as many shuffles as you can. Write down how many you ran. Fewer than 1,000 runs is a small sample.
  3. For each run, record the maximum drawdown and the longest losing streak. Then record the 5th, 50th and 95th percentile of each.
  4. Write the figures in the table in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready). Write the number of runs beside it.
  5. Write one sentence: what the range does and does not tell you.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The output is a range of possible orders for the same trades. It is not a forecast of your next drawdown.
  • The trades are treated as independent. Real losses can cluster in hard market periods, so the streaks can be understated.
  • The 95th percentile is not the worst case. Some runs can be worse than it.
  • The result depends on the trade list. A short or lucky list gives a range that looks safer than it is.
  • Costs, slippage and gaps only count if they are already in the trade list (Slippage).

Remember

  • Reordering trades keeps the expectancy the same. It can change the drawdown and the streaks.
  • Read the 5th, 50th and 95th percentiles, not only the average.
  • Each trade is treated as independent in this test. That is a limit.
  • A probability of ruin needs a level you write down before the run.
  • The result is a range of paths for the same trades. It is not a forecast.

Sources and verification

  • This book names no single reference on risk of ruin. Treat any figure from a simulation as one possible range, not a promise.
  • The backtest lab’s notes on the Monte Carlo experiment.

Paper trading and forward testing

Hook

A rule shows a profit on past prices. This is a hypothetical example. You then try the same rule on paper for a few weeks, and the result looks different. Why? Past prices are fixed, and every fill is known. Real trading has delays, partial fills and your own choices. This chapter shows the difference between backtest, forward and live results. It also shows the four gaps that make paper results look kinder than real ones.

The idea

Four words describe where a result comes from. Use the right one every time.

  • Backtest. Past data. The rules are applied after the fact, to prices that already happened.
  • Forward test. Fixed rules, applied to prices as they arrive, day by day. The rules do not change during the test.
  • Paper or hypothetical. A trade recorded as if it were filled. Nothing is sent to a broker.
  • Live. Real orders, with real money.

Label every result with its type. A paper profit is not a live profit.

A paper fill is an assumption. A real fill can differ in four ways:

  1. Fill price. The price you get can differ from the price you assumed. The spread is the gap between the best buy price and the best sell price (see Order matching, spread and liquidity). Prices can also jump at the open.
  2. Queue position. In a busy market, orders at the same price wait in line. Your order may not be first.
  3. Partial fills. You may get only part of the shares you wanted.
  4. Your own behaviour. You may skip a signal, move a stop, or add a trade that the plan did not include.

The forward-test script in Lab 5 is built to be honest. When data is missing or bad, it stops and reports. It does not skip the symbol and carry on. It is safe to run twice, because a second run does not add the same trades again. It places no orders and holds no login details for any broker.

The Kite demo is for practice with the screens, using dummy data. It is not a forward test. Zerodha’s support page says: “No, Zerodha does not offer demo accounts for paper trading. However, you can access a demo version of Kite with dummy data.” (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support. Check support.zerodha.com for changes.)

The script uses a market close time and a holiday list. NSE’s normal session for shares runs from 9:15 am to 3:30 pm, but NSE has changed session times for some shares and has announced more changes, so read the NSE market timings page. Take the holiday list from the NSE trading holidays page. The terms of the price data source also matter (see Historical data).

See it

Follow one order from the trader to the exchange. Notice the steps that a paper fill skips. Those skipped steps are where the four gaps appear.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Day 1 close is Rs 800. The rule gives a buy signal. The script plans to buy 12 shares.

Step 1. The assumed fill. Day 2 opens at Rs 806. The script fills 12 shares at Rs 806. Value = 12 x 806 = Rs 9,672.

Step 2. A worse real fill. Suppose a real fill would have been Rs 808. Value = 12 x 808 = Rs 9,696.

Step 3. The rupee effect. 9,696 - 9,672 = Rs 24. Check: 12 x (808 - 806) = 12 x 2 = 24.

Step 4. Many trades. The same gap on 10 trades gives 10 x Rs 24 = Rs 240. This is hypothetical. It assumes the same gap on each trade. It shows how small assumptions add up.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Fill one row of a forward log for the worked-example trade. Use trade ID H-001 and the made-up symbol EXAMPLE. The fields are trade_id, mode, symbol, planned entry, fill price and shares.

Hints:

  1. Set mode to forward. Never set it to live.
  2. The fill price in this example is hypothetical. Mark it as hypothetical in your note.

Answer:

trade_idmodesymbolplanned entryfill priceshares
H-001forwardEXAMPLERs 806Rs 806 (hypothetical)12

Independent exercise

Task. Write the honesty note that must appear beside a forward-test result on the dashboard.

Check your answer

A sample note: “Hypothetical paper results. No orders were sent. Fills are assumed, not real. Real fills may be worse because of the spread, gaps, queue position, partial fills and my own choices. A short test proves little. These results are not a forecast of live results.”

Quiz

The quiz checks hypothetical and live results, failing closed on bad data, the role of the Kite demo, and holiday handling.

Paper task

Mode: forward, which means hypothetical paper data. No orders are placed.

  1. Run the forward-test script on the recorded 10-day test data. If Lab 5 is not ready, write a made-up 10-day price list on paper.
  2. Check that each output row has a trade ID, mode, symbol, planned entry, fill price and shares.
  3. Import the output into the journal as mode forward (or copy the rows onto paper).
  4. Write one sentence on each of the four gaps. For each one, say whether it could make a real fill worse in this example.
  5. Do not place any orders. Real daily runs are optional. They need a data source whose terms you have checked.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A forward test sends no real orders. It cannot show how real fills would have gone, so the four gaps remain.
  • A short forward test proves little. Thirty trades is a floor for a first look. It is not a verdict on the rule.
  • Paper profit is not live profit. A paper result does not show how you act when real money is at risk.
  • The Kite demo is not a forward test. It uses dummy data. Zerodha says it offers no demo account for paper trading.
  • Paper trading does not prove you can do it with real money. It only shows how the rule ran on the days recorded.

Remember

  • Label every result as backtest, forward (paper) or live.
  • The four gaps are fill price, queue position, partial fills and your own behaviour.
  • When data is missing or bad, the script should stop. It should not guess.
  • The Kite demo is for practice with dummy data. It is not a forward test.
  • Paper results are hypothetical. Mark them that way every time.

Sources and verification

  • Zerodha support pages on the demo and virtual trading.
  • NSE market timings and holiday pages.
  • The Lab 5 notes on honesty.

Strategy validation assessment

Hook

Two people read the same backtest. This is a hypothetical example. One writes: “The strategy is profitable and ready.” The other writes: “In-sample looks positive. Out-of-sample rests on 18 trades, so the result is inconclusive.” Only one of these matches the evidence. This assessment checks whether you can write a verdict that the evidence supports. Writing the verdict is the skill being tested.

The idea

A validation write-up ends in one of three verdicts:

  • Inconclusive. The sample is too small to read, so the written criteria cannot be judged. No decision follows from it.
  • Not validated. The sample is large enough, and the result fails the written criteria. A good result in another part of the test does not change this.
  • Validated for further forward testing. The result passes the written criteria. This means the rule is worth testing forward. It does not mean the rule is profitable. It does not mean the rule is ready for real money.

Before you write a verdict, check the seven items below. For each item, write the file or screen where the evidence is stored. If you cannot show the evidence, mark the item as not done. Do not mark it as passed.

  1. Costs are on in the run.
  2. The held-out period was used once.
  3. The look-ahead demo was run.
  4. The survivorship demo was run.
  5. The number of trials you tried is recorded.
  6. The Monte Carlo run is done.
  7. The sample size is stated.

A result can still fail live, even after it passes this check. Five reasons:

  • Fills. Real fills can be worse than the test assumed (Paper trading and forward testing).
  • Costs. Real costs may differ from the costs in the run.
  • Regime change. A regime is a period with its own kind of market behaviour. The market may change, and a rule may stop working.
  • Small sample. A short test has a wide range of possible results.
  • Liquidity. A stock may trade less easily later, which changes the fills.

Writing a verdict is the skill. The verdict must follow from the checklist and the criteria. It cannot follow from a wish for a good result.

See it

Use the verdict tool, the quiz and the checklist below. The checklist is the structure to fill in.

#Checklist itemEvidence (file or screen)Status
1Costs on in the run
2Held-out period used once
3Look-ahead demo run
4Survivorship demo run
5Trial count recorded
6Monte Carlo run
7Sample size stated

Worked example

Hypothetical. A Lab 4 output on Rs 50,000 of capital shows:

  • In-sample expectancy: +0.40R.
  • Out-of-sample expectancy after costs: +0.05R.
  • Out-of-sample trades: 18.

Verdict: inconclusive.

Reasons:

  1. 18 trades is below the lab’s 30-trade confidence threshold.
  2. The out-of-sample edge is small, +0.05R. It moves a lot when costs change.

The write-up must state both reasons. A verdict without reasons is not a verdict.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Complete the seven-item checklist for the worked example above.

Hints:

  1. For each item, write the file or screen where the evidence would be stored.
  2. If you cannot show the evidence, mark the item as not done, not as passed.

Answer:

  1. Costs on: done. The out-of-sample figure is stated after costs, so the run had costs on.
  2. Held-out period used once: not done. The example does not show that the period was used once.
  3. Look-ahead demo run: not done. No evidence is given.
  4. Survivorship demo run: not done. No evidence is given.
  5. Trial count recorded: not done. No count is given.
  6. Monte Carlo run: not done. No evidence is given.
  7. Sample size stated: done. 18 out-of-sample trades.

Two of the seven items have evidence. The other five are not done yet. Those five stop a write-up from calling the result validated.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a two-paragraph validation memo for a second hypothetical result. In this result, the in-sample expectancy is positive and the out-of-sample expectancy is negative.

Check your answer

Paragraph 1. Hypothetical result on Rs 50,000 of capital. The in-sample expectancy was +0.30R. The out-of-sample expectancy after costs was -0.10R, across 35 trades. The sample is above the 30-trade threshold, so the result can be read. The out-of-sample result fails the written criteria. Verdict: not validated. A positive in-sample result cannot override a failed out-of-sample test.

Paragraph 2. Checklist: costs are on, and the sample size is stated. The held-out period is used once only if the journal records it. The look-ahead demo, the survivorship demo, the trial count and the Monte Carlo run are not yet done. Next step: record this as a failed test. Do not change the rule to chase the held-out result. Any new idea needs a new held-out period. Nothing here supports using real money.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the three verdicts, the held-out period, costs, and how to spot an overclaimed conclusion.

Paper task

Mode: backtest, which means the trades come from past data.

  1. Write the seven-item checklist in your journal learning note. Use Lab 4 for the evidence if it is ready. If it is not, use paper.
  2. For each item, write where the evidence is stored. Mark an item as not done where you have no evidence.
  3. Write one verdict from the three categories. Give one sentence of reason.
  4. Attach the Lab 4 run settings record. If the lab does not produce one yet, write that it is missing.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A verdict is only as good as its evidence. An item with no evidence is not done.
  • Passing the criteria does not make a strategy profitable. It means the rule is worth more testing.
  • A result from one market period may not hold in another.
  • Costs and fills in the run may not match real costs and fills.
  • A write-up that says “validated” can be read as permission to trade. It is not permission.

Remember

  • Three verdicts: inconclusive, not validated, and validated for further forward testing.
  • Validated for further testing means ready for more testing. It does not mean ready for real money.
  • Costs must be on in the run before any verdict is written.
  • A held-out period used after a change is no longer held out.
  • Mark every checklist item without evidence as not done.

Sources and verification

  • Robert Pardo, The Evaluation and Optimization of Trading Strategies, 2nd edition (Wiley, 2008).
  • Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado and Zhu (2014), “Pseudo-Mathematics and Financial Charlatanism: The Effects of Backtest Overfitting on Out-of-Sample Performance”, Notices of the American Mathematical Society, 61(5), 458-471.
  • The backtest lab’s acceptance criteria.

8. Psychology and journaling

  1. Fear, greed and the brain on losses
  2. Loss aversion and revenge trading
  3. FOMO, overconfidence, confirmation bias
  4. Discipline
  5. The journal as a tool
  6. Living through a losing streak
  7. When to stop trading altogether

Fear, greed and the brain on losses

Hook

A trader loses Rs 2,000 in one day. This is a hypothetical example. His plan says to stop after a loss, and he feels a strong urge to win the money back. He places one more trade, which the plan did not include. The loss that hurts most is the one he chose to take. This chapter explains why these feelings appear, and how to see them before they cost money.

The idea

Fear and greed are useful in many parts of life. In markets they often push you the wrong way. Prices move for many reasons, and a single trader cannot see most of them. A feeling that is useful in one place can mislead you in another.

A loss sets off a stress response. The urge is to act, so the feeling stops. Acting to stop a feeling is a different thing from following the plan. Many people feel a loss more strongly than the same rupee amount gained. That is why the same Rs 500 can feel worse to lose.

Frame each loss in R. One R is your planned risk on one trade. A loss of 1R is the planned cost of one stopped trade. This turns a loss into a known cost of your method. It is not a personal failure.

Feelings show up at decision points. There are four:

  • After a loss.
  • After a win.
  • When price moves without you.
  • When your plan says “no trade”.

At each point, a feeling can override a written rule. Naming the feeling helps you pause. The pause gives the rule a chance to decide.

This book does not diagnose anyone. If trading causes you real distress, see When to stop trading altogether.

See it

flowchart LR
  A[Signal or outcome] --> B[Feeling]
  B --> C[Pause]
  C --> D{Rule allows it}
  D -- Yes --> E[Act as the plan says]
  D -- No --> F[Do not act and record it]

Follow the arrows from a signal or an outcome. Notice the pause before any action. The rule check decides what happens next, not the feeling.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. One R is Rs 500, which is 1 percent of capital. The day ends with a loss of Rs 2,000.

MeasureCalculationResult
Loss in R2,000 / 5004R
Loss as a share of capital2,000 / 50,0004%

The loss is the same size whether it came from four planned stops or from one unplanned trade. The second case is a rule break. Your journal records it as one.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Classify each of these five sentences as “feeling”, “rule” or “outcome”.

  1. “Price fell to Rs 240 and my stop was hit.”
  2. “I want to get back the Rs 2,000 right now.”
  3. “The trade lost Rs 500, which is 1R.”
  4. “The plan says no new trade after two losses.”
  5. “I feel I must act now, or I will miss the move.”

Hints:

  1. A rule says what to do when a condition is met.
  2. A feeling is about what you want to do next.
  3. An outcome is something that has already happened.

Answer:

  1. Outcome.
  2. Feeling.
  3. Outcome.
  4. Rule.
  5. Feeling.

Independent exercise

Task. List five emotion tags you expect to use. Write one sentence for each, describing when it appears. Use the tag list in your journal if it has one.

Check your answer

A sample list:

  • Calm. It appears when the plan is followed and no trade is needed.
  • Fear. It appears when a stop is near and you feel like leaving early.
  • Greed. It appears after a win, when you feel like adding size.
  • Frustration. It appears after a loss, when you want to act at once.
  • Relief. It appears when a trade ends as the plan said it would.

Quiz

The quiz checks losses in R and the decision points. It also checks a loss followed by a wish to win it back, and why calm traders still need a check.

Paper task

Mode: learning note.

  1. Before each of five learning-note sessions, write one emotion tag. This is emotion_before.
  2. After the session, write one emotion tag. This is emotion_after.
  3. Note any moment when a feeling pushed you toward an action outside the rule.
  4. After five sessions, write one sentence about the pattern you see.

Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready). If the journal’s emotion fields are not ready, use the five tags from the independent exercise.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This chapter does not tell you what your rule should be after a loss. Your written plan does (The capital-first rules).
  • Naming a feeling does not stop it. The pause and the rule check protect your capital.
  • This book does not diagnose anyone and does not offer therapy. If trading causes real distress, talk to a trusted person or a qualified professional. See When to stop trading altogether.
  • An emotion tag is a record for learning. It is not a score of how good or bad you are as a trader.
  • Feelings after a loss are normal. They are not a reason to trade.

Remember

  • Feelings are signals, not instructions.
  • Record each loss in R and as a share of capital.
  • Four decision points: after a loss, after a win, when price moves without you, and when the plan says no trade.
  • Pause and check the rule before any action.
  • Trading to win back a loss is a rule break. Record it.

Sources and verification

  • Daniel Kahneman, Thinking, Fast and Slow (Farrar, Straus and Giroux, 2011, first edition).
  • The journal’s emotion fields in Lab 3.

Loss aversion and revenge trading

Hook

You lose Rs 2,000 before lunch. You feel a strong need to win it back before the market closes. So you trade again, with twice your usual size. That trade also loses. The day now costs Rs 3,000, and the hole is deeper than before. This chapter explains why the urge is so strong. It also shows the arithmetic that makes the hole deeper, and the rule that stops it.

The idea

Loss aversion means a loss feels heavier than a gain of the same rupee size. Researchers who study choice have found this pattern in many people. A loss of Rs 2,000 often hurts more than a gain of Rs 2,000 feels good.

Recovery is the gain you need to get back to where you started. The gain is measured on the smaller amount that is left. So a loss needs a bigger percentage to recover than you might expect.

  • A loss of L% of capital needs a gain of L / (100 - L) % to recover.
  • A 10% loss needs 10 / 90 = 11.1%, not 10%.
  • A 50% loss needs 50 / 50 = 100%, which means the account must double.

Revenge trading is a trade taken to win back a loss. It often comes with a bigger size, a shorter gap after the loss, or a looser stop. It often has no written plan behind it.

Your plan sets a daily limit. The daily limit is the largest loss you accept in one day. The rule is simple:

  • Once the daily limit is reached, trading stops for the day.
  • No increase in size after a loss.

A limit only helps if you follow it on a bad day. That is when the urge is strongest.

See it

Look at the path after each loss. Compare the gain needed after Rs 2,000 with the gain needed after Rs 3,000. The second number is higher. The revenge trade made the loss bigger, so the climb back is longer.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. You lose Rs 2,000 on a planned trade. Your usual risk per trade is 1R, which is Rs 500.

StepCalculationResult
1. Loss so far50,000 - 48,000Rs 2,000 (4% of capital)
2. Recovery needed2,000 / 48,0004.17%
3. Revenge trade risk2 x Rs 500Rs 1,000
4. Loss on the revenge trade1R at the new sizeRs 1,000
5. Total loss2,000 + 1,000Rs 3,000 (6% of capital)
6. Recovery needed now3,000 / 47,0006.38%

The doubled size made the loss bigger. The recovery needed also rose, from 4.17% to 6.38%. The revenge trade broke the rule of no size increase after a loss.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Your capital is Rs 50,000. You lose Rs 5,000. Work out the recovery needed, as a percentage of the capital that is left.

Hints:

  1. The remaining capital is Rs 50,000 - Rs 5,000 = Rs 45,000.
  2. Recovery = loss / remaining capital.

Answer: Recovery = 5,000 / 45,000 = 0.111, which is 11.1%. The gain needed is more than the 10% loss, because the base is smaller.

Independent exercise

Task. After three planned trades that each lose Rs 500, you double your size for the next trade. That trade also loses. Work out the total loss. Then name the rule that was broken.

Check your answer
  • Three planned losses: 3 x Rs 500 = Rs 1,500.
  • Revenge trade at double size: 2 x Rs 500 = Rs 1,000 loss.
  • Total loss: Rs 1,500 + Rs 1,000 = Rs 2,500.
  • Rule broken: no size increase after a loss.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks recovery arithmetic, the daily limit and the signs of revenge trading.

Paper task

Mode: paper or learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. For the next five trading sessions, look at every trade taken after a loss in the same session. Include every paper trade. The Kite demo is for looking at screens only, so practice trades are paper trades.
  2. For each one, tag it as a rule break if the size went up, the gap was short, or the plan did not cover it.
  3. Write the total loss for the session, in rupees and as a percent of capital.
  4. Write the recovery needed after that loss, using the formula from this chapter.
  5. If the journal is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The recovery formula shows how big the hole is. It does not show a safe way out of the hole.
  • A trade taken after a loss is not always revenge. Check the size, the gap and the plan before you decide.
  • The daily limit stops trading for the day. It does not make the loss smaller.
  • Loss aversion is a pattern seen in many people. It does not prove how you will react on a given day.
  • A bigger size after a loss does not fix the plan. It only makes the next loss bigger.

Remember

  • A 10% loss needs an 11.1% gain. It does not need 10%.
  • Recovery = loss / capital left after the loss.
  • Revenge trading often means a bigger size, a shorter gap or no plan.
  • Once the daily limit is reached, trading stops for the day.
  • No size increase after a loss.

Sources and verification

  • Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky (1979), “Prospect Theory: An Analysis of Decision under Risk”, Econometrica, 47(2), starting on page 263.
  • Your own daily limit, as written in your plan.

FOMO, overconfidence, confirmation bias

Hook

Your plan says buy at Rs 400, with a stop at Rs 390. The price never comes back to Rs 400. Instead it jumps 12% in one morning, to Rs 448. You feel you must buy now, before it goes higher. That feeling has a name: fear of missing out, or FOMO. This chapter shows how FOMO changes a planned trade into a different trade. It also covers two quieter habits that bend your thinking, overconfidence and confirmation bias.

The idea

FOMO is buying after a big move, because others seem to be buying. The planned price is gone. The trade you take is a new trade, with a new entry and a new stop.

A late entry needs its own numbers. Its stop is usually far from the plan. With the same Rs 500 of risk, a wider stop buys fewer shares.

Overconfidence means you read a few wins as proof of skill. A short run of wins is often luck. Treat a short run as a small sample, not a trend.

Confirmation bias means you look only for evidence that agrees with your idea. You skip the news and signs that disagree.

The counter-habit is simple. Before you enter, write at least one reason the trade could fail. Then test the idea against that reason, not only against the reasons you like.

See it

Look at where the planned entry sits, and where the late entry sits. Read the stop for each. Ask whether the late entry is still the planned trade.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Planned entry Rs 400, stop Rs 390. Risk per share Rs 10. Risk budget Rs 500. This example looks only at the risk limit. It leaves out the cash limit to keep the sums short.

StepCalculationResult
1. Planned shares500 / 1050 shares
2. Planned cash50 x 400Rs 20,000
3. Price after the 12% day400 x 1.12Rs 448
4. New stopgivenRs 420
5. New risk per share448 - 420Rs 28
6. Late-entry sharesfloor(500 / 28) = floor(17.86)17 shares
7. Late-entry cash17 x 448Rs 7,616

The late entry buys fewer shares for the same Rs 500 of risk. It also uses less cash. But the entry price, the stop and the reason for the trade are all different from the plan.

The trade is no longer the planned trade. Fewer shares do not make it the planned trade.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Use the same figures as the worked example. Your late entry is at Rs 448. This time the new stop is Rs 435. Work out the risk per share, the shares, and the cash. Then say whether the trade is still the planned one.

Hints:

  1. Risk per share = price now minus the new stop.
  2. Shares = 500 divided by the risk per share, rounded down.
  3. The planned trade had an entry of Rs 400 and a stop of Rs 390.

Answer:

  • Risk per share: 448 - 435 = Rs 13.
  • Shares: 500 / 13 = 38.46, rounded down to 38 shares. Check: 13 x 38 = 494, which is within Rs 500. 13 x 39 = 507, which is over.
  • Cash: 38 x 448 = Rs 17,024.
  • The trade is not the planned one. The entry, the stop and the cash all differ from the plan.

Independent exercise

Task. Write three reasons the trade idea you hold could fail. Then write one rule that would stop you from entering.

Check your answer

There is no single right list. A good answer has three reasons that could really happen, written before you test the idea. Examples of the kind of reason to write:

  • The move may already have used up most of its expected gain.
  • The news you read may be old, or it may only support one side.
  • The price may not be near your planned entry any more.

A rule might read: “I do not enter if the price is above my planned entry.” Each rule must be a yes or no test.

All figures in this chapter are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks FOMO signs, the share count for a late entry, overconfidence and confirmation bias.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Pick three trade ideas from your reading. Use learning notes only. Do not place any order.
  2. For each idea, write the planned entry price and the planned stop first.
  3. Write one reason the idea could fail. Do this before you test the idea.
  4. If you imagine a late entry, write the new stop and the new shares. Mark the entry “not the planned trade” if the plan price has gone.
  5. If the journal is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A disconfirming reason helps you think more clearly. It does not tell you what the price will do.
  • A late entry is a new trade. It needs its own entry, stop and checks before you act on it.
  • A short run of wins does not prove skill. A short run of losses does not prove the method is broken either.
  • Knowing these habits does not remove them. Write the counter-habit down each time, so it is there when you need it.
  • The numbers in this chapter are made up. They show the arithmetic, not a real stock.

Remember

  • FOMO is buying after a big move, when the planned price is gone.
  • A late entry usually has a wider stop, so fewer shares fit the same rupee risk.
  • Five wins in a row is a small sample. It is not proof.
  • Confirmation bias is looking only for evidence that agrees with you.
  • Before you enter, write one reason the trade could fail.

Sources and verification

  • This book names no study on overconfidence or confirmation bias. Researchers in psychology have written much on both, so search a library for current work.
  • The worked numbers in this chapter come from this book’s own Lab 1 example of a tight stop and a cash cap.

Discipline

Hook

On Monday you see a setup that looks strong, so you take it. You did not write an entry price, a stop or a target first. The trade loses, and you cannot say why you took it. A written plan would have asked three simple questions before you clicked. Each question would have had a clear answer. This chapter shows how to write a plan and a checklist that give you those answers.

The idea

A trading plan is written before the market opens and before you consider any trade. It says which setups you accept, how much you risk, and when you do not trade.

A checklist is a list of items you check before you enter. Each item must be a yes or no question, with evidence. “Looks strong” is not an item. It is a feeling, and a feeling cannot be checked.

  • Good item: “Is the entry price written in my plan? Yes or no.”
  • Poor item: “Does the setup look strong?”

No-trade rules are part of the plan too. They say when the method does not run. They may name a time of day, a kind of market day, or a condition you can check in advance. A “no trade” result is a valid result of the plan.

Reward-to-risk compares what you aim to gain with what you risk. It is written as R:

  • R = reward / risk.
  • Break-even win rate = 1 / (1 + R). This is the share of trades you must win, before costs, for the trades to pay back their losses.

Your plan can set a minimum R. A setup below that minimum is rejected. A rejected setup is a plan result, not a judgement about the share or the market.

The journal tool in this book reports rule conflicts in a neutral way. It names the rule and the conflict. It does not say a trade is good or bad.

See it

Mark each item yes or no. An item with no evidence is not a yes. Look at which items would stop the trade.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Entry Rs 250. Stop Rs 240. Risk per share Rs 10.

StepCalculationResult
1. Target ARs 270Reward Rs 20
2. R for target A20 / 10R = 2
3. Break-even for target A1 / (1 + 2)33.3%
4. Target BRs 260Reward Rs 10
5. R for target B10 / 10R = 1
6. Plan minimumgivenR of 1.5
7. Result for target B1 is below 1.5Rejected

Target A gives R = 2, so the break-even win rate before costs is 33.3%. Target B gives R = 1, which is below the plan’s minimum of 1.5. The setup is rejected. The reject is the plan working, not a judgement of the market.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Use the same entry Rs 250 and stop Rs 240. The target is Rs 275. Work out the reward, the R, and the break-even win rate. Then say whether the setup passes the plan minimum of 1.5.

Hints:

  1. Reward = 275 - 250.
  2. Break-even = 1 / (1 + R).

Answer:

  • Reward: 275 - 250 = Rs 25.
  • R: 25 / 10 = 2.5.
  • Break-even win rate before costs: 1 / (1 + 2.5) = 1 / 3.5 = 28.6%.
  • R of 2.5 is above the plan minimum of 1.5, so the setup passes that rule. It still needs its other checklist items.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a six-item pre-trade checklist. Each item must be a yes or no question. Then write a short no-trade list of three conditions.

Check your answer

There is no single right list. Check that each item:

  • can be answered yes or no,
  • names the evidence you would look at,
  • can be checked before you enter.

Example items:

  • “Is the entry price written in my plan? Yes or no.”
  • “Is the stop price written in my plan before entry? Yes or no.”
  • “Is R at least the plan minimum? Yes or no.”

Example no-trade conditions are the times or market states when your method does not run. Each one should be a condition you can check, not a feeling.

Quiz

The quiz checks checklist items, the R and break-even arithmetic, and no-trade rules.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write plan version 1 in your journal learning note. Put the date at the top.
  2. Include the setups you accept, the maximum risk you accept, and your reject rule for R.
  3. Write six checklist items. Each one is a yes or no question.
  4. Write a short no-trade list of three conditions.
  5. Later changes go in a new version, with a new date. Do not edit version 1.
  6. If the journal is not ready, do the task on paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A plan does not guarantee results. It limits the damage from poor decisions.
  • A checklist makes you look at evidence before you enter. It cannot make a weak idea win.
  • R uses your planned stop and target. If the price jumps past your stop, the loss can be bigger than the plan. See Slippage.
  • Break-even is shown before costs. Costs can raise the win rate you need. See Costs on a trade.
  • A no-trade day is not a failure. It is the plan working as written.

Remember

  • A plan is written before the market opens.
  • Each checklist item must be a yes or no question with evidence.
  • “Looks strong” is not a checklist item.
  • R = reward / risk. Break-even win rate = 1 / (1 + R).
  • A setup below the plan minimum is rejected. That is a plan result.

Sources and verification

  • Lab 1 outputs and the reject panel, for the R and reject rules.
  • The fields of the trading plan used for gate G7, for the plan layout.

The journal as a tool

Hook

Three weeks ago you took a trade. Today you cannot remember your planned stop. You also cannot remember whether you moved it. Memory tends to keep the reasons that make you look sensible. It often drops the rest. A journal keeps the record as it was, so you can judge your behaviour fairly. This chapter shows how to set up that record, and how to read it.

The idea

A journal records two sets of fields for each trade.

  • Planned fields are written before you enter. They include the planned entry, the stop, the target and the planned shares.
  • Executed fields are written after the trade. They include the fill price, the time and the exit.

The gap between the two sets is what you study. Comparing them shows what changed, and why.

Locked fields. The planned fields lock at entry. You can add notes later, but you cannot rewrite the plan. This stops a plan from being changed to fit the result.

Modes. Each trade belongs to one mode: paper, forward, backtest, live or kite_demo. Keep the modes separate. Do not mix practice results with live results by default.

Three numbers come from the comparison:

  • Slippage in rupees = (fill price - plan price) x shares, for a long entry. It is the extra rupees you paid above the plan price.
  • Plan adherence % = trades where the plan was followed / total trades x 100.
  • Rule breaks are tagged by category. Your weekly review looks at the most frequent category.

Two rules keep the record honest:

  • Journal entries before the gates are never marked live.
  • The journal records what you did. It is not a tool to make losses look smaller.

See it

Fill the plan form first. Then fill the execution form after the trade. Compare the two. The weekly review asks you to name one change.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Plan entry Rs 250. Fill price Rs 251. Shares 40.

StepCalculationResult
1. Price difference251 - 250Rs 1
2. Slippage1 x 40Rs 40

Hypothetical. Ten trades in the week. Nine followed the plan.

StepCalculationResult
3. Plan adherence9 / 10 x 10090%

One trade broke two rules. One break was a late exit, tagged as exit late. The other was oversize, tagged as size. The weekly review looks at the tags and names one change: fix the exit timing check.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Plan entry Rs 250. Fill price Rs 252.50. Shares 40. Work out the slippage in rupees.

Hints:

  1. Find the difference first, then multiply by the shares.
  2. The difference is Rs 2.50.

Answer: Slippage = (252.50 - 250) x 40 = 2.50 x 40 = Rs 100.

Independent exercise

Task. Complete a weekly review for three learning-note trades. Write what went well, one rule break, and one change.

Check your answer

There is no single right review. A useful review:

  • names one thing that followed the plan, for example “I wrote the plan before entry”,
  • names one rule break, using a tag,
  • names one change that is small enough to test next week.

Example: “Went well: the plan was written before entry. Rule break: exit later than the plan. Change: add an exit-time check before entry.”

All trades in this exercise are learning-note trades. None are live.

Quiz

The quiz checks slippage, adherence, locked fields, mode separation and the order of journal entries.

Paper task

Mode: paper for practice entries, never live. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Log three learning-note entries. For each, write the planned fields first: entry, stop, target and shares.
  2. After each trade, write the executed fields: fill price, time and exit.
  3. For each entry, work out the slippage in rupees and the plan adherence for the set.
  4. Write one line for a weekly review. Name one change.
  5. Mark every entry as paper. If the lab is not ready, do this in a spreadsheet.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The journal shows what you did. It cannot explain why a trade won or lost.
  • Slippage from one fill is one number. It does not show your average trade quality.
  • Plan adherence counts followed rules, not profits. A trade can follow the plan and still lose.
  • A journal is useful only when you fill it in on time. Notes written from memory are less accurate.
  • Journal entries are for judging behaviour. They are not a way to make losses look smaller.

Remember

  • Planned fields are written before entry. Executed fields are written after.
  • Planned fields lock at entry.
  • Slippage (Rs) = (fill price - plan price) x shares, for a long entry.
  • Plan adherence % = trades where the plan was followed / total trades x 100.
  • Keep paper, forward, backtest, live and kite_demo entries separate.

Sources and verification

  • Lab 3, the record definition and features of the journal.

Living through a losing streak

Hook

You have lost four trades in a row. Each loss was the planned 1R, which is Rs 500. You start to feel sure the method is broken. Another part of you says a win must be due soon. Both feelings are normal. This chapter shows how often streaks happen, and what to write down before one starts. It also shows why a streak alone does not prove the method is broken.

The idea

Streaks happen at any win rate. A lower win rate makes long losing runs more common. A higher win rate does not stop them completely.

Gambler’s fallacy is the wrong belief that a run of losses makes a win “due”. In the simple model used in this chapter, each trade has the same chance, whatever came before. So a losing run does not make the next trade more likely to win.

Real markets may not match that simple model. Treat the numbers here as a way to understand the odds, not as a forecast.

Stages of a drawdown. Write an action for each stage before a streak begins:

  • Normal.
  • Limit approached.
  • Daily limit reached.
  • Weekly limit reached.

The action for each stage is set in advance. You do not decide it in the middle of the streak.

Size stays fixed in a drawdown. Reducing exposure follows a written rule. The rule is covered in When to stop trading altogether. A feeling is not a rule.

See it

Watch the longest losing run in the summary. Then look at the feelings tags beside it. Ask which tags came before you changed a rule, and which came after.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Win rate 40%. So the chance of a loss on one trade is 1 - 0.4 = 0.6. Risk 1R = Rs 500, which is 1% of Rs 50,000.

StepCalculationResult
1. Chance of a loss on one trade1 - 0.40.6
2. Six losses in a row, starting at one given trade0.6 x 0.6 x 0.6 x 0.6 x 0.6 x 0.6 = 0.6^60.0467, about 4.7%
3. Rupee loss of six losses6 x Rs 500Rs 3,000
4. Share of capital3,000 / 50,0006%
5. Planned action at four losses in a rowwritten before the run beginsNo change in size. Check the weekly limit.

Six losses in a row can happen at a 40% win rate. From one given trade, the chance is about 4.7%. The plan action at four losses is written in advance, so you do not decide it while you feel the pressure.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. At a 40% win rate, the chance of a loss on one trade is 0.6. Work out 0.6 to the power of 4. Then work out the rupee loss of four losses at 1R of Rs 500.

Hints:

  1. Multiply 0.6 by itself four times.
  2. Four losses cost 4 x Rs 500.

Answer:

  • 0.6 x 0.6 x 0.6 x 0.6 = 0.36 x 0.36 = 0.1296, about 13.0%.
  • 4 x Rs 500 = Rs 2,000.

Independent exercise

Task. Run the streak simulator three times, each with a fresh random run. Write what changed in the feelings log between the runs.

Check your answer

There is no single right answer. A useful log names:

  • the longest losing run in each run,
  • the feeling tag you wrote at the point the streak was longest,
  • whether the feeling made you want to change size or trade more.

Example: “Run 1 had a long run early, and I tagged fear. Run 2 had a short run, and I tagged calm. Run 3 had two runs of three, and I tagged a wish to get back.” Each run is a simulation, and none of them is proof of how the method performs.

Quiz

The quiz checks the gambler’s fallacy, streak probability, pre-set actions and the drawdown stages.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Run the streak simulator three times, using a 40% win rate.
  2. For each run, note the longest losing run and the rupee loss at 1R.
  3. For each run, log the feelings tags in your journal learning note.
  4. Write the planned action for four losses in a row, before you read the results.
  5. If the simulator is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet, using the 0.6 chance of a loss.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A simulated streak uses a fixed win rate. Real trades may not match it, so simulated results are not proof of future results.
  • The odds of a run tell you how common it is. They do not tell you when the next run will start or end.
  • Feelings tags record how you felt. They do not measure whether the method works.
  • A losing streak does not prove the method is broken. A winning streak does not prove it works either.
  • The simple model assumes each trade is separate. Real markets may not behave that way.

Remember

  • Streaks happen at any win rate, and a lower win rate makes them more common.
  • Gambler’s fallacy: a run of losses does not make a win due.
  • The chance of six losses in a row from one given trade, at 0.6, is about 4.7%.
  • Write the action for each drawdown stage before a streak starts.
  • Size stays fixed in a drawdown. Reducing exposure follows a written rule.

Sources and verification

  • The losing-streak experiment in Lab 4.
  • Amos Tversky and Daniel Kahneman (1971), “Belief in the Law of Small Numbers”, Psychological Bulletin, 76(2), 105-110. It is about the mistaken belief that short runs of chance events must even out.

When to stop trading altogether

Hook

Your monthly loss limit is Rs 3,000 on a Rs 50,000 account. In the third week you reach it. You feel that one more trade will fix things and bring the month back on track. This is the moment a written rule matters most. This chapter explains the stopping levels in a plan. It also shows how to restart without rushing, and why stopping is part of the method.

The idea

A plan uses three levels of stopping:

  1. Stop for the day. Trading ends for the day when the daily limit is reached.
  2. Stop for the week. Trading ends for the week when the weekly limit is reached.
  3. Suspend until review. Trading stops until you have reviewed the cause, when a serious trigger is reached.

Suspension triggers are the conditions that move you to a stop. Each trigger must be a yes or no test. They can include:

  • a limit breach,
  • a set number of losses in a row, with the number written in your plan,
  • a drop from your peak account value,
  • a rule that was broken,
  • a feeling you cannot manage.

Reducing exposure means cutting the risk per trade, or the capital at risk, by a written step. The step is set in the plan. It is not chosen by feel. After a drop from peak, a written cut in size often comes before a full stop. The smaller size keeps you active while the loss is still small.

Restart needs three things:

  • A written review of the cause.
  • A waiting period that you write in advance. You choose its length.
  • A plan change if the cause was a broken rule.

Stopping is part of the method. It is not a failure.

See it

Enter a sequence of losses. Watch when the monthly limit triggers. Then read the state the simulator shows after the trigger.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. The limits below are illustrative. You set your own real figures.

StepCalculationResult
1. Monthly loss limit6% of Rs 50,000Rs 3,000
2. Trigger pointcumulative monthly loss reaches Rs 3,000Trading suspended
3. Drop from peak50,000 - 45,000Rs 5,000 (10% of Rs 50,000)
4. Risk before the cut1% of Rs 50,000Rs 500
5. Risk after the cut0.5% of Rs 45,000Rs 225

After a 10% drop from peak, the written step halves the risk percentage, from 1% to 0.5%. In rupees, the risk per trade falls from Rs 500 to Rs 225. The cut is measured on the current capital of Rs 45,000, not on the old peak.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Work out the monthly loss limit in rupees for a 5% limit on Rs 40,000.

Hints:

  1. 5% of 40,000.
  2. Then work out the risk per trade at 1% of the capital left after a full loss of that limit.

Answer:

  • Monthly loss limit: 5% x Rs 40,000 = Rs 2,000.
  • If you lose the full Rs 2,000, capital falls to 40,000 - 2,000 = Rs 38,000.
  • Risk per trade at 1% of Rs 38,000 = Rs 380. Check: 0.01 x 38,000 = 380.

Independent exercise

Task. Write the suspension and restart rules for your plan. Make each trigger a yes or no test.

Check your answer

There is no single right set of rules. Check that each trigger:

  • can be answered yes or no,
  • uses a number or a fact you can see in your journal,
  • does not depend on how you feel that day.
