Example trigger: “Did my monthly loss reach my written limit? Yes or no.” Example restart rule: “I restart only after a written review, and after the waiting period I wrote in advance.”

Quiz

The quiz checks trigger levels, exposure cuts, restart rules and limits that fall on the last trading day of the month.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Log it in your journal (or on paper until the journal app is ready).

  1. Write the suspension rules into your plan, in the learning note. Each trigger is a yes or no test.
  2. Write the restart rules. Include the written review and the waiting period you choose.
  3. Run the risk-limit simulator once with the illustrative limits.
  4. Log each change of state the simulator shows, in order.
  5. If you have your own limits, use them instead, and mark the illustrative figures as illustrative. If the simulator is not ready, do steps 3 and 4 on paper.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The 6% and 5% figures in this chapter are illustrations, not defaults. Set your own limits in writing before you trade.
  • A limit does not stop a loss inside one trade. If the price gaps past your stop, one loss can be bigger than the planned 1R. See Slippage.
  • Limits apply to your trading money only. Money you need for daily life, such as household savings, stays outside any trading loss limit.
  • Stopping does not mean giving up on trading. It is a pause that protects your capital.
  • If trading causes you real distress, talk to a trusted person or a professional. Do not wait for a limit to be reached before you do this.

Remember

  • Three levels: stop for the day, stop for the week, suspend until review.
  • Each suspension trigger is a yes or no test.
  • Reduce exposure by a written step, not by feel.
  • A restart needs a written review, a waiting period you wrote in advance, and a plan change if a rule broke.
  • Stopping is part of the method.

Sources and verification

  • The gate G4 and gate G7 fields of the book’s trading plan.
  • Lab 3, the limit-breach log.

Futures and why they are leveraged

Hook

Hypothetical. You have Rs 1,00,000 in cash. A friend says a futures contract worth Rs 10,00,000 needs only Rs 1,00,000 as a deposit. You think a small price move must be a small risk. That idea is wrong in an important way. This chapter shows why the deposit is not the full picture.

The idea

A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell something at a set price on a set future date. It is traded on an exchange. The price is fixed when you enter the agreement. The market price can move a lot before the date arrives.

Think of paying a deposit to reserve a house. You pay a part of the price now. The full price is still the value of the deal. The deposit is only the part you pay first.

These terms matter here:

  • Margin is the deposit you pay to hold the contract. It is smaller than the value of the contract.
  • Leverage means you control a large value with a small amount of money. Futures have leverage because the margin is small compared with the contract value.
  • Expiry is the last date of the contract.
  • Daily settlement means gains and losses move in and out of your account as prices change. They do not wait for expiry. NSE Clearing, the body that guarantees and settles trades, marks each futures position to a daily settlement price. That price is based on the volume weighted average price of the contract over the last 30 minutes of the day. Gains and losses are then paid in or out of your broker’s account, and the position is reset to that price. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Clearing settlement mechanism page. Rules change, so check the NSE Clearing site.)
  • Expected result is the average result you would get over many similar bets.

Three facts follow from these terms.

  1. Leverage makes gains and losses bigger by the same factor. It does not change the expected result of a bet. Leverage makes each swing bigger. The average result per rupee of exposure stays the same.
  2. A small deposit does not mean a small risk. The risk comes from the full contract value. The deposit only tells you how much you paid first.
  3. Losses can be larger than the margin deposited. If prices move against you, you may have to pay more money into the account. The margin rules and any extra payment are set by the exchange and your broker. NSE Clearing sets the starting margin from a risk model. For index and stock futures, it is worked out for a two-day price move, at a 99 percent confidence level. Your broker must collect the margin from you before you take the position, and may ask for more if your losses grow. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE Clearing margin page. Rules change, so check the NSE Clearing site.)

Contract size, margin and expiry are set by the exchange. They change over time. Check them at the source before you rely on any number.

Real figures, for reference. The Rs 10,00,000 contract and the 10 percent margin in this chapter are round numbers for practice. They are not real. The table below shows a few real rules. They are here only so you can see how real contracts are set. This is not an offer or advice to trade.

ItemWhat the official source saysSource and date
Index contract valueSEBI says a new index derivative contract must be worth at least Rs 15 lakh when it is introduced. At each review, the lot size is set so the value stays between Rs 15 lakh and Rs 20 lakh.SEBI circular of 1 Oct 2024
Nifty 50 lot size65 units, changed from 75. It applies from the January 2026 contracts. NSE reviews lot sizes from time to time.NSE circular of 3 Oct 2025
Expiry dayNifty 50 contracts expire on a Tuesday. If that Tuesday is a holiday, they expire on the trading day before.NSE contract specifications page, updated 11 Aug 2026, and NSE circular of 25 Jun 2025

(As of 11 Nov 2026. Rules change, so check the NSE and SEBI sites.)

See it

The table compares the same price move on cash and on a futures position.

Cash holdingFutures position
Money you put inRs 1,00,000Rs 1,00,000 as margin
Value you controlRs 1,00,000Rs 10,00,000
Rupee move for a 2% price moveRs 2,000Rs 20,000
Move as a share of the money you put in2%20%

Look at the last row. The same 2% move is ten times bigger against the money you put in. Try a 5% move with the same numbers. The futures move is Rs 50,000, which is half of the margin. The move grows with the contract value, not with the deposit.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Contract value Rs 10,00,000. Margin Rs 1,00,000. The price moves 2% against you.

StepCalculationResult
1. Rupee move on the contract2% x Rs 10,00,000Rs 20,000
2. Move as a share of the marginRs 20,000 / Rs 1,00,00020%
3. Same 2% move on Rs 1,00,000 of cash2% x Rs 1,00,000Rs 2,000
4. Move as a share of the cashRs 2,000 / Rs 1,00,0002%

The same price move costs 20% of the margin in the futures case. It costs 2% of the cash in the cash case. The futures move is ten times larger, because the contract is ten times the margin. All figures are made up. Margin rates and contract sizes in this example are not real facts.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Read the worked example. In one sentence, say why the same percentage move is bigger against the deposit.

Hints:

  1. Compare the rupee move with the deposit, not with the contract value.
  2. The deposit is the number that matters for your capital.
  3. Look at the last row of the table in See it.

Answer: The margin is one tenth of the contract value. So a 2% move on the contract is 20% of the margin. The same 2% on cash is only 2% of the cash.

Independent exercise

Task. Explain in writing why “leverage raises the expected return” is a misconception. Say what leverage changes instead.

Check your answer

A good answer has these points.

  • Expected return is the average result over many similar bets. Leverage does not change that average per rupee of exposure.
  • Leverage multiplies gains and losses by the same factor. A bet with a negative average stays negative in proportion.
  • Leverage changes the size of each swing. It also makes a large loss larger against your own money.
  • Leverage makes the rupee amount at risk bigger for the same price move. It does not make the trade more likely to succeed.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks leverage, daily settlement, losses against the deposit, and one scenario.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This chapter has no practice. It asks for reflection only.

  1. Write one sentence that says what leverage changes.
  2. Write one sentence that says why a small deposit does not mean a small risk.
  3. Save both sentences in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready.
  4. Do not look for a contract to trade. This book does not teach futures practice.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This chapter explains how futures work in theory. It does not teach how to trade them.
  • Margin, contract size and expiry rules change over time. Check them at the exchange before you rely on any number.
  • The Rs 10,00,000 contract and the Rs 1,00,000 margin are made up. Real contracts differ.
  • A futures position is not a shortcut to a daily target. See The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check.
  • Knowing how leverage works does not tell you whether any trade will make money.

Remember

  • A futures contract is an agreement for a set price on a set date.
  • Margin is a deposit. It is smaller than the contract value.
  • Leverage makes gains and losses bigger. It does not raise the expected result.
  • Gains and losses move daily. Losses can be larger than the margin deposited.
  • Check the current contract rules at the source.

Sources and verification

  • NSE derivatives contract specifications and margin pages

  • SEBI circulars on derivatives margin

  • Zerodha Varsity, futures material (reference only)

  • SEBI circular of 1 Oct 2024 on measures to strengthen the equity index derivatives framework

  • NSE circular of 3 Oct 2025 on the revision in market lots of index derivatives

  • NSE Clearing pages on settlement mechanism and margins

Options basics and Greeks

Hook

Hypothetical. A friend says options are cheap, so buying them is low risk. An option on a share costs Rs 20 per share. The friend thinks a small price means a small risk. This chapter shows what the buyer and the seller can gain and lose. A low price does not make a trade safe.

The idea

An option is a contract that gives a right, not a duty.

  • A call gives the right to buy at a set price, called the strike.
  • A put gives the right to sell at the strike.
  • The premium is the price of the option. The buyer pays it. The seller receives it.
  • The expiry is the last date the right can be used.

Think of paying a small token to hold a shop unit at a fixed price for one month. If prices rise, the right to buy at the fixed price becomes worth more. If prices do not rise, you lose the token. The token is the premium.

The premium has two parts.

  • Intrinsic value is how far the option is in the money. For a call, it is the share price minus the strike, if that is above zero.
  • Time value is the rest of the premium. It pays for the chance that the price moves further before expiry.

The Greeks are measures of how a premium responds to change. This chapter explains them in words only.

  • Delta measures how much the premium moves when the share price moves a little. Think of it as how closely the premium follows the share.
  • Theta measures how much the premium loses as time passes, other things equal. It works like a monthly bus pass that loses value as the month runs out. Time decay works against the buyer.
  • Vega measures how much the premium changes when expected price swings change. It works like insurance. The price of cover rises when a storm is forecast.

The risk of each side is different.

  • The buyer’s most loss is the premium. The option can expire with no value.
  • The seller’s most gain is the premium. The seller’s loss can grow far larger than the premium, because the seller must meet the buyer’s right.

Why scalping, option buying and martingale are dangerous explains why these approaches are risky.

See it

The table is an original drawing for this book. It shows a call with strike Rs 500 and premium Rs 20 per share. It is illustrative only.

Price at expiryIntrinsic valueBuyer profit per shareSeller profit per share
Rs 490Rs 0minus Rs 20plus Rs 20
Rs 500Rs 0minus Rs 20plus Rs 20
Rs 515Rs 15minus Rs 5plus Rs 5
Rs 520Rs 20Rs 0Rs 0
Rs 530Rs 30plus Rs 10minus Rs 10
Rs 540Rs 40plus Rs 20minus Rs 20

Read the buyer column from top to bottom. The buyer loses at most Rs 20 per share. The seller column is the mirror image.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Call option, strike Rs 500, premium Rs 20 per share. Results are at expiry and before costs.

StepCalculationResult
1. Price at expiry Rs 530: intrinsic value530 - 500Rs 30
2. Buyer profit per share30 - 20Rs 10
3. Price at expiry Rs 490: intrinsic valuePrice is below the strike, so no valueRs 0
4. Buyer profit per share0 - 20minus Rs 20

At Rs 530 the buyer gains Rs 10 per share. At Rs 490 the buyer loses the Rs 20 premium. The buyer’s loss is capped at the premium. Costs, which this example leaves out, would reduce both results.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Using the call example, compute the buyer’s profit per share if the share price is Rs 515 at expiry.

Hints:

  1. Intrinsic value is 515 - 500.
  2. Subtract the premium from the intrinsic value.
  3. Check whether the result is smaller than the premium.

Answer: Intrinsic value = 515 - 500 = Rs 15. Profit per share = 15 - 20 = minus Rs 5. The buyer loses Rs 5 per share, which is less than the Rs 20 premium.

Independent exercise

Task. Explain in writing why an option seller’s most gain is the premium, but the loss can be far larger than the premium.

Check your answer

A good answer has these points.

  • The seller keeps the premium if the option expires with no value. So the most gain is the premium.
  • For a call seller, the loss grows as the price rises above the strike. There is no fixed limit on the price rise.
  • Example, hypothetical: strike Rs 500, premium Rs 20, price Rs 600 at expiry. Intrinsic value is Rs 100. Seller’s loss = 100 - 20 = Rs 80 per share. Check: Rs 80 is four times the Rs 20 premium.
  • The seller’s risk sits on the other side of the buyer’s right. That is why option selling is not a low-risk shortcut.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the buyer’s loss, theta, intrinsic and time value, one scenario and one spot-the-mistake.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This chapter has no practice. It asks for reflection only.

  1. Write one plain sentence each for call, put, strike, premium and expiry.
  2. Write one sentence on why the buyer’s loss is capped at the premium.
  3. Write one sentence on why a cheap option is not the same as a safe one.
  4. Save these in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready. Do not look for an option to buy or sell.

Limits and common mistakes

  • This chapter is theory. The book does not teach buying or selling options.
  • Strike intervals, expiry dates and contract rules change over time. NSE’s contract page (updated 11 Aug 2026) says Nifty 50 index options expire on a Tuesday, with weekly and monthly expiries for Nifty 50 and monthly expiries for the other index contracts. The gap between strikes depends on the price of the underlying, so read it on the NSE page. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE contract specifications page. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)
  • Charges on options change over time. Zerodha’s charges page lists a flat Rs 20 brokerage per executed options order, a securities transaction tax (STT) of 0.15 percent on the premium on the sell side, and NSE transaction charges of 0.03553 percent on the premium. GST, SEBI charges and stamp charges are added. These figures are for reading only, not advice. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha charges page. Rates change, so check zerodha.com/charges.)
  • The Rs 20 premium and the Rs 500 strike are made up. Real premiums differ.
  • The table leaves out costs. Costs reduce results on both sides.

Remember

  • A call gives the right to buy at the strike. A put gives the right to sell at the strike.
  • The premium is the buyer’s cost and the seller’s income.
  • Time value falls as expiry gets closer, other things equal. Theta measures that fall.
  • A buyer’s loss is capped at the premium.
  • A seller’s gain is capped at the premium, but the seller’s loss can be much larger.

Sources and verification

  • NSE derivatives pages
  • Zerodha Varsity, options module (reference only)
  • NSE derivatives contract specifications page
  • Zerodha charges page (zerodha.com/charges)
  • For who may trade F&O and what a broker must check, read the broker’s rules and the SEBI and NSE circulars on the derivatives segment. This book states no eligibility rule.

Hedging

Hook

A farmer grows rice. He worries that prices will fall before harvest. He pays for a cover that pays him if prices drop. The cover costs money, and it does not stop every loss. Hedging in trading works in the same way.

The idea

A hedge is a second position that offsets part of the risk of the first. The first position is the one you already hold or plan to hold. The hedge is like the cover. It pays out in one kind of bad case, and it has a price.

Five points matter.

  • A hedge has a cost. The cost can be a premium, a spread, or a gain you give up if prices rise. The cost reduces returns in every period, including good ones.
  • Basis risk. The hedge may not move exactly with the position it protects. It is like a cover that pays for rain measured at one place, when your field is in another place.
  • A hedge changes the shape of the loss. It does not remove the loss. A hedged position can still lose money.
  • Small accounts feel the cost more. The cost is a bigger share of a small amount of capital. For example, Rs 1,200 on a Rs 50,000 holding is 2.4%.
  • A hedge does not replace position sizing. A hedge is not a reason to take a bigger position. Keep the limits from Position sizing step by step.

See it

The table shows the same holding with and without the hypothetical put. The put has strike Rs 470 and costs Rs 1,200. The results exclude other costs.

Price at expiryUnhedged resultHedged result
Rs 500 (flat)Rs 0minus Rs 1,200
Rs 470minus Rs 3,000minus Rs 4,200
Rs 455minus Rs 4,500minus Rs 4,200
Rs 400minus Rs 10,000minus Rs 4,200

Read down the hedged column. The loss stops getting worse below Rs 470. The hedge also costs Rs 1,200 in the flat case, where nothing else goes wrong.

Worked example

Hypothetical. You hold 100 shares at Rs 500. The holding is worth Rs 50,000. You also buy a hypothetical put with strike Rs 470. The premium is Rs 12 per share. That is Rs 1,200 in total. Rs 1,200 is 2.4% of Rs 50,000.

The price falls to Rs 400 at expiry.

ItemCalculationResult
Loss on shares, unhedged100 x (500 - 400)Rs 10,000
Put payout100 x (470 - 400)Rs 7,000
Loss on shares after the put10,000 - 7,000Rs 3,000
Hedged total loss3,000 + 1,200 premiumRs 4,200

The unhedged loss is 20% of the holding. The hedged loss is 8.4% of the holding. Check: Rs 4,200 / Rs 50,000 = 8.4%. The hedge cut the loss from Rs 10,000 to Rs 4,200. It also cost Rs 1,200 in every case.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. The price falls to Rs 455 with the same hypothetical put. Compute the hedged loss.

Hints:

  1. Below Rs 470, the put offsets the fall, so the hedged loss is capped.
  2. The loss from Rs 500 to Rs 470 is 100 x 30 = Rs 3,000.
  3. Add the Rs 1,200 premium.

Answer: Hedged loss = Rs 3,000 + Rs 1,200 = Rs 4,200. Check: the unhedged loss is 100 x 45 = Rs 4,500. The put pays 100 x (470 - 455) = Rs 1,500. Rs 4,500 - Rs 1,500 = Rs 3,000, plus the Rs 1,200 premium, is Rs 4,200.

Independent exercise

Task. Write a short paragraph explaining why hedging does not replace the risk-per-trade rule.

Check your answer

A good answer has these points.

  • A hedge covers part of a price move after you enter. It does not set how much you can lose before you enter.
  • A hedge has its own cost. It can also have basis risk, so it may not cover the loss fully.
  • The risk-per-trade rule sets the size of the position. A hedge does not change that size.
  • A hedge can tempt a trader to take a bigger position. That is the mistake the rule prevents.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks hedge cost, basis risk, hedging and sizing, one scenario and one spot-the-mistake.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This chapter has no practice. It asks for reflection only.

  1. Write three sentences. Say what a hedge is, what it costs, and what basis risk means.
  2. Using the worked example, write the unhedged loss and the hedged loss at Rs 400, in rupees.
  3. Write one sentence on why hedging does not allow a bigger position.
  4. Save these in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The example assumes the put matches the holding exactly. Real hedges may not match. That gap is basis risk.
  • Option premiums, charges and rules change over time. Lot sizes, expiry days and charges are on the NSE contract page and on the Zerodha charges page (zerodha.com/charges). This chapter uses made-up premiums only. (As of 11 Nov 2026. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)
  • A hedge can lose money in a flat market, because its cost is paid either way.
  • Hedging with derivatives is theory in this book. There is no practice path.
  • A hedge does not replace the risk-per-trade rule. See Risk per trade and stop-loss distance.

Remember

  • A hedge offsets part of a risk. It does not remove all risk.
  • Every hedge has a cost, and that cost is paid in good periods too.
  • Basis risk means the hedge may not move exactly with the position.
  • Hedging does not allow a bigger position.
  • Hedging costs weigh more on a small account, as a share of its capital.

Sources and verification

  • NSE derivatives pages
  • Zerodha charges page (zerodha.com/charges)
  • The chapters on futures and on options in this track, for contract and charge facts

SEBI’s F&O studies as a case study

Hook

A headline says, “Most F&O traders lose money.” You read it and think, “So I will lose too.” One headline percentage does not tell you what will happen to you. This chapter shows what a study of traders can say, and what it cannot say.

The idea

F&O means futures and options. A study of traders counts what happened to a group of people over a period. It describes the past. It does not forecast what will happen to one person.

Before you quote any study, ask these five method questions.

  1. Which people were in the sample, and how were they chosen?
  2. Which period did the study cover?
  3. Were costs included in the gains and losses?
  4. How were gains and losses measured?
  5. Did people leave the study, and were the same people followed over time?

Expected value is the average result you would get over many similar tries. A percentage of losers does not give that average by itself. You also need the size of the wins and the size of the losses, together.

Survivorship is a bias that counts only people who stayed in the sample. People who left are missing from the totals. A study can then look better than the full group. See Survivorship bias.

Quoting figures. Quote a figure only from its source, with the study name, the date and the period. Never quote a figure from memory.

What SEBI’s own studies say. SEBI, India’s market regulator, has published three studies on individual traders in equity futures and options. Each is described below with its name, date, period and sample. The figures come from SEBI’s own documents.

StudyPeriod and sampleWhat it reports
“Analysis of Profit and Loss of Individual Traders dealing in Equity F&O Segment”, SEBI, January 2023Financial years 2019 and 2022, data from the top 10 brokersIn FY22, 89 percent of individual F&O traders had losses. The average loss was about Rs 1.1 lakh.
“Analysis of Profits & Losses in the Equity Derivatives Segment (FY22-FY24)”, SEBI, 23 September 2024Financial years 2022 to 2024, data from the top 15 brokersIn FY24, 91.1 percent of individual traders (about 73 lakh people) lost money, with an average net loss of Rs 1.20 lakh. Over the three years, 1.13 crore unique traders had a combined net loss of Rs 1.81 lakh crore, including costs. About 93 percent were loss-makers. Only 1 percent earned more than Rs 1 lakh after costs.
“Profitability of Individual Traders in the Equity Derivatives Segment (FY25-FY26)”, SEBI, August 2026Financial years 2025 and 2026, data from the top 15 brokers, about 90 percent of the tradersThe share of loss-makers was 90.9 percent in FY25 and 87.7 percent in FY26. Total net losses were Rs 91,685 crore in FY26. The average loss per person was Rs 1.17 lakh. Options made up 92 percent of the losses.

Read the table with the five method questions. The three studies use different brokers and different years, so do not join their numbers into one line. The studies describe groups. They do not say what will happen to you. (As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI studies on its website. Check the SEBI site for newer studies.)

See it

Use this checklist before you read any headline. Read the method section of the report first, then the headline.

QuestionWhy it matters
Who was in the sample?A small or chosen group may not represent others.
Which period?A short or unusual period can mislead.
Are costs included?Costs change the net result.
How are gains and losses measured?Different measures give different totals.
Did people leave, and were the same people tracked?People who left can be missing, which changes the picture.

Worked example

Hypothetical group. 1,000 traders. 600 had net losses. 400 had net gains.

GroupNumberTotal resultAverage per trader
Winners400Rs 4,00,000 gainRs 1,000 gain
Losers600Rs 12,00,000 lossRs 2,000 loss
Whole group1,000Rs 8,00,000 net lossRs 800 net loss

Check the whole group: Rs 4,00,000 - Rs 12,00,000 = minus Rs 8,00,000. Divide by 1,000 traders to get minus Rs 800 each.

A percentage alone seems to show a poor result. A different hypothetical group with 400 losers and larger average wins could show a positive net result. The lesson is to read the totals and the method, not one percentage.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Using the worked example, compute the average gain per winner and the average loss per loser. State which average is larger.

Hints:

  1. The average gain per winner is Rs 4,00,000 divided by 400.
  2. The average loss per loser is Rs 12,00,000 divided by 600.
  3. Compare the two averages.

Answer: Average gain per winner = Rs 4,00,000 / 400 = Rs 1,000. Average loss per loser = Rs 12,00,000 / 600 = Rs 2,000. The average loss is larger. That is why the whole group nets a loss, even though 400 traders gained.

Independent exercise

Task. Write the five method questions you would ask of a SEBI study before you quote it.

Check your answer
  1. Which people were in the sample, and how were they chosen?
  2. Which period did the study cover?
  3. Were costs included in the gains and losses?
  4. How were gains and losses measured?
  5. Did people leave the study, and were the same people followed over time?

A good answer also says why each question matters. The checklist in See it gives the reasons.

All figures in the worked example are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks percentage against expected value, the study method, quoting figures, and one scenario.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This chapter has no practice. It asks for reflection only.

  1. Write the five method questions in your learning note.
  2. Write one sentence on why a percentage of losers is not an expected value by itself.
  3. Write one sentence on how you would quote a figure from a study.
  4. Save these in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The figures here are only a short summary. Read the SEBI documents themselves for the method and the full tables. A summary can leave out important details.
  • A study describes a group over a period. It does not forecast any one trader.
  • Even a sound study does not show that your own trades will match the group.
  • Headlines often drop the period, the sample or the costs. Check the method first.
  • Do not quote a figure from memory, even when you remember it well.

Remember

  • A study describes a group over a period. It does not forecast you.
  • A percentage of losers does not give an expected value by itself.
  • Check the sample, period, costs and measures before you quote a study.
  • Quote figures from the source, with the date and period.
  • Survivorship can make a study look better than the full group.

Sources and verification

  • SEBI, “Analysis of Profit and Loss of Individual Traders dealing in Equity F&O Segment”, January 2023 (sebi.gov.in)
  • SEBI, “Analysis of Profits & Losses in the Equity Derivatives Segment (FY22-FY24)”, 23 September 2024 (sebi.gov.in)
  • SEBI, “Profitability of Individual Traders in the Equity Derivatives Segment (FY25-FY26)”, August 2026 (sebi.gov.in)
  • The methodology section of each report

Algo trading and why this book does not automate

Hook

A reader thinks, “If a script can watch prices for me, it can place my orders while I sleep. Then I will never make a mistake.” This chapter explains why that idea is risky. It also explains why this book does not automate orders.

The idea

Algo trading means trading rules run by a program. A rule might say, “If the price crosses a level I wrote, do something.” The something can be one of two very different things.

  • An alert tells you that a level was crossed. It places nothing.
  • An order sends an instruction to a broker to buy or sell. Sending an order is a separate step, and it is regulated.

A trading API is a way for software to talk to a broker. Plans differ. Some plans may not include the market data you need.

Zerodha’s Kite Connect is one example. Its Personal plan is free. It covers orders, GTT orders, alerts, margin checks and portfolio tools, but it gives no live data and no historical data. The paid Connect plan costs Rs 500 per month for each app, and it adds live data and historical candles. Zerodha says historical data cannot be bought alone, and it offers no test sandbox for Kite Connect. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support pages on Kite API charges and FAQs. Plans and prices change, so check support.zerodha.com.)

The SEBI retail algo framework sets rules for brokers and for retail use of algorithms. It includes a way to identify algorithms, called Algo-ID. Here is what the official papers say.

  • SEBI’s circular of 4 February 2025 on safer participation of retail investors in algorithmic trading makes the broker responsible. The broker is the principal, and the algo provider is its agent.
  • Every order sent through an API carries an identifier given by the exchange. This is the Algo-ID.
  • Brokers may not leave APIs open. A client needs an API key tied to that client and a fixed internet address that the broker has approved. Login must use OAuth and two-step checks.
  • An exchange circular of 5 May 2025 sets a limit of 10 orders per second at first. Below that limit, a client’s own algo needs no extra registration through the broker. A client who wants to go above it must register the algo and get an Algo-ID from the exchange. Orders above the limit are rejected.
  • Algo providers must be listed with the exchanges. A provider who sells ready-made “black box” algos must also register as a research analyst.
  • A later SEBI circular of 30 September 2025 set a phased start from 1 October 2025, with brokers’ milestones up to 3 January 2026.
  • Zerodha says a fixed (static) internet address is needed to place orders through its API from 1 April 2026. It can be changed once a week, and family members may share one.

(As of 11 Nov 2026, SEBI circulars of 4 Feb 2025 and 30 Sep 2025, NSE circular of 5 May 2025, and Zerodha support pages. Rules change, so check the SEBI and NSE sites.)

Automation does not remove risk. It can repeat one mistake many times, quickly. A bug can send orders you did not intend. Costs and data limits add up too.

This book is offline. It has no broker calls, no stored login details and no order placement.

See it

Read the diagram from left to right. The first two steps are an alert and your own decision. The last step, sending an order, is not part of this book.

flowchart LR
    A[Price crosses a written level] --> B[Alert to you]
    B --> C[You decide, using your journal]
    C -.->|not in this book| D[Order sent to a broker]

Worked example

Hypothetical, no figures. Two scripts watch the same written price level.

Script AScript B
What it does when the level is crossedSends you a messageSends an order to a broker
Places an orderNoYes
Duties that applyYour own checksBroker and regulatory duties, as in the section above

The code length is not the difference. The order step is the difference. That step brings compliance and risk duties that an alert does not.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Classify five described scripts. For each one, choose a label: alert only, places orders, or needs a compliance check first.

  1. Sends you a message when a share’s price crosses Rs 300. It places nothing.
  2. Buys 10 shares through a broker when the price crosses Rs 300.
  3. Sends a sell order to a broker at a set time each day.
  4. A script you found online. You cannot tell whether it places orders.
  5. Shows a chart and sends you a message when your rule is met. It has no link to any broker.

Hints:

  1. Ask whether any order leaves your machine toward a broker.
  2. A script that only sends a message is an alert.
  3. If you are unsure, choose “needs a compliance check first.”

Answer: 1 is alert only. 2 places orders. 3 places orders. 4 needs a compliance check first. 5 is alert only.

Independent exercise

Task. Write three questions to answer from official sources before you consider any trading API. Do not include a date or a cost in your questions.

Check your answer

Examples of good questions:

  • Which rules apply to a script that sends orders, and who must meet them?
  • What does an Algo-ID require, and when is it needed? (The official circulars answer this. Read them, and do not rely on a summary.)
  • Which broker plans include the data I need, and what does each plan contain?

A good question names the source to check. It does not assume an answer.

Quiz

The quiz checks alerts against orders, free APIs, automation risk, one scenario and one spot-the-mistake.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This chapter has no practice. It asks for reflection only.

  1. Write the three verification questions in your learning note.
  2. Write one sentence on the difference between an alert and an order.
  3. Write one sentence on why automation does not remove risk.
  4. Save these in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready.

Limits and common mistakes

  • SEBI, NSE and BSE circulars and their dates change. Check them at the source before you act.
  • Kite Connect plan contents and charges change. Check the Zerodha support pages.
  • This chapter gives no code and no setup steps for any API.
  • An alert script still needs your own checks. It can be wrong too.
  • Automation does not replace the capital rules. See Risk limits.

Remember

  • An alert tells you something happened. An order sends an instruction to a broker.
  • Sending orders is a separate, regulated step.
  • A free API does not mean no rules apply.
  • Automation can repeat a mistake faster.
  • This book does not automate orders, and it has no broker calls.

Sources and verification

  • SEBI circular of 4 February 2025 on safer participation of retail investors in algorithmic trading
  • SEBI circular of 30 September 2025 on the phased start (glide path)
  • NSE circular of 5 May 2025 on implementation standards for retail algo trading
  • Zerodha support pages on Kite API charges, static IP and Kite Connect FAQs

Microstructure, order flow and auctions

Hook

A reader sees Rs 250.00 on the screen and expects to pay Rs 250.00. The reader imagines a market order for 200 shares. The shares would fill at two prices, not one. A paper log that records only Rs 250.00 hides the difference.

The idea

Microstructure is the way orders meet and prices form. It is the detail behind the price you see.

  • Order book. The order book lists buy orders (bids) and sell orders (asks) at each price. The best ask is the lowest sell price on offer. The best bid is the highest buy price on offer. The spread is the gap between them. See Order matching, spread and liquidity.
  • Depth. Depth is how many shares sit at each price. A thin book has few shares at each price. See Market depth and charts in Kite.
  • Market order. A market order buys or sells now at the prices available. It takes shares one price level at a time. Its average fill can be worse than the best ask.
  • Limit order. A limit order sets the highest price you will pay, or the lowest you will take. See Order types.
  • Price-time priority. At the same price, the earlier order is filled first. This works like a queue at a bus stop. NSE says it stores orders by price first, then by time. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE trading system page. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)
  • Queue position. A limit order waits in that line. A price that touches your limit does not always fill it. Orders ahead of you may take the shares first.
  • Auctions. In an auction, orders are collected first. Then one price is set from them. It works like a village auction where bids come in before one price is chosen. The pre-open session is run this way.

NSE’s pre-open session runs from 9:00 am to 9:15 am. In the first part, you can enter, change or cancel orders. In the middle part, orders are matched at one price and confirmed. The last few minutes are a buffer before the normal session. NSE sets that one price as the price with the most shares that can trade. If more than one price does that, it picks the one with the smallest leftover imbalance. If there is still a tie, it picks the price closest to the previous close. That price becomes the day’s opening price. NSE has changed the exact minutes inside the pre-open session in the past, so read its current page for the split.

NSE also runs a closing auction session for some shares. In the version NSE described in March 2026, the reference price is the volume weighted average price of trades from 3:00 pm to 3:15 pm. The auction runs after 3:15 pm, and the price can move up to 3 percent from that reference. NSE has announced more changes to the closing auction, so read the current NSE circulars for the exact times and the list of shares. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSE pre-open page and NSE circular of 18 Mar 2026. Rules change, so check the NSE site.)

A thin book makes all of this more important. Paper results that ignore these points can look better than real results.

See it

The order book below shows the ask side. Watch the 200-share market buy use the first price level, then the second. Note the average price the order fills at. The numbers are made up.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Best ask Rs 250.00 for 100 shares. Next ask Rs 250.50 for 100 shares. A market buy of 200 shares.

StepCalculationResult
1. First 100 shares100 x Rs 250.00Rs 25,000
2. Next 100 shares100 x Rs 250.50Rs 25,050
3. Total paidRs 25,000 + Rs 25,050Rs 50,050
4. Average fillRs 50,050 / 200Rs 250.25 per share
5. Slippage against the best ask(Rs 250.25 - Rs 250.00) x 200Rs 50

Check: 200 x Rs 250.00 = Rs 50,000. Rs 50,050 - Rs 50,000 = Rs 50. A paper fill at Rs 250.00 would show Rs 50,000 and hide this Rs 50. The amount is small here. In a thin stock the gap can be much wider.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Buy 150 shares with the same book. Compute the average fill and the slippage.

Hints:

  1. The first 100 shares fill at Rs 250.00. The next 50 fill at Rs 250.50.
  2. Slippage is the total paid minus the shares multiplied by the best ask.
  3. Work out the total paid first, then the average.

Answer:

  • First 100 shares: 100 x Rs 250.00 = Rs 25,000.
  • Next 50 shares: 50 x Rs 250.50 = Rs 12,525.
  • Total paid: Rs 37,525.
  • Average fill: Rs 37,525 / 150 = about Rs 250.17 per share.
  • Slippage: Rs 37,525 - (150 x Rs 250.00) = Rs 37,525 - Rs 37,500 = Rs 25.
  • Check: 50 shares, each Rs 0.50 above the best ask, is 50 x 0.50 = Rs 25.

Independent exercise

Task. Explain in writing why a paper fill at the best quoted price overstates the outcome of a market order in a thin book.

Check your answer

A good answer has these points.

  • A paper fill at the best quoted price assumes all shares fill at that one price.
  • In a thin book, the best price level holds few shares. The rest of the order fills at worse prices.
  • The paper result is therefore better than a real market order would give.
  • For a limit order, paper also ignores queue position. A price touch does not mean a real fill.
  • Example, hypothetical: 200 shares at Rs 250.00 on paper gives Rs 50,000. The real cost in the worked example is Rs 50,050.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks average fill, auctions, queue position, one scenario and one spot-the-mistake.

Paper task

Mode: Kite demo, look only. You view the screen and place no order.

  1. Open the Kite demo or the view you have access to. Do not place any order.
  2. Find the market depth view. Read the page for how it shows bids and asks. Write only the fields you can see on your own screen.
  3. Write the fields it shows in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready.
  4. Write one sentence on what the depth view does not show about your own fill.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The book in this chapter is made up. Real books change every second.
  • Auction times, pre-open rules and matching rules change. Check the NSE market timings and pre-open pages.
  • A paper log does not show your place in the queue, or the real fill of a market order.
  • In a thin stock, slippage can be much larger than the Rs 50 in this example.
  • Circuit limits can affect auctions and trading on a given day. The limits differ by share and change over time. This book states none. See the NSE price band pages.

Remember

  • The best ask is the lowest sell price. A market buy can fill above it.
  • The average fill can be worse than the best ask.
  • A limit order waits in a queue. A touch of its price does not guarantee a fill.
  • Auctions collect orders first, then set one price.
  • A paper log that uses only the best price can look better than reality.

Sources and verification

  • NSE market timings and pre-open session pages
  • NSE market microstructure or matching documents
  • Order book, in the Kite chapter, for the order book reference

FII/DII data and its limits

Hook

A news line says, “FIIs bought Rs 600 crore today, so the market will rise.” This chapter shows what that number means. It also shows what the number cannot tell you.

The idea

FII means foreign institutional investors. DII means domestic institutional investors. Both are groups in the published daily data. The official depositories NSDL and CDSL publish foreign investor data under the name FII/FPI. FPI means foreign portfolio investor, which is the term used in current rules. The exact list of what NSE counts as DII is on the NSE FII/DII page, so read it there. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSDL FPI reports page. Rules change, so check the NSDL and NSE sites.)

A crore is ten million rupees, written Rs 1,00,00,000. Daily flow figures are given in Rs crore.

Some figures are provisional. NSDL says the stock exchanges prepare provisional foreign investor data from the trading members’ reports for the trade day. The final figures come later, from the custodians’ reports to the depositories. So a number you read on the day can change. NSE also marks its figures as provisional. (As of 11 Nov 2026, NSDL FPI reports page. Check the NSDL and NSE sites.)

Four terms describe the figures.

  • Gross buy is the total value bought in the day.
  • Gross sell is the total value sold in the day.
  • Net is gross buy minus gross sell. A negative net means net selling.
  • Gross turnover is gross buy plus gross sell. It shows the total activity.

Use the net figure only with its gross context. A net of Rs 600 crore can come from a small amount of activity or a large amount. The gross figure shows the size of the activity.

Three limits matter most.

  1. The data is a total for a group. It does not say which stock was bought or sold, or why.
  2. The data appears after the session. Any use must respect that timing. The exact time of day differs, so check the NSE page for when it updates.
  3. Flow data is one input among many. This book does not treat it as a trading signal.

See it

The table shows the arithmetic for one hypothetical day.

ItemCalculationResult
FII gross buygivenRs 3,000 crore
FII gross sellgivenRs 2,400 crore
Net3,000 - 2,400Rs 600 crore net buying
Gross turnover3,000 + 2,400Rs 5,400 crore
Net as a share of gross600 / 5,40011.1%

The net figure is 11.1% of the gross activity. A reader who sees only “FIIs net buyers Rs 600 crore” misses the size of the gross activity.

Worked example

Hypothetical day, in crore. FII buy Rs 3,000 crore. FII sell Rs 2,400 crore.

  • Net = 3,000 - 2,400 = Rs 600 crore of net buying.
  • Gross turnover = 3,000 + 2,400 = Rs 5,400 crore.
  • Net as a share of gross = 600 / 5,400 = 11.1%. Check: 5,400 x 11.1% is about 600.

The day shows net buying. The same day also shows a large amount of selling. Both facts belong in the reading.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Compute the net and the gross turnover for FII buy Rs 1,800 crore and FII sell Rs 2,100 crore.

Hints:

  1. Net = buy minus sell. A negative number means net selling.
  2. Gross = buy plus sell.
  3. Check that the gross figure is larger than the net figure.

Answer:

  • Net = 1,800 - 2,100 = minus Rs 300 crore. This is net selling.
  • Gross turnover = 1,800 + 2,100 = Rs 3,900 crore.

Independent exercise

Task. Write one paragraph on why “FIIs bought, so the market will rise” is not an inference the data supports.

Check your answer

A good paragraph has these points.

  • The figure is a total for one day, across a whole group.
  • It does not say which stocks were bought, at what prices, or why.
  • It shows what happened, not what will happen next.
  • Prices already reflect many facts. One day of flow does not show a link to later prices.
  • The net figure needs its gross context. Even then, it describes the past day only.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks net against gross, stock-specific limits, one scenario on a day with net selling, timing and one spot-the-mistake.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. This chapter has no practice. It asks for a record.

  1. Record the day’s published FII and DII figures in your learning note.
  2. Write their definitions and units next to the figures.
  3. Write the source and the time you read them.
  4. Save this in your journal, or on paper until the journal app is ready.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Definitions, categories and publication time of the flow data can change. Read the NSE and NSDL pages.
  • Provisional figures can be revised later. Note whether the figure you read is provisional or final.
  • Flow data does not say which stock was bought or sold, or why.
  • Flow data is one input among many. This book does not use it as a trading signal.
  • Net buying does not mean the price will rise. Net selling does not mean it will fall.

Remember

  • FII means foreign institutional investors. DII means domestic institutional investors.
  • Net is buy minus sell. Gross turnover is buy plus sell.
  • Read the net figure with its gross context.
  • Aggregate flow does not name a stock or give a reason.
  • The data appears after the session, so it cannot guide trades made during that session.

Sources and verification

  • NSE FII/DII daily activity page
  • NSDL FPI reports page (fpi.nsdl.co.in)
  • SEBI pages on foreign portfolio investors

10. Graduation gates

  1. Gate G1 market knowledge
  2. Gate G2 money math

Gate G1 market knowledge

Hook

Imagine you read a chapter on share prices and feel sure you understand it. Then a question asks what happens when more buyers than sellers want a share. You freeze. Feeling that you understand is not the same as being able to explain it. This gate checks whether you can explain the basics of the Indian market in your own words.

The idea

This gate covers the market basics from the first chapters of this book. It covers what a share is, how orders meet, how prices move, what an index is and what a corporate action does.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

The gate has two parts. You must pass both.

  1. Quiz. You need at least 85% correct on the questions drawn for you.
  2. Own-words prompts. Each prompt has a short list of points. You tick each point you covered and write a short answer. A prompt is complete only when every point is ticked and your text box is not empty.

Some rules for the quiz:

  • Each set has at least 30 questions. This makes the 85% mark a fair measure.
  • Every topic in the earlier chapters appears at least once in each set.
  • The order of the answer choices changes each time.
  • You can retake straight away after you read the explanations. Each retake has new questions. There is no timer and no penalty.
  • An explanation is shown for every wrong answer. Read them before you retake.

Your attempts, your written answers and your result stay on your device. No result is sent anywhere.

Passing this gate shows that you can explain the market basics. It does not say you should trade. It does not unlock anything in this book.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. The two parts work like this:

PartWhat you doWhat counts as done
QuizAnswer the questions drawn for youAt least 85% correct
Own-words promptsWrite an answer and tick each pointEvery point ticked and the text box not empty

Passing the quiz alone is not enough. Both parts must be complete.

Worked example

Hypothetical. A set of 30 questions. The pass mark is 85%.

85% of 30 is 25.5. You cannot get half a question right, so you need 26 correct.

Correct answersScoreResult for the quiz part
2525 / 30 = 83.3%Not passed. Below 85%.
2626 / 30 = 86.7%Passed. Above 85%.

Even with 26 correct, the own-words prompts must also be complete.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. A hypothetical set of 40 questions. How many correct answers are needed to reach 85%?

Hints:

  1. Multiply 40 by 0.85.
  2. If the answer is a decimal, round up to the next whole number.
  3. Check your answer. Divide it by 40. Is the result at least 85%?

Answer: 40 x 0.85 = 34. This is a whole number, so you need 34 correct answers. Check: 34 / 40 = 85.0%. Then 33 / 40 = 82.5%, which is not enough.

Independent exercise

Task. Write, in your own words and in no more than 40 words, what the order book shows. Then say what the difference between the bid and the ask is called.

Check your answer

A good answer covers these points:

  • The order book lists buy orders and sell orders at different prices.
  • The bid is the highest price a buyer is offering to pay right now.
  • The ask is the lowest price a seller is willing to accept right now.
  • The gap between the bid and the ask is called the spread.

Quiz

The quiz checks the pass mark, the rule for completing prompts and the basic market ideas.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. On a blank page, write four short answers in your own words. Cover what a share is, what the order book shows, what a circuit limit is for and what an index is for.
  2. Read the matching chapter for each answer. Check your answer against it.
  3. Tick each point you covered. Rewrite any answer that is missing a point.
  4. Keep the finished page with your learning notes.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Passing shows that you can explain the basics. It does not show that you can read a chart or choose a trade.
  • The quiz draws from a pool of questions. A pass does not cover every fact in the book.
  • Market rules and timings can change. Check the exchange’s own pages before you rely on any detail.
  • Ticking a point you have not checked defeats the purpose. Read the chapter first, then tick.
  • A common mistake is to think that a familiar word means you understand it. Explain the word in your own sentence to test it.

Remember

  • You need at least 85% correct on the quiz, and every point ticked in each own-words prompt.
  • Each set has at least 30 questions.
  • Read the explanation for every wrong answer before you retake.
  • Passing this gate does not mean you should trade.

Sources and verification

  • The sources named in the earlier market chapters of this book.

Gate G2 money math

Hook

A quick sum says a trade will make Rs 100. You forget the costs. After the costs are taken out, the trade loses money. Small sums matter, and this gate checks that you can do them correctly. It covers costs, position sizes and expectancy. It also asks you to finish the reality check.

The idea

This gate tests the money math from the second track of this book. It covers three kinds of sums: trading costs, position sizes and expectancy. Expectancy is the average result per trade, after you count wins and losses together.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

The gate has three parts:

  1. Numeric questions. Every numeric question in a fresh set must be correct. One miss fails the attempt.
  2. Scenario and multiple-choice questions. These check whether you can choose the right method.
  3. The reality check. You must finish The Rs 2,000-3,000 reality check and tick its checklist.

The rules for each kind of answer are these:

  • Position-size answers must match exactly, in whole shares.
  • Rupee answers must be within Rs 1 of the worked solution.
  • Cost answers are checked within the tolerance stored with each question.

The cost figures come from the book’s facts list. Each figure shows the date it was checked. If a cost figure is out of date, the cost questions pause, and the gate says so.

Each retake uses a fresh set of numbers. A missed numeric question shows the worked solution before you retake. There is no timer.

The Rs 2,000-3,000 per day figure is not a target or a promise. Work through the reality check before you try this gate.

Passing this gate does not mean you should trade. It shows that you can do the sums.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. Use the table below to check your own sums before you start.

SumWhat to check
Risk budgetCapital x risk %
Risk per shareEntry minus stop
SharesThe smallest of the three limits, each rounded down
Planned lossShares x risk per share

If any line does not match your working, go back to the step that differs.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Example B from Position sizing step by step. Capital Rs 10,000. Risk 1%. Entry Rs 250. Stop Rs 248.

StepCalculationResult
1. Risk budget10,000 x 1%Rs 100
2. Risk per share250 - 248Rs 2
3. Raw sharesfloor(100 / 2)50
4. Cash capfloor(10,000 / 250)40
5. Concentration capfloor(2,500 / 250)10
Sharesminimum of 50, 40 and 1010
Planned loss10 x 2Rs 20

The binding limit is the concentration cap. The planned loss is Rs 20 before costs. The costs are covered in Costs on a trade. The arithmetic here does not add them.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Capital Rs 20,000. Risk 1%. Entry Rs 100. Stop Rs 95. Find the shares, the binding limit and the planned loss.

Hints:

  1. Work out the risk budget first. Then find the risk per share.
  2. Find all three limits and round each one down.
  3. The planned loss is the shares multiplied by the risk per share.

Answer:

  • Risk budget: 20,000 x 1% = Rs 200.
  • Risk per share: 100 - 95 = Rs 5.
  • Raw shares: floor(200 / 5) = 40.
  • Cash cap: floor(20,000 / 100) = 200.
  • Concentration cap: floor(20,000 x 25% / 100) = floor(50) = 50.
  • Shares: minimum of 40, 200 and 50 = 40.
  • Binding limit: risk budget.
  • Planned loss: 40 x 5 = Rs 200.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Capital Rs 10,000. Risk 1%. Entry Rs 500. Stop Rs 490. Find the shares, the binding limit, the position value and the planned loss.

Check your answer
  • Risk budget: 10,000 x 1% = Rs 100.
  • Risk per share: 500 - 490 = Rs 10.
  • Raw shares: floor(100 / 10) = 10.
  • Cash cap: floor(10,000 / 500) = 20.
  • Concentration cap: floor(10,000 x 25% / 500) = floor(2,500 / 500) = 5.
  • Shares: minimum of 10, 20 and 5 = 5.
  • Binding limit: concentration cap.
  • Position value: 5 x 500 = Rs 2,500, which is 25% of capital.
  • Planned loss: 5 x 10 = Rs 50.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the five steps of position sizing, the planned loss and how costs and facts are shown.

Paper task

Mode: learning note or paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write three hypothetical position-size sums. Each one needs capital, risk %, entry and stop.
  2. For each sum, write the five steps on separate lines.
  3. Record the shares, the binding limit and the planned loss.
  4. Work each sum twice. Only keep the answer if both results match.
  5. Mark every line as hypothetical.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The gate checks your sums. It does not check whether a trade idea is any good.
  • The sums use made-up numbers. Real costs and rules differ, and they change over time.
  • The gate reads your reality-check tick. It does not test whether you understood the figure behind it.
  • Rounding up is a common mistake. Round every limit down, every time.
  • A correct sum does not prove that a trade fits your real cash. Check your real cash before any real decision.

Remember

  • Shares are the smallest of the three limits, each rounded down.
  • Planned loss is shares multiplied by risk per share.
  • Costs are separate from the planned loss in this gate.
  • Work every sum twice before you trust it.
  • Passing this gate does not mean you should trade.

Sources and verification

  • The book’s cost facts list, with the date each figure was checked.
  • The earlier money math chapters in this book.

Gate G3 Kite readiness

Hook

A plan looks right on screen. The order type is correct, but the product type is set to the wrong option. The error is small to see and large in effect. This gate checks that you know what each Kite feature, order type and product type does. It also checks the common execution mistakes. Passing it does not mean you should trade.

The idea

This gate has two parts. You must pass both.

  1. Demo checklist. Every item from the Kite chapters must be ticked. Each item has a short reflection box. You write what you did and what you noticed. An empty reflection fails that item.
  2. Execution quiz. You need at least 90% correct on the questions drawn for you.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

Some points to keep in mind:

  • This checklist is self-attested. You tick it yourself. The book cannot check your Kite demo.
  • Zerodha does not offer demo accounts for paper trading. It does offer a demo version of Kite that uses dummy data, at kite-demo.zerodha.com/dashboard. Use it to practise the screens, not to test a strategy. (As of 11 Nov 2026, Zerodha support. Check Zerodha support for changes.)
  • Any Kite screen you see in this book is a recreated illustration. It is dated, and menus and names may change. The Kite screens in this book were last checked against Zerodha’s public pages on 11 Nov 2026.
  • The quiz is retakeable, like the other gates. You can re-tick the checklist at any time. Each change is logged with its date.
  • Nothing in this gate asks you to place an order. This book never places orders for you.

Your ticks, reflections, quiz results and dates stay on your device.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. Use this table to see what the checklist needs.

Checklist itemYour tickYour reflection
A Kite feature from the tour chapterTicked after you explored it in the demoWritten, in your own words

A product typeTicked after you can say what it doesWritten, in your own words

An item with a tick but no reflection still fails.

90% of 10 is 9. So you need 9 correct answers.
