Gate G4 risk discipline

Hook

Imagine a simulated day with a daily loss limit of Rs 1,000. Hypothetical. After two losing trades, you are down Rs 1,000. A third setup looks tempting, and you want to win the money back. This gate asks one question: do you stop? It is not a test of market skill. It tests whether you follow a limit you wrote before the day began.

The idea

The gate runs simulated days. Each simulated day is one scenario. The daily limit is hit at a random point in the day, so you cannot know when it will come.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

To pass, you must do both of these in every scenario:

  1. Stop when the limit is hit. “Stopped” means you make no further entry in that day after the limit is reached.
  2. Do not increase your size after a loss. In no scenario may you raise your size after a loss.

You must also answer two multiple-choice questions on the rule set. Both must be correct.

The pass mark is 10 of 10 scenarios.

Each attempt draws ten new simulated days. The earlier attempt does not carry over. There is no timer and no penalty for retaking.

If you fail, the gate shows which day broke the rule and what you did on that day. Then you can retake it.

The simulator is not a test of market skill. It tests whether you follow a written limit.

Passing this gate does not mean you should trade. A pass only shows that you followed the rule in simulated days.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. Use this table to see what each outcome means.

What you did after the limit was hitResult for that day
Made no further entryStopped. Rule kept.
Made one more entry, even a small oneNot stopped. Rule broken.
Raised size after a lossSize rule broken.

One broken rule in one day fails the attempt.

Worked example

Hypothetical. Ten simulated days. The daily limit is Rs 1,000 in each day.

DayLimit hit?What you didResult
1YesStoppedKept
2NoTraded normallyKept
3YesStoppedKept
4YesTook one more tradeBroken
5 to 10MixedStopped each time the limit was hitKept

Nine days kept the rule and one day broke it. That is 9 of 10, which is 90%. The gate needs 10 of 10, so this attempt fails. The result shows day 4 as the broken scenario.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Your daily limit is Rs 1,500. You lose Rs 600 on trade 1 and Rs 900 on trade 2. Have you hit the limit? What should you do next?

Hints:

  1. Add the two losses together.
  2. Compare the total with the limit. Has it reached the limit?
  3. Think about what the rule says to do once the limit is reached.

Answer: 600 + 900 = Rs 1,500. That equals the limit, so the limit is hit. You make no further entry for that day.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Your daily limit is Rs 1,000. Your losses are Rs 350, then Rs 400, then Rs 200, then Rs 100. After which trade is the limit hit? What should you do after that?

Check your answer
  • After trade 1: Rs 350. Not hit.
  • After trade 2: Rs 350 + Rs 400 = Rs 750. Not hit.
  • After trade 3: Rs 750 + Rs 200 = Rs 950. Not hit.
  • After trade 4: Rs 950 + Rs 100 = Rs 1,050. The limit of Rs 1,000 is hit.

The limit is hit after trade 4. You make no further entry for that day. All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the limit arithmetic, the meaning of “stopped” and the size rule.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper. If the simulator is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

  1. Write a hypothetical daily loss limit on paper. Use a made-up figure.
  2. Write three rules under it. Stop when the limit is hit. Make no entry after that. Do not raise size after a loss.
  3. Make a table of ten made-up days. For each day, write the losses in order and the point where the limit is hit.
  4. For each day, write whether you stopped. Mark any day where you did not.
  5. Mark the whole page as hypothetical.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The simulator does not measure market skill. A pass does not show you can make money.
  • Simulated days use random outcomes. Real days can move in ways the simulator does not show.
  • A rule only helps if you write it down before the day starts. Writing it after a loss is too late.
  • Ten of ten is a learning standard. It does not promise how you will act with real money.
  • If a loss is making you want to win it back, read Living through a losing streak before you trade again.

Remember

  • Stop when the daily limit is hit. Make no further entry that day.
  • Never raise your size after a loss.
  • The pass mark is 10 of 10 simulated days.
  • The gate tests a written limit, not market skill.
  • Passing this gate does not mean you should trade.

Sources and verification

  • The book’s rule for daily limits, in the risk chapters of this book.
  • The simulator notes in the earlier risk chapters of this book.

Gate G5 strategy validation

Hook

A backtest of a made-up strategy shows steady gains on past data. You want to believe it. This gate asks you to run the test with costs on, to hold back a period of data, and to explain why the result may not hold in live trading. Explaining the result well matters more than the result itself.

The idea

A backtest runs a set of trading rules on past prices. An in-sample period is the data you used while you shaped the idea. A held-out period is a later stretch of data you did not use to shape the idea. This gate asks you to report both separately.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

To pass, you must complete all of these:

  1. Save a run. Save the run with its record. The record shows the unique code for the data used, the strategy written as settings, the parameters, the version of the test program and the random setting used.
  2. Turn costs on. Costs must be on in the run.
  3. Report two periods. Report the in-sample period and the held-out period separately.
  4. Write the explanation. Cover the result. Check at least three biases, such as look-ahead, survivorship or overfitting. Give the trial count, which is how many settings you tried. Give at least three reasons the result may not hold live. Reasons can include fills, costs, changing market conditions, sample size and liquidity.
  5. Tick the rubric. Each required point in the explanation rubric must be ticked, with text written for it.

The rubric rejects the words “profitable” and “ready” when they describe the result. Describe the result in plain words instead.

Each retake uses a different documented dataset. Earlier runs stay in your history.

A passing explanation is not a validation of a strategy. A strategy that passes this gate still needs forward evidence, shown in Gate G6 paper and forward record. It may still fail live.

If the backtester is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. Use this table to set out the two periods.

PeriodWhat it is forWhat you report
In-sampleShaping the ideaResult, with costs on
Held-outChecking the idea on data you did not use to shape itResult, reported separately

Keep the two rows apart. Never blend them into one number.

Worked example

Hypothetical. A made-up strategy on a made-up capital of Rs 50,000.

PeriodRoleResultPercent of Rs 50,000
In-sampleShaping the ideaRs 4,000 gain8.0%
Held-outChecked laterRs 500 loss-1.0%

The held-out result is Rs 4,500 lower than the in-sample result. That gap is the point to explain. A written explanation might say the idea did not hold on the later data, and list the reasons.

Trial count. You tried 8 settings and kept the best. The explanation must say so. The best of 8 can look better than it really is.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Using the hypothetical table above, state how many rupees lower the held-out result is than the in-sample result.

Hints:

  1. The in-sample result is Rs 4,000.
  2. The held-out result is minus Rs 500, which is a loss.
  3. Subtract the held-out result from the in-sample result.

Answer: 4,000 - (-500) = 4,000 + 500 = Rs 4,500. The held-out result is Rs 4,500 lower.

Independent exercise

Task. In your own words, list three reasons a backtest result may not hold live. Use no more than 60 words in total.

Check your answer

A good answer covers at least three of these points:

  • Fills. The price you get live may differ from the price the test assumed.
  • Costs. Live costs can differ from the costs in the test, or be missed.
  • Changing conditions. Market behaviour in the future may differ from the past period.
  • Sample size. A short test has few trades, so a result can be luck.
  • Liquidity. Some prices may not trade in the size you planned.

Quiz

The quiz checks the two periods, the trial count, the words the rubric rejects and the reasons a result may not hold live.

Paper task

Mode: learning note. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper. If the backtester is not ready, do the task on paper or in a spreadsheet.

  1. Pick one made-up strategy. You can use the example in The fully specified swing strategy.
  2. Split the test period into an in-sample part and a held-out part. Write the dates or the stretch of data for each.
  3. Write the trial count. Count every setting you tried, not only the one you kept.
  4. Write three reasons the result may not hold live.
  5. Write a five-line explanation of the result. Do not use the words “profitable” or “ready”.

Limits and common mistakes

  • The gate checks your explanation. It does not show that the strategy works.
  • One saved run uses one dataset. Other periods can give different results.
  • Costs in a test are only as good as the cost figures used. Real costs can differ.
  • Every setting you try adds a chance of a good-looking result by luck. Count them all.
  • A passing explanation is not a validation. The gate says so on its own screen.

Remember

  • Report the in-sample and held-out periods separately.
  • Keep costs on in every run.
  • State the trial count, including every setting you tried.
  • Give at least three reasons the result may not hold live.
  • Passing this gate does not mean you should trade.

Sources and verification

  • The backtest acceptance notes for the backtester in this book.
  • The strategy chapters and the testing chapters of this book, by their titles.

Gate G6 paper and forward record

Hook

You log 30 paper trades over several weeks. Most follow your written plan. On one day you break your daily limit, and you log it anyway. This gate does not grade how good the trades were. It reads your journal and counts what it shows. Then it reports the counts and the percentages.

The idea

This gate is a record, not a quiz. Nothing is drawn at random, and you cannot retake it in the usual way. It is a record that keeps going. You can keep logging trades at any time.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

The gate checks these points for the same period:

  1. Trade count. At least 30 trades in mode forward or paper. Thirty is a small sample, so treat it as a minimum, not as proof.
  2. Plan adherence. At least 90% of those trades followed your plan.
  3. No limit breaches. The journal’s limit-breach log shows no breach for the same period.

Only these rows count:

  • Forward rows from the forward-test script (Lab 5), counted on their own.
  • Manual paper rows, counted on their own.

Both counts are shown. Backtest rows and live rows are not counted.

Manual entries are self-reported. Forward-script rows can be reproduced. Keep that difference in mind when you read the counts.

You cannot delete entries to improve your count. A deleted trade stays in the record, marked as deleted. If you made a mistake, add a correction note. Do not erase the line.

The gate screen shows the current count and the thresholds in use.

A record of 30 trades does not show that a strategy works. Passing this gate does not mean you should trade.

If the forward-test script is not ready, keep your paper record in your journal or on paper, and do the task as written.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. Use this table to see how each row counts.

Row typeCounts toward the gate?Counted as
Forward-script rowYesIts own count
Manual paper rowYesIts own count
Backtest rowNoNot counted
Live rowNoNot counted

Worked example

Hypothetical. Your journal for one period has 30 trades. 18 are forward-script rows and 12 are manual paper rows. Five backtest rows exist too. They are not counted.

CheckYour figureThresholdResult
Trade count30At least 30Met
Plan adherence27 of 30 = 90.0%At least 90%Met
Limit breaches0NoneMet

Now change one thing. If only 26 of the 30 trades followed the plan, adherence is 26 / 30 = 86.7%. That is below 90%, so the check is not met.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. You have 40 trades in one period. How many must follow the plan to reach 90%?

Hints:

  1. Multiply 40 by 0.90.
  2. Check your answer. Divide it by 40.
  3. Test one fewer. Does it still reach 90%?

Answer: 40 x 0.90 = 36. Check: 36 / 40 = 90%. One fewer, 35 / 40 = 87.5%, is not enough.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. You have 30 trades in one period. Two trades broke your plan. Your journal also shows one limit breach in the same period. What is the plan adherence, and does the gate pass for that period?

Check your answer
  • Plan adherence: 28 of 30 followed the plan. 28 / 30 = 93.3%. This is at or above 90%, so this check is met.
  • Limit breaches: one breach is recorded in the same period. The gate needs no breaches, so this check is not met.
  • The gate does not pass for that period. The adherence figure does not make up for the breach.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the counts, the thresholds and the rules for keeping a record.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Log each paper trade on its own line. Use these columns: date, mode (paper or forward), followed the plan (yes or no), limit breach (yes or no), and a short note.
  2. Keep backtest rows on a separate page. Do not mix them with paper rows.
  3. At the end of the period, count the trades. Work out the percentage that followed the plan.
  4. If you make a mistake, add a correction note on a new line. Do not erase the old line.
  5. Use only paper trades here. Do not place any real order.

Limits and common mistakes

  • Paper and manual entries are self-reported. Your honesty decides how accurate they are.
  • A record of 30 trades does not show that a strategy works.
  • 90% adherence means one trade in ten may break the plan. Read each break and write what you learned.
  • Thirty trades is a floor, not a verdict. The gate says so, and more record gives a clearer picture.
  • A period of paper trades may be one kind of market. Your record does not show how the strategy did in other conditions.

Remember

  • The gate counts your record. It does not judge the trades for being good.
  • At least 30 trades and at least 90% followed the plan, for the same period.
  • No limit breaches are allowed in that period.
  • Forward-script rows and manual paper rows are counted separately.
  • Never delete an entry to improve the count.

Sources and verification

  • The journal rules and the limit-breach log, in the journal chapter of this book.
  • The forward-test chapter of this book, by its title.

Gate G7 live-readiness review and the one-page trading plan

Hook

You write a one-page plan. It covers your capital, your risk per trade, your daily limit and the amount you keep untouched. Then the form shows that your risk per trade is 6%. Hypothetical. The form fails it and says why. This gate checks the plan you wrote. It does not give permission to trade.

The idea

This gate asks you to fill in a written review form. The form has a fixed set of questions. Each one must be filled in.

This measures educational competence. It is not proof of profitability. Nothing here requires or recommends trading with real money.

The form asks for:

  • The capital you plan to use, in rupees.
  • The risk per trade, as a percentage of that capital.
  • Your daily, weekly and monthly loss limits, in rupees.
  • Your stop rules.
  • The most trades you will take in a day.
  • Your withdrawal policy.
  • The amount that stays untouched.

The risk per trade is checked against two levels from the risk tool in this book:

  • At or below 2%, the check passes.
  • Above 2%, the check fails, with a note.
  • Above 5%, the check fails, with the hard note from the risk tool.

The capital you enter is your own number. The book does not set a range for it and does not pick a number for you. It should be money you could lose without hardship. The capital chapters show how to work that out.

To pass, all of these must be true:

  1. Every field is filled in.
  2. The risk per trade is at or below 2%.
  3. The capital figure is filled in, and it is money you could lose without hardship.
  4. Gates G1 to G6 are all passed.

The one-page plan is made from the form. When you print it, you see the saved plan. The print is a view of the saved plan, not a second copy.

This gate does not set a waiting period. Even so, it is wise to put the plan away for a few days, then read it again before you rely on it.

You can edit the form and re-check it at any time. Each save has a date, and older versions stay in your history.

Passing all gates shows a message. It says you may consider a small real-money stage, and it adds caveats. It does not say you are ready to profit. Real money is your decision, made outside this book. You can stop at any time. There is no pressure to go live.

Passing this gate does not mean you should trade. It authorises nothing.

See it

This gate has no picture to explore. Use this table to check the risk line before you go on.

Risk per tradeResult
2% or lessCheck passes
More than 2%Check fails, with a note
More than 5%Check fails, with the hard note

Worked example

Hypothetical. Capital Rs 50,000. Three different risk figures.

Risk per tradeRupees at risk on one tradeResult
1.5%50,000 x 1.5% = Rs 750Passes the risk check
3%50,000 x 3% = Rs 1,500Fails with a note
6%50,000 x 6% = Rs 3,000Fails with the hard note

For this capital, 2% is Rs 1,000 and 5% is Rs 2,500. The rupee levels only help you read the table. The form checks the percentage.

Try it

Guided exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Capital Rs 40,000. Risk per trade 2%. How many rupees is the risk per trade? Does it pass the risk check?

Hints:

  1. Multiply the capital by the percentage. Write 2% as 0.02.
  2. Compare the percentage with the 2% level.
  3. A figure at 2% is still at the level, not above it.

Answer: 40,000 x 0.02 = Rs 800. The figure is 2%, which is at the level, so the risk check passes.

Independent exercise

Task. Hypothetical. Capital Rs 40,000. Risk per trade 3%. How many rupees is the risk per trade? What does the form show?

Check your answer
  • Rupees at risk: 40,000 x 0.03 = Rs 1,200.
  • The figure is above 2% and below 5%. The risk check fails, and the form shows a note.
  • The note asks you to lower the risk per trade and check the figure again.

All figures are hypothetical.

Quiz

The quiz checks the risk levels, the wording of the pass message and how the saved plan works.

Paper task

Mode: paper. Write it in your journal. If the journal is not ready, use paper.

  1. Write a one-page plan on paper with the seven fields from this chapter.
  2. Use made-up numbers only for this practice. Your real plan is a separate decision.
  3. Work out the risk per trade in rupees, using your capital and your percentage.
  4. Check the percentage against the 2% and 5% levels. Write the result beside it.
  5. Keep the page. Do not treat this practice plan as your real plan.

Limits and common mistakes

  • A written plan does not make you ready. It is one check among several.
  • The 2% and 5% levels are warning levels from the risk tool in this book. They are not market rules.
  • The gate does not cover tax. Tax rules are in Taxes on trading profits, and they have their own checks. The main rules, with their dates and sources, were read on 11 Nov 2026. Rates change with each Budget, so check incometax.gov.in each year and ask a qualified tax professional.
  • Passing gives a message with caveats. It does not authorise any trade.
  • Choosing your capital, risk and limits is a personal decision. The book does not choose a number for you.

Remember

  • The risk per trade must be at or below 2% to pass this check.
  • Every field of the form must be filled in.
  • All gates G1 to G6 must be passed first.
  • The printed plan shows the saved plan. It is not a separate copy.
  • Passing this gate does not mean you should trade. It authorises nothing.

Sources and verification

  • The risk tool levels in Risk limits and in the risk chapters of this book.
  • Capital allocation and Scaling and withdrawal policy, chapters of this book.
  • Taxes on trading profits, a chapter of this book, with its own dated sources (read on 11 Nov 2026).

Labs

Tools you can use while you study. Everything runs on your device.

  1. Cost calculator
  2. Forward test
  3. Trade journal
  4. Position size calculator
  5. Strategy tester

Cost calculator

See what one trade really costs on Zerodha, and how far the price must move before you break even.

Type a buy price, a sell price and a quantity. The table shows every charge. The rates come from Zerodha’s own charges page, and the date is shown at the top of the tool.

How to read it

  • Break-even sell price is the lowest sell price where you make no loss after costs.
  • On small delivery trades, the fixed DP charge is a large share of the cost.
  • Intraday costs look small on one trade. They add up fast if you trade every day.

This tool shows costs only. It does not include tax on your profit, and it does not tell you whether to trade.

Forward test

A backtest looks at the past. A forward test looks at days that come after you fix your rule. That makes it a much fairer check.

You run a small script on your own computer once a day, after the market closes. It reads that day’s prices and writes down what your rule would have done. It places no orders. It needs no login and no broker.

Then you bring its two files here. This page adds the trades to your journal in Forward mode and compares them with the same rule on earlier days.

Read the result with care

  • The script assumes you buy at the next morning’s open. Real orders can fill at a worse price.
  • Price files you save yourself can have mistakes. The script stops if it sees an odd price, a missing day or a big jump.
  • Fewer than 30 trades proves very little. Keep going for months, not days.
  • If the new days look much worse than the earlier days, be careful. The earlier result may have been luck.

This is a study tool. It does not tell you what to buy or sell.

Trade journal

Write your plan before you buy. Record what you did. Look at the numbers later.

Your journal stays on this device. It does not need the internet and nothing is sent anywhere. Because the browser can clear its own data, make a backup now and then from the Backup tab.

How to use it

  1. Plan. Write the entry, the stop-loss and your reason before you buy.
  2. Record. After you buy, tap I bought. When you sell, tap I sold. You can sell in parts.
  3. Review. Say whether you followed your plan and how you felt. This is the part that teaches you most.
  4. Look. The Dashboard shows your results. With fewer than 30 closed trades, treat them as a first look, not proof.

R means the loss you planned. A trade that makes twice the planned loss is +2 R.

This tool keeps records. It does not tell you whether to trade.

Position size calculator

Turn “how much can I lose” into “how many shares”, before you place the trade.

Enter your capital, the share of it you are willing to lose on this trade, your entry price and your stop-loss price. The tool shows how many shares fit your rules, and what limits the number.

How to read it

  • Loss if the stop is hit is the planned loss plus costs. It stays inside your risk budget.
  • What limits the size tells you if the risk limit, the position cap or your cash decided the number.
  • Things to check lists limits you set that the trade breaks. It never says a trade is good or bad.

Long trades in the cash segment only. No short selling and no borrowed money.

Strategy tester

Try a simple trading rule on past prices. See what it would have made after costs. Then see how easily a test like this can fool you.

The prices are made up by a computer, so no real share is involved. You can also test on your own price files, one file for each share. Your settings, files and results stay on this device.

How to use it

  1. Pick a rule and press Run the test. Start with the defaults.
  2. Read the result. Look at money at the end, the biggest drop, and the number of trades. Fewer than 30 trades proves very little.
  3. Look at one trade. Pick any trade to see the prices around it, where you got in, and where you got out.
  4. Try the experiments below the result. Each one changes a single thing, so you can see why a good-looking test can still be wrong.
  5. Keep the trades if you want to study them in your journal. They are kept apart from your real trades.

Your own price files

Open Prices: made-up or your own and choose one CSV file for each share, named like RELIANCE.csv. Each file needs the columns date, open, high, low, close and volume. A file with odd prices is refused, and the page tells you why. Use prices already adjusted for splits and bonuses.

The honest rules this tool follows

  • The rule reads the price at the close. It buys at the next morning’s open, never at the price it just saw.
  • If a share opens below your stop-loss, you get the open price, not the stop. Gaps hurt in real life, so they hurt here.
  • Every sale pays the real Zerodha delivery costs.
  • Small slippage is added on each side, because real orders rarely fill at the exact price.
  • You never borrow money.

A good result here does not mean a rule will work with real money. It only means the rule is worth testing further on prices it has never seen.

This tool is for learning. It does not tell you what to trade.
